# The World Economy and Population since 1900 - Live Learning Session

> **What this session teaches:** It teaches the twentieth century as one connected economic and demographic argument rather than as two separate chapters. It begins where the century began - with a world that already had a single economy in 1900, in which a few industrial states supplied manufactures while the rest supplied food and raw materials, and in which colonial powers decided what their colonies grew and fixed the prices at both ends, so that, in the formulation this session keeps, the colonised "had to sell cheap and buy dear". It teaches the transfer of industrial leadership from Britain to the United States, dated by coal and pig iron between 1880 and 1900, and then teaches the two world wars as economic events: the wartime loss of European suppliers that sent China and Latin America to American and Japanese factories, the war loans whose interest made the United States a creditor, the American lending that tied European recovery to Wall Street in the 1920s, and the war production that left the United States making 43 per cent of the world's iron ore, 45 per cent of its crude steel, 60 per cent of its railway locomotives and 74 per cent of its motor vehicles in 1945. It teaches the Great Depression completely and in four movements - causes, transmission, instruments and verdict - holding throughout to the decisive point that the Wall Street Crash was a symptom of a problem whose real causes lay much deeper. It teaches the designed order that followed: Bretton Woods in 1944, the Fund and the Bank, trade liberalisation through the General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade and, from 1995, the World Trade Organization, and the break of the fixed-rate system in 1971, when a dollar once thought as good as gold was devalued by about 12 per cent and a 10 per cent duty was placed on most imports. It teaches the postwar settlement as a contest between two models of capitalism and between two blocs, with the enormous armament and space budgets of the Cold War treated as the opportunity cost that contemporaries themselves named. It teaches the North-South divide as the century's second fault line: the origin of the term "Third World", non-alignment with Nehru as its unofficial leader, the Havana conference of 1979 with 92 nations representing roughly 70 per cent of the world's population, and the Brandt Report of 1980 with its aid targets, its World Development Fund proposal and its almost complete non-implementation by 1985. It teaches why the South stayed poor as six named mechanisms, not as a complaint, and follows them into the debt of $500 billion owed by 1980 on which the annual interest alone was about $50 billion. It teaches the oil decision of 1973 as a mechanism - conservation and revenue, producer surpluses, importer strain, and the commodity reversal recorded in the collapse of what a tonne of copper, cocoa, coffee or cotton could buy between 1975 and 1980 - while marking exactly where the evidence stops. It teaches the split inside the South: Libya's welfare state built on oil, the four Pacific tigers, the relocation of production to cheaper labour, and the labour-standards argument of 1996. It teaches the neoliberal turn through monetarism, deregulation and the definition its own supporters gave it. It teaches globalisation as uneven integration, with the doubling of the world's labour force in the twenty years after 1990, the complaint that globalisation meant Americanization, and the named cases of leverage through the Fund, the trade rules and the patent settlement of 2003. It teaches the crisis of 2008 as the failure of an integrated system - deregulation, sub-prime lending, collateralised debt, leverage of 44 to one, shadow banking and rating agencies paid by the issuers they rated - and then its transmission to Europe and to India, whose exports, currency and current account are the session's clearest Global South illustration. It teaches the environment as economic history, because the growth model that produced the wealth also produced the resource exhaustion, the pollution and the warming, and the awareness of the 1970s belongs in the same paragraph as the oil shock and the North-South debate. It then teaches population as the other half of the same century: the explosion as a mortality event, with growth rates of 0.6, 0.9 and 1.9 per cent in successive phases and a total that passed 2 billion in 1927, 6 billion by 2000 and 7 billion at the end of 2011; the demographic transition as the framework and the handover of the model itself to its own owner; the consequences in ageing Europe and in crowded Asia; the green revolution and its ceiling; the control campaigns from the Geneva congress of 1925 to the family-planning clinics of India and Pakistan and the Chinese one-child policy of 1980 with the coercion its own historians record; the consumption argument that answers the numbers argument; and the HIV/AIDS epidemic as the clearest single proof that health and development are one subject. It closes with the explanatory argument itself - dependency against domestic institutional capability - the discipline of separating correlation from causation, and the architecture that converts a century into marks. Five boundaries are held throughout and stated in the lessons: the first Industrial Revolution belongs to its own owner, the political settlement of the First World War to its owner, the fascist regimes to theirs, Latin America's regional experience to its own topic, and the focused post-1991 political order to the topic that owns it.

## How to read this session

Work through the lessons in order. The order is not the order of the chapters that supply most of the evidence, and the difference is deliberate. A chapter narrates the changing economy, then the divide, then the split, then the environment, then the crisis, and puts population in a separate chapter altogether. That arrangement produces the commonest failing script on this topic: a chronicle of the century with a paragraph about population bolted on at the end. Here the structure of the 1900 world economy comes first, then the wars that broke it, then the Depression that discredited it, then the order designed to replace it - so that by the time the North-South divide appears the reader already knows who designed the rules and why the argument of the 1970s was about their authorship. The oil decision is placed after the divide and before the split, because it is the event that turned one South into several. Globalisation and the crisis of 2008 come after the neoliberal turn, because the crisis is unintelligible without the deregulation that preceded it. The environment comes next, not as an afterthought, but because it states the physical limit of the growth model the previous lessons have described. Population then follows as the century's other great magnitude, and it is taught late among the substantive lessons because its causes - public health, medicine, clean water, income - are themselves outputs of the economic story already told. The explanatory lesson comes last of all, because a theory offered before the evidence becomes a template, and a template is exactly what this topic punishes.

Inside a lesson, take the visual first, then the plain explanation, then the exact evidence, then the objection and the reply, and only then the examination use.

Four labels run through this session. Separating them matters here because this is a part of the syllabus where confident sentences about capitalism, about globalisation and about overpopulation are all easy to write, and where almost none of the confident sentences carry a source.

| Label | Meaning | Use in an answer |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Fact** | Directly supported by a verified source - the two canonical owners held for this topic, the single core world-history account read for this session, the cross-topic owners consulted for boundaries, the verified question ledgers, or a checked item from the one verification pass and its declared correction extension, both recorded in the final ledger | Assert it. Dates, named institutions, recorded policies, stated proportions and stated outcomes can be written flatly |
| ⚠️ **Analytical inference** | Interpretation, weighting, comparison or synthesis built on those facts | Argue it, signal it, and expect a counter-position |
| 📰 **Current linkage** | A dated official item, used exactly once in this session and limited there in the text itself | One dated sentence at most, and never as the substance of a history answer |
| **Dated series figure** | Any percentage, ratio, price, population total or growth rate, always carried with the year it describes and, where applicable, the body that produced it | Attach the year in the sentence. Never let a figure float free of its date |

That fourth label has no symbol because it must be visible in the sentence itself. ⚠️ This is the discipline that separates a world-economy answer from an economics answer. A script that writes "the world economy grew rapidly after 1945" has asserted something no examiner can check; a script that writes "at the end of the Second World War the United States was producing 43 per cent of the world's iron ore and 74 per cent of its motor vehicles" has made a checkable statement about a specific year. ❌ No figure anywhere in this session is rounded, totalled, averaged, updated across years, converted between currencies or merged with a figure of a different date.

Seven disciplines are specific to this topic.

**The mortality-before-fertility discipline.** ⚠️ The single most examined mechanism in the population half of this topic is that the twentieth-century explosion was a mortality event before it was a fertility event. ✅ The account is explicit that the rise in poorer regions came first from modern medical and hygiene techniques bringing killer diseases such as smallpox, malaria and typhoid under control, so that child mortality fell and people lived longer, while the vast majority made no attempt to limit their families. ⚠️ The "explosion" is therefore the widening gap between a death rate that fell quickly and a birth rate that adjusted slowly. ❌ An answer that explains the explosion as "more births" has described the wrong curve.

**The symptom-not-cause discipline.** ✅ The account states flatly that the Wall Street Crash "did not cause the depression; it was just a symptom of a problem whose real causes lay much deeper". ⚠️ This is the most frequently tested discrimination in the whole topic, and it generalises: in this session a visible collapse is never the cause of the condition it reveals. The Crash of 1929, the Lehman bankruptcy of 2008 and the Greek deficit revision of 2009 are all symptoms with structural causes behind them.

**The uneven-integration discipline.** ⚠️ Globalisation in this topic is judged as uneven integration, not as pure success or pure failure. ✅ The evidence for gain is real - some states industrialised rapidly and with great success - and ✅ the evidence for loss is equally real, since the same integration that carried growth carried contagion. ❌ Neither "globalisation lifted the world out of poverty" nor "globalisation impoverished the South" survives contact with the record.

**The internal-divergence discipline.** ✅ The developing world did not move together: some states industrialised far more successfully than others, which is why the single category "Third World" stopped being analytically useful. ⚠️ Any answer that treats the South as one bloc has already lost marks, and the fastest way to demonstrate the opposite is to place a tiger economy beside a one-commodity exporter and explain what differed.

**The correlation-and-causation discipline.** ⚠️ This topic is unusually rich in pairs of things that moved together and unusually poor in proof that one moved the other. Population growth and poverty rose together in some regions; growth and inequality rose together in others; deregulation and the credit boom coincided. ⚠️ In each case this session states the mechanism that would have to be true for the causal claim to hold, and states plainly when the evidence supports only the association. ❌ No sentence in this session infers a cause from a coincidence of dates.

**The no-statistics-dump discipline.** ⚠️ Every figure in this session earns its place by proving a mechanism. ❌ There is no survey of current world output, no table of present-day growth rates, no league table of economies and no contemporary population projection beyond the single dated current item recorded once in Lesson 15. ⚠️ A history answer that opens with this year's growth forecast has announced that it does not know which century it is writing about.

**The boundary discipline.** Five neighbouring topics own material this session touches constantly, and the handovers are stated in the lessons rather than hidden. The first Industrial Revolution and its social consequences belong to the industrial-revolution owner; this session begins from the world that revolution had already made. The political settlement of the First World War belongs to its own owner; this session takes only the economic consequences. The fascist regimes belong to their owner; this session uses German, Italian and Japanese evidence only to show what the Depression did. Latin America's regional experience of import substitution and debt belongs to the Latin American topic; this session teaches the general mechanism and hands the regional case across. The focused political order after 1991 belongs to the topic that owns it; this session stops at the economic character of the integration. The demographic transition model itself, with its stages, criticisms and population-resource debates, belongs to the geography owner; this session owns the historical population question - why the mortality decline happened when it did, what it did to societies, how states responded, and how an epidemic interacted with development.

## Roadmap

Twenty-two lessons. The count is set by the number of distinct causal problems this topic contains, not by a template, and the lessons are deliberately unequal: the Depression needs four because it is examined in four distinct forms, while the environment needs one because its historical claim is single and sharp.

| # | Stage | Lesson | What it settles |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | Foundation | One world economy in 1900, and the two roles inside it | What integration meant before anyone called it globalisation |
| 2 | Foundation | The two wars as economic events | How industrial leadership crossed the Atlantic |
| 3 | Core | The Great Depression I: causes | Why the Crash was a symptom |
| 4 | Core | The Great Depression II: transmission | How an American slump became a world crisis |
| 5 | Core | The Great Depression III: the instruments | What each measure was designed to fix |
| 6 | Core | The Great Depression IV: verdict and counter-case | What the New Deal did and did not achieve |
| 7 | Core | Designing the order: Bretton Woods to 1971 | The first attempt to design a world economy |
| 8 | Core | The postwar settlement and two models of capitalism | Growth, welfare and the state as spender |
| 9 | Core | Two blocs, two systems | Capitalism, socialism and their effect on society |
| 10 | Core | The Third World and the North-South divide | A category, its evidence and the Brandt Report |
| 11 | Core | Why the South stayed poor | Six mechanisms and the debt that followed |
| 12 | Core | Oil, 1973 | From a price decision to a debt trap |
| 13 | Core | The split in the South | Why "the Third World" stopped being one thing |
| 14 | Core | The neoliberal turn | Strain, monetarism and deregulation |
| 15 | Core | Globalisation as uneven integration | What deepened, who gained and who was exposed |
| 16 | Core | 2008: the crisis of an integrated capitalism | How the same channels carried the losses |
| 17 | Core | Growth and its physical limits | The environment as economic history |
| 18 | Core | Population I: the explosion as a mortality event | Why the curve bent when it did |
| 19 | Core | Population II: consequences | Ageing, cities, food and resource diversion |
| 20 | Advanced | Population III: control and its ethics | Welfare route, coercive route and the consumption answer |
| 21 | Advanced | HIV/AIDS as a development shock | Why health and development are one subject |
| 22 | Advanced | Explaining the century | Dependency, institutions and the answer architecture |

Practice is embedded: every lesson ends with a concept check, and cumulative checks follow each major block. Verified question routing, original Mains practice with model answers, remediation, master maps and consolidated register notes come after the lesson sequence.

## Lesson 1 - One world economy in 1900, and the two roles inside it

Progress: 1/22 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: what "a single world economy" already meant in 1900; the division between manufacturers and primary producers; the imperial determination of what colonies produced and at what prices; the American sphere in the western hemisphere; and the transfer of industrial leadership from Britain to the United States

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's chapter summary for the changing world economy, which records British leadership for much of the nineteenth century, the catching-up of Germany and the United States in its last quarter and American industrial primacy by 1914, together with the opening of its first section on the single world economy of 1900
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 15 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one economy, two roles, and the price mechanism that held them apart

```text
   |     - Britain: Uganda and Sudan grow COTTON for Lancashire     |
   |     - Portugal: the same in Mozambique                         |
   |   => the terms of trade were an administrative decision        |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+

                 "the Africans had to sell cheap and buy dear"

   +---------------------------------------------------------------+

   |   area of "influence" - the same relationship without the      |
   |   formal flag                                                  |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+

   AND, UNDERNEATH IT ALL, A LEADERSHIP CHANGE IN PROGRESS
   ------------------------------------------------------
   1880   Britain produces ROUGHLY TWICE as much coal and pig iron as the USA
   1900   reversed: USA produces MORE COAL than Britain
                    and ABOUT TWICE as much pig iron and steel
   1914   the USA is the world's leading industrial nation
```

⚠️ Read the diagram downwards and one thing becomes obvious that most scripts miss: the inequality of 1900 was not only an inequality of wealth but an inequality of *role*, and the roles were assigned rather than chosen.

### 1. Start with the puzzle, not with the indignation

Here is the problem that opens the century. If the world economy of 1900 was already integrated - and it was - why did integration not produce convergence? Trade was flowing, capital was moving, shipping and telegraphy had collapsed distance. On the standard expectation, poorer regions supplying what richer regions needed should have been catching up.

They were not. And the reason lies in the structure itself rather than in anyone's malice. ⚠️ Integration distributes gains according to what each participant is able to supply, and in 1900 what most of the world was able to supply had been decided for it.

### 2. What "a single world economy" actually meant

✅ **Fact.** In one sense, in 1900 there was already a single world economy. ✅ A few highly industrialised countries - mainly the USA, Britain and Germany - provided the world's manufactured goods, while the rest of the world provided raw materials and food, known as **primary products**.

Define the term precisely on first use, because examiners build close options on it. ⚠️ A **primary product** is an unprocessed output of agriculture, forestry, fishing or mining. Its defining economic feature is not that it is agricultural but that almost all of the value added to it - spinning, refining, smelting, roasting, assembling - happens somewhere else. ⚠️ A country that exports primary products exports its raw material *and* its opportunity to add value.

### 3. Who decided what a colony produced

This is the step that converts an economic description into a historical argument.

✅ **Fact.** European nations usually decided what should be produced in their colonies. ✅ The British made sure that Uganda and the Sudan grew cotton for their textile industry; ✅ the Portuguese did the same in Mozambique. ✅ They fixed the prices at which colonial products were sold as low as possible, and also fixed the prices of manufactured goods exported to the colonies as high as possible. ✅ In the historian Basil Davidson's formulation, quoted by the account, "the Africans had to sell cheap and buy dear".

⚠️ Notice what this does to the usual argument about free trade. The relationship was not a market outcome that happened to be unequal; it was an administered outcome designed to be unequal at both ends of the transaction. ⚠️ Two separate margins are being captured: a low price on the way out and a high price on the way in. That double squeeze is why the phrase "sell cheap and buy dear" is worth memorising in exactly those five words - it is a complete mechanism in one clause.

### 4. The variant without a flag

✅ **Fact.** The USA treated Latin America, especially Mexico, as an area of "influence", in the same way that the European states treated their colonies in Africa and elsewhere.

⚠️ This matters for the shape of the whole century. It establishes at the outset that the economic relationship this topic studies does not require formal empire. A state can be legally sovereign and still occupy Role B. ⚠️ When decolonisation arrived after 1945, it removed the flags without automatically removing the roles - which is precisely why the North-South divide of Lesson 10 outlived the empires that created it.

> **Boundary.** The regional history of Latin America in the twentieth century - its revolutions, its import substitution, its debt crisis and its politics - belongs to the Latin American topic. This session takes from it only what is a general fact about the world economy.

### 5. The leadership change already under way

✅ **Fact.** For much of the nineteenth century Britain led the rest of the world in industrial production and trade. ✅ In the last quarter of the century, Germany and the USA began to catch up, and by 1914 the USA was the world's leading industrial nation.

✅ The account dates the crossing with two commodities. ✅ In 1880 Britain produced roughly twice as much coal and pig iron as the USA. ✅ By 1900 the roles had been reversed: the USA produced more coal than Britain and about twice as much pig iron and steel.

⚠️ Two things deserve notice. First, the crossing happened *before* either world war, which disposes of the lazy claim that the wars alone made the United States dominant; the wars accelerated and confirmed a shift already complete in heavy industry. Second, coal and pig iron are the right indicators for 1900 precisely because they are inputs rather than outputs - they measure the capacity to build an industrial economy rather than the current size of one.

### 6. Why the pre-1914 picture belongs in this topic at all

⚠️ The required scope of this topic runs from 1900, and the pre-1914 world is included only so far as the later century cannot be explained without it. Three things are needed and no more:

1. ⚠️ that integration already existed, so that nothing after 1945 can be described as the world economy becoming connected for the first time;
2. ⚠️ that the roles within that integration were structured and, in the colonial case, administered; and
3. ⚠️ that industrial leadership was already moving west.

> **Boundary.** The origins of industrialisation itself - the technologies, the factory system, urbanisation, and the labour and social consequences of the first Industrial Revolution - belong to the industrial-revolution owner. This session begins from the world that process had already produced.

### Comparative note: why this is not "the colonies were exploited" in different words

⚠️ The difference is analytical, and it is worth stating because it is the difference between two mark bands. "Exploitation" is a moral verdict; it does not tell an examiner what happened to prices, to industrial capacity or to the terms of trade. The mechanism does. ⚠️ Say instead: production decisions were externally determined; output was concentrated in goods with little value added; prices were administratively suppressed on the export side and supported on the import side; and the resulting structure was self-reinforcing, because a country earning little from its exports could not finance the industry that would have let it export something else. ⚠️ Every clause in that sentence can be checked. The moral verdict can only be agreed with or disagreed with.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The General Studies Paper I syllabus covers world history from the eighteenth century onward, including the industrial revolution, colonisation and decolonisation, and political philosophies such as capitalism, socialism and communism, their forms and their effect on society. ⚠️ This lesson is the economic foundation on which the colonisation clause and the capitalism clause meet.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ This lesson is almost never examined alone. It is the opening movement of an answer on changes in the world economy since 1900, on why the North-South gap persisted, or on whether globalisation is new. ⚠️ The mark is earned by compressing it to three or four sentences that establish *integration without convergence*, not by narrating imperial history again.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing that globalisation began in the 1990s. ✅ A single world economy is recorded as already existing in 1900; what changed later was its depth, its speed and the mobility of finance and production.
2. ⚠️ Treating the colonial price structure as an ordinary market outcome. ✅ Prices were fixed at both ends as a matter of policy.
3. ⚠️ Dating American industrial leadership to 1945. ✅ By 1914 the USA was already the world's leading industrial nation, and the coal and pig-iron crossing happened by 1900.
4. ⚠️ Assuming formal empire was necessary for the relationship. ✅ The American treatment of Latin America, especially Mexico, is recorded as the same relationship conducted as "influence".

**Answer use.** ⚠️ One sentence to deploy: "The twentieth century did not begin by integrating the world economy; it began with an integrated world economy in which the roles had already been assigned, and the century's argument was about whether those roles could be changed."

### Revision notes

1. ✅ In 1900 there was already a single world economy: a few highly industrialised countries - mainly the USA, Britain and Germany - provided manufactured goods; the rest supplied raw materials and food, called primary products.
2. ✅ European nations usually decided what their colonies produced: Britain made Uganda and the Sudan grow cotton for its textile industry; Portugal did the same in Mozambique.
3. ✅ Prices were fixed as low as possible for colonial products and as high as possible for manufactured exports to the colonies.
4. ✅ Basil Davidson's formulation, as quoted: "the Africans had to sell cheap and buy dear."
5. ✅ The USA treated Latin America, especially Mexico, as an area of "influence", in the same way European states treated their colonies.
6. ✅ For much of the nineteenth century Britain led the world in industrial production and trade; in its last quarter Germany and the USA began to catch up.
7. ✅ In 1880 Britain produced roughly twice as much coal and pig iron as the USA; by 1900 the USA produced more coal than Britain and about twice as much pig iron and steel.
8. ✅ By 1914 the USA was the world's leading industrial nation.
9. ⚠️ A primary product is defined by where the value is added, not by whether it is agricultural; exporting it exports the chance to add value.
10. ⚠️ Integration without convergence is the master formulation of this lesson: gains are distributed according to what each participant can supply, and what most of the world could supply had been decided for it.
11. ⚠️ Formal empire is not necessary to the relationship; sovereignty and Role B are compatible, which is why decolonisation did not by itself end the divide.
12. ⚠️ Boundary: the first Industrial Revolution belongs to its own owner; the Latin American regional experience belongs to the Latin American topic.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes that "globalisation created a divided world economy at the end of the twentieth century". Using the structure of 1900, identify exactly what is wrong with that sentence and rewrite it accurately.

**Model answer:** Two things are wrong, one chronological and one causal. Chronologically, the world economy was already single in 1900: a few highly industrialised states - mainly the USA, Britain and Germany - supplied manufactures while the rest of the world supplied primary products, so integration is not a late-century invention. Causally, integration did not create the division; it operated on a division that already existed and had in part been administered into existence, since European nations usually decided what their colonies produced - cotton in Uganda and the Sudan for British textiles, the same in Mozambique for Portugal - and fixed colonial export prices as low as possible while fixing manufactured import prices as high as possible, which is what Basil Davidson's phrase "sell cheap and buy dear" compresses. The accurate rewriting is: "The world economy was already integrated and already divided by role in 1900; the late twentieth century deepened and accelerated that integration, and in doing so redistributed the gains and the risks rather than creating the division." That version is checkable, and it leaves room for the genuine later change, which was the mobility of finance and production rather than the fact of connection.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "integrated" and "converging" as the same word. They are opposites in this topic. Integration distributes gains according to existing capability, so an economy able to supply manufactures gains from the same trade that keeps an economy supplying only primary products where it is. An answer that assumes connection must produce equalisation has assumed away the entire subject.

## Lesson 2 - The two wars as economic events: 1914-18, 1939-45 and the making of American dominance

Progress: 2/22 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: wartime disruption of European supply and the redirection of trade; war loans and the creditor position; American lending and the financial tying of Europe in the 1920s; the Second World War as industrial mobilisation; the American production shares of 1945; the conversion to consumer goods; and Marshall Aid with its recorded motives

⚠️ The diagram is built so that its two halves rhyme. Both wars moved production to the United States; the difference is that after 1918 the United States lent into the vacuum without designing anything, while after 1945 it lent into the vacuum with a design attached. Lesson 7 is about that design.

### 1. Why wars belong in an economic topic at all

Because they reallocate production faster than any peacetime process can. A war removes suppliers, guarantees demand and suspends the usual rules about government spending, and it does all three at once. ⚠️ The historian's question is never whether a war disturbs an economy - it always does - but which of its disturbances survive the peace.

### 2. The First World War: three surviving changes

✅ **Fact.** The First World War and its aftermath gave a big boost to the American economy.

**The trade switch.** ✅ Many countries which had bought goods from Europe during the war - such as China and the states of Latin America - were unable to get hold of supplies because the war had disrupted trade. ✅ This forced them to buy goods from the USA, and also Japan, instead, and after the war they continued to do so.

⚠️ The decisive clause is the last one. A wartime substitution became a peacetime habit. ⚠️ The mechanism is not mysterious: once purchasing relationships, credit lines, spare-part supplies and technical standards have been rebuilt around a new supplier, switching back is costly. This is the first appearance in the topic of a pattern that recurs at every stage - an emergency creates an arrangement, and the arrangement outlives the emergency.

**The creditor position.** ✅ The USA was the economic winner of the First World War and became even richer thanks to the interest on the war loans it had made to Britain and her allies.

⚠️ Note precisely what this means and what it does not. It does not mean that America grew rich by lending; it means that the war converted the United States from a destination for European capital into a creditor of European governments. ⚠️ That reversal of direction is the structural fact, and Lesson 3 will show its sting: a creditor that also raises tariffs makes it impossible for its debtors to earn the currency in which the debt is owed.

**The lending that tied Europe to Wall Street.** ✅ Only the USA was rich enough to provide loans to encourage German recovery during the 1920s, ✅ but this had the unfortunate effect of linking Europe too closely with the USA financially and economically. ✅ When the USA suffered its great slump, dated in the account as 1929-35, Europe and the rest of the world were also thrown into depression.

✅ The scale is recorded: in **1933** American unemployment stood at around **14 million**, about **a quarter** of the total labour force, and industrial production was only **half** the 1929 total.

> ⚠️ **Source divergence, stated rather than resolved.** ⚠️ The core account's chapter on the changing world economy separately states that in 1933 about **25 million** people were out of work in the USA and as many as **50 million** in the world as a whole, while its Depression chapter gives the American figure as around **14 million**, about a quarter of the labour force. ❌ The two American numbers cannot both be measuring the same quantity, and nothing held here establishes which definition each uses. ✅ Write the canonical measure - around 14 million, about a quarter of the labour force - and, if the larger pair is used at all, attribute it to the account and mark it as a divergent measure rather than a flat fact. ❌ Derive no ratio between the American and world totals from them, because a ratio computed across two incompatible measures is not evidence of anything.

### 3. The Second World War: mobilisation and its output

✅ **Fact.** The Second World War left the USA the world's greatest industrial and military power. ✅ The Americans entered the war relatively late and their industry did well out of supplying war materials for Britain and her allies.

✅ At the end of the war, with Europe almost at a standstill economically, the USA was producing **43 per cent** of the world's iron ore, **45 per cent** of its crude steel, **60 per cent** of its railway locomotives and **74 per cent** of its motor vehicles.

⚠️ Learn these four figures as a set rather than individually, because their pattern is the argument. The American share rises as you move along the chain from raw material towards finished manufacture - 43 per cent of the ore, but 74 per cent of the cars. ⚠️ That gradient is the clearest single measure of what the war did: it did not merely leave the United States with more resources; it left the United States with a near-monopoly of complex assembly.

✅ The income comparison of the same moment completes the picture: in 1945 incomes in the USA were twice as high as in Britain and seven times higher than in the USSR, and during the next thirty years American production almost doubled again.

### 4. Reconversion: why 1945 did not repeat 1919

✅ **Fact.** When the war was over, the industrial boom continued as industry switched to producing consumer goods, which had been in short supply during the war.

⚠️ This is a genuinely important non-event, and strong answers notice it. Wartime economies usually face a demobilisation slump: government orders vanish, servicemen return to the labour market, and demand collapses. It did not happen, because the war had built up an unspent stock of consumer demand behind rationing and shortage, and because the physical plant built for war could be turned to civilian goods. ⚠️ The point generalises to Lesson 8: postwar growth had a demand foundation, and it lasted as long as that demand kept being renewed.

### 5. Marshall Aid, and the honest statement of its motives

✅ **Fact.** Once again, only the USA was rich enough to help western Europe, which it did with Marshall Aid.

✅ And the account is careful about motive: it was not simply that the Americans wanted to be kind to Europe; they had at least two other ulterior motives - ✅ a prosperous western Europe would be able to buy American goods and thus keep the great American wartime boom going; ✅ a prosperous western Europe would be less likely to go communist.

⚠️ Notice the analytical shape of that sentence. It does not deny generosity; it refuses to treat generosity as a complete explanation. ⚠️ That is exactly the register an examiner rewards in a question about motive: state the declared purpose, state the interest it served, and decline to reduce the action to either one.

### Criticism and reply: is this just "the USA won both wars economically"?

**The objection.** ⚠️ A critic could say this lesson is a single claim repeated twice, and that repeating it adds nothing.

**The reply.** ⚠️ The two wars produced structurally different American positions, and the difference is examinable. After 1918 the United States became a creditor without becoming a manager: it lent, but it raised tariffs against the goods with which the loans could have been repaid, and it built no institution to handle the resulting strain. After 1945 it became a creditor *and* a designer: it lent through a public programme with named political objectives, and it had already, in 1944, helped construct institutions intended to prevent the previous sequence from recurring. ⚠️ The distinction can be put in one line - **1918 produced American money without American rules; 1945 produced American money with American rules** - and that line, deployed in an answer, converts a narrative into an argument.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ World history from the eighteenth century onward, with the two world wars examined for their consequences as well as their causes. ⚠️ This lesson supplies the economic consequence that the political owners of the war topics hand across.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Rarely alone. It is the second paragraph of "trace the major changes in the world economy since 1900" and the first paragraph of any answer on how the Depression became global. ⚠️ It can also be deployed in a question on why the United States became the dominant power of the twentieth century, where the four production shares of 1945 do more work than any amount of assertion.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Saying the wars made the United States the leading industrial nation. ✅ It already was by 1914; the wars confirmed and extended the lead.
2. ⚠️ Treating the 1920s American loans to Germany as simple assistance. ✅ The account records the consequence as linking Europe too closely with the USA financially and economically, which is why the slump transmitted.
3. ⚠️ Presenting Marshall Aid as pure altruism or as pure self-interest. ✅ Both the assistance and the two ulterior motives are recorded; an answer must carry both.
4. ⚠️ Quoting the larger unemployment pair as a flat fact. ✅ Around 14 million, about a quarter of the total labour force, is the canonical American measure for 1933; the account's separate 25 million American and 50 million world figures diverge from it, so attribute them to the account if they are used at all and compute no share of world unemployment from them.

> **Boundary.** The military course of the wars, the peace settlement of 1919 and the diplomacy of the 1930s belong to the owners of those topics. This session takes only what changed in production, trade and credit.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The First and Second World Wars caused important changes in the world economy, and the USA gained most economically from both.
2. ✅ Wartime disruption forced China and the states of Latin America to buy from the USA and Japan, and after the war they continued to do so.
3. ✅ The USA was the economic winner of the First World War and became richer still through interest on war loans to Britain and her allies.
4. ✅ Only the USA was rich enough to lend for German recovery in the 1920s; the effect was to link Europe too closely to the USA financially and economically.
5. ✅ When the USA suffered its great slump, dated 1929-35, Europe and the rest of the world were thrown into depression.
6. ✅ In 1933 American unemployment stood at around 14 million, about a quarter of the total labour force, with industrial production only half the 1929 total. ⚠️ The account's separate pair - about 25 million in the USA and as many as 50 million worldwide - diverges from that measure; attribute it if it is used and derive no ratio from it.
7. ✅ The Second World War left the USA the world's greatest industrial and military power; it entered late and its industry did well supplying war materials to Britain and her allies.
8. ✅ At the end of the war the USA produced 43 per cent of the world's iron ore, 45 per cent of its crude steel, 60 per cent of its railway locomotives and 74 per cent of its motor vehicles.
9. ✅ In 1945 incomes in the USA were twice as high as in Britain and seven times higher than in the USSR; over the next thirty years American production almost doubled again.
10. ✅ The boom continued after 1945 as industry switched to consumer goods that had been in short supply during the war.
11. ✅ Marshall Aid was possible because only the USA was rich enough; its two recorded ulterior motives were keeping the American boom going through European purchases and making western Europe less likely to go communist.
12. ⚠️ Master formulation: 1918 produced American money without American rules; 1945 produced American money with American rules.
13. ⚠️ The 1945 share gradient - 43 per cent of ore rising to 74 per cent of motor vehicles - measures dominance in complex assembly, not merely in resources.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the American loans of the 1920s are treated in this topic as a cause of the Depression's international spread rather than as a separate act of assistance.

**Model answer:** Because of what they did to the structure of obligations rather than because of anything wrong with the lending itself. After 1918 the United States was the only state rich enough to provide the loans that encouraged German recovery, and the account records the consequence directly: the effect was to link Europe too closely with the USA financially and economically. That linkage created a channel of a specific kind. European recovery was now financed by American credit, so European solvency became a function of American conditions rather than of European production alone. The channel was also fast, because credit can be withdrawn in days whereas trade flows change over seasons. Hence when the USA suffered its great slump, dated in the account as 1929-35, Europe and the rest of the world were also thrown into depression, and by 1933 American unemployment stood at around 14 million, about a quarter of the total labour force. The loans are therefore not a separate topic from the Depression's transmission; they are the wiring along which the transmission travelled, which is why Lesson 4 treats credit withdrawal as the first and fastest of the channels.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the lending was therefore a mistake or a trap. Nothing in the record supports that reading, and an answer that adopts it has substituted blame for mechanism. The recovery the loans financed was real; the vulnerability they created was a by-product of its being externally and short-term financed. The examinable point is structural - external short-term finance transmits the lender's crises - and it is the same point that returns in the debt sequence of Lesson 12 and in the capital flows of Lesson 16.

## Lesson 3 - The Great Depression I: the Crash was a symptom, not the cause

Progress: 3/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: domestic overproduction and the self-reinforcing contraction; the unequal distribution of income and the limits of credit; falling export demand and the tariff that strangled it; speculation and self-fulfilling expectation; and the Crash of October 1929 as symptom rather than cause

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's sections on the arrival of the Great Depression in October 1929, comprising its four numbered causes, the speculation narrative with its share-price series, the Crash of 24 October 1929 and the explicit statement that the Crash did not cause the depression
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 15 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four causes, one symptom, and the conditional that examiners reward

```text
   CAUSE 1: DOMESTIC OVERPRODUCTION            CAUSE 2: UNEQUAL INCOME
   -------------------------------             -----------------------
   high profits + mechanization                industrial WAGES  +8%  (1923-29)
        -> too many goods for the                 (only +1.4% in real terms)
           home market to absorb                industrial PROFITS +72% (1923-29)
        -> unsold stocks build up                      |
        -> manufacturers produce less                  v
        -> men laid off                        not enough buying power to
        -> NO unemployment benefit             sustain the boom; credit only
        -> those families buy less             postpones the limit
        -> [back to the top of the loop]       "by 1929 they were fast
                                                approaching the limit"
                \                                      /
                 \                                    /
                  v                                  v
        ==>  "Even so, a slump was still NOT INEVITABLE, provided the
              Americans could export their surplus products."
                                 |
                                 v
   CAUSE 3: FALLING EXPORT DEMAND - and why
   ----------------------------------------
   Fordney-McCumber tariff (1922) keeps foreign goods out
        -> Europe cannot earn dollars from trade with the USA
        -> Europe cannot buy American goods
        -> Europe cannot service its war debts
        -> "many states retaliated by introducing tariffs against
            American goods"
        => "A slump of some sort was clearly on the way."
                                 |
                                 v
   CAUSE 4: SPECULATION (momentum from about 1926)
   -----------------------------------------------
   average share value    $9 (1924)  ->  $26 (1929)
   Radio Corporation      $85 (early 1928) -> $505 (Sept 1929)
                          ... and it PAID NO DIVIDENDS
   savings spent, money borrowed, brokers selling on credit,
   banks speculating with depositors' cash
                                 |
                                 v
   THE SYMPTOM: 24 October 1929, "Black Thursday"
   ----------------------------------------------
   nearly 13 million shares dumped at very low prices
   by mid-1930: about 25% of the previous year's peak
   rock bottom: 1932
                                 |
                                 v
   "The Wall Street Crash did not cause the depression; it was just a
    symptom of a problem whose real causes lay much deeper."
```

⚠️ The diagram is deliberately arranged so that the conditional sits at its centre rather than at its end. That conditional - a slump was still not inevitable provided exports held - is the sentence that converts a list of causes into an argument, because it tells you which cause was load-bearing.

### 1. The learner's problem

Every candidate knows that the Wall Street Crash and the Great Depression are connected. Almost every candidate states the connection backwards. ⚠️ The Crash is the most visible event in twentieth-century economic history, and visibility is routinely mistaken for causation.

So begin with the question the examiner is actually asking. If a stock market collapse caused the Depression, why did an economy with full employment and record output in 1928 have unsold stocks, falling production and lay-offs already building? ⚠️ Something was wrong before the share prices fell, and the four causes below describe what it was.

### 2. Cause one: domestic overproduction, stated as a loop

✅ **Fact.** American industrialists, encouraged by high profits and helped by increased mechanisation, were producing too many goods for the home market to absorb - in the same way as the farmers. ✅ This was not apparent in the early 1920s, but as the decade closed, unsold stocks of goods began to build up, and manufacturers produced less. ✅ Since fewer workers were required, men were laid off; ✅ and as there was no unemployment benefit, these men and their families bought less. ✅ And so the vicious circle continued.

⚠️ Read that as a loop rather than as a list, because the loop is the examinable content. Falling output *causes* the falling demand that then justifies the next cut in output. ⚠️ The absence of unemployment benefit is not a moral detail: it is the reason the loop had no floor. In an economy with income support, a lay-off reduces spending partially; in an economy without it, a lay-off reduces a household's spending to almost nothing, so the contraction of demand tracks the contraction of employment one for one.

⚠️ Note also that the account applies the same logic to farmers. ❌ Do not present overproduction as an industrial problem only; agriculture was producing a glut on exactly the same terms, which is why Lesson 5 finds an entire family of policy instruments aimed at farm output.

### 3. Cause two: an income distribution too narrow to absorb the output

✅ **Fact.** The enormous profits being made by industrialists were not being distributed equally among the workers. ✅ The average wage for industrial workers rose by about **8 per cent** between 1923 and 1929, but during the same period industrial profits increased by **72 per cent**. ✅ An 8 per cent increase in wages - only **1.4 per cent** in real terms - meant that there was not enough buying power in the hands of the general public to sustain the boom.

✅ The account continues with precision: they could manage to absorb goods produced for a limited time, with the help of credit, but by 1929 they were fast approaching the limit. ✅ Manufacturers, usually super-corporations, were not prepared to reduce prices or to increase wages substantially, and so a glut of consumer goods built up. ✅ At the beginning of 1929 there were still millions of Americans who had no radio, no electric washing machine and no car because they could not afford them.

⚠️ Two analytical moves follow, and both are worth marks.

First, **credit is a postponement, not a solution**. Buying on credit raises today's demand by borrowing from tomorrow's; it therefore conceals the gap between output and purchasing power for as long as the borrowing can be serviced and not one month longer. ⚠️ This is the earliest appearance in the topic of a mechanism that recurs exactly in Lesson 16, where consumption in the 2000s was sustained by "a massive increase in credit" while real wages in the leading western economies fell.

Second, the unsold goods and the unmet wants coexist. ⚠️ Millions of Americans wanted radios, washing machines and cars while warehouses held unsold radios, washing machines and cars. That is not a shortage of desire or a shortage of production; it is a mismatch of distribution, which is precisely why the syllabus phrase about the effect of economic systems on society belongs here.

### 4. The conditional that decides the answer

✅ **Fact, and the single most useful sentence in this lesson:** even so, a slump was still not inevitable, provided the Americans could export their surplus products.

⚠️ Quote the conditionality. It does three things at once. It concedes that the domestic causes alone were not sufficient; it identifies the export route as the available escape; and it therefore makes the tariff regime of cause three the decisive variable rather than a supporting one. ⚠️ An answer that states the four causes without the conditional has listed them; an answer that states the conditional has ranked them.

### 5. Cause three: falling export demand, and the self-defeating tariff

✅ **Fact.** Exports began to fall away, partly because foreign countries were reluctant to buy American goods when the Americans themselves put up tariff barriers to protect their industries from foreign imports. ✅ Although the **Fordney-McCumber tariff of 1922** helped to keep foreign goods out, at the same time it prevented foreign states, especially those in Europe, from making much-needed profits from trade with the USA. ✅ Without those profits, the nations of Europe would be unable to afford American goods, and they would be struggling to pay their war debts to the USA. ✅ To make matters worse, many states retaliated by introducing tariffs against American goods. ✅ A slump of some sort was clearly on the way.

⚠️ State the contradiction in its sharpest form, because it is the bridge from a national to a world crisis: **a creditor cannot be repaid in goods it refuses to admit**. The United States was simultaneously demanding dollar repayment of war debts and closing the only door through which Europeans could earn dollars. ⚠️ Combine that with the lending of Lesson 2 and the position becomes clear: Europe was paying American debts with American loans, which is a circuit rather than a settlement, and circuits stop when the lender stops.

### 6. Cause four: speculation, and the mechanism that made it collapse

✅ **Fact.** The situation was worsened by a great rush of speculation on the New York stock market, which began to gather momentum about **1926**. ✅ Speculation is the buying of shares in companies; people with cash to spare did it either to get the dividend - the annual sharing-out of a company's profits among its shareholders - or to make a quick profit by selling the shares for more than they paid.

✅ In the mid-1920s it was the second motive that most attracted investors. ✅ The average value of a share rose from **$9 in 1924** to **$26 in 1929**. ✅ The stock of the Radio Corporation of America stood at **$85** a share early in **1928** and had risen to **$505** in **September 1929** - ✅ and this was a company which did not pay dividends.

⚠️ Pause on that last clause, because it is the diagnostic fact of the entire bubble. A share that pays no dividend has value only if someone else will pay more for it later. Its price therefore measures expectation and nothing else.

✅ The account records how far the practice spread: ordinary people spent their savings or borrowed money to buy a few shares; stockbrokers sold shares on credit; banks speculated in shares using the cash deposited with them. ✅ There was enormous confidence that prosperity would continue indefinitely.

✅ Then the reversal, and the account names its mechanism precisely. ✅ When the first signs appeared that sales of goods were slowing, some better-informed investors decided to sell while prices were still high; suspicion spread; confidence wavered; more people sold. ✅ This is what economists call **self-fulfilling expectation** - by their own actions, investors actually caused the dramatic collapse of share prices which they were afraid of.

⚠️ Learn the term and use it. It is a more powerful explanation than "greed" because it is symmetrical: the same mechanism inflated the boom, since each purchase confirmed the expectation of rising prices. ❌ Do not moralise about greed in an answer; name the expectation mechanism and you have explained both halves of the cycle with one idea.

### 7. The Crash itself, and its exact status

✅ **Fact.** By October 1929 there was a flood of people rushing to sell shares, but because confidence had been shaken, there were far fewer people wanting to buy. ✅ One especially bad day was **24 October - "Black Thursday"** - when nearly **13 million** shares were dumped on the stock market at very low prices. ✅ By mid-1930 share prices were, on average, about **25 per cent** of their peak level the previous year, and still falling. ✅ Rock bottom was reached in **1932**.

✅ And then the sentence on which this lesson turns: **"The Wall Street Crash did not cause the depression; it was just a symptom of a problem whose real causes lay much deeper."**

✅ The account also records the immediate mechanism by which the symptom did real damage: many people in financial difficulties rushed to the banks to draw out their savings, so thousands of banks had to close; as the demand for goods fell, factories closed down and unemployment rose alarmingly. ✅ The great boom had suddenly turned into the Great Depression, and because it rapidly affected other countries as well, it became known as the **world economic crisis**.

⚠️ Hold the distinction carefully, because it is subtle and examiners test the subtlety. The Crash was not the cause of the Depression, but it was not irrelevant either: it destroyed confidence, triggered the bank runs and converted a developing contraction into a collapse. ⚠️ The precise formulation is that the Crash was a **transmission event, not a causal one** - it changed the speed and the violence of the slump without creating the conditions that produced it.

### Criticism and reply: is "symptom, not cause" too neat?

**The objection.** ⚠️ If the Crash destroyed savings, closed banks and ended confidence, in what useful sense was it not a cause? A distinction that survives only by definition is not worth marks.

**The reply.** ⚠️ The distinction survives on evidence, not definition, and the evidence is the timing. Unsold stocks, reduced production and lay-offs are recorded as building *before* the Crash; the wage-profit gap ran from 1923; the tariff dates from 1922; the speculative momentum from 1926. ⚠️ A market collapse in 1929 cannot explain conditions present in 1928. What it can explain is why the adjustment was catastrophic rather than gradual. ⚠️ The honest formulation therefore keeps both halves: the causes made a slump likely and the Crash made it violent - and an answer that says exactly that will outscore one that picks a side.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ World history from the eighteenth century onward; the Depression is the world economy's single most examined episode and it is also the causal variable used by the topics on interwar international relations and on the collapse of parliamentary democracy. ⚠️ Those topics use the Depression; this lesson owns its mechanism.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Three distinct demand families exist and they require different answers: *what caused it* (this lesson), *how it became a world crisis* (Lesson 4), and *what policy instruments were deployed to contain it* (Lesson 5). ⚠️ Identify which one has been asked before writing a word; a causes answer given to an instruments question scores almost nothing.

**Verified question linkage.** ⚠️ A General Studies Paper I demand on the policy instruments deployed to contain the Great Economic Depression is a recorded recurring demand from the pre-2018 period. ⚠️ The routing ledgers held in this repository begin at 2018, so no verbatim wording, question number or mark allocation is held locally; the demand is therefore rendered neutrally here and answered fully in Lesson 5, and ❌ no stem is reconstructed.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing that the Crash caused the Depression. ✅ The account denies it in terms.
2. ⚠️ Omitting the conditional. ✅ A slump was still not inevitable provided exports held; without that clause the tariff looks like a minor cause instead of the decisive one.
3. ⚠️ Presenting overproduction as industrial only. ✅ The same logic is applied to farmers.
4. ⚠️ Explaining the bubble by greed. ✅ The account names self-fulfilling expectation, which explains the rise and the fall with one mechanism.
5. ⚠️ Quoting share prices without their dates. ✅ $9 in 1924 and $26 in 1929; Radio Corporation $85 early in 1928 and $505 in September 1929.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Cause one, domestic overproduction: high profits and mechanisation produced more than the home market could absorb; unsold stocks built up; manufacturers produced less; men were laid off; with no unemployment benefit their families bought less; the vicious circle continued. The same applied to farmers.
2. ✅ Cause two, unequal income: average industrial wages rose about 8 per cent between 1923 and 1929 - only 1.4 per cent in real terms - while industrial profits rose 72 per cent.
3. ✅ Credit allowed the public to absorb the output for a limited time; by 1929 they were fast approaching the limit.
4. ✅ Manufacturers, usually super-corporations, would not cut prices or raise wages substantially; at the beginning of 1929 millions of Americans still had no radio, no electric washing machine and no car.
5. ✅ The conditional: even so, a slump was still not inevitable provided the Americans could export their surplus products.
6. ✅ Cause three, exports: the Fordney-McCumber tariff of 1922 kept foreign goods out, denied Europeans the profits needed to buy American goods and to service war debts, and provoked retaliatory tariffs; a slump of some sort was clearly on the way.
7. ✅ Cause four, speculation: momentum from about 1926; average share value $9 in 1924 to $26 in 1929; Radio Corporation $85 early 1928 to $505 September 1929, paying no dividends; savings spent, money borrowed, brokers selling on credit, banks speculating with deposits.
8. ✅ The reversal mechanism is self-fulfilling expectation: by their own selling, investors caused the collapse they feared.
9. ✅ 24 October 1929, "Black Thursday": nearly 13 million shares dumped at very low prices; by mid-1930 prices about 25 per cent of the previous year's peak; rock bottom in 1932.
10. ✅ The Crash triggered runs on the banks so thousands had to close; factories closed; unemployment rose alarmingly; the boom became the Great Depression and, as it spread, the world economic crisis.
11. ✅ The decisive sentence: the Crash did not cause the depression; it was just a symptom of a problem whose real causes lay much deeper.
12. ⚠️ Master formulation: the causes made a slump likely; the Crash made it violent. The Crash is a transmission event, not a causal one.
13. ⚠️ Credit postpones the limit set by purchasing power; it does not remove it - the same mechanism recurs in the credit boom before 2008.

### Concept check

**Question:** A script argues that the Depression was caused by speculation, and supports the claim with the Radio Corporation share price. Assess the argument.

**Model answer:** The evidence is accurate but the causal weight is misplaced. The Radio Corporation's rise from $85 early in 1928 to $505 in September 1929, in a company that paid no dividends, is a precise demonstration that prices had detached from earnings, and speculation is correctly listed as one of the four causes. But it cannot bear the argument alone for two reasons. First, the account places the decisive pressure elsewhere: unsold stocks, reduced production and lay-offs were already accumulating, wages had risen about 8 per cent between 1923 and 1929 against a 72 per cent rise in profits, and the explicit conditional is that a slump was still not inevitable provided the Americans could export their surplus - which makes the Fordney-McCumber tariff of 1922 and the retaliation it provoked the load-bearing cause rather than the stock market. Second, the account treats the collapse itself as a symptom in terms. The correct role for speculation in an answer is therefore twofold: it identifies the mechanism of the collapse, self-fulfilling expectation, by which investors' own selling produced the fall they feared; and it explains the severity of the banking damage, since banks had speculated with depositors' cash and brokers had sold on credit, so the fall destroyed the savings of people who had never bought a share.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that demoting speculation means dismissing it. The four causes are not competitors for a single slot; they are a structure. Overproduction and unequal income created the gap, the tariff closed the escape route, and speculation determined how the reckoning arrived. An answer that ranks them with the conditional and then shows how they interlock will beat one that argues for a single cause, however confidently.

Progress: 4/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the transmission channels - credit withdrawal, banking failure, trade contraction, tariff retaliation, commodity and specialised-export collapse, currency policy as a constraint - and the political transmission that followed
