━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the transmission chain, with the fastest channel first

```text
   US STRUCTURAL IMBALANCE (Lesson 3)
        |
        v
   WALL STREET CRASH, OCTOBER 1929  -- destroys confidence
        |
        +--> CHANNEL 1: CREDIT WITHDRAWAL            [FASTEST]
        |    US lending abroad stops; short-term loans already
        |    made to Germany are CALLED IN
        |    => requires no fall in trade at all
        |
        +--> CHANNEL 2: BANKING FAILURE / CONTAGION
        |    USA: over 25,000 banks in 1929 -> fewer than 15,000 by 1933
        |    Germany: "a crisis of confidence in the currency and a run
        |    on the banks, many of which had to close"
        |    => savings and credit destroyed together, so consumption
        |       and investment fall at the same moment
        |
        +--> CHANNEL 3: TRADE CONTRACTION
        |    worldwide over-production; German exports "severely reduced";
        |    Japan: "Exports shrank disastrously"; Italy: "Exports fell further"
        |
        +--> CHANNEL 4: TARIFF RETALIATION
        |    tariffs raised in self-defence; "other countries introduced or
        |    raised tariffs against Japanese goods to safeguard their own
        |    industries"  => each rational defence worsens the aggregate
        |
        +--> CHANNEL 5: COMMODITY / SPECIALISED-EXPORT COLLAPSE
        |    Japanese RAW SILK, sold mostly to the USA, fell by 1932 to
        |    LESS THAN ONE-FIFTH of its 1923 price; about half of Japan's
        |    farmers depended on silk as well as rice
        |    => the transmission reaches the village, not just the exchange
        |
        +--> CHANNEL 6: CURRENCY POLICY AS A CONSTRAINT
        |    Mussolini refused to devalue the lira until 1936; instead
        |    "wages and salaries were cut"; wages fell faster than prices,
        |    so workers suffered a real loss
        |    => refusing EXTERNAL devaluation forces INTERNAL devaluation
        |
        v
   MASS UNEMPLOYMENT
   Germany: over 6 million by 1932   |   Italy: 1.1 million
   USA: around 14 million, about a quarter of the labour force
        |
        v
   POLITICAL TRANSMISSION
   the crisis "caused extreme right-wing governments to come to power
   in Japan and Germany"; without it "Hitler would probably never have
   been able to come to power"
```

⚠️ The order of the channels is the argument. They are arranged by speed, not by size, because the examinable insight is that the fastest channel was financial and required no change in trade at all.

### 1. The question this lesson answers

An American problem is an American problem. How did it become everybody's?

⚠️ The weak answer is "because economies are connected". That is true of every year in the twentieth century and therefore explains nothing about 1929-33. ⚠️ The strong answer names the specific channels, ranks them by speed and shows why each one amplified the others.

### 2. Channel one: credit, and why it moved first

✅ **Fact.** After the Crash the USA stopped any further loans and began to call in many of the short-term loans already made to Germany.

⚠️ This is the channel to lead with, and the reason is structural. Recall from Lesson 2 that the German recovery of the 1920s had been financed precisely by American short-term lending, and that the account had already identified this as linking Europe too closely with the USA financially and economically. ⚠️ Calling in a short-term loan requires no fall in demand, no change in prices and no adjustment of trade. It is instantaneous, and it removes the working capital of a banking system in weeks.

⚠️ Two analytical payoffs follow. First, this is why the Depression arrived in central Europe faster than any decline in exports could have delivered it. Second, it explains the otherwise puzzling severity of the German experience: Germany suffered not one shock but two simultaneously, the loss of export markets and the withdrawal of the credit on which its recovery rested.

### 3. Channel two: banks, and the double destruction

✅ **Fact.** In the United States the banking collapse is recorded exactly: there were over **25,000** banks in the country in **1929**, but by **1933** there were fewer than **15,000**. ✅ The consequence is stated in human terms: millions of ordinary people who had had nothing to do with the speculation were ruined as their life savings disappeared.

✅ In Germany the same mechanism appears as a crisis of confidence in the currency and a run on the banks, many of which had to close.

⚠️ A banking collapse is uniquely destructive because it destroys two things at once. It destroys **savings**, which cuts consumption; and it destroys **credit**, which cuts investment. ⚠️ Most economic shocks hit one of those and leave the other to cushion the fall. This one removed both cushions in the same season. ❌ Note also what the record does not supply: no count of German bank failures is given, and none should be asserted.

### 4. Channel three and four: trade and the tariffs that answered it

✅ **Fact.** The industrial boom had led to worldwide over-production, and German exports, along with those of other countries, were severely reduced. ✅ In Japan, "exports shrank disastrously". ✅ In Italy, "exports fell further".

✅ And then the defensive response: ✅ other countries introduced or raised tariffs against Japanese goods to safeguard their own industries, just as many states had retaliated against American tariffs in the 1920s.

⚠️ Name the logical structure, because it is worth a mark on its own: this is a **collective-action failure**. Each government's tariff is individually rational - it protects domestic employment against a flood of cheap imports - and the sum of those rational decisions is a contraction of world trade that leaves every one of them worse off. ⚠️ Note that tariffs appear twice in this topic, as a *cause* in Lesson 3 and as an *amplifier* here; an answer must say which role it means.

### 5. Channel five: the commodity collapse that reached the village

✅ **Fact.** Japanese **raw silk**, sold mostly to the USA, fell by **1932** to less than **one-fifth** of its **1923** price, and about half of Japan's farmers depended on silk as well as rice.

⚠️ This single case does more analytical work than any aggregate. It demonstrates that transmission was not confined to banks and stock exchanges but reached the household economy of a rural population on the other side of the world, through a chain with three links: an American consumer stops buying a luxury; a Japanese export price collapses; a Japanese farming family loses half its cash income. ⚠️ It is also the cleanest available illustration of the vulnerability of specialised export dependence, which becomes the central mechanism of Lessons 11 and 12. ❌ It is a single-commodity case; do not generalise the ratio to other products or other countries.

### 6. Channel six: currency policy, and the choice it forces

✅ **Fact.** Mussolini refused to devalue the lira until **1936**; instead, wages and salaries were cut, and because wages fell faster than prices, workers suffered a real loss.

⚠️ State the mechanism generally, because it is the most technically demanding point in the lesson. A country facing collapsing export demand has two ways to restore competitiveness. It can lower the external value of its currency, so its goods become cheaper abroad without any domestic price changing - **external devaluation**. Or it can refuse to move the currency and instead force domestic costs down through wage and salary cuts - **internal devaluation**. ⚠️ The second route works, but it works by reducing the incomes of the people who buy domestic output, so it deepens the demand collapse it was meant to relieve. ⚠️ That is exactly what the Italian evidence records: workers lost over 10 per cent in real terms.

> ⚠️ **Evidence boundary, stated openly.** The sources held for this session do not discuss the gold standard, convertibility or the sequence of departures from it for the interwar period, and no gold-standard mechanism is constructed here. ⚠️ Their single gold reference belongs to the postwar chapter - the annual American deficits of the late 1960s covered by selling gold reserves - and it is used in Lesson 7 as a recorded strain, never as a monetary-regime mechanism. The Italian case is used as evidence of the *choice* between external and internal devaluation, and the wider monetary regime of the period is left outside the evidence this session holds.

### 7. The political transmission, and how far it can be pushed

✅ **Fact.** The crisis caused extreme right-wing governments to come to power in Japan and Germany. ✅ Germany had over **six million** unemployed by **1932**; ✅ Italy's unemployment rose to **1.1 million**; ✅ in the United States unemployment stood at around **14 million**, about a quarter of the total labour force, and industrial production in 1933 was only half the 1929 total. ✅ Without the crisis, Hitler would probably never have been able to come to power.

⚠️ Handle this with the qualification the evidence requires. The Depression is a **necessary condition** in that argument, not a sufficient one: the same slump struck the United States, Britain and France without producing the same constitutional outcome. ⚠️ The correct claim is that the Depression destroyed the legitimacy of governments that could not answer it, and that what filled the vacancy depended on what each political system contained. ⚠️ The fuller argument about why parliamentary democracy failed in particular states belongs to the topic that owns the fascist regimes; this session supplies the economic mechanism it uses.

### Criticism and reply: does ranking the channels by speed overstate finance?

**The objection.** ⚠️ Placing credit first may flatter the financial channel. Most of the world in 1930 had no exposure to American short-term lending at all, and for those countries the Depression arrived entirely through trade and commodity prices.

**The reply.** ⚠️ The objection is correct and improves the answer rather than damaging it. The ranking is by speed and not by breadth, and the two differ by region. For central Europe, and Germany above all, credit withdrawal was the first and heaviest blow. For primary exporters and for Japan, the trade and commodity channels did the work, and they did it just as thoroughly, as the silk case shows. ⚠️ The disciplined formulation is therefore regional: *finance transmitted the crisis fastest to those who had borrowed, and trade transmitted it most widely to those who had specialised*. ⚠️ An answer that makes that distinction has demonstrated exactly the comparative control examiners look for.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ World history from the eighteenth century onward. ⚠️ This lesson is the mechanism that the interwar international-relations and rise-of-fascism topics borrow when they treat the Depression as a cause.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ "How did the Depression become a world crisis?" or "examine the consequences of the Great Depression outside the United States". ⚠️ The structure that scores is channel-by-channel with one named piece of evidence each, closing on the political consequence with its qualification.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing "it spread because economies are connected". ✅ Name the channels; connection is the precondition, not the mechanism.
2. ⚠️ Treating trade as the only route. ✅ Credit withdrawal preceded and outran it in central Europe.
3. ⚠️ Constructing a gold-standard argument. ✅ The evidence held here does not support one; use the lira case and say so.
4. ⚠️ Making the Depression a sufficient cause of Nazi power. ✅ It is a necessary condition; the constitutional outcome varied with what each political system contained.
5. ⚠️ Giving German bank-failure counts. ❌ None is sourced; only the American series is.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Credit withdrawal: after the Crash the USA stopped further loans and called in many of the short-term loans already made to Germany - the fastest channel, requiring no fall in trade.
2. ✅ American banking collapse: over 25,000 banks in 1929, fewer than 15,000 by 1933; ordinary depositors with no part in the speculation lost their life savings.
3. ✅ German banking: a crisis of confidence in the currency and a run on the banks, many of which had to close.
4. ✅ Trade contraction: worldwide over-production; German exports severely reduced; Japanese exports shrank disastrously; Italian exports fell further.
5. ✅ Tariff retaliation: other countries introduced or raised tariffs against Japanese goods to safeguard their own industries - a collective-action failure.
6. ✅ Commodity collapse: Japanese raw silk, sold mostly to the USA, fell by 1932 to less than one-fifth of its 1923 price; about half of Japan's farmers depended on silk as well as rice.
7. ✅ Currency choice: Mussolini refused to devalue the lira until 1936 and cut wages and salaries instead; wages fell faster than prices and workers suffered a real loss of over 10 per cent.
8. ⚠️ External devaluation lowers the currency; internal devaluation lowers wages. The second deepens the demand collapse it is meant to cure.
9. ✅ Unemployment: Germany over 6 million by 1932; Italy 1.1 million; the USA around 14 million, about a quarter of the labour force, with industrial production in 1933 only half the 1929 total.
10. ✅ Political transmission: the crisis caused extreme right-wing governments to come to power in Japan and Germany; without it Hitler would probably never have been able to come to power.
11. ⚠️ Necessary but not sufficient: the same slump hit the USA, Britain and France without the same constitutional result.
12. ⚠️ Regional formulation: finance transmitted fastest to those who had borrowed; trade transmitted most widely to those who had specialised.
13. ❌ No gold-standard mechanism is asserted anywhere in this session, and no German bank-failure count is given.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using one piece of evidence for each, show that the Depression reached a German bank, a Japanese farmhouse and an Italian wage packet by three different routes.

**Model answer:** The German bank was reached through credit. After the Crash the United States stopped further lending and began to call in the short-term loans already made to Germany, on which the recovery of the 1920s had been built; the result was a crisis of confidence in the currency and a run on the banks, many of which had to close. No fall in German exports was needed for that to happen, which is why this channel moved first. The Japanese farmhouse was reached through a commodity price. Raw silk, sold mostly to the United States, fell by 1932 to less than one-fifth of its 1923 price, and about half of Japan's farmers depended on silk as well as rice, so an American consumer's decision to stop buying a luxury removed a large part of a rural household's cash income on the other side of the world. The Italian wage packet was reached through a policy choice. Mussolini refused to devalue the lira until 1936, so competitiveness had to be restored internally: wages and salaries were cut, and because wages fell faster than prices the workers suffered a real loss of over 10 per cent. Three routes, three victims, one origin - and the ranking by speed puts credit first, trade second and the policy response third.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming the three routes are alternative explanations of which only one can be correct. They are simultaneous and mutually reinforcing: credit withdrawal caused bank failures, bank failures destroyed the purchasing power that would have bought imports, falling imports collapsed commodity prices, and the resulting unemployment made tariffs politically irresistible, which contracted trade further. An answer that presents them as a sequence of amplifications rather than as a list of causes has described a crisis instead of cataloguing one.

## Lesson 5 - The Great Depression III: the policy instruments, grouped by what each was for

Progress: 5/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Roosevelt's three declared aims; the Brain Trust and the doctrinal break with laissez-faire; and the instruments grouped by function - financial stabilisation, agricultural and income support, coordinated production and public works, labour standards, social insurance, taxation and trade, fiscal expansion and institutional centralisation

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on Roosevelt and the New Deal, comprising the three aims of relief, recovery and reform, the Brain Trust, the banking and securities measures, the Farmers' Relief Act and the Agricultural Adjustment Administration, the Fair Labour Standards Act, the Tennessee Valley Authority and the other measures, together with the canonical owner's functional grouping of the instruments. Two pages of the extraction covering the first-phase codes and agencies are degraded and are recorded in the final ledger
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 15 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four functional families, and what each was designed to fix

| Family | ✅ Named measures | Designed to fix | ⚠️ Result or limitation |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1. Financial stabilisation** | Government took over the banks temporarily and guaranteed that depositors would not lose their cash in a future crisis; the **Securities Exchange Commission (1934)** reformed the stock exchange, insisting that people buying shares on credit make a down payment of at least **50 per cent** instead of only **10 per cent** | Stop the runs, restore confidence and remove the credit-fuelled speculation that produced the bubble | ✅ "This restored confidence, and money began to flow into the banks again" - the clearest single success |
| **2. Income and agricultural support** | **Farmers' Relief Act (1933)** and the **Agricultural Adjustment Administration**, paying compensation to farmers who reduced output and thereby raising prices; **Fair Labour Standards Act (1938)** with a maximum working week, a minimum wage in certain low-paid trades and most child labour made illegal; **Social Security Act (1935)**; loans for householders facing mortgage default; increased taxes on the incomes of the wealthy | Restore purchasing power at the bottom, so that recovery is not bought by cutting incomes | ⚠️ The farm measure "certainly helped farmers, but it threw many farm labourers out of work" and did little for the drought-and-erosion "dustbowl" of Kansas, Oklahoma and Texas |
| **3. Coordinated production and public works** | Public works schemes; the **Tennessee Valley Authority (1933)**, which built dams for cheap electricity and organised conservation, irrigation and afforestation in a region ruined by soil erosion and careless farming; slum clearance and the building of houses and flats; the codes and agencies of the first phase including the **NRA** | Create employment directly, set prices and conditions by coordination rather than by deflation, and leave durable assets behind | ✅ "The public works schemes and the Tennessee Valley Authority provided services of lasting value"; ⚠️ but the Supreme Court ruled several measures, including the NRA, unconstitutional, holding up their operation |
| **4. Institutional capacity and doctrine** | Willingness to intervene "as much as possible" and to spend government cash; the **Brain Trust** of economists and university academics; national direction of resources; accepted collective bargaining; increased federal authority over the state governments | Give Washington a permanent capacity to manage the economy and social policy | ⚠️ Roosevelt "was too cautious in the amounts of money he was prepared to spend to stimulate industry", and the 1938 spending cut produced another recession |

⚠️ The table is organised by **function** rather than chronology on purpose. An examiner asking what instruments were deployed is asking what each measure was designed to do; a candidate who answers with a dated list of Acts has supplied a catalogue instead of an analysis.

### 1. The problem the instruments had to solve

By 1933 the American economy had four simultaneous failures, each of which required a different tool. The banks had stopped working. Household incomes had collapsed. Prices, in both industry and agriculture, had fallen so far that producing anything was unprofitable. And no institution existed that could act on all three at once. ⚠️ That last failure is the deepest, and it is why the most durable output of the New Deal turns out to be institutional rather than economic.

### 2. Three aims, stated by the administration itself

✅ **Fact.** Basically Roosevelt had three aims:

- ✅ **relief** - to give direct help to the poverty-stricken millions who were without food and homes;
- ✅ **recovery** - to reduce unemployment, stimulate the demand for goods and get the economy moving again;
- ✅ **reform** - to take whatever measures were necessary to prevent a repeat of the economic disaster.

⚠️ This is a ready-made classification and it should be used, because it distinguishes the two things a weak answer confuses. **Recovery** measures aim at the present slump. **Reform** measures aim at the next one. ⚠️ Judging a reform measure by whether it ended the Depression is therefore a category error - the Social Security Act was never intended to do that.

✅ The doctrinal context is equally explicit: drastic measures were needed and Roosevelt's methods were a complete change from those of the laissez-faire Republicans; ✅ he gathered advice from a small group of economists and university academics whom he called his **Brain Trust**; ✅ he was prepared to intervene in economic and social affairs as much as possible and to spend government cash to pull the country out of depression, which the Republicans had always been reluctant to do.

> ⚠️ **Attribution control.** The account names the Brain Trust and describes the practice of spending. ❌ It does not attribute the fiscal expansion to Keynes or to a named economic doctrine, and neither does this session. Describe what the administration did; do not award the policy to a theory that the evidence held here does not connect to it.

### 3. Family one: getting the banks working

✅ **Fact.** It was important to get the banking and financial systems working properly again. ✅ This was achieved by the government taking over the banks temporarily and guaranteeing that depositors would not lose their cash if there was another financial crisis. ✅ "This restored confidence, and money began to flow into the banks again."

✅ The **Securities Exchange Commission (1934)** then reformed the stock exchange; among other things it insisted that people buying shares on credit must make a down payment of at least **50 per cent** instead of only **10 per cent**.

⚠️ Notice how precisely the second measure targets the diagnosis of Lesson 3. The problem was not that people bought shares; it was that they bought them with money they did not have, at a tenth of the price, using credit supplied by the same banks that held the public's deposits. ⚠️ Raising the margin from 10 to 50 per cent does not forbid speculation; it forces the speculator to carry the loss himself. That is a reform measure in the exact sense of aim three - designed against the next crisis rather than this one.

### 4. Family two: putting income back at the bottom

✅ **Fact.** It was important to help farmers, whose main problem was that they were still producing too much, which kept prices and profits low. ✅ Under the **Farmers' Relief Act (1933)**, the government paid compensation to farmers who reduced output, thereby raising prices, administered through the **Agricultural Adjustment Administration**.

⚠️ The logic is counter-intuitive and examiners like it for that reason: in a depression the government paid people to produce *less*. It follows directly from the overproduction diagnosis - with a glut, extra output lowers the price and so lowers the producer's income. ⚠️ And the honest qualification is recorded: ✅ it certainly helped farmers, but it threw many farm labourers out of work, and ✅ it did little for the parts of **Kansas, Oklahoma and Texas** which in the mid-1930s were badly hit by drought and soil erosion, turning them into a huge "dustbowl". ⚠️ Supporting the price of a product supports whoever owns the product; it does nothing for whoever merely works on it.

✅ The income floor was then raised from the other direction. ✅ The **Fair Labour Standards Act (1938)** introduced a maximum working week and a minimum wage in certain low-paid trades, and made most child labour illegal. ✅ Collective bargaining between workers and management became accepted, and ✅ both **General Motors** and **US Steel** were forced to recognise unions.

✅ And a floor under the household was laid for the future: ✅ welfare benefits such as the **Social Security Act (1935)** were "an important step towards a welfare state", and ✅ the American government had accepted that it had a duty to help those in need - although ✅ "rugged individualism" was still a vital ingredient in American society.

✅ Other income measures ran alongside: ✅ loans for householders in danger of losing their homes because they could not afford mortgage repayments; ✅ increased taxes on the incomes of the wealthy; ✅ and trade agreements which at last reduced American tariffs in return for tariff reductions by the other party to the treaty, in the hope of increasing American exports.

⚠️ That last measure deserves a sentence of its own, because it closes the circle opened in Lesson 3. The Fordney-McCumber logic of 1922 - protect the home market, strangle the export escape - was now partially reversed by negotiated, reciprocal reduction. ⚠️ Partially, not abolished; and an answer should say which.

### 5. Family three: building things, and coordinating production

✅ **Fact.** Public works schemes were central, and the outstanding example is the **Tennessee Valley Authority (1933)**, which revitalised a huge area of rural America that had been ruined by erosion and careless farming. ✅ The new authority built dams to provide cheap electricity, and organised conservation, irrigation and afforestation to prevent erosion. ✅ Slum clearance and the building of houses and flats accompanied it.

⚠️ The TVA is the single best illustration available of what a public works programme is *for*, because it produced four outputs at once: employment now, electricity permanently, a repaired ecology, and a demonstration that a public authority could plan a river basin. ✅ The verdict recorded on this family is the most positive in the whole record: "The public works schemes and the Tennessee Valley Authority provided services of lasting value."

✅ Alongside the works came coordination of industry itself through the codes and agencies of the first phase, including the **NRA**. ⚠️ Expand the acronym as the National Recovery Administration; the extraction held for this session supplies the acronym and the fate of the agency rather than a full account of its operation, and that limit is recorded in the final ledger.

⚠️ The purpose of industrial coordination was to set prices, output and labour conditions by agreement instead of letting deflation set them - which is to say, to stop the loop of Lesson 3 by removing the competitive pressure to cut. ✅ And its fate is the sharpest irony in the topic: the **Supreme Court** claimed that the president was taking on too much power and ruled that several measures, including the NRA, were unconstitutional, which held up their operation.

### 6. Family four: money, and the machinery of the state

✅ **Fact.** Roosevelt was prepared to intervene in economic and social affairs as much as possible and to spend government cash to pull the country out of depression.

⚠️ This is the doctrinal break, and it is worth stating as such: the change was not that a particular sum was spent but that government spending was now treated as an instrument of recovery rather than as a regrettable necessity. ✅ Yet the account's verdict on its execution is critical: Roosevelt "was too cautious in the amounts of money he was prepared to spend to stimulate industry", and ✅ in **1938** he reduced government spending, causing another recession which sent unemployment up to **10.5 million**.

⚠️ Read that pair together and a genuine empirical finding emerges: the instrument worked in proportion to its size. Expansion reduced unemployment to under 8 million by 1937; contraction in 1938 sent it back above 10 million. ⚠️ That is about as close to a natural experiment as interwar economic history offers, and it is far more persuasive in an answer than any assertion about what the state "should" do.

✅ Finally, the machinery: ✅ national direction of resources, ✅ accepted collective bargaining, ✅ increased federal government authority over the state governments, and the summary judgement that ✅ Roosevelt "had put in place the structures to enable Washington to manage the economy and social policy".

### Comparative note: relief, recovery and reform, sorted

| Aim | ✅ Instruments that served it | ⚠️ How to judge success |
|---|---|---|
| **Relief** | Direct help to the destitute and jobless; public works employment; mortgage loans for householders | Did the destitute receive help? ✅ Yes - relief for the destitute and jobless and the creation of millions of extra jobs is recorded as the chief early success |
| **Recovery** | Banking rescue; agricultural price support; industrial coordination; public works; fiscal expansion | Did unemployment end? ✅ No - it fell to under 8 million by 1937, remained serious and rose to 10.5 million after the 1938 cut |
| **Reform** | Deposit guarantee; Securities Exchange Commission 1934; Social Security Act 1935; Fair Labour Standards Act 1938; federal institutional capacity | Did it prevent recurrence and change the state's role? ✅ Yes in institutional terms - a step towards a welfare state and permanent structures for managing the economy |

⚠️ Use this table as the spine of any instruments answer. It allows a candidate to concede the failure on recovery without conceding failure overall, which is exactly the graded verdict Lesson 6 develops.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ World history from the eighteenth century onward, and the syllabus clause on political philosophies and their effect on society - the New Deal being the clearest twentieth-century case of capitalism being altered to survive.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ The recorded demand family is about the **policy instruments deployed to contain the Great Economic Depression**. ⚠️ Structure by the four functional families, illustrate each with two or three named measures, and reserve the last paragraph for the limits. ❌ Do not list eleven measures; an examiner reads a list as an absence of judgement.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Narrating the New Deal chronologically. ✅ Group by function; the aims of relief, recovery and reform are supplied by the administration itself.
2. ⚠️ Crediting the fiscal policy to Keynes. ✅ Only the Brain Trust is named in the evidence held here.
3. ⚠️ Adopting the "First, Second and Third New Deal" periodisation. ⚠️ Michael Heale is recorded as regarding it as an oversimplification, and the administration as never governed by a single political ideology; cite the caution rather than the scheme.
4. ⚠️ Treating the agricultural measure as an unqualified success. ✅ It helped farmers and threw many farm labourers out of work.
5. ⚠️ Dating measures loosely. ✅ Measures ran 1933-1940; Farmers' Relief Act and TVA 1933; Securities Exchange Commission 1934; Social Security Act 1935; Fair Labour Standards Act 1938.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Three aims: relief - direct help to the destitute; recovery - reduce unemployment, stimulate demand, restart the economy; reform - prevent a repeat.
2. ✅ Measures ran over the years 1933 to 1940; advice came from the Brain Trust of economists and university academics; the method was a complete change from the laissez-faire Republicans.
3. ✅ Banking: government took the banks over temporarily and guaranteed depositors against loss in a future crisis; confidence was restored and money began to flow back in.
4. ✅ Securities Exchange Commission (1934): down payment of at least 50 per cent instead of 10 per cent on shares bought on credit.
5. ✅ Farmers' Relief Act (1933) and the Agricultural Adjustment Administration: compensation for reducing output, thereby raising prices; it helped farmers but threw many farm labourers out of work and did little for the Kansas, Oklahoma and Texas dustbowl.
6. ✅ Tennessee Valley Authority (1933): dams for cheap electricity, conservation, irrigation and afforestation in a region ruined by erosion and careless farming; with public works, it provided services of lasting value.
7. ✅ Industrial coordination through the first-phase codes and agencies including the NRA; the Supreme Court ruled several measures, including the NRA, unconstitutional, holding up their operation.
8. ✅ Fair Labour Standards Act (1938): maximum working week, minimum wage in certain low-paid trades, most child labour illegal; collective bargaining became accepted and General Motors and US Steel were forced to recognise unions.
9. ✅ Social Security Act (1935): an important step towards a welfare state; the government accepted a duty to help those in need, though rugged individualism remained a vital ingredient.
10. ✅ Other measures: mortgage loans for householders, increased taxes on the incomes of the wealthy, the end of Prohibition in 1933, and trade agreements reducing American tariffs in return for reductions by the other party.
11. ✅ Fiscal expansion was the doctrinal break, but Roosevelt was too cautious in the amounts he was prepared to spend; the 1938 spending cut caused another recession and 10.5 million unemployed.
12. ✅ Institutional output: national direction of resources, accepted collective bargaining, increased federal authority over the states, and structures enabling Washington to manage the economy and social policy.
13. ⚠️ Judge relief, recovery and reform separately; a reform measure is not refuted by the continuation of the slump.
14. ❌ No attribution of the fiscal policy to Keynes, and no adoption of the First/Second/Third New Deal periodisation.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is it a mistake to judge the Social Security Act of 1935 and the Securities Exchange Commission of 1934 by whether they reduced unemployment?

**Model answer:** Because both belong to the third of the administration's own three aims, and that aim is not about the present slump at all. Relief meant direct help to the destitute millions without food or homes; recovery meant reducing unemployment, stimulating demand and getting the economy moving; reform meant taking whatever measures were necessary to prevent a repeat of the disaster. The Securities Exchange Commission of 1934 is a reform instrument in that exact sense: by insisting on a down payment of at least 50 per cent instead of 10 per cent for shares bought on credit, it did nothing for anyone unemployed in 1934, and everything to ensure that the next speculative episode would be financed by the speculator rather than by depositors' money. The Social Security Act of 1935 is the same kind of instrument aimed at households instead of markets: it is recorded as an important step towards a welfare state, establishing that government had a duty to help those in need, which protects income in the *next* downturn. Judging either by the unemployment series is therefore a category error, and it produces the weak conclusion that the New Deal failed. The disciplined judgement separates the tests - decisive on reform, partial on relief, insufficient on recovery.

**Misconception to avoid:** Inferring that because the reform measures cannot be judged by unemployment, they were somehow secondary. The opposite is closer to the truth. The recovery instruments were superseded by war spending within a decade, whereas deposit guarantees, securities regulation, social insurance, labour standards and expanded federal capacity outlasted the Depression entirely. An answer that grasps this will reach the strongest available formulation: the New Deal changed what an American government was *for*, and that change outlived the emergency that produced it.

---

## Lesson 6 - The Great Depression IV: achievements, opposition, limits and the authoritarian counter-case

Progress: 6/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the social effects of the slump; the New Deal's recorded achievements; opposition from business, the states, the Supreme Court and the left; the unemployment record and the 1938 recession; what war spending did; the German rearmament counter-case with its mandatory qualification; and the graded verdict on whether the New Deal saved capitalism

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's record of how the depression affected people, its list of New Deal achievements, its account of opposition from businessmen, state governments, the Supreme Court and socialists including Huey Long, its statement that only the war effort brought unemployment below a million in 1943, and its figures for American war production and employment

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the balance sheet, kept honestly on both sides

```text

   ABROAD: Germany over 6 million
   unemployed by 1932; Japan's north in

   lost over 10 per cent in real terms

   ------------------------------------------------------------------

   ------------------                        ------------------------
   Unemployment under 8 million by 1937      BUSINESS: unions, wage and hour
   but "still a serious problem"             regulation, higher taxes -> would
                                             "stifle private enterprise"
   1938 spending cut -> another recession

                                             interference in internal affairs

   unemployment below the million mark       others unconstitutional
   in 1943"
                                             THE LEFT: "not drastic enough";
   US GNP almost DOUBLED 1939-45;            Huey Long's Share Our Wealth
   8 million still out of work June 1940;    (1934), financed by taxing the
   almost full employment by end-1942;       rich; assassinated September 1935
   about 7 million extra jobs by 1945
```

⚠️ Keep both columns visible when you write. The commonest failure on this topic is a script that picks one column and defends it; the graded verdict at the end of this lesson requires both.

### 1. The social effects, because legitimacy is the real subject

✅ **Fact.** The stock-market crash ruined millions of investors who had paid high prices for their shares; ✅ those who had bought on credit or with borrowed money took their creditors down with them, since the creditors had no hope of receiving payment.

✅ Then the decisive fact for legitimacy: banks were in a shaky position, having themselves speculated unsuccessfully; when millions rushed to withdraw their savings, many banks were overwhelmed and closed down for good, ✅ so millions of ordinary people who had had nothing to do with the speculation were ruined as their life savings disappeared.

⚠️ That sentence is the political core of the entire episode. A system can survive punishing the reckless. It cannot easily survive punishing the prudent alongside them. ⚠️ Every instrument in Lesson 5 aimed at the banks is, read politically, an attempt to repair that breach.

✅ The scale in the real economy: ✅ industrial production in **1933** was only **half** the 1929 total; ✅ unemployment stood at around **14 million**, about **a quarter** of the total labour force; ✅ **one in eight** farmers lost all their property. ✅ There was a drop in living standards, with people queuing for bread, charity soup kitchens and evictions.

✅ And abroad: ✅ Germany had over **6 million** unemployed by 1932; ✅ Japan's north suffered "desperate poverty", for which factory workers and peasants blamed the government and big business; ✅ Italian workers lost over 10 per cent in real terms.

### 2. The achievements, stated as the record states them

✅ In the early days the chief success of the New Deal was in providing relief for the destitute and jobless, and in the creation of millions of extra jobs.
✅ Confidence was restored in the financial system and in the government, and some historians think it may even have prevented a violent revolution.
✅ The public works schemes and the Tennessee Valley Authority provided services of lasting value.
✅ Welfare benefits such as the 1935 Social Security Act were an important step towards a welfare state; the American government had accepted that it had a duty to help those in need.
✅ Many other innovations were continued - national direction of resources, accepted collective bargaining, increased federal authority over the state governments - and Roosevelt had put in place the structures to enable Washington to manage the economy and social policy.

⚠️ The strongest single formulation available: **the New Deal changed what an American government was for, and that change outlived the Depression.**

### 3. The opposition, from four directions at once

✅ **Business and the right.** Businessmen objected strongly to the growth of trade unions, the regulation of hours and wages, and increased taxation; ✅ these would encourage socialists and communists and might even lead to revolution; ✅ in their view governments should not interfere so massively in economic affairs, because that would only stifle private enterprise with new rules and taxes.

⚠️ Hold on to the paradox, because it will carry a twenty-mark answer: the New Deal was attacked as socialism precisely because it was preserving capitalism. ⚠️ Those two descriptions are not contradictory. A programme that raises wages, recognises unions and taxes the wealthy does redistribute power within capitalism; whether that saves the system or erodes it is exactly what the two sides disagreed about.

✅ **The states.** Some state governments resented the extent to which the federal government was interfering in what they considered internal state affairs.

⚠️ This is federalism operating as a real constraint on crisis management, and it is a transferable observation: emergency economics requires central capacity, and central capacity is contested wherever authority is divided.

✅ **The Supreme Court.** It claimed that the president was taking on too much power, ruled that several measures including the NRA were unconstitutional, and held up their operation. ✅ The nine members were all elderly and were not Roosevelt's appointees. ✅ The Court became more amenable during the second term after he had appointed five more co-operative judges to replace those who had died or resigned.

⚠️ Two lessons here. Constitutional limits bind emergency economics; and constitutional limits shift over time as their personnel change. ⚠️ An answer that notes both has understood why the American response was slower and more partial than the authoritarian alternative of section five - and why that slowness is not simply a defect.

✅ **The left.** There was opposition from socialists, who felt that the New Deal was not drastic enough and still left too much power in the hands of big business. ✅ One of the most vociferous critics was **Huey Long**, governor of Louisiana and a member of the United States Senate, who believed governments should spend heavily wherever necessary to help the poor. ✅ In **1934** he set up a scheme in Louisiana called **Share Our Wealth**, planning to ensure every family had at least $5,000, a house and a car, and old-age pensions, financed by taxing the rich; he wanted Roosevelt to do something similar nationally. ✅ He was assassinated in **September 1935**.

⚠️ Keep Long in the answer. He prevents the lazy framing in which the New Deal was the radical option; it was in fact the centre, attacked from both flanks.

### 4. The limits, conceded honestly

✅ Although unemployment was reduced to less than **8 million** by **1937**, it was still a serious problem. ✅ Part of the failure was due to the Supreme Court's opposition. ✅ Another reason was that although he was bold in many ways, Roosevelt was too cautious in the amounts of money he was prepared to spend to stimulate industry. ✅ In **1938** he reduced government spending, causing another recession, which sent unemployment up to **10.5 million**.

✅ And the concession that any honest answer must make: **"The New Deal therefore did not rescue the USA from the depression; it was only the war effort which brought unemployment below the million mark in 1943."**

### 5. What the war revealed about scale

✅ **Fact.** It was the war that finally put an end to the depression. ✅ The Gross National Product of the USA almost **doubled** between **1939** and **1945**. ✅ In **June 1940** there were still **8 million** people out of work, but by the end of **1942** there was almost full employment. ✅ It was calculated that by **1945** the war effort had created **7 million** extra jobs, and about 15 million Americans served in the armed forces.

⚠️ The analytical payoff is precise and it is the best available evidence on the fiscal question. The instrument that ended mass unemployment was government spending; what the New Deal lacked was not the instrument but its magnitude. ⚠️ Say it in that form and you have converted a moral argument about the state into an empirical claim about scale.

### 6. The authoritarian counter-case, with its mandatory qualification

✅ **Fact.** Nazi Germany combined vast schemes of public works - slum clearance, land drainage and autobahn building - with **rearmament from 1934** and **conscription from 1935**. ✅ Unemployment fell from over **6 million** at Hitler's accession to just over **2 million** by the end of **1935**, and to negligible levels by 1939. ✅ By **1938-9 the military budget was 52 per cent of government spending**.

⚠️ The comparison proves something important and something dangerous. It proves that mass unemployment was solvable by public spending at sufficient scale - which is the same finding the American war economy produced. ⚠️ And it shows that the scale required was politically available only to a regime that did not have to win a re-election, satisfy a supreme court or persuade state governments.

⚠️ **Mandatory qualification, and it must appear in the same paragraph.** ✅ The same programme rested on the abolition of trade unions and on purges of Jews and anti-Nazis from the civil service, law, education, journalism, broadcasting, theatre and music, leaving large numbers of vacancies. ⚠️ Full employment achieved by exclusion and by rearmament is not a policy model, and ❌ it must never be presented as the successful alternative without this sentence attached.

> **Boundary.** The fascist regimes themselves - their ideology, their seizure of power, their institutions and their foreign policy - belong to the topic that owns them. This session uses the German evidence only for the economic comparison.

### Criticism and reply: did the New Deal save capitalism?

**The case that it did.** ⚠️ Confidence in banks, in government and in the constitutional order returned; ✅ some historians think it may even have prevented a violent revolution; and, unlike Germany, Japan and Italy, the American constitutional system survived the Depression intact.

**The case that it did not.** ✅ Unemployment remained in the millions until war production began; ✅ the 1938 cut produced another recession; ✅ and the account's own verdict is that the New Deal did not rescue the USA from the depression.

**The graded verdict.** ⚠️ It saved the *political legitimacy* of capitalism without restoring its *output*. ⚠️ The formulation to carry into an answer is: **decisive for the survival of democratic capitalism, insufficient for recovery.** ⚠️ Note the structure of that verdict - it answers "did it work?" with "at which of its two tasks?", which is the move that separates a strong script from a confident one.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ World history from the eighteenth century onward, and the political-philosophies clause. ⚠️ The New Deal is the century's clearest case of a capitalist system being altered by its own government in order to survive, and the German comparison is the clearest case of the same economic problem answered without constitutional constraint.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ "Did the New Deal save capitalism?", "assess the effects of the Great Depression on society", or "compare democratic and authoritarian responses to the Depression". ⚠️ In all three, the mark ceiling is reached by separating the two tests and by attaching the qualification to the German case.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Claiming the New Deal ended the Depression. ✅ The record denies it; the war did.
2. ⚠️ Claiming it achieved nothing. ✅ Relief, confidence, lasting public assets, social insurance and permanent federal capacity are all recorded.
3. ⚠️ Presenting the German recovery as a policy success. ⚠️ Not without the trade-union abolition and the racial and political purges.
4. ⚠️ Forgetting the left-wing opposition. ✅ Socialists thought it insufficient; Huey Long's Share Our Wealth of 1934 is the named alternative.
5. ⚠️ Giving war figures without their dates. ✅ GNP almost doubled 1939-45; 8 million unemployed in June 1940; almost full employment by end-1942; about 7 million extra jobs by 1945; below a million in 1943.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Investors and their creditors were ruined; bank collapse destroyed the life savings of millions who had had nothing to do with the speculation.
2. ✅ Industrial production in 1933 was only half the 1929 total; unemployment around 14 million, about a quarter of the labour force; one in eight farmers lost all their property; bread queues, soup kitchens and evictions.
3. ✅ Abroad: Germany over 6 million unemployed by 1932; desperate poverty in northern Japan, blamed on government and big business; Italian workers losing over 10 per cent in real terms.
4. ✅ Achievements: relief and millions of extra jobs; confidence restored in the financial system and in government, possibly preventing a violent revolution; public works and the TVA of lasting value; the 1935 Social Security Act an important step towards a welfare state; national direction of resources, accepted collective bargaining and increased federal authority made permanent.
5. ✅ Business opposition: unions, hours and wages regulation and higher taxes would encourage socialists and communists and stifle private enterprise.
6. ✅ State opposition: resentment of federal interference in internal state affairs.
7. ✅ Supreme Court: the president was taking too much power; several measures including the NRA were unconstitutional; the Court became more amenable after five more co-operative judges were appointed in the second term.
8. ✅ Left opposition: not drastic enough, leaving too much power with big business; Huey Long's Share Our Wealth of 1934, financed by taxing the rich; assassinated September 1935.
9. ✅ Limits: unemployment under 8 million by 1937 but still serious; the 1938 spending cut caused another recession and 10.5 million unemployed; the New Deal did not rescue the USA from the depression - only the war effort brought unemployment below a million in 1943.
10. ✅ The war: US GNP almost doubled 1939-45; 8 million still out of work in June 1940; almost full employment by the end of 1942; about 7 million extra jobs by 1945.
11. ✅ German counter-case: public works plus rearmament from 1934 and conscription from 1935; unemployment from over 6 million to just over 2 million by end-1935 and negligible by 1939; military budget 52 per cent of government spending in 1938-9.
12. ⚠️ Mandatory qualification: that programme rested on the abolition of trade unions and the purging of Jews and opponents from professional employment.
13. ⚠️ Graded verdict: decisive for the survival of democratic capitalism, insufficient for recovery.
14. ⚠️ The paradox worth a top band: the New Deal was attacked as socialism precisely because it was saving capitalism.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two governments faced the same slump and both used public spending. Explain what the comparison between the American and German responses does and does not prove.

**Model answer:** It proves a fiscal point and disproves a political one. The fiscal point is that mass unemployment was solvable by government spending at sufficient scale. In Germany, vast public works - slum clearance, land drainage and autobahn building - combined with rearmament from 1934 and conscription from 1935 brought unemployment down from over six million at Hitler's accession to just over two million by the end of 1935, and by 1938-9 the military budget was 52 per cent of government spending. The American case confirms the same finding from the other direction: Roosevelt was too cautious in the sums he would spend, the 1938 cut produced another recession and 10.5 million unemployed, and only the war effort brought unemployment below a million in 1943, with GNP almost doubling between 1939 and 1945. What the comparison does not prove is that the authoritarian route was better policy. The German programme rested on the abolition of trade unions and on purges of Jews and anti-Nazis from the civil service, law, education, journalism, broadcasting, theatre and music, which themselves created vacancies; and its central instrument was rearmament, an output with a known destination. Meanwhile the American programme was constrained by a Supreme Court that struck down the NRA, by state governments and by elections, which is why it was slower - and why the constitutional order survived. The correct conclusion is therefore about scale and constraint, not about regime superiority.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the speed of the German fall in unemployment as a measure of policy quality. Speed here is a measure of the absence of constraint, not of competence. Any government that need not win a re-election, satisfy a constitutional court or persuade sub-national units can spend at a scale a constitutional one cannot, and if it spends on armaments it need not even find a civilian market for the output. An answer that scores full employment as a success without naming the exclusions that produced it has not been neutral; it has adopted the regime's own accounting.

---
## Lesson 7 - Designing the order: Bretton Woods, the Fund, the Bank, the trade rules and the break of 1971

Progress: 7/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: why the postwar order was designed rather than allowed to emerge; the Bretton Woods conference of 1944 and the institutions it created; trade liberalisation through the General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade and the World Trade Organization from 1995; the strains of the late 1960s; and the devaluation and import duty of 1971 that ended the fixed-rate element

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

The founding dates and membership of the 1944 conference come from the single declared verification pass recorded in the final ledger

CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the designed order, its three pillars and the joint that failed

```text

   * competitive tariffs raised in defence  -> world trade contracts

   * no institution able to lend, arbitrate or coordinate anything

   MONETARY FUND           RECONSTRUCTION AND       GATT signed 1947
   manage balance-of-      DEVELOPMENT (IBRD)       -> WORLD TRADE
   payments adjustment     finance reconstruction      ORGANIZATION
   under FIXED exchange    and development             from 1995
   rates
        |                       |                        |
        +--- UN specialized -----+                       |
             agencies (both)                    NOT a UN specialized
                                                agency - part of the
                                                wider multilateral
                                                trade system
                                |
                                v
   THE JOINT THAT FAILED: THE FIXED-RATE ELEMENT
   =============================================
   late 1960s  a budget deficit every year; the difference covered by
               SELLING GOLD RESERVES
   by 1971     "the dollar, which was once considered to be as good as
               gold, was weakening in value"
   1971        Nixon "forced to devalue the dollar by about 12 per cent
               and to put a 10 per cent duty on most imports"
                                |
                                v
   AFTER THE BREAK: floating rates and freer capital movement become
   the norm -> the institutional precondition for the financial
   globalisation of the 1990s (Lessons 15-16)
```

⚠️ Read the diagram top to bottom and the design logic becomes legible: each pillar answers a specific failure of 1929-33. The Fund answers the balance-of-payments crisis that forced devaluation or wage cuts; the Bank answers the absence of long-term development finance; the trade rules answer the tariff spiral.

### 1. The learner's problem: what is new about 1944?

Every previous lesson has described a world economy that *happened* - its structure emerged from industrial capability, colonial administration and private lending. ⚠️ 1944 is the first time in this topic that a group of governments sat down and attempted to *design* one. That is the conceptual novelty, and it is worth stating in exactly those terms, because it also explains the politics of Lesson 10: a designed system has authors, and authorship can be contested in a way that emergence cannot.

### 2. The conference and its two institutions

⚠️ **Fact established by the declared verification pass.** The Bretton Woods conference met in **July 1944** at Bretton Woods, New Hampshire, with delegates from **44** Allied nations, and created the **International Monetary Fund** and the **International Bank for Reconstruction and Development**, the latter becoming the core of the World Bank group.

⚠️ The division of labour between them is the examinable content and it is simple once stated. The **Fund** exists to manage **balance-of-payments adjustment**: when a country cannot pay for its imports, the alternatives are devaluation, deflation, default or import controls, and every one of those was a transmission channel in Lesson 4. The Fund was designed to supply short-term finance so that adjustment could be gradual rather than catastrophic. The **Bank** exists for the opposite time horizon: **long-term finance** for reconstruction and, later, development.

⚠️ Their legal status is a standard close-option in objective questions: ✅ both are United Nations specialized agencies.

### 3. The exchange-rate system, described only as far as the evidence goes

⚠️ The monetary system built at Bretton Woods was a **fixed-exchange-rate** system, in which currencies held declared parities and adjustment was supposed to be negotiated rather than unilateral.

> ⚠️ **Evidence boundary, stated openly.** This session does not construct a gold-standard or monetary-regime mechanism, because the sources held for it do not supply one. What it does hold, and uses, is the account's own record of the strain and of the break, given in section five below. ❌ No quota share, voting weight, lending figure or named programme of either institution appears anywhere in this session, and none should appear in a history answer.

### 4. The trade pillar, and one distinction worth marks

⚠️ **Fact established by the declared verification pass.** Trade liberalisation proceeded through the **General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade**, signed in **1947**, after the separate International Trade Organization planned at the time was never established.

✅ **Fact from the core account.** The **World Trade Organization** succeeded the General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade in **1995**.

⚠️ Two precise distinctions follow, and both are exactly the kind of thing a close-option question is built on. First, the WTO belongs to the wider multilateral trade system but is **not** a United Nations specialized agency, whereas the Fund and the Bank are. Second, the General Agreement was an *agreement* operating through negotiating rounds, while the WTO is an *organization* with a standing dispute mechanism - which is why Lesson 15 can record cases of states being taken to the WTO that would have had no forum in the 1950s.

### 5. The strain, and the break of 1971

✅ **Fact.** After many years of continual economic success the United States began to experience problems. ✅ Defence costs and the war in Vietnam were a constant drain on the economy and the treasury. ✅ There was a budget deficit every year in the late 1960s - that is, the government was spending more money than it was collecting in taxes, and the difference had to be covered by selling gold reserves. ✅ By **1971** the dollar, which was once considered to be as good as gold, was weakening in value. ✅ President Nixon was forced to devalue the dollar by about **12 per cent** and to put a **10 per cent** duty on most imports, in **1971**. ✅ Rising oil prices worsened America's balance-of-payments deficit and led to the development of more nuclear power.

⚠️ Now read those five sentences as a mechanism rather than as a sequence of events, because the mechanism is what an examiner is testing.

1. ⚠️ A fixed-rate system anchored on one national currency requires that currency's issuer to be beyond suspicion.
2. ✅ Persistent deficits, covered by selling gold reserves, are precisely what erodes that standing.
3. ✅ Once the anchor currency is weakening, the system's central promise cannot be maintained, and the anchor devalues.
4. ⚠️ And note the *second* half of the 1971 action, which most scripts omit: a 10 per cent duty on most imports. ⚠️ The designer of the open trading order reached for a tariff. That is the clearest possible evidence that the rules bound the strong only so long as the strong found them convenient - and it is the historical grievance that Lesson 10's North-South argument rests on.

⚠️ **The consequence that matters for the rest of the century.** After the fixed-rate element broke down in the early 1970s, floating exchange rates and freer capital movement became the norm. ⚠️ That is the institutional precondition of everything in Lessons 15 and 16: if capital cannot move freely, there can be no financial globalisation and no global contagion.

### Comparative note: 1918 and 1944, the same problem answered twice

| Dimension | After 1918 | After 1944 |
|---|---|---|
| Finance for recovery | ✅ Private American short-term lending to Germany | ⚠️ Public institutional lending through the Fund and the Bank, plus ✅ Marshall Aid |
| What happened when the lender's economy turned | ✅ Loans called in; ✅ banking crisis and currency panic abroad | ⚠️ A standing institution existed whose purpose was to finance adjustment instead |
| Trade policy | ✅ Fordney-McCumber (1922) and retaliation | ⚠️ Negotiated reciprocal reduction through the General Agreement from 1947 |
| Exchange rates | ⚠️ Unilateral choices, including ✅ Mussolini's refusal to devalue and consequent wage cuts | ⚠️ Declared parities with negotiated adjustment, until the break of 1971 |
| Who designed it | ⚠️ Nobody; the arrangements emerged | ⚠️ 44 governments in one conference |

⚠️ This table is the fastest way to demonstrate in an answer that the postwar order was a *response to a diagnosis*, not a fresh start. Each row of the right-hand column is an attempt to prevent the corresponding row of the left-hand column.

### Criticism and reply: was the order genuinely multilateral?

**The objection.** ⚠️ Forty-four nations attended, but most of Africa and much of Asia were still colonies in 1944 and were therefore not represented as states at all. An order designed before decolonisation cannot claim the consent of the majority of the world's population that later joined it.

**The reply, and it is a partial one.** ⚠️ The objection is historically accurate and this session does not dispute it; it is the foundation of the grievance that surfaces in the Brandt Report's own recommendation for a new World Development Fund in which ✅ decision-making would be more evenly shared between lenders and borrowers, explicitly unlike the Fund and the Bank, ✅ which that report describes as dominated by the USA. ⚠️ What can be said on the other side is that the design was aimed at a real and demonstrated failure, that the institutions did supply capacities which had not existed in 1931, and that the alternative on offer in 1944 was not a more representative order but no order at all. ⚠️ The balanced formulation is therefore: **the postwar order solved the problem its designers had lived through, and reproduced the distribution of power that existed while they were designing it.**

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ World history from the eighteenth century onward, including the effects of economic systems on society; and the institutions themselves are cross-owned by the topic on the United Nations and global governance, which holds their legal status.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Almost always as a paragraph inside a larger answer - on changes in the world economy since 1900, on the North-South divide, or on whether globalisation was designed or spontaneous. ⚠️ Four sentences are usually enough: the diagnosis, the two institutions with their different time horizons, the trade pillar with its 1995 succession, and the break of 1971 with its consequence.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Calling the WTO a UN specialized agency. ✅ The Fund and the Bank are; the WTO is not.
2. ⚠️ Dating the WTO to 1947 or the General Agreement to 1995. ✅ The Agreement was signed in 1947; the WTO succeeded it in 1995.
3. ⚠️ Writing a gold-standard mechanism into the answer. ⚠️ Use the recorded strain - annual deficits covered by selling gold reserves, a weakening dollar, the 1971 devaluation of about 12 per cent - and stop there.
4. ⚠️ Omitting the 10 per cent import duty of 1971. ⚠️ It is the detail that shows the rule-maker departing from its own rules.
5. ⚠️ Describing present-day quotas, voting weights or programmes. ❌ None is sourced here and none belongs in a history answer.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ The Bretton Woods conference met in July 1944 at Bretton Woods, New Hampshire, with delegates from 44 Allied nations.
2. ⚠️ It created the International Monetary Fund, to manage balance-of-payments adjustment under fixed exchange rates, and the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development, to finance reconstruction and development.
3. ✅ Both the Fund and the Bank are United Nations specialized agencies.
4. ⚠️ The General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade was signed in 1947 after the planned International Trade Organization was never established.
5. ✅ The World Trade Organization succeeded the General Agreement in 1995; it belongs to the wider multilateral trade system but is not a UN specialized agency.
6. ✅ Defence costs and the war in Vietnam were a constant drain on the American economy and treasury.
7. ✅ There was a budget deficit every year in the late 1960s, covered by selling gold reserves.
8. ✅ By 1971 the dollar, once considered as good as gold, was weakening in value.
9. ✅ In 1971 Nixon was forced to devalue the dollar by about 12 per cent and to put a 10 per cent duty on most imports.
10. ✅ Rising oil prices worsened the American balance-of-payments deficit and led to the development of more nuclear power.
11. ⚠️ After the fixed-rate element broke down in the early 1970s, floating rates and freer capital movement became the norm - the institutional precondition of 1990s financial globalisation.
12. ⚠️ Master formulation: 1944 was the first attempt to design a world economy rather than let one emerge, which is why its authorship could be contested afterwards.
13. ⚠️ Balanced verdict: the order solved the problem its designers had lived through and reproduced the distribution of power that existed while they were designing it.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the events of 1971 are treated in this topic as a turning point rather than as a routine currency adjustment.

**Model answer:** Because they removed the fixed element of a system whose whole purpose was to make adjustment negotiated rather than unilateral, and because of who removed it. The recorded strain is specific: defence costs and the war in Vietnam were a constant drain, there was a budget deficit every year in the late 1960s and the difference had to be covered by selling gold reserves, so that by 1971 the dollar, which was once considered to be as good as gold, was weakening in value. The response was that Nixon was forced to devalue by about 12 per cent and to place a 10 per cent duty on most imports. Both halves matter. The devaluation ended the credibility of a fixed-rate system anchored on that currency, and the import duty showed the principal architect of the open trading order reaching for exactly the instrument the order was designed to discourage. The consequence runs forward through the rest of the century: with the fixed-rate element gone, floating rates and freer capital movement became the norm, and free capital movement is the precondition for the financial globalisation of the 1990s and for the contagion of 2008. A routine adjustment changes a price; this changed the rules under which all later prices were set.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining 1971 as a failure of American policy alone. The strain was American, but the vulnerability was structural: a fixed-rate system anchored on a single national currency places the anchor country under a permanent obligation that its own domestic and military commitments can undermine, and no mechanism existed to resolve the conflict between those obligations. An answer that names only the deficits has described the trigger; an answer that names the structural conflict has explained why the break was always possible.
