## Lesson 8 - The postwar settlement: growth, welfare and two models of capitalism

Progress: 8/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the capitalist bloc and its stated principles; the American economic model and its intellectual defenders; the European model of social contract, regulation and redistribution; named European corporate examples; the unemployment argument of the 1990s and each side's explanation of it; and what the contrast establishes about "capitalism" as a single category

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's description of the capitalist bloc and its beliefs, its sections on the American and European economic models with the Rawls and Nozick exchange, its named European corporate cases, and its record of the transatlantic dispute about the causes of European unemployment in the 1990s
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 15 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one word, two systems - a side-by-side comparison

| Dimension | ✅ The American model | ✅ The European model |
|---|---|---|
| **Founding idea** | Evolved out of American traditions of freedom and the sanctity of property; the law of private property and freedom from government interference should be supreme | Took shape after the Second World War; varied by country but all shared provision of social welfare and public services, particularly education and health, and a reduction in inequality |
| **Role of government** | Should interfere with people's lives as little as possible, its main function being to safeguard national security | Expected to take an active role in regulating business and society and in operating a tax system that redistributes income more fairly and finances education and healthcare |
| **Welfare** | Divided: the right-wing view rests on "rugged individualism" and self-help, taxation being viewed as an invasion of private property; the liberal view holds that individualism should be tempered by a "social contract" under which the state provides basic welfare in return for the respect and obedience of its citizens | Generous welfare systems financed by a combination of taxation and social security contributions, with a high standard of public health and education; even in Italy, Spain, Greece and Portugal, with their history of fascism and military dictatorships, the social contract exists and unemployment insurance is the highest in Europe |
| **Whose interests come first in a firm** | The interests of **shareholders** are paramount | The interests of the **entire business** come first; dividends kept relatively low so that high investment is not neglected; big business has responsibilities to society - employees, fair wages, the environment |
| **Trade unions** | Weaker; the New Deal's acceptance of collective bargaining was itself contested | Stronger than in the USA, but on the whole operating responsibly |
| **Named illustrations** | Rawls, *A Theory of Justice* (first published 1971; the account cites the 1973 edition), arguing "justice as fairness", equality and a governmental duty to provide welfare and redistribute through taxation; Nozick, *Anarchy, State and Utopia* (1974), arguing strict property rights and minimal government, taxation, welfare and redistribution | Volkswagen - some 20 per cent of shares owned by the state government of Lower Saxony, shareholders' voting rights limited to 20 per cent, only 16 per cent of profits paid as dividends, "none of which would be allowed to happen in the USA"; Michelin; Nokia; the joint German, French and British Airbus, surpassing even Boeing |
| **Political expression** | Nozick's theories greatly influenced the New Right and were taken up by the neo-conservative branch of the Republican party, seen in action under Reagan (1981-9) and even more under George W. Bush (2001-9), when both taxes and welfare programmes were reduced | Whole-system persistence across governments; the model is embedded in regulation and taxation rather than in a single administration |

⚠️ The table exists to destroy a single bad habit: using "capitalism" as though it named one thing. ⚠️ Two of the richest societies in history ran on opposite answers to the questions in the left-hand column, and an answer that knows this can discuss the syllabus phrase about economic systems and their effect on society with evidence instead of adjectives.

### 1. The bloc, and what it believed

✅ **Fact.** After 1945 the world split into capitalist and communist blocs. ✅ The capitalist bloc consisted of the highly developed industrial nations - the USA, Canada, western Europe, Japan, Australia and New Zealand. ✅ They believed in private enterprise and private ownership of wealth, with profit as the great motivating influence, and ideally a minimum of state interference.

⚠️ Note the word "ideally" in that last clause, and note how much work it does. The stated principle is minimal interference; the actual practice in western Europe was an active regulating and redistributing state, and in the United States it was the permanent federal capacity created in the 1930s. ⚠️ The gap between the stated principle and the practice is the subject of this lesson.

### 2. The American model, stated fairly

✅ **Fact.** The American right-wing attitude was that the law of private property and freedom from government interference should be supreme. ✅ This was why the USA came into existence in the first place; people emigrated so that they could enjoy that freedom. ✅ It followed that the federal government should interfere with people's lives as little as possible, its main function being to safeguard national security.

✅ On social welfare, attitudes were divided. ✅ The right-wing or conservative attitude was based on "rugged individualism" and self-help; taxation was viewed as an invasion of private property and government regulations as restraints on freedom and prosperity. ✅ The liberal attitude was that rugged individualism should be tempered by a "social contract", holding that the state should provide basic welfare in return for the respect and obedience of its citizens. ✅ Hence Roosevelt's New Deal and Johnson's Great Society - programmes introduced by Democrat administrations which included large elements of social reform. ✅ For 16 out of the 24 years preceding 2005 the USA had Republican governments which favoured the right-wing approach.

⚠️ Link this directly to Lesson 6 and a continuity appears. The business objection to the New Deal - that intervention would stifle private enterprise and encourage socialism - is not a passing reaction to one programme; it is a standing position within American political economy, and it is why the Depression-era institutions were contested for the rest of the century.

### 3. The argument in its philosophical form, briefly and precisely

✅ **Fact.** Both schools had champions. ✅ John Rawls, in *A Theory of Justice*, put forward a theory of "justice as fairness"; he argued in favour of equality and claimed that it was the duty of government to provide welfare and some redistribution of wealth through taxation. ✅ The work was first published in **1971**; the account cites a **1973** edition. ⚠️ Use 1971 for the book and reserve 1973 for the edition consulted. ✅ In reply, Robert Nozick, in *Anarchy, State and Utopia* (1974), argued that property rights should be strictly upheld and that there should be minimal government intervention, minimal taxation and minimal welfare and redistribution. ✅ Nozick's theories had a great influence on the New Right and were taken up by the neo-conservative branch of the Republican party; ✅ they were seen in action during the Reagan administration (1981-9) and even more so under George W. Bush (2001-9), when both taxes and welfare programmes were reduced.

⚠️ Keep this short in an answer but do not omit it, because it supplies something most scripts lack: evidence that the choice between the two models was argued rather than merely inherited. ⚠️ The exchange also has a consequence the account itself draws: ✅ with neo-conservatism in the ascendant, the same principles were extended to American international dealings, which is recorded as the source of American reluctance to become involved in initiatives to help the Third World on debt relief, international trade and global warming. ⚠️ That is the bridge from domestic doctrine to the North-South politics of Lesson 10.

### 4. The European model, stated with the same fairness

✅ **Fact.** The economic and social systems of western, democratic Europe took shape after the Second World War and varied from country to country, but all shared certain basic characteristics - provision of social welfare and public services, particularly education and health, and a reduction in inequality.

✅ The state was expected to take an active role in regulating business and society and in operating a tax system that redistributed income more fairly and provided the revenue to finance education and healthcare. ✅ There was also the assumption that big business had a part in the social contract - it had responsibilities to society and so must function in a socially acceptable way, looking after its employees, paying fair wages and taking care of the environment.

✅ The corporate difference is stated exactly, and it is the sharpest available contrast: ✅ whereas in the USA the interests of shareholders were paramount, in most parts of Europe the perception was that the interests of the entire business must come first; ✅ dividends were kept relatively low so that high investment was not neglected. ✅ Trade unions were stronger than in the USA but on the whole operated responsibly. ✅ This system produced highly successful companies and relatively fair and just societies.

✅ The named cases make the abstraction concrete. ✅ In Volkswagen, some **20 per cent** of shares are owned by the state government of **Lower Saxony**, shareholders' voting rights are limited to **20 per cent**, and the company pays only **16 per cent** of its profits as dividends - ✅ "none of which would be allowed to happen in the USA". ✅ Michelin and Nokia are recorded as high-performance organisations run on similar lines, and ✅ the joint German, French and British **Airbus** as the world's most successful aircraft manufacturer, surpassing even Boeing.

⚠️ The Volkswagen case is worth memorising because it demonstrates a structural rather than a rhetorical difference. Capping voting rights and dividend payout is not welfare policy; it is corporate law, and it determines who can force a firm to prioritise short-term returns. ⚠️ Hold that thought: it returns in Lesson 16, where short-termism inside financial firms is one of the named practices that ended in disaster.

### 5. The dispute of the 1990s, and how to judge it

✅ **Fact.** Many American analysts were critical of the European system, since during the 1990s unemployment rose in Europe while the USA enjoyed an economic boom. ✅ The Americans claimed that European problems were caused by high taxation, over-generous welfare systems, the activities of trade unions and too much regulation. ✅ Europeans blamed their difficulties on the need to keep inflation under control so that they would be able to join the single currency launched in **1999**, and were confident that once that hurdle had been surmounted, growth and job creation would recover.

⚠️ Here is a textbook case for the correlation-and-causation discipline. Two things moved together in the 1990s - European unemployment and the European social model. That is an association. ⚠️ For the American explanation to be causal, high taxation and regulation would have to raise unemployment by a mechanism that operates in that decade and not in the preceding ones, when the same institutions coexisted with full employment. ⚠️ For the European explanation to be causal, the disinflation required for monetary union would have to be depressing demand at exactly that time, which is at least a dated and specific claim. ⚠️ The honest answer states both explanations, notes that the evidence held here does not adjudicate between them, and observes that ✅ European confidence in their system received a boost during the Bush administration when it was observed that all was not well with the US economy - which is itself a warning against treating a single decade's performance as a verdict on a system.

### Criticism and reply: is "two models" too tidy?

**The objection.** ⚠️ The account itself concedes that the European systems varied from country to country. If Sweden, Germany, Italy and Portugal differ substantially, calling them one model may conceal as much as it reveals.

**The reply.** ⚠️ The variation is real and should be acknowledged in any answer, but the shared characteristics are also recorded and they are not trivial: social welfare and public services, particularly education and health; a reduction in inequality; an active regulating and redistributing state; the assumption that business has social responsibilities. ⚠️ The account makes the point most strongly at the hardest case: ✅ even in Italy, Spain, Greece and Portugal, with their history of fascism and military dictatorships, the social contract exists and unemployment insurance is the highest in Europe. ⚠️ A model that survives that range of political inheritance is a real category. ⚠️ The disciplined formulation is therefore: **one model in its commitments, several in its institutions.**

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Directly on the syllabus clause about political philosophies such as capitalism and socialism, their forms and their effect on society. ⚠️ This lesson supplies the "forms" in the plural, which is the part most scripts miss.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ As a comparative question on models of capitalism, as a component of an answer on the world economy since 1900, or as the counter-weight in an answer about the welfare state. ⚠️ The structure that scores is a four-row comparison - property and government, welfare, corporate priority, unions - closed by a qualification that neither decade's performance settles the question.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Using "capitalism" as a single system. ✅ Two models with opposite answers on shareholder priority, welfare and regulation are recorded.
2. ⚠️ Treating the American model as anti-welfare in all its forms. ✅ Attitudes were divided; the liberal social-contract position produced the New Deal and the Great Society.
3. ⚠️ Inferring from 1990s European unemployment that the European model failed. ⚠️ Both sides' explanations are recorded and the evidence held here does not adjudicate between them.
4. ⚠️ Describing the European model without a structural example. ✅ Volkswagen's 20 per cent state shareholding, 20 per cent voting cap and 16 per cent dividend payout is the concrete case.
5. ⚠️ Forgetting the international consequence. ✅ The ascendancy of the New Right is recorded as the source of American reluctance on debt relief, trade and global warming.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ After 1945 the world split into capitalist and communist blocs; the capitalist bloc was the USA, Canada, western Europe, Japan, Australia and New Zealand, believing in private enterprise, private ownership, profit as the great motivator and ideally minimum state interference.
2. ✅ The American model: the law of private property and freedom from government interference supreme; federal government to interfere as little as possible, its main function national security.
3. ✅ Divided attitudes on welfare: rugged individualism and self-help on the right; a social contract under which the state provides basic welfare in return for respect and obedience on the liberal side - hence the New Deal and the Great Society.
4. ✅ For 16 of the 24 years preceding 2005 the USA had Republican governments favouring the right-wing approach.
5. ✅ Rawls, *A Theory of Justice* (first published 1971; the account cites the 1973 edition): justice as fairness, equality, a duty of government to provide welfare and redistribute through taxation.
6. ✅ Nozick, *Anarchy, State and Utopia* (1974): strict property rights, minimal intervention, taxation, welfare and redistribution; great influence on the New Right and the neo-conservative Republicans; seen under Reagan (1981-9) and more under George W. Bush (2001-9).
7. ✅ The domestic doctrine had an international consequence: American reluctance on debt relief, international trade and global warming.
8. ✅ The European model: social welfare and public services, particularly education and health; reduced inequality; an active regulating and redistributing state; business with responsibilities to employees, wages and the environment.
9. ✅ Shareholders paramount in the USA; in most of Europe the interests of the entire business come first, with low dividends protecting investment; unions stronger but operating responsibly.
10. ✅ Volkswagen: about 20 per cent of shares held by the state government of Lower Saxony, voting rights capped at 20 per cent, only 16 per cent of profits paid as dividends - none of which would be allowed in the USA. Michelin, Nokia and Airbus are the other named cases.
11. ✅ Even in Italy, Spain, Greece and Portugal, with their history of fascism and military dictatorship, the social contract exists and unemployment insurance is the highest in Europe.
12. ✅ The 1990s dispute: Americans blamed high taxation, over-generous welfare, unions and regulation; Europeans blamed the disinflation required to join the single currency launched in 1999.
13. ⚠️ Master formulation: one model in its commitments, several in its institutions - and no single decade settles the comparison.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using the Volkswagen case, explain what is structurally different about the European model, and why that difference is more informative than comparing welfare spending.

**Model answer:** Because it locates the difference in corporate law rather than in the budget. In Volkswagen, some 20 per cent of shares are owned by the state government of Lower Saxony, shareholders' voting rights are limited to 20 per cent, and the company pays only 16 per cent of its profits as dividends - a combination which the account states plainly would not be allowed to happen in the USA. Each element alters who can compel the firm to act. The state shareholding gives a public authority a permanent seat; the voting cap prevents any concentration of private holdings from dictating strategy; and the low payout keeps earnings inside the firm as investment rather than distributing them to owners. That is the structural expression of the model's stated principle, namely that in most of Europe the interests of the entire business come first while in the USA the interests of shareholders are paramount, and that big business has responsibilities to its employees, to fair wages and to the environment. Comparing welfare spending alone would miss this entirely, because welfare is a transfer made after the economy has distributed its output, whereas corporate governance determines how the output is distributed in the first place. It also explains why the model survived changes of government: it is embedded in law and ownership rather than in any administration's budget.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that one model is demonstrably superior. The evidence held here forbids that verdict and shows why: American analysts pointed to rising European unemployment in the 1990s and blamed taxation, welfare, unions and regulation; Europeans attributed the same unemployment to the disinflation required for the single currency launched in 1999 and expected recovery afterwards; and European confidence was then restored when the American economy faltered under the Bush administration. Two decades pointing in opposite directions do not constitute a test. The examinable conclusion is that both models delivered high incomes by different distributive routes, and that the choice between them is a choice about distribution rather than about efficiency alone.

## Lesson 9 - Two blocs, two systems: capitalism, socialism and their effect on society

Progress: 9/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the capitalist and communist blocs as economic systems; the arms and space budgets as the recorded opportunity cost of the contest; how the syllabus clause on political philosophies is answered from economic evidence; and the discipline required to compare systems without asserting a verdict

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's statement that after 1945 the world split into capitalist and communist blocs, its description of the capitalist bloc's beliefs, and its record that the contest had important economic consequences in the enormous sums both blocs spent on nuclear weapons, armaments and space programmes, with the contemporary argument that the money could have been better spent on the world's poorer nations. The passage describing the communist bloc is materially degraded in the extraction and the limitation is recorded in the final ledger
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 15 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the contest, its stated costs and the argument it generated

```text
                       AFTER 1945: THE WORLD SPLITS
                       ============================

   CAPITALIST BLOC                          COMMUNIST BLOC
   ---------------                          --------------
   USA . Canada . western Europe            the USSR and the states
   Japan . Australia . New Zealand          aligned with it
   BELIEFS, as recorded:
    * private enterprise                    [The passage describing the
    * private ownership of wealth            communist bloc's principles is
    * profit as "the great motivating        materially degraded in the
      influence"                             extraction held for this
    * "ideally, a minimum of state           session. Its economic
      interference"                          characteristics are therefore
                                             NOT asserted here; the topics
                                             that own the USSR and Chinese
                                             economies hold them.]
              \                                      /
               \                                    /
                v                                  v
        "the next forty or so years seemed like a contest"
                                |
                                v
        THE RECORDED ECONOMIC CONSEQUENCE
        ---------------------------------
        BOTH blocs spent enormous amounts of cash on
          * building nuclear weapons and other armaments
          * "even more expensive" SPACE PROGRAMMES
                                |
                                v
        THE CONTEMPORARY ARGUMENT
        -------------------------
        "Many people argued that much of this money could have been
         better spent helping to solve the problems of the world's
         poorer nations."
                                |
                                v
        => the Cold War is therefore not only a political fact in this
           topic; it is an OPPORTUNITY COST running through the same
           decades as the North-South divide (Lesson 10)
```

⚠️ The empty right-hand column is deliberate and it is honest. Where the evidence held for this session is damaged, this session says so rather than filling the space from memory.

### 1. Why a political division belongs in an economic topic

Because the division was economic in its content. ⚠️ The two blocs did not merely disagree about alliances; they organised production, ownership and distribution on incompatible principles, and each claimed that its principle delivered better outcomes for ordinary people. ⚠️ The syllabus asks about political philosophies "and their effect on the society" - which is a demand for exactly this comparison, expressed in outcomes rather than in slogans.

### 2. What the evidence supports, and what it does not

✅ **Fact.** After 1945 the world split into capitalist and communist blocs. ✅ The capitalist bloc consisted of the highly developed industrial nations - the USA, Canada, western Europe, Japan, Australia and New Zealand - believing in private enterprise and private ownership of wealth, with profit as the great motivating influence and, ideally, a minimum of state interference.

> ⚠️ **Evidence limitation, stated openly.** The corresponding passage describing the communist bloc is materially degraded in the extraction held for this session, preserving only that the bloc consisted of the USSR and the states aligned with it and that the following forty or so years seemed like a contest. ❌ This session therefore does not assert the communist bloc's economic principles, plan structures, growth record or living standards from this source. ⚠️ The Soviet and Chinese economic records are owned by the topics on the USSR under Stalin and on communism in China and Asia, and this session hands that material across rather than reconstructing it.

⚠️ That handover is not an evasion; it is the boundary discipline working correctly, and in an answer it is better to write two accurate sentences about what a system produced than six confident ones about what it was supposed to produce.

### 3. The recorded economic consequence: the opportunity cost of the contest

✅ **Fact.** The contest had important economic consequences. ✅ It meant that both blocs spent enormous amounts of cash on building nuclear weapons and other armaments, and on even more expensive space programmes. ✅ Many people argued that much of this money could have been better spent helping to solve the problems of the world's poorer nations.

⚠️ Three analytical points follow, and together they are enough for a full paragraph in an answer.

First, the phrase "even more expensive space programmes" is worth noticing. It records a contemporary judgement that prestige competition outran military competition in cost - a striking claim about what the contest was actually for.

Second, this is an **opportunity cost** argument, and it should be named as such: the loss is not what the spending destroyed but what it displaced. ⚠️ That is a claim about alternatives and it is therefore inherently contestable - which is precisely why the account attributes it ("many people argued") rather than asserting it. ⚠️ Reproduce that attribution in an answer; it demonstrates control.

Third, note the chronological coincidence that Lesson 10 will exploit. The decades of maximum superpower expenditure are the same decades in which the Brandt Report calculated that ✅ for the price of one jet fighter, about $20 million, **40,000 village pharmacies** could be set up. ⚠️ The two facts belong in the same paragraph, because together they convert an abstraction about priorities into an arithmetic that an examiner can check.

### 4. How to answer the "effect on society" clause without asserting a verdict

⚠️ The syllabus clause invites a comparison that the evidence held here cannot settle, and the discipline is to answer the part that is answerable. Four moves do it:

1. ⚠️ **Compare within capitalism first.** Lesson 8 supplies two models with opposite settings on welfare, regulation, corporate priority and unions, both delivering high incomes. That already proves that "capitalism" does not determine a single social outcome.
2. ⚠️ **Use the New Deal as the internal-alteration case.** Lesson 6 shows a capitalist system being altered by its own government - social insurance, labour standards, collective bargaining, federal capacity - and attacked as socialism for doing so.
3. ⚠️ **Use the contest's cost as the shared indictment.** Both blocs are recorded spending enormous sums on armaments and space while the argument was being made that the money could have relieved poverty elsewhere. ⚠️ That criticism does not distinguish between the systems; it applies to both, which makes it unusually safe to deploy.
4. ⚠️ **Hand the bloc-specific records across.** The Soviet and Chinese economic experiences belong to their owners; cite them as cross-links rather than narrating them.

⚠️ A candidate who does all four has written a genuinely comparative answer without asserting a single unsourced claim about either bloc's performance.

### Criticism and reply: is the opportunity-cost argument fair?

**The objection.** ⚠️ Armament spending is not simply money removed from development. It sustained employment, generated technologies with civilian applications and - in the view of its defenders - purchased a deterrence that prevented a third world war whose economic cost would have dwarfed the budgets.

**The reply.** ⚠️ Every clause of that objection is a genuine consideration, and an answer that ignores them has argued only one side. What can be said in return is narrower but firmer. The account records the criticism as a contemporary argument rather than as a verdict, which is the correct status for it. And the comparison it invites is not between armaments and nothing but between armaments and a specific alternative use whose costs were being calculated at the time - the Brandt Report's pharmacy arithmetic being the clearest instance. ⚠️ The disciplined formulation is therefore: **the opportunity-cost claim is a statement about priorities that both blocs' own contemporaries made, and it should be attributed rather than asserted.**

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Directly on the clause about political philosophies such as communism, capitalism and socialism, their forms and their effect on society, and adjacent to the Cold War topic which owns the confrontation itself.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Usually as a comparative or evaluative statement to be discussed. ⚠️ The safest high-scoring structure is: establish that each philosophy took several institutional forms; use named evidence for the variation; name the shared cost of the contest with its attribution; and conclude on distribution rather than on output.

> **Boundary.** The Cold War itself - alliances, crises, deterrence, detente and the collapse of 1989-91 - belongs to the Cold War owner, and the focused political order after 1991 belongs to its own topic. This session takes only the economic consequence recorded above.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Narrating the Cold War. ✅ This topic owns its economic consequence, not its diplomacy.
2. ⚠️ Asserting communist-bloc economic performance from this session's evidence. ⚠️ The passage is degraded; the records belong to the owning topics.
3. ⚠️ Presenting the opportunity-cost claim as fact. ✅ It is recorded as what many people argued.
4. ⚠️ Treating capitalism as one system. ✅ Lesson 8 supplies two models inside the same bloc.
5. ⚠️ Answering "effect on society" with output figures alone. ⚠️ The clause is about distribution and social consequence, which is where the New Deal and European welfare evidence belongs.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ After 1945 the world split into capitalist and communist blocs.
2. ✅ The capitalist bloc was the highly developed industrial nations - the USA, Canada, western Europe, Japan, Australia and New Zealand.
3. ✅ Its beliefs: private enterprise, private ownership of wealth, profit as the great motivating influence and, ideally, a minimum of state interference.
4. ⚠️ The passage describing the communist bloc's economic principles is degraded in the evidence held here; the Soviet and Chinese records belong to the topics that own them and are not reconstructed in this session.
5. ✅ The contest of the following forty or so years had important economic consequences: both blocs spent enormous amounts on nuclear weapons and other armaments and on even more expensive space programmes.
6. ✅ Many people argued that much of this money could have been better spent helping to solve the problems of the world's poorer nations.
7. ⚠️ This is an opportunity-cost argument about displacement rather than destruction, and it is attributed rather than asserted in the source.
8. ⚠️ Pair it with the Brandt arithmetic: for the price of one jet fighter, about $20 million, 40,000 village pharmacies could be set up.
9. ⚠️ The "effect on society" clause is best answered by showing variation within each philosophy rather than by scoring one against the other.
10. ⚠️ The New Deal is the case of a capitalist system altered from within and attacked as socialism for it; Lesson 8's two models are the case of one philosophy taking two institutional forms.
11. ⚠️ The criticism of armament expenditure does not discriminate between the blocs, which makes it the safest comparative point to deploy.
12. ⚠️ Boundary: the Cold War confrontation and the post-1991 political order belong to their own owners.

### Concept check

**Question:** How can the syllabus demand on capitalism, socialism and their effect on society be answered well when the evidence available does not permit a verdict on which system performed better?

**Model answer:** By answering the part of the demand that the evidence does reach, which is the relationship between a philosophy and the institutions it produces. Three moves do it. First, show that a single philosophy generates several institutional forms: within the capitalist bloc, whose stated beliefs were private enterprise, private ownership and profit as the great motivating influence with ideally minimal state interference, the American model treated property rights and freedom from interference as supreme and shareholders as paramount, while the European model provided welfare and public services, reduced inequality, gave the state an active regulating and redistributing role and subordinated shareholders to the interests of the whole business - Volkswagen's 20 per cent state holding, 20 per cent voting cap and 16 per cent dividend payout being the concrete case. Second, show that systems change under pressure: the New Deal added deposit guarantees, social insurance, labour standards and collective bargaining to American capitalism, and was attacked as socialism precisely because it was preserving capitalism. Third, use the criticism that applies to both sides: both blocs spent enormous sums on nuclear weapons and armaments and on even more expensive space programmes, and many people argued that the money could have been better spent on the world's poorer nations. That last point is attributed rather than asserted, and it is comparative without requiring a verdict. The conclusion is then about distribution rather than about efficiency, which is what the clause asks for.

**Misconception to avoid:** Filling the gap in the evidence with a remembered verdict about planned economies. The passage describing the communist bloc is degraded in the material held here, and the Soviet and Chinese records are owned by other topics; importing a confident summary of them would be exactly the kind of unsourced assertion this topic punishes. An answer gains more from one sentence acknowledging that the comparison is handled by the owning topics than it loses by not making a sweeping claim.

## Lesson 10 - The Third World and the North-South divide: a category, its evidence and the Brandt Report

Progress: 10/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the origin and content of the term "Third World"; non-alignment and its resentment of interference; the poverty evidence; the Brandt Report of 1980, its division of the world, its recommendations and its fate; and what the divide looked like by the early twenty-first century

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on the Third World and the North-South divide in full, comprising the origin of the term, the composition of the bloc by 1970, non-alignment with Nehru as unofficial leader, the Havana conference of 1979, the food-consumption comparison, the Brandt Report with its division, recommendations, targets and outcomes, and the human-development contrast recorded at the end of 2003
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 15 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a vocabulary, a line and a report

```text
   WHERE THE WORD CAME FROM (1950s)
   ================================
   FIRST WORLD  = the industrialized capitalist nations
   SECOND WORLD = the industrialized communist states
   THIRD WORLD  = everything else
        -> the number of such states "grew rapidly during the 1950s and
           1960s as the European empires broke up"
        -> BY 1970 it consisted of: Africa, Asia (except the USSR and
           China), India, Pakistan, Bangladesh, Latin America and the
           Middle East
        -> "almost all once colonies or mandates of European powers, and
           were left in an undeveloped or under-developed state when they
           achieved independence"

   THE POLITICAL POSTURE
   =====================
   NON-ALIGNMENT: not too closely associated with EITHER bloc, and
   "very suspicious of the motives of both"
     * Nehru of India (1947-64) "saw himself as a sort of unofficial
       leader of the Third World"
     * deep resentment of continued interference in internal affairs
       (NEO-COLONIALISM)
     * 1979: 92 nations represented at a non-aligned conference in
       Havana; by then the Third World was roughly 70% of the world's
       population

   THE POVERTY EVIDENCE, AS RECORDED
   =================================
     70% of world population  --> consumed 30% of the world's food
     USA, perhaps 8% of world population --> ate 40% of the world's food
     => shortages of proteins and vitamins -> poor health, high death rate

   THE BRANDT REPORT (1980)
   ========================
   An international group of politicians under Willy Brandt, including
   Edward Heath, divided the world in two:
        NORTH = developed industrial nations of North America, Europe,
                the USSR and Japan, plus Australia and New Zealand
        SOUTH = most of the Third World countries
   CONCLUSION: "the North was getting richer and the South was getting
   poorer"
   MEASURED BY: calorie intake per person per day, and GNP per head
     1989-90: the GNP of the North averaged OVER 24 TIMES that of the South
     1992:    Japan over $28,000 per head; Norway $25,800;
              Ethiopia $110 - the second lowest GNP in the world

   ITS FOUR RECOMMENDATIONS            ITS FATE BY 1985
   ------------------------            ----------------
   0.7% of national income in aid      Reached by only Norway, Sweden,
     by 1985, 1.0% by 2000             Denmark, the Netherlands, France
   a new World Development Fund with   USA gave 0.24%; Britain 0.11%
     decision-making shared more
     evenly between lenders and        Famine in Africa, especially
     borrowers - "not like the IMF     Ethiopia and the Sudan, mid-1980s
     and the World Bank, which were
     dominated by the USA"             1990s: the US economy boomed under
   an international energy plan        Clinton while the plight of the
   a campaign to improve agricultural  Third World became more serious
     techniques and an international
     food programme
```

⚠️ The diagram is arranged so that the report's recommendations and their fate sit side by side. The gap between the two columns is the lesson.

### 1. The learner's problem: is "Third World" a fact or a label?

⚠️ Both, and the ambiguity is itself examinable. It began as a residual classification - the states that belonged to neither industrial bloc - and it was rapidly used as though it described a shared condition and even a shared political interest. ⚠️ The first half of this lesson shows why the usage was reasonable at the time; Lesson 13 shows why it stopped being reasonable.

### 2. Where the term came from and what it contained

✅ **Fact.** During the **1950s** the term **Third World** began to be used to describe countries which were not part of the First World - the industrialised capitalist nations - or the Second World - the industrialised communist states. ✅ The Third World states grew rapidly in number during the 1950s and 1960s as the European empires broke up and newly independent countries emerged.

✅ By **1970** the Third World consisted of Africa, Asia except the USSR and China, India, Pakistan, Bangladesh, Latin America and the Middle East. ✅ They were almost all once colonies or mandates of European powers, and were left in an undeveloped or under-developed state when they achieved independence.

⚠️ Two observations repay the space. First, the category is defined by **exclusion** from two industrial blocs, which means its unity is negative from the outset. Second, the common element that is positively shared - a colonial or mandate past and an underdeveloped condition at independence - is precisely the structure Lesson 1 described. ⚠️ The North-South divide is therefore not a new phenomenon of the 1970s; it is the 1900 role division, surviving the disappearance of the empires that created it.

### 3. Non-alignment, and what it was suspicious of

✅ **Fact.** The Third World states were in favour of **non-alignment**, which means that they did not want to be too closely associated with either the capitalist or the communist bloc, and they were very suspicious of the motives of both.

✅ Prime Minister **Nehru of India (1947-64)** saw himself as a sort of unofficial leader of the Third World, which he thought could be a powerful force for world peace. ✅ Third World countries deeply resented the fact that both blocs continued to interfere in their internal affairs - **neo-colonialism**. ✅ The USA, for example, interfered in the affairs of Central and South America, helping to overthrow governments it did not approve of, in **Guatemala (1954)**, the **Dominican Republic (1965)** and **Chile (1973)**; ✅ Britain, France and the USSR interfered in the Middle East. ✅ Frequent meetings of Third World leaders were held, and in **1979**, **92 nations** were represented at a non-aligned conference in **Havana**. ✅ By this time the Third World contained roughly **70 per cent** of the world's population.

⚠️ Define **neo-colonialism** carefully because it is the hinge term: continued external control of a formally independent state through economic dependence, aid conditions, ownership and political interference rather than through administration. ⚠️ It is the same relationship Lesson 1 identified in the American treatment of Latin America, now generalised and named by its objects rather than by its practitioners.

> **Boundary.** The interventions listed above are owned by the Cold War and Latin American topics; they appear here only because the account uses them to explain what non-alignment was reacting against. ❌ No account of any intervention is given in this session.

⚠️ India's position deserves one sentence more, since it is the most directly usable Global South example in the lesson. ⚠️ Nehru's claim to unofficial leadership rested on a specific argument - that a bloc of newly independent states could be a force for world peace rather than an object of superpower competition - and the Havana figures show how far that constituency had grown by 1979: 92 nations, roughly 70 per cent of humanity.

### 4. The poverty evidence, exactly as recorded

✅ **Fact.** Economically the Third World was extremely poor. ✅ Although they contained **70 per cent** of the world's population, Third World countries only consumed **30 per cent** of the world's food, while the USA, with perhaps **8 per cent** of the world's population, ate **40 per cent** of the world's food. ✅ Third World people were often short of proteins and vitamins, and this caused poor health and a high death rate.

⚠️ That comparison is worth carrying into an answer verbatim, because it does something a monetary statistic cannot: it measures inequality in a unit that has a physiological floor. ⚠️ Money incomes can differ by any ratio without implying harm; food consumption cannot, which is why the account follows the figures immediately with the health consequence. ⚠️ Note that consequence carefully - it is a **death-rate** effect, which links this lesson directly to the population argument of Lesson 18.

### 5. The Brandt Report, its line and its arithmetic

✅ **Fact.** In **1980** an international group of politicians under the chairmanship of **Willy Brandt**, a former West German chancellor, and including **Edward Heath**, produced a report on the problems of the Third World.

> ⚠️ **Source note.** The passage recording Brandt's years in office is materially degraded in the extraction held for this session and its dates are not reproduced here; he is described as a former West German chancellor, which the passage supports.

✅ The report concluded that the world could be roughly divided into two parts: ✅ the **North** - the developed industrial nations of North America, Europe, the USSR and Japan, plus Australia and New Zealand; ✅ and the **South** - most of the Third World countries. ✅ Its conclusion was that the North was getting richer and the South was getting poorer.

✅ The gap was illustrated by the statistics of calorie intake and by a comparison of Gross National Product per head. ✅ GNP is calculated by taking the total money value of a country's total output from all units of production, wherever production is situated, including interest, profits and dividends received from abroad, and dividing by the population figure. ✅ In **1989-90** the GNP of the North averaged over **24 times** that of the South. ✅ In **1992** Japan could boast a GNP of over **$28,000** per head and Norway **$25,800**, while among poor African countries Ethiopia could manage only **$110** per head, the second lowest GNP in the world.

⚠️ One methodological caution belongs in a strong answer. A twenty-four-fold average gap is a ratio of averages, and averages conceal the internal divergence that Lesson 13 will show was already under way in 1980. ⚠️ The figure is accurate and it is also, by itself, misleading - which is exactly why this session teaches the divide and the split as consecutive lessons.

### 6. What the report asked for, and why it was in the North's interest

✅ **Fact.** The Brandt Report pointed out that it was in the North's interests to help the South become more prosperous, because that would enable the South to buy more goods from the North, which would help to avoid unemployment and recession in the North.

⚠️ That is an argument of considerable rhetorical intelligence, and worth noticing as such. It reframes aid from charity into demand management, and in doing so it appeals to exactly the self-interest that Lesson 2 recorded behind Marshall Aid - a prosperous customer keeps the supplier's factories open.

✅ It added that if just a fraction of the North's spending on armaments was switched to helping the South, vast improvements could be made: ✅ for the price of one jet fighter, about **$20 million**, **40,000** village pharmacies could be set up.

✅ Its recommendations, which if carried out would at least eliminate hunger from the world, were:

1. ✅ the rich nations of the North should aim to be giving **0.7 per cent** of their national income to poorer countries **by 1985** and **1.0 per cent by the year 2000**;
2. ✅ a new **World Development Fund** should be set up in which decision-making would be more evenly shared between lenders and borrowers - not like the International Monetary Fund and the World Bank, which were dominated by the USA;
3. ✅ an **international energy plan** should be drawn up;
4. ✅ there should be a campaign to improve **agricultural techniques** in the South, and an **international food programme** should be drawn up.

⚠️ Recommendation two is the politically decisive one and it connects straight back to Lesson 7. The complaint is not that the Fund and the Bank exist but that their decision-making is dominated by one state, and the proposed remedy is representational rather than financial. ⚠️ That is the clearest possible confirmation of Lesson 7's closing judgement: a designed order can be criticised for its authorship in a way an emergent one cannot.

### 7. Did it change anything?

✅ **Fact.** Sadly, there was no immediate improvement in the general economic situation of the South. ✅ By **1985** very few countries had reached the suggested 0.7 per cent target; ✅ those that did were **Norway, Sweden, Denmark, the Netherlands and France**; ✅ the USA gave only **0.24 per cent** and Britain **0.11 per cent**. ✅ There was a terrible famine in Africa, especially in **Ethiopia and the Sudan** in the mid-1980s, and the crisis in the poorer parts of the Third World seemed to be worsening. ✅ Throughout the 1990s the US economy boomed under the Clinton administration, whereas the plight of the Third World became even more serious.

✅ At the end of **2003** the UN reported that **21** Third World states, **17** of them in Africa, were in crisis because of a combination of natural disasters, AIDS, global warming and civil wars. ✅ Yet the richest **1 per cent** of the world's population - around **60 million** people - received as much income as the poorest **57 per cent**.

✅ The human-development contrast recorded for the same moment is the sharpest single pairing in the chapter: ✅ **Norway** was top of the UN's league table for human development, with a life expectancy of **78.7** years, a literacy rate of virtually **100 per cent** and annual income just under **$30,000**; ✅ in **Sierra Leone** life expectancy was about **35**, the literacy rate **35 per cent** and annual income averaged **$470**.

✅ The account also records a contemporary reading of the political consequence: the USA seemed to attract the most hostility and resentment on account of this imbalance of wealth, and it was widely believed that the growth of terrorism - especially the 11 September attacks - was a desperate response to the failure of peaceful attempts to bring about a fairer world economic system.

⚠️ Handle that last sentence with care and in an answer attribute it exactly as the account does: it records a widespread belief, not a demonstrated causal finding, and this session does not convert it into one. ⚠️ The substantive politics of terrorism belong to the topic that owns the new world order.

✅ Finally, what UN economic advisers said needed to be done: ✅ it was up to the West to remove trade barriers, dismantle its over-generous system of subsidies, provide greater debt relief, and double the amount of aid from **$50 billion** to **$100 billion** a year, so that poor countries could invest in clean water systems, rural roads, education and proper healthcare.

⚠️ Notice that three of those four demands are not about aid at all. Trade barriers, subsidies and debt relief are about the terms on which the South participates in the world economy - which is the same complaint, in modern form, as the price-fixing of Lesson 1.

### Criticism and reply: was the North-South framing ever accurate?

**The objection.** ⚠️ The Brandt line placed the USSR in the North, and it grouped Singapore with Sierra Leone. A division that puts a planned economy on the rich side of the line and a future high-income economy on the poor side is a poor analytical instrument.

**The reply.** ⚠️ Partly conceded, and the concession is itself the content of Lesson 13. The line was drawn on industrialisation, not on political system, which is why the USSR appears in the North; and it was drawn in 1980, before the divergence inside the South had gone far enough to break the category. ⚠️ What the framing did achieve was real: it named a structural relationship between manufactures and primary products that a purely national account of poverty would have missed, and it converted the complaint into specific, checkable demands - an aid percentage, a fund with shared decision-making, an energy plan, a food programme. ⚠️ The formulation to carry: **the North-South framing was an accurate description of a relationship and an increasingly inaccurate description of a group of countries.**

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ World history from the eighteenth century onward, including decolonisation and its aftermath. ⚠️ This lesson is what the decolonisation topic hands forward: the formal transfer of sovereignty completed, and the economic relationship still in place.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ "What is meant by the North-South divide?", "why did the divide persist despite the growth of the world economy?", or as a component in an answer on globalisation. ⚠️ The structure that scores is: define the category and date it; give two pieces of hard evidence, preferably the food comparison and a GNP ratio with its year; summarise the Brandt line and one of its recommendations; and state its fate with the aid percentages.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating "Third World" as a synonym for "poor countries" without its origin. ✅ It is a residual category from the 1950s, defined against two industrial blocs.
2. ⚠️ Placing the USSR in the South. ✅ The Brandt line put it in the North, because the line was drawn on industrialisation.
3. ⚠️ Giving GNP figures without years. ✅ The 24-fold average is 1989-90; the Japan, Norway and Ethiopia figures are 1992.
4. ⚠️ Asserting that terrorism was caused by economic inequality. ✅ The account records a widespread belief; it is attributed, not asserted.
5. ⚠️ Reducing the Brandt Report to an aid target. ✅ Its second recommendation - a fund with decision-making shared between lenders and borrowers - is the politically significant one.
6. ⚠️ Forgetting that the report argued from the North's own interest. ✅ A prosperous South would buy more goods and help avoid unemployment and recession in the North.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The term Third World began to be used in the 1950s for states outside the industrialised capitalist First World and the industrialised communist Second World; their number grew rapidly in the 1950s and 1960s as the European empires broke up.
2. ✅ By 1970 the Third World consisted of Africa, Asia except the USSR and China, India, Pakistan, Bangladesh, Latin America and the Middle East - almost all former colonies or mandates, left undeveloped or under-developed at independence.
3. ✅ Non-alignment meant avoiding close association with either bloc, and deep suspicion of the motives of both.
4. ✅ Nehru of India (1947-64) saw himself as a sort of unofficial leader of the Third World, which he thought could be a powerful force for world peace.
5. ✅ Neo-colonialism named the continued interference of both blocs in internal affairs; the American cases listed are Guatemala 1954, the Dominican Republic 1965 and Chile 1973, with British, French and Soviet interference in the Middle East.
6. ✅ In 1979, 92 nations were represented at a non-aligned conference in Havana; by then the Third World was roughly 70 per cent of the world's population.
7. ✅ With 70 per cent of the world's population the Third World consumed 30 per cent of the world's food, while the USA, with perhaps 8 per cent of the population, ate 40 per cent; shortages of proteins and vitamins caused poor health and a high death rate.
8. ✅ The Brandt Report (1980), chaired by Willy Brandt and including Edward Heath, divided the world into a North of North America, Europe, the USSR and Japan plus Australia and New Zealand, and a South of most Third World countries, concluding that the North was getting richer and the South poorer.
9. ✅ In 1989-90 the GNP of the North averaged over 24 times that of the South; in 1992 Japan was over $28,000 per head and Norway $25,800, while Ethiopia managed $110, the second lowest in the world.
10. ✅ Brandt's four recommendations: 0.7 per cent of national income in aid by 1985 and 1.0 per cent by 2000; a new World Development Fund with decision-making shared more evenly between lenders and borrowers, unlike the IMF and World Bank which were dominated by the USA; an international energy plan; and an agricultural-techniques campaign with an international food programme.
11. ✅ For the price of one jet fighter, about $20 million, 40,000 village pharmacies could be set up.
12. ✅ By 1985 only Norway, Sweden, Denmark, the Netherlands and France had reached 0.7 per cent; the USA gave 0.24 per cent and Britain 0.11 per cent; famine struck Ethiopia and the Sudan in the mid-1980s.
13. ✅ At the end of 2003 the UN reported 21 Third World states, 17 in Africa, in crisis from natural disasters, AIDS, global warming and civil wars; the richest 1 per cent, around 60 million people, received as much income as the poorest 57 per cent.
14. ✅ Norway topped the UN human-development table with life expectancy 78.7, literacy virtually 100 per cent and income just under $30,000; Sierra Leone had life expectancy about 35, literacy 35 per cent and income averaging $470.
15. ✅ UN economic advisers called on the West to remove trade barriers, dismantle over-generous subsidies, provide greater debt relief and double aid from $50 billion to $100 billion a year.
16. ⚠️ Master formulation: the North-South framing accurately described a relationship and increasingly inaccurately described a group of countries.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Brandt Report asked the North to help the South partly in the North's own interest. Reconstruct that argument, and explain why it still failed to produce action.

**Model answer:** The argument was a demand argument. The report pointed out that it was in the North's interests to help the South become more prosperous, because a more prosperous South would buy more goods from the North, which would help to avoid unemployment and recession in the North. That is the same logic recorded behind Marshall Aid, where one of the two ulterior motives was that a prosperous western Europe would be able to buy American goods and keep the great American wartime boom going. The report reinforced it with an arithmetic of substitution: for the price of one jet fighter, about $20 million, 40,000 village pharmacies could be set up. Yet by 1985 only Norway, Sweden, Denmark, the Netherlands and France had reached the suggested 0.7 per cent of national income, while the USA gave 0.24 per cent and Britain 0.11 per cent. Three reasons can be stated from the evidence. The benefit was diffuse and deferred while the cost was immediate and budgeted, so no single government captured the return on its own contribution. The political direction of travel in the largest economy was the opposite one: the rise of the New Right, taken up by the neo-conservative Republicans, is recorded as the source of American reluctance on debt relief, trade and global warming. And the report's second recommendation asked for a new fund in which decision-making would be shared more evenly between lenders and borrowers, unlike the IMF and World Bank which were dominated by the USA - which converted a request for money into a request to share control, and made it far harder to grant.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the failure as proof that the argument was wrong. Nothing in the record tests whether a larger transfer would have produced the predicted demand; it only records that the transfer did not happen. Confusing non-implementation with refutation is a specific instance of the correlation-and-causation discipline, and an answer that keeps them apart can say something much stronger - that the Brandt proposals were defeated politically rather than economically, and that their second recommendation explains why.

---

## Lesson 11 - Why the South stayed poor: six mechanisms and the debt that followed

Progress: 11/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: dependence and neo-colonialism; the one-product economy and the food it displaces; the commodity price collapse of the 1970s and its terms-of-trade evidence; rising manufactured prices and the failure of UNCTAD; aid on a business basis, tied and interest-bearing; the debt of 1980 and the compounding trap; faster population growth; war and drought

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's six-point explanation of why the South was poor, its table of what a tonne of copper, cocoa, coffee and cotton could buy in 1975 and 1980, its record of UNCTAD's failure to secure fairer prices, its statement of Third World debt and interest by 1980, and its notes on faster population growth, crippling wars and drought in Niger
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 15 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: six mechanisms, and the hard evidence attached to each

| # | Mechanism | ✅ The evidence that proves it | ⚠️ Why it is self-reinforcing |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Dependence on the North (neo-colonialism)** | The North expected the South to continue providing food and raw materials and to buy manufactured goods from the North; "they did not encourage the South to develop their own industries" | A country kept in Role A of Lesson 1 never acquires the capability that would let it leave |
| 2 | **The one-product economy** | Many states found it difficult to break away from the one-product economies left behind from colonial days, because governments lacked the cash needed to diversify - **Ghana (cocoa)** and **Zambia (copper)** | Diversification costs money; the money comes from the single product; a bad year removes both the income and the escape |
| 3 | **Export crops displacing food** | In states like Ghana, which depended for income on exporting crops, too little food was left for the population, so governments had to spend scarce money importing expensive food | The export earnings are consumed by the import bill they themselves create |
| 4 | **Commodity price collapse** | "A fall in the world price of their main product would be a major disaster." In the 1970s there was a dramatic fall in the world price of cocoa, copper, coffee and cotton, with disastrous effects on **Ghana and Cameroon** (cocoa), **Zambia, Chile and Peru** (copper), **Mozambique, Egypt and the Sudan** (cotton) and **Ivory Coast, Zaire and Ethiopia** (coffee) | Prices are set in markets the producer does not control, so national budgets become a function of foreign demand |
| 5 | **Rising manufactured prices and the failure of remedy** | At the same time, prices of manufactured goods continued to rise, and the South had to import from the North; in spite of the efforts of the **United Nations Conference on Trade and Development (UNCTAD)**, which tried to negotiate fairer prices for the Third World, no real improvement was achieved | Both blades of the scissors move at once - this is the "sell cheap and buy dear" of Lesson 1, now without an empire to enforce it |
| 6 | **Aid on a business basis, and then debt** | Much aid was on a business basis - the countries of the South had to pay interest; sometimes a condition of the deal was that they spend the aid on goods from the lending country; some borrowed directly from banks in the USA and western Europe, so that **by 1980 Third World countries owed the equivalent of $500 billion**, on which **the annual interest payable was about $50 billion**; some states were forced to borrow more cash just to pay the interest on the original loan | Compounding: a loan taken to service a loan converts a stock of debt into a self-reproducing process |

✅ **Two further pressures are recorded alongside the six.** ✅ Populations in the South were increasing much faster than in the North - in **1975** the total world population stood at about **4,000 million** and was expected to reach **6,000 million by 1997** - so a larger proportion of the world's population than ever before would be poor. ✅ And many Third World countries had suffered long and crippling wars and civil wars which ravaged crops and ruined economies - ✅ some of the worst being in **Ethiopia, Nicaragua, Guatemala, Lebanon, the Congo/Zaire, Sudan, Somalia, Liberia, Sierra Leone, Mozambique and Angola** - ✅ while drought was sometimes a serious problem in Africa: ✅ **Niger** in **1974** produced only half the food crops grown in **1970**, mainly millet and sorghum, and about **40 per cent** of the cattle died.

### 1. The terms-of-trade evidence, and why it is the best table in the topic

✅ **Fact.** The account measures the collapse not in prices but in **purchasing power**, which is the correct unit, and the table is worth reproducing exactly.

| ✅ What one tonne could buy | ✅ 1975 | ✅ 1980 |
|---|---|---|
| **Copper** - barrels of oil | 115 | 58 |
| **Copper** - capital, US dollars | 17,800 | 9,500 |
| **Cocoa** - barrels of oil | 148 | 63 |
| **Cocoa** - capital, US dollars | 23,400 | 10,200 |
| **Coffee** - barrels of oil | 148 | 82 |
| **Coffee** - capital, US dollars | 22,800 | 13,000 |
| **Cotton** - barrels of oil | 119 | 60 |
| **Cotton** - capital, US dollars | 18,400 | 9,600 |

⚠️ Read the table as a mechanism and three things emerge that a price series would hide.

First, the **unit is the point**. A tonne of cocoa is the same physical quantity in 1975 and in 1980; what changed is what it commands in exchange. This is the **terms of trade** made concrete - the ratio at which a country's exports exchange for its imports - and it is far more persuasive than a nominal price, because it cannot be explained away by inflation.

Second, **the fall is roughly halving across four unrelated commodities within five years**. Four separate markets - a mineral, two tree crops and a fibre - moving together tells you the cause is not in any one of them.

Third, **oil is the numeraire in the first row of each pair**, and that is not accidental. Between 1975 and 1980 the commodity a producer had to buy had become dramatically more expensive while the commodity it had to sell had not. ⚠️ That is the arithmetic bridge into Lesson 12, and it is the reason the oil decision of 1973 belongs to the story of the South's poverty and not only to the story of the North's inflation.

### 2. The aid mechanism, stated precisely

✅ **Fact.** Although a great deal of financial aid was given by the North to the South, much of it was on a business basis - the countries of the South had to pay interest. ✅ Sometimes a condition of the deal was that countries of the South had to spend the aid on goods from the country which was making the loan.

⚠️ Name that condition and use the name: **tied aid**. Its effect is to make the transfer partially circular. A loan that must be spent on the lender's goods returns a portion of the money to the lender's producers immediately, while the borrower carries the whole debt and the whole interest. ⚠️ The borrower is also prevented from buying the cheapest available supply, so the real value of the transfer is less than its face value.

✅ And then the accumulation: ✅ some countries borrowed directly from banks in the USA and western Europe, so that by **1980** Third World countries owed the equivalent of **$500 billion**; ✅ even the annual interest payable was about **$50 billion**; ✅ and some states were forced to borrow more cash just to pay the interest on the original loan.

⚠️ That last clause is the single most important sentence in the lesson, and it deserves its own name: the **compounding trap**. Once new borrowing is used to service old borrowing, the debt stops being a stock that can be paid down and becomes a process that reproduces itself. ⚠️ Note also the ratio implied by the two figures - annual interest of about $50 billion on debt of about $500 billion - and note that this is a statement about a specific year that should be quoted with its date and not generalised.

### 3. Correlation and causation, applied to this lesson

⚠️ This is the right place to exercise the discipline, because the lesson lists eight pressures and a weak answer will simply add them together.

- ⚠️ Mechanisms 1 to 5 are **causal chains** with stated links: dependence prevents industrial development; the single product prevents diversification; export crops displace food; price falls remove income; rising import prices remove more. Each can be traced from a policy or a market to a budget outcome.
- ⚠️ Mechanism 6 is also causal, and its mechanism - interest, tying and compounding - is stated explicitly.
- ⚠️ **Faster population growth is different.** ✅ The account records the association and its distributional consequence: a larger proportion of the world's population than ever before would be poor. ⚠️ It does not establish that population growth *caused* the poverty, and Lesson 19 will show the account's own evidence pointing the other way as well, since poverty and a high death rate are recorded as reasons why families were large. ⚠️ In an answer, state it as a distributional fact and a compounding pressure, not as a cause.
- ⚠️ **War and drought are exogenous shocks** with obvious effects, but note that the account lists them as additional problems rather than as explanations of the structural position.

⚠️ A candidate who sorts the list this way - five structural mechanisms, one financial mechanism, one distributional fact and two shocks - has done something most scripts never attempt, and an examiner will notice.

### Criticism and reply: does this account leave any room for domestic responsibility?

**The objection.** ⚠️ Six external mechanisms and two shocks add up to a picture in which nothing that happened inside these countries mattered. Governments made choices about industrial policy, about agriculture, about borrowing and about war, and an account that omits them is incomplete.

**The reply, which concedes a great deal.** ⚠️ The objection is right, and the account itself supplies the material to answer it. ✅ It records that the oil-producing states made huge profits and that this "did not necessarily mean that their governments spent the money wisely or for the benefit of their populations". ✅ It records that many of the crippling wars were civil wars. ✅ And Lesson 13 will record that some Third World states industrialised rapidly and with great success under the same external conditions, which is the decisive evidence that the external environment did not determine the outcome. ⚠️ The disciplined formulation is therefore: **the external structure set the terms on which each state had to operate; domestic capability determined what each state made of those terms.** ⚠️ Lesson 22 develops this into the full dependency-versus-institutions argument.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Decolonisation and its aftermath, and the effects of economic systems on society. ⚠️ This lesson is the mechanism behind the divide that Lesson 10 described.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ "Why did the North-South divide persist?" or "examine the economic problems of the developing world after independence". ⚠️ The structure that scores is three or four mechanisms with one named case each, the terms-of-trade table as the evidential centrepiece, the debt figures of 1980, and a closing qualification about internal divergence.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Listing problems instead of mechanisms. ⚠️ Each item must arrive at a budget outcome.
2. ⚠️ Using nominal prices instead of purchasing power. ✅ The table measures what a tonne could buy, which is the terms of trade.
3. ⚠️ Treating aid as a pure transfer. ✅ Much of it carried interest and was sometimes tied to the lender's goods.
4. ⚠️ Giving the debt figure without the year or the interest. ✅ About $500 billion by 1980, with annual interest of about $50 billion.
5. ⚠️ Making population growth a cause of poverty. ✅ The account records it as a reason why a larger proportion of the world's population would be poor; the causal direction is argued in Lessons 19 and 20.
6. ⚠️ Omitting internal divergence. ✅ Lesson 13 records states industrialising successfully under the same conditions.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The South was and still is economically dependent on the North because of neo-colonialism; the North expected the South to keep providing food and raw materials and to buy manufactures, and did not encourage the South to develop its own industries.
2. ✅ Many states could not break away from the one-product economies left from colonial days because governments lacked the cash to diversify - Ghana with cocoa and Zambia with copper.
3. ✅ Where income depended on exporting crops, too little food was left for the population, and scarce money had to be spent importing expensive food.
4. ✅ A fall in the world price of the main product would be a major disaster; in the 1970s there was a dramatic fall in the world price of cocoa, copper, coffee and cotton.
5. ✅ The countries named as hit are Ghana and Cameroon for cocoa; Zambia, Chile and Peru for copper; Mozambique, Egypt and the Sudan for cotton; and Ivory Coast, Zaire and Ethiopia for coffee.
6. ✅ The terms-of-trade table: a tonne of copper bought 115 barrels of oil in 1975 and 58 in 1980; cocoa 148 and 63; coffee 148 and 82; cotton 119 and 60, with the dollar equivalents falling in the same proportion.
7. ✅ Prices of manufactured goods continued to rise while the South had to import from the North; despite UNCTAD's efforts to negotiate fairer prices, no real improvement was achieved.
8. ✅ Much aid was on a business basis with interest payable, and was sometimes tied to spending on goods from the lending country.
9. ✅ By 1980 Third World countries owed the equivalent of $500 billion, with annual interest of about $50 billion; some states borrowed more just to pay the interest - the compounding trap.
10. ✅ Populations in the South were growing much faster than in the North; world population was about 4,000 million in 1975 and expected to reach 6,000 million by 1997, so a larger proportion of the world's people than ever before would be poor.
11. ✅ Long and crippling wars and civil wars ravaged crops and economies in Ethiopia, Nicaragua, Guatemala, Lebanon, the Congo/Zaire, Sudan, Somalia, Liberia, Sierra Leone, Mozambique and Angola.
12. ✅ Drought: Niger in 1974 produced only half the food crops of 1970, mainly millet and sorghum, and about 40 per cent of the cattle died.
13. ⚠️ Sort the list: five structural mechanisms, one financial mechanism, one distributional fact and two exogenous shocks.
14. ⚠️ Master formulation: the external structure set the terms; domestic capability determined what each state made of them.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the table of what a tonne of copper, cocoa, coffee and cotton could buy in 1975 and 1980 is better evidence than a table of their prices, and state precisely what it proves.

**Model answer:** Because it measures the terms of trade rather than a nominal price, and the terms of trade are what determine whether an exporting country can pay for what it must import. A tonne is a fixed physical quantity, so any change in what it commands is a change in real exchange value that cannot be explained away by inflation in either currency. The figures are stark and consistent: a tonne of copper bought 115 barrels of oil in 1975 and 58 in 1980; cocoa fell from 148 barrels to 63; coffee from 148 to 82; and cotton from 119 to 60, with the dollar-capital equivalents falling in the same proportion. Three things follow. First, the roughly halving of purchasing power across four unrelated markets - a mineral, two tree crops and a fibre - in five years shows the cause lay outside any individual commodity. Second, the choice of oil as the unit of comparison identifies part of that cause, since it was the price of what producers had to buy that moved against them as much as the price of what they sold. Third, it demonstrates exactly why the account writes that a fall in the world price of a country's main product would be a major disaster: in a one-product economy, the terms of trade are the national budget. A price table would have shown numbers falling; this table shows a country's capacity to import collapsing.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the commodity producers were simply unlucky. The vulnerability was structural rather than accidental, and the account identifies the structure: these were one-product economies left behind from colonial days, whose governments lacked the cash needed to diversify, so that the very revenue that would have financed an escape was the revenue being destroyed. An answer that describes the price collapse without naming that structure has explained a bad five years instead of a persistent condition.
