## Lesson 12 - Oil, 1973: from a price decision to a debt trap

Progress: 12/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the producers' decision of 1973 and its stated motive; the surpluses it created and what was done with them; the strain on importers; the bounded question of whether surpluses financed the borrowing; the reversal when commodity prices turned; and the disciplined handling of the term "stagflation"

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's record that in 1973 the members of the Organization of Petroleum Exporting Countries, partly in an attempt to conserve oil supplies, began to charge more for their oil; its record of the profits made by Middle Eastern producers, Nigeria and Libya with the Libyan welfare-state case; its statement that rising oil prices worsened America's balance-of-payments deficit; and its statement that resource exhaustion was one of the reasons OPEC tried to conserve oil during the 1970s. The founding of the organisation and the second shock of 1979 come from the single declared verification pass recorded in the final ledger
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 15 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the chain, with the weak links marked rather than hidden

```text
   [1] SUPPLY ACTION BY PRODUCERS                                  ✅ SOURCED
       1973: OPEC members, "partly in an attempt to conserve oil
       supplies", began to charge more for their oil
       -- motive recorded as CONSERVATION as well as revenue
       -- and resource exhaustion is separately recorded as "one of
          the reasons why OPEC tried to conserve oil during the 1970s"
                     |
                     v
   [2] SHARP RISE IN THE PRICE OF IMPORTED ENERGY                  ✅ SOURCED
                     |
        +------------+------------------------------+
        |                                           |
        v                                           v
   [3] PRODUCER SURPLUSES              ✅        [4] IMPORTER STRAIN      ✅
   "The Middle East oil-producing          "Rising oil prices worsened
    states made huge profits, as did        America's balance-of-payments
    Nigeria and Libya"                      deficit, and led to the
   -- but "this did not necessarily         development of more nuclear
      mean that their governments           power"
      spent the money wisely"           -- and the commodity table of
   -- LIBYA is the recorded exception:     Lesson 11 shows importers of
      Gaddafi (in power from 1969)         oil paying in cocoa, copper,
      used much of the oil profits on      coffee and cotton whose
      agricultural and industrial          purchasing power was halving
      development and to set up a
      WELFARE STATE; GNP of GBP 5460
      in 1989
        |                                           |
        v                                           v
   [5] RECYCLING INTO LENDING        ⚠️ NOT ESTABLISHED BY THIS EVIDENCE
       The sources held here record the OUTCOME - heavy borrowing and
       a compounding debt - but do NOT establish that oil surpluses
       were the specific source of those loans.
       => WRITE THE SEQUENCE, NOT THE PLUMBING:
          heavy borrowing preceded the crisis, and the debt then
          became self-compounding (Lesson 11: $500bn owed by 1980,
          about $50bn annual interest, some borrowing again to pay it)
                     |
                     v
   [6] THE TERMS OF TRADE TURN                                     ✅ SOURCED
       1975 -> 1980: a tonne of copper buys 115 barrels of oil, then 58
                     cocoa 148 -> 63 ; coffee 148 -> 82 ; cotton 119 -> 60
                     |
                     v
   [7] SOVEREIGN VULNERABILITY
       "A fall in the world price of their main product would be a
        major disaster"

   ⚠️ TERM CONTROL: "STAGFLATION"
   The sources held for this session do not use or define the term and
   supply no inflation or unemployment series. If it is used at all it
   must be defined in the candidate's own words as an analytical label
   for simultaneous stagnation and price rises, and NO figures attached.
```

⚠️ The diagram marks its own weak link. That is deliberate and it is the lesson's method: a candidate who knows exactly where the evidence stops can write confidently everywhere else.

### 1. The learner's problem: why one price matters this much

⚠️ Oil is not simply an expensive commodity; it is an input into almost every other commodity. Transport, fertiliser, plastics, electricity generation and mechanised farming all embed an oil price. ⚠️ A change in it therefore behaves less like a price change and more like a change in the cost of operating an industrial economy at all - which is why a producers' decision in 1973 is treated in this topic as an event of the same order as a war or a depression.

### 2. The decision, and its recorded motive

✅ **Fact.** During the 1970s some Third World states began to become more prosperous, sometimes thanks to the exploitation of natural resources such as oil. ✅ In **1973** the members of the **Organization of Petroleum Exporting Countries (OPEC)**, partly in an attempt to conserve oil supplies, began to charge more for their oil.

⚠️ **Fact established by the declared verification pass.** OPEC was founded at a conference in **Baghdad in September 1960** by five states - **Iran, Iraq, Kuwait, Saudi Arabia and Venezuela**.

⚠️ Now notice the motive clause, because almost every script omits it and it is the most analytically interesting phrase in the lesson. The account does not describe the action as a purely political or purely revenue-maximising decision; it records conservation as part of the purpose. ✅ And the environment section confirms the link independently: fossil fuels cannot be replaced and are rapidly being used up, oil production increased enormously during the twentieth century, and this was **one of the reasons why OPEC tried to conserve oil during the 1970s**.

⚠️ That cross-reference is worth deploying in an answer, because it does something unusual: it connects the century's most famous price shock to the resource-exhaustion argument of Lesson 17, and shows that the two are not separate subjects. ❌ Do not reduce the 1973 decision to a single political motive; the recorded account is more complicated and more interesting.

### 3. What the surpluses did, and the qualification that must travel with them

✅ **Fact.** The Middle East oil-producing states made huge profits, as did **Nigeria** and **Libya**. ✅ This did not necessarily mean that their governments spent the money wisely or for the benefit of their populations.

✅ One African success story, however, was provided by **Libya**, the richest country in Africa thanks to its oil resources and the policies of its leader, **Colonel Gaddafi**, who took power in **1969**. ✅ He used much of the profits from oil on agricultural and industrial development, and to set up a **welfare state**. ✅ This was one country where ordinary people benefited from oil profits; ✅ with a GNP of **£5,460 in 1989**, Libya could claim to be almost as economically successful as Greece and Portugal, the poorest members of the European Community.

⚠️ Two analytical points, and the second is the one that scores.

First, the qualification about spending is the account's own and should be reproduced rather than softened: resource wealth is an opportunity, not an outcome.

Second, ⚠️ the Libyan case is the earliest clean demonstration in this topic that **the same external windfall produces different results according to what a state does with it**. ⚠️ That observation is the foundation of Lesson 13 and of the whole dependency-versus-institutions argument of Lesson 22, and it arrives here from within the evidence rather than as an editorial comment.

### 4. The importers, and the two ways the shock reached them

✅ **Fact.** Rising oil prices worsened America's balance-of-payments deficit, and led to the development of more nuclear power.

⚠️ For the strongest economy in the world, that is the whole of the recorded damage - a worsened external account and a shift in energy policy. ⚠️ For a one-product exporter the same shock arrived twice over, and the arithmetic of Lesson 11 is the proof. A country selling copper, cocoa, coffee or cotton to buy oil faced a rising oil price and a falling commodity price at once: ✅ a tonne of copper bought 115 barrels of oil in 1975 and 58 in 1980.

⚠️ State the asymmetry explicitly in an answer, because it is what turns an energy event into a development event. **An oil shock is a cost for a diversified industrial economy and a solvency crisis for a single-commodity one.**

### 5. The link this session will not assert

⚠️ It is widely said that the producers' surpluses were lent back to developing countries and that this financed the borrowing which became the debt crisis. ⚠️ The sources held for this session do **not** establish that link. What they record is the outcome rather than the plumbing: ✅ aid carried interest and was sometimes tied; ✅ some countries borrowed directly from banks in the USA and western Europe; ✅ by 1980 the Third World owed the equivalent of $500 billion with about $50 billion of annual interest; ✅ and some states were forced to borrow more merely to pay that interest.

⚠️ The disciplined way to write the sequence is therefore: **heavy borrowing preceded the crisis, and the debt then became self-compounding.** ❌ Do not assert that oil surpluses were the specific source of the loans. ⚠️ An answer that states the sequence accurately and declines to assert the unproven link is stronger than one that asserts it, because the sequence is what the question is actually about.

> ⚠️ **Term control.** The sources held here do not use or define "stagflation" and supply no inflation or unemployment series for the period. ⚠️ If the word is used at all, define it in your own words as an analytical label for simultaneous economic stagnation and rising prices, and ❌ attach no figures to it. ⚠️ The specific American policy response to that condition - monetarism under Reagan - is taught in Lesson 14 from the evidence that does exist.

### 6. The second shock, and what it adds

⚠️ **Fact established by the declared verification pass.** A second oil shock followed in **1979**, associated with the **Iranian revolution** and the sharp reduction in Iranian output.

⚠️ Keep this brief and use it for one purpose only: it shows that the 1973 event was not a single episode but the beginning of a decade in which energy prices were a recurring source of macroeconomic instability. ⚠️ The political history of the Iranian revolution belongs to the topic that owns it.

### 7. The reversal, and the symmetry that examiners reward

⚠️ The final move in the chain is the one most scripts miss. A commodity boom and a commodity bust are **the same structural vulnerability**, not two different problems.

⚠️ A state whose budget rises automatically when a world price rises has a budget that falls automatically when it falls, and it has, in the interval, usually acquired commitments - salaries, subsidies, infrastructure programmes, debt service - that do not fall with it. ⚠️ That is why the account's warning applies to oil exporters as much as to cocoa exporters: ✅ a fall in the world price of a country's main product would be a major disaster. ⚠️ The oil producers of the 1970s were, structurally, one-product economies with a temporarily favourable price.

### Criticism and reply: was 1973 really the turning point?

**The objection.** ⚠️ The strains on the world economy predate 1973. Lesson 7 records annual American budget deficits in the late 1960s, a dollar weakening by 1971 and a devaluation with an import duty in that year - all before any oil decision. Treating 1973 as the hinge may be a case of dating a structural change to its most dramatic moment.

**The reply, which largely accepts the point.** ⚠️ The objection is correct and it should be conceded in an answer, because conceding it demonstrates chronological control. The fixed-rate system broke in 1971, before the oil decision, and the American deficits that broke it began in the previous decade. ⚠️ What 1973 added was not the beginning of instability but a specific redistribution of world purchasing power towards producers, a specific cost shock to importers, and a specific interaction with collapsing commodity terms of trade for the poorest exporters. ⚠️ The precise formulation is: **1971 ended the monetary order; 1973 redistributed the real economy inside the disorder that followed.**

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ World history from the eighteenth century onward, and decolonisation's economic aftermath. ⚠️ This lesson also feeds the topic that owns Latin America, whose regional debt crisis is a case of this mechanism, and the topic that owns the decolonised economies of Africa.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ "Examine the effects of the oil shocks of the 1970s", or as a paragraph inside answers on the North-South divide or on the world economy since 1900. ⚠️ The structure that scores is: the decision with its stated motive, the two-sided effect on producers and importers, the terms-of-trade interaction, the debt sequence stated without the unproven link, and the symmetry of boom and bust.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing "oil got expensive" as the whole analysis. ⚠️ Name the redistribution of purchasing power and the asymmetric effect on diversified and single-commodity economies.
2. ⚠️ Reducing the 1973 decision to one motive. ✅ Conservation is recorded as part of the purpose, and resource exhaustion is independently recorded as one of the reasons.
3. ⚠️ Asserting that oil surpluses financed the developing world's debt. ⚠️ The evidence held here does not establish it.
4. ⚠️ Using "stagflation" with figures. ⚠️ The term is not used in these sources and no series is held.
5. ⚠️ Treating oil producers as structurally secure. ⚠️ A single-commodity economy with a high price is still a single-commodity economy.
6. ⚠️ Dating the collapse of the monetary order to 1973. ✅ The devaluation and import duty were in 1971.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ In 1973 the members of OPEC, partly in an attempt to conserve oil supplies, began to charge more for their oil.
2. ⚠️ OPEC was founded at a conference in Baghdad in September 1960 by Iran, Iraq, Kuwait, Saudi Arabia and Venezuela.
3. ✅ Resource exhaustion is separately recorded as one of the reasons why OPEC tried to conserve oil during the 1970s; fossil fuels cannot be replaced and were being rapidly used up.
4. ✅ The Middle East oil-producing states made huge profits, as did Nigeria and Libya; this did not necessarily mean their governments spent the money wisely or for the benefit of their populations.
5. ✅ Libya is the recorded exception: Gaddafi, in power from 1969, used much of the oil profits on agricultural and industrial development and to set up a welfare state; with a GNP of £5,460 in 1989 Libya was almost as successful as Greece and Portugal.
6. ✅ Rising oil prices worsened America's balance-of-payments deficit and led to the development of more nuclear power.
7. ⚠️ Asymmetry: an oil shock is a cost for a diversified industrial economy and a solvency crisis for a single-commodity one.
8. ⚠️ The recycling link is not established by the evidence held here; write the sequence - heavy borrowing preceded the crisis and the debt became self-compounding - not the plumbing.
9. ✅ The debt position of 1980: about $500 billion owed, about $50 billion annual interest, and some states borrowing again to pay it.
10. ⚠️ A second oil shock followed in 1979, associated with the Iranian revolution and the reduction of Iranian output.
11. ⚠️ Boom and bust are the same vulnerability: a budget that rises with a world price falls with it, and the commitments acquired in between do not fall.
12. ⚠️ "Stagflation" is not used in these sources; if used at all, define it in your own words and attach no figures.
13. ⚠️ Master formulation: 1971 ended the monetary order; 1973 redistributed the real economy inside the disorder that followed.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does this session refuse to say that oil surpluses financed developing-world debt, and what should a candidate write instead?

**Model answer:** Because the link is not established by the evidence held, and asserting it would be exactly the kind of plausible reconstruction this topic punishes. What the sources do record is each end of the supposed chain but not its middle. At one end, in 1973 OPEC members, partly in an attempt to conserve oil supplies, began to charge more for their oil, and the Middle East oil-producing states made huge profits, as did Nigeria and Libya. At the other end, much aid was on a business basis carrying interest and was sometimes tied to purchases from the lender, some countries borrowed directly from banks in the USA and western Europe, by 1980 Third World countries owed the equivalent of $500 billion with annual interest of about $50 billion, and some states were forced to borrow more cash merely to pay that interest. What is missing is any statement that the surpluses of the first passage were the source of the loans in the second. A candidate should therefore write the sequence rather than the plumbing: heavy external borrowing preceded the crisis; the terms of trade then turned sharply against commodity exporters, a tonne of copper buying 115 barrels of oil in 1975 and 58 in 1980; and the debt became self-compounding once new loans were needed to service old ones. That account answers the question, keeps every claim checkable, and is stronger for its restraint.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that refusing to assert the link means denying it. This session takes no position on whether producer surpluses were lent onward; it records that the evidence held does not settle the question. The examinable skill is to notice the difference between a claim you can support and a claim you have merely heard, because an examiner who spots an unsupported mechanism in one sentence will read the rest of the answer with suspicion.

## Lesson 13 - The split in the South: why "the Third World" stopped being one thing

Progress: 13/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: prosperity from resources and from industrialisation; the four Pacific tigers and the wider group; the relocation of production to cheaper labour and its effect on northern employment; the next stage of relocation in the mid-1990s; the labour-standards argument of 1996 and the International Labour Organization; and the consequence for the North-South category

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two routes out, and a moving frontier of cheap labour

```text
   ONE CATEGORY IN 1970 ................ SEVERAL TRAJECTORIES BY 1995
   ==================================================================

   ROUTE A: RESOURCE WEALTH              ROUTE B: INDUSTRIALIZATION
   ------------------------              --------------------------
   1973 oil price rise                   The four "PACIFIC TIGERS":
     Middle East producers, Nigeria        SINGAPORE . TAIWAN
     and Libya make huge profits           SOUTH KOREA . HONG KONG
     -- but spending was not               "GNPs compared favourably with
        necessarily wise                    those of many European
     -- LIBYA the recorded exception:       Community countries"
        agriculture, industry and a       And among others:
        welfare state                       THAILAND . MALAYSIA
                                            BRAZIL . MEXICO

   THE ENGINE OF ROUTE B, STATED PLAINLY
   =====================================
   Northern firms were "keen to take advantage of the much cheaper
   labour available in the Third World"; some "even shifted ALL their
   production to newly industrialized countries, where low production
   costs enabled them to sell their goods at lower prices than goods
   produced in the North"
                     |
                     v
   THE EFFECT BACK IN THE NORTH
   ============================
   "serious problems for the industrialized nations of the North, which
   were all suffering high unemployment during the 1990s"
   => "the golden days of western prosperity might have gone ... unless
       their workers were prepared to accept lower wages, or unless
       companies were prepared to make do with lower profits"
                     |
                     v
   THE FRONTIER MOVES AGAIN (mid-1990s)
   ====================================
   the Asian "tigers" themselves start LOSING JOBS to
        MALAYSIA and the PHILIPPINES
   and behind them INDONESIA and CHINA,
        "where wages were even lower and hours of work longer"
                     |
                     v
   THE POLITICAL RESPONSE (April 1996)
   ===================================
   Jacques Chirac: developing countries "should not compete with Europe
   by allowing miserable wages and working conditions"; he called for
   recognition of basic human rights -
     * freedom to join trade unions and for unions to bargain
       collectively, protecting workers against exploitation
     * abolition of forced labour and child labour
   ⚠️ Most developing countries had accepted this on joining the
      International Labour Organization - "but accepting conditions and
      keeping to them were two different things"
```

⚠️ The diagram has a direction of travel built into it. The frontier of cheap labour keeps moving, and the countries that were the destination in one decade become the origin in the next. That is the single most transferable idea in the lesson.

### 1. The learner's problem: what breaks a category?

⚠️ A category stops being useful when the differences inside it become larger than the differences across its boundary. ⚠️ By the 1990s the GNPs of the four Pacific tigers compared favourably with those of many European Community countries while Ethiopia stood at $110 per head. ⚠️ At that point "the Third World" is no longer a description; it is a residue of a classification made for the politics of the 1950s.

### 2. Route A: resource wealth, already qualified

✅ **Fact.** During the 1970s some Third World states began to become more prosperous, sometimes thanks to the exploitation of natural resources such as oil, and also because of industrialisation.

⚠️ The oil route was taught in Lesson 12, with its recorded qualification that huge profits did not necessarily mean the money was spent wisely or for the benefit of populations, and with Libya as the recorded exception where the profits went into agricultural and industrial development and a welfare state. ⚠️ Carry that qualification forward: resource wealth changed a country's income, and only sometimes its capability.

### 3. Route B: industrialisation, and the states that achieved it

✅ **Fact.** Some Third World states industrialised rapidly and with great success. ✅ These included **Singapore, Taiwan, South Korea and Hong Kong** - known as the four **Pacific tiger** economies - ✅ and, among others, **Thailand, Malaysia, Brazil and Mexico**. ✅ The GNPs of the four tiger economies compared favourably with those of many European Community countries.

⚠️ This single sentence is the most consequential in the lesson, and it should be quoted rather than paraphrased in an answer, because it does two jobs at once. It destroys the claim that the world economy prevented development - development demonstrably occurred, inside the same world economy, in the same decades. And it destroys the unity of "the Third World" - a category containing both Singapore and Sierra Leone has stopped carrying information.

### 4. The engine, stated exactly as the account states it

✅ **Fact.** The success of the newly industrialised countries in world export markets was made possible partly because they were able to attract firms from the North who were keen to take advantage of the much cheaper labour available in the Third World. ✅ Some firms even shifted all their production to newly industrialised countries, where low production costs enabled them to sell their goods at lower prices than goods produced in the North.

⚠️ Read that carefully, because it contains the topic's most important double effect. The same movement of production is:

- ⚠️ from the perspective of the receiving country, **industrialisation** - employment, export earnings, technology transfer and the beginnings of a manufacturing base; and
- ⚠️ from the perspective of the sending country, **deindustrialisation** - lost employment and downward pressure on wages.

⚠️ And the account draws that second consequence explicitly: ✅ this posed serious problems for the industrialised nations of the North, which were all suffering high unemployment during the 1990s, and ✅ it seemed that the golden days of western prosperity might have gone, at least for the foreseeable future, unless their workers were prepared to accept lower wages or unless companies were prepared to make do with lower profits.

⚠️ That final sentence is worth dwelling on because of its structure: it names **two** possible adjustments - lower wages or lower profits - and does not choose between them. ⚠️ That is a distributional question, not a technical one, and it is precisely the question that makes globalisation politically contested inside rich countries as well as between rich and poor ones. ⚠️ Lesson 16 records which adjustment actually occurred, when real wages fell in the leading western economies after 1990 while consumption was sustained by credit.

### 5. The frontier moves, and the pattern generalises

✅ **Fact.** In the mid-1990s the world economy was moving into the next stage, in which the Asian tigers found themselves losing jobs to workers in countries such as **Malaysia** and the **Philippines**. ✅ Other Third World states in the process of industrialising were **Indonesia** and **China**, where wages were even lower and hours of work longer.

⚠️ This is the observation that converts the lesson from a list of success stories into a mechanism. ⚠️ Cost-driven relocation has no natural resting place: whichever country is currently cheapest becomes, by succeeding, less cheap, and production moves on. ⚠️ Two consequences follow for an answer. First, the "tiger" outcome is not automatically repeatable, because part of what a tiger economy gained was a temporary cost position that its own success eroded. Second, a development strategy built only on being cheap is a strategy with a time limit; what distinguishes a lasting transformation is what a state builds while the window is open.

### 6. The labour-standards argument, and why it is not simply protectionism

✅ **Fact.** **Jacques Chirac**, the French president, expressed the fears and concerns of many when he pointed out in **April 1996** that developing countries should not compete with Europe by allowing miserable wages and working conditions. ✅ He called for a recognition that there are certain basic human rights which need to be encouraged and enforced - ✅ freedom to join trade unions and the freedom for those unions to bargain collectively, for the protection of workers against exploitation; ✅ and the abolition of forced labour and child labour.

✅ And the account's own qualification: ✅ in fact most developing countries accepted this when they joined the **International Labour Organization**, ✅ but accepting conditions and keeping to them were two different things.

⚠️ Handle this passage with the balance it requires, because it is genuinely two-sided.

⚠️ **The case that it is principled.** The specific demands - union freedom, collective bargaining, abolition of forced and child labour - are labour rights recognised by the organisation that developing countries had themselves joined. Asking that accepted obligations be honoured is not a trade barrier.

⚠️ **The case that it is protective.** The intervention came from a country losing production, at a moment when the North was suffering high unemployment, and the practical effect of raising a competitor's labour costs is to slow the relocation. ⚠️ The account signals this by placing the remark immediately after the passage about northern unemployment and the golden days of western prosperity.

⚠️ **The disciplined formulation.** A demand can be both genuinely principled and conveniently self-interested, and an answer that says so - naming the specific rights invoked and the specific economic position of the state invoking them - is doing exactly what a "critically examine" directive asks.

### Criticism and reply: does the tiger evidence refute dependency?

**The objection.** ⚠️ If Singapore, Taiwan, South Korea and Hong Kong could reach GNPs comparable with European Community countries, and if Thailand, Malaysia, Brazil and Mexico industrialised substantially, then the structural argument of Lesson 11 must be wrong: the world economy evidently did not prevent development.

**The reply.** ⚠️ It refutes the strong form of the argument and leaves the weak form standing, and the distinction is what a top-band answer states. ⚠️ The **strong form** - that integration into the world economy makes development impossible - is indeed refuted by the tiger record. ⚠️ The **weak form** - that integration rewards existing capability and penalises its absence - is confirmed rather than refuted by the same record: the states that converted the relocation of production into lasting industrial bases were those already able to supply skilled labour, infrastructure and policy direction, while states whose only asset was a single crop or mineral continued to face the terms of trade recorded in Lesson 11. ⚠️ The formulation to carry into an answer: **the same world economy that produced Singapore produced Sierra Leone, and any explanation that cannot accommodate both is not an explanation.**

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Decolonisation and after, and the effects of economic systems on society. ⚠️ This lesson is the direct answer to any question that treats the developing world as a single unit.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ "Why did globalisation not remove the North-South divide?" or "examine the divergence within the developing world since 1970". ⚠️ The structure that scores places the tiger evidence early, names the relocation mechanism, states the double effect on North and South, notes the moving frontier, and closes on the category's loss of analytical value.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing about "the Third World" as one economic actor. ✅ Some states industrialised rapidly and with great success; the category split.
2. ⚠️ Presenting relocation as purely beneficial or purely harmful. ✅ It industrialised the receiver and created serious problems for the North simultaneously.
3. ⚠️ Treating the tiger model as universally available. ✅ The frontier moved on to Malaysia, the Philippines, Indonesia and China; cheapness is a self-eroding advantage.
4. ⚠️ Dismissing the labour-standards argument as simple protectionism, or accepting it as simple principle. ✅ Both readings are supported and both should be stated.
5. ⚠️ Forgetting the International Labour Organization qualification. ✅ Most developing countries had accepted these conditions on joining it, but accepting and keeping were two different things.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ During the 1970s some Third World states became more prosperous through natural resources such as oil and through industrialisation.
2. ✅ The four Pacific tiger economies were Singapore, Taiwan, South Korea and Hong Kong; among others, Thailand, Malaysia, Brazil and Mexico also industrialised.
3. ✅ The GNPs of the four tigers compared favourably with those of many European Community countries.
4. ✅ Their export success was made possible partly by attracting northern firms keen to take advantage of much cheaper labour; some firms shifted all their production, and low costs let them undersell northern goods.
5. ✅ This posed serious problems for the industrialised nations of the North, all suffering high unemployment during the 1990s.
6. ✅ The golden days of western prosperity might have gone unless workers accepted lower wages or companies accepted lower profits - a distributional choice, not a technical one.
7. ✅ In the mid-1990s the tigers themselves began losing jobs to Malaysia and the Philippines, with Indonesia and China industrialising on even lower wages and longer hours.
8. ⚠️ Cost-driven relocation has no resting place: success erodes the cheapness that attracted the production.
9. ✅ Chirac, April 1996: developing countries should not compete with Europe by allowing miserable wages and working conditions; basic rights to be encouraged and enforced are union membership and collective bargaining, and the abolition of forced and child labour.
10. ✅ Most developing countries accepted these conditions on joining the International Labour Organization, but accepting conditions and keeping to them were two different things.
11. ⚠️ The labour-standards demand is simultaneously principled and self-interested; say both.
12. ⚠️ The tiger record refutes the strong form of the dependency argument and confirms the weak form: integration rewards existing capability.
13. ⚠️ Master formulation: the same world economy that produced Singapore produced Sierra Leone, and any explanation that cannot accommodate both is not an explanation.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes that the industrialisation of the Pacific tigers proves that the world economy was fair. Assess this claim using the mechanism by which they industrialised.

**Model answer:** It proves less than the candidate thinks, because of how the industrialisation happened. The account is explicit that the success of the newly industrialised countries in world export markets was made possible partly because they were able to attract firms from the North who were keen to take advantage of the much cheaper labour available in the Third World, with some firms shifting all their production so that low costs let them undersell northern goods. The gain was therefore real - the GNPs of Singapore, Taiwan, South Korea and Hong Kong compared favourably with those of many European Community countries - but the initial mechanism was a wage differential rather than an equalisation of opportunity. Two consequences follow. First, the advantage was self-eroding, which is why by the mid-1990s the tigers themselves were losing jobs to Malaysia and the Philippines, with Indonesia and China industrialising on even lower wages and longer hours. Second, the same movement created serious problems for the industrialised nations of the North, all suffering high unemployment during the 1990s, and posed the distributional question of whether workers would accept lower wages or companies lower profits. The accurate claim is therefore narrower and stronger: the world economy did not prevent development, and the development it permitted arrived through a cost advantage that moved on. Fairness is not demonstrated by either fact.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the tiger record and the persistence of extreme poverty as contradictory pieces of evidence, one of which must be wrong. They are simultaneously true and they are the whole point of the lesson. In 1992 Japan's GNP was over $28,000 per head and Ethiopia's $110, while the tiger economies stood comparison with western Europe. An account that cannot hold all three of those facts at once has not understood the uneven-integration discipline, which is that the same system produced convergence for some participants and none for others, according to what each was able to supply.

## Lesson 14 - The neoliberal turn: American strain, monetarism and deregulation

Progress: 14/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the American economic problems of the 1970s and 1980s; Nixon's devaluation and import duty of 1971; Reagan, Friedman and monetarism; the parallel British policy; neoliberalism as its own supporters defined it; the deregulation of American banking in 1999-2000; and the correlation-and-causation discipline applied to a policy era

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's record of the American problems of the 1970s and 1980s including the Vietnam drain, the annual budget deficits, the 1971 devaluation and import duty, and Reagan's refusal to cut defence spending and adoption of Milton Friedman's monetarism with its parallel in Margaret Thatcher's Britain; together with its later definition of neo-liberalism through Sir Keith Joseph and its account of the 1999-2000 banking deregulation
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 15 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: strain, doctrine, instrument, consequence

```text
   STRAIN (from the late 1960s)
   ============================
   * defence costs and the war in Vietnam (1961-75): "a constant drain
     on the economy and the treasury"
   * "a budget deficit every year in the late 1960s" - the difference
     covered by SELLING GOLD RESERVES
   * by 1971 the dollar "was weakening in value"
   * rising oil prices worsened the balance-of-payments deficit
                     |
                     v
   FIRST RESPONSE (1971): devalue by about 12% + a 10% duty on most imports
                     |
                     v
   DOCTRINE (from 1981)
   ====================
   Reagan (1981-9) REFUSED to cut defence spending and tried new
   economic policies recommended by MILTON FRIEDMAN, who argued that
   governments should
        * ABANDON all attempts to plan their economies, and
        * concentrate on MONETARISM:
             "exercising a tight control on the money supply by
              keeping INTEREST RATES HIGH"
        * theory: this would FORCE BUSINESSES TO BE MORE EFFICIENT
   -- "These were policies which Margaret Thatcher was already trying
      in Britain"
   -- "At first the new ideas seemed to be working"
                     |
                     v
   THE DOCTRINE NAMED BY ITS OWN SUPPORTERS
   ========================================
   Sir Keith Joseph, a UK Conservative supporter of the free-market
   system, on NEO-LIBERALISM:
        "the strict and unflinching control of money supply,
         substantial cuts in tax and public spending and bold
         incentives and encouragements to the wealth creators"
                     |
                     v
   INSTRUMENT (1999-2000): DEREGULATION OF US BANKING
   ==================================================
   Nov 1999  an act "designed to promote economic growth through
             competition and freedom" CANCELLED the regulation
             DATING FROM THE DEPRESSION OF THE 1930s that prevented
             investment banks from handling the savings and deposits
             of the general public
             -> they now had access to far larger funds
             -> banks were also allowed to act as insurance companies
   A year later  futures and all other derivatives were EXEMPTED from
             being classified as gambling, and all attempts to regulate
             the derivatives market were DECLARED ILLEGAL
                     |
                     v
   => the instrument that ended the Depression-era settlement is the
      same instrument that Lesson 16 identifies as step one towards 2008
```

⚠️ Follow the arrows and the historical irony states itself: the regulation cancelled in November 1999 was the regulation built in the 1930s in response to the events of Lesson 3, and its cancellation is the first step in the sequence of Lesson 16.

### 1. The learner's problem: what was the turn a turn away from?

⚠️ Away from the settlement of Lessons 5 to 8 - a state that managed demand, regulated finance, underwrote welfare and accepted responsibility for employment. ⚠️ To understand why that settlement was abandoned rather than merely criticised, you need the strain that preceded it, which is why this lesson begins with the American position rather than with the doctrine.

### 2. The strain, and the first response

✅ **Fact.** After many years of continual economic success, the United States began to experience problems. ✅ Defence costs and the war in **Vietnam (1961-75)** were a constant drain on the economy and the treasury. ✅ There was a budget deficit every year in the late 1960s, covered by selling gold reserves. ✅ By **1971** the dollar, once considered as good as gold, was weakening in value, and President **Nixon** was forced to devalue it by about **12 per cent** and to put a **10 per cent** duty on most imports. ✅ Rising oil prices worsened America's balance-of-payments deficit, and led to the development of more nuclear power.

⚠️ Taught in Lesson 7 as the end of the designed monetary order, the same facts do a second job here: they establish that the postwar settlement was under fiscal pressure *before* any doctrinal challenge to it succeeded. ⚠️ Doctrines do not displace settlements on their own; they displace settlements that are already failing to deliver.

### 3. The doctrine, stated precisely

✅ **Fact.** President **Reagan (1981-9)** refused to cut defence spending and tried new economic policies recommended by the American economist **Milton Friedman**. ✅ Friedman argued that governments should abandon all attempts to plan their economies and concentrate on **monetarism**: ✅ this meant exercising a tight control on the money supply by keeping interest rates high. ✅ His theory was that this would force businesses to be more efficient. ✅ These were policies which **Margaret Thatcher** was already trying in Britain. ✅ At first the new ideas seemed to be working.

⚠️ Three things are worth extracting, and each earns a mark.

First, ⚠️ **the internal tension**. The programme combined a refusal to cut defence spending with a doctrine of restricting the state's economic role. A government that maintains high military expenditure while restraining the money supply is not shrinking the state; it is changing what the state does and how it finances it.

Second, ⚠️ **the transmission mechanism is interest rates**. High interest rates restrain the money supply, and they do so by making borrowing expensive - for firms, for households and, critically for this topic, **for sovereign borrowers**. ⚠️ Hold that thought: the developing world of Lesson 11 was carrying about $500 billion of debt by 1980, some of it being serviced by further borrowing. A policy that raises interest rates in the world's principal lending centres raises the cost of that servicing for every borrower simultaneously. ⚠️ This session does not assert a quantified link, but it states the transmission channel plainly, because it is the same channel that Lesson 4 identified: monetary decisions in the largest economy travel outward faster than trade.

Third, ⚠️ **the qualification is the account's own**: at first the new ideas *seemed* to be working. ⚠️ That verb is doing careful work, and an answer should keep it.

### 4. The doctrine's own definition

✅ **Fact.** The system known as **neo-liberalism** had been in operation for the last quarter of a century by the time of the 2008 crisis. ✅ In the words of **Sir Keith Joseph**, a UK Conservative supporter of the free-market system, neo-liberalism involved "the strict and unflinching control of money supply, substantial cuts in tax and public spending and bold incentives and encouragements to the wealth creators".

⚠️ Use this definition rather than an editorial one, for a reason worth stating: it comes from a supporter, so a candidate citing it cannot be accused of characterising the doctrine through its opponents. ⚠️ It also usefully separates three distinct commitments that are often blurred - a monetary commitment, a fiscal commitment and an incentive commitment - and an answer can then assess them separately rather than as a package.

### 5. The instrument: deregulation, and what exactly was cancelled

✅ **Fact.** In **November 1999** the US Congress passed an act designed to promote economic growth through competition and freedom. ✅ This cancelled the regulation, **dating from the Depression of the 1930s**, that prevented investment banks from handling the savings and deposits of the general public, and meant that they now had access to far larger funds. ✅ Banks were also allowed to act as insurance companies. ✅ A year later, futures and all other derivatives were exempted from being classified as gambling, and all attempts to regulate the derivatives market were declared illegal.

⚠️ Stop and register what that first sentence means in the context of this session. The separation cancelled in 1999 was built in response to precisely the failure of Lesson 3 - banks speculating with the cash deposited with them, so that ordinary depositors who had had nothing to do with the speculation lost their life savings. ⚠️ Removing it restored, by design, the structural condition that the 1930s had legislated against.

⚠️ The second measure is equally telling. Classifying derivatives as gambling would have subjected them to one regime; exempting them subjected them to none, and declaring attempts at regulation illegal removed even the possibility of a later correction. ⚠️ Note the asymmetry of that legislative choice: it did not merely deregulate, it pre-committed against re-regulating.

### 6. Correlation and causation, applied to a policy era

⚠️ This is the lesson where the discipline matters most, because the temptation to write "neoliberalism caused the 2008 crisis" is strong and the evidence held here supports something more precise.

- ⚠️ **What is established.** A named deregulation of November 1999 removed a specific Depression-era separation; a further measure exempted derivatives from regulation; and Lesson 16 will record that derivative investments reached a total value of $596 trillion in 2007 against $63 trillion for the world's stock market companies. The link from the legal change to the instrument's growth is stated in the sources rather than inferred.
- ⚠️ **What is attributed.** ✅ Paul Mason argues that the system known as neo-liberalism, in operation for the previous quarter of a century, was mainly responsible for the catastrophe, and ✅ he assigns responsibility to bankers who broke the law, to regulators, to politicians and to the media who failed to hold them to scrutiny. ⚠️ That is an author's judgement, and it should be reported as one.
- ⚠️ **What is not established.** That monetarism in the 1980s produced the crisis of 2008 through any traced chain. ⚠️ Twenty-five years of a policy orientation is a context, not a mechanism, and an answer that names the specific legal changes of 1999 and 2000 is making a far stronger claim than one that gestures at an era.

### Criticism and reply: was the turn a failure?

**The objection.** ⚠️ An answer that runs from Friedman to Lehman implies the whole programme was mistaken. But the account records that at first the new ideas seemed to be working, and the strain that produced them - a war-driven drain, annual deficits, a weakening currency and an oil-driven payments problem - was real and required an answer.

**The reply.** ⚠️ Conceded, and the concession improves the answer. The disciplined judgement separates the phases. The monetary tightening of the early 1980s was a response to a genuine crisis of the previous settlement, and the account does not record its failure. The financial deregulation of 1999-2000 is a different decision taken two decades later, and its consequences are traced in Lesson 16 through named instruments. ⚠️ The formulation to carry: **the turn began as an answer to a real breakdown and ended by dismantling the protections built after the previous one.** ⚠️ That sentence concedes the strongest point on each side and is defensible on the evidence held.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The effects of economic systems on society, and the world economy since 1900. ⚠️ This lesson is also the necessary bridge between the postwar settlement and the crisis, and no answer on 2008 is complete without it.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Usually as a component: "explain why there was a crisis of capitalism in the decade leading up to 2012", or as the middle section of an answer on changes in the world economy. ⚠️ The structure that scores is strain, doctrine with its named economists, instrument with its dates, and a qualified verdict.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Describing monetarism vaguely. ✅ It is tight control of the money supply by keeping interest rates high, with the stated aim of forcing businesses to be more efficient.
2. ⚠️ Presenting the turn as pure ideology. ✅ It followed a decade of drain, deficits and currency weakness.
3. ⚠️ Saying neoliberalism caused 2008 without a mechanism. ⚠️ Name the November 1999 act and the derivatives exemption; attribute the broader judgement to its author.
4. ⚠️ Forgetting the internal tension. ✅ Reagan refused to cut defence spending while adopting a doctrine of restraint.
5. ⚠️ Characterising the doctrine through its critics. ✅ Sir Keith Joseph's definition comes from a supporter.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The strain: defence costs and the Vietnam war (1961-75) a constant drain; a budget deficit every year in the late 1960s covered by selling gold reserves; the dollar weakening by 1971; rising oil prices worsening the payments deficit.
2. ✅ First response, 1971: devaluation of about 12 per cent and a 10 per cent duty on most imports.
3. ✅ Reagan (1981-9) refused to cut defence spending and adopted policies recommended by Milton Friedman.
4. ✅ Monetarism: abandon attempts to plan the economy and exercise tight control of the money supply by keeping interest rates high, so as to force businesses to be more efficient.
5. ✅ Margaret Thatcher was already trying these policies in Britain; at first the new ideas seemed to be working.
6. ✅ Sir Keith Joseph's definition of neo-liberalism: strict and unflinching control of money supply, substantial cuts in tax and public spending, and bold incentives and encouragements to the wealth creators.
7. ✅ November 1999: an act designed to promote growth through competition and freedom cancelled the Depression-era regulation preventing investment banks from handling the savings and deposits of the general public, giving them access to far larger funds; banks could also act as insurance companies.
8. ✅ A year later futures and all other derivatives were exempted from classification as gambling and attempts to regulate the derivatives market were declared illegal.
9. ⚠️ High interest rates raise borrowing costs for sovereign borrowers as well as firms - the channel that connects this lesson to the debt position of Lesson 11.
10. ⚠️ The internal tension: maintaining defence spending while restraining the state's economic role changes what the state does rather than shrinking it.
11. ⚠️ Paul Mason's judgement that neo-liberalism was mainly responsible for the 2008 catastrophe, with responsibility shared by bankers, regulators, politicians and the media, is attributed rather than asserted.
12. ⚠️ Master formulation: the turn began as an answer to a real breakdown and ended by dismantling the protections built after the previous one.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the deregulation of November 1999 is a sharper piece of evidence in an answer than a general reference to twenty-five years of neoliberalism.

**Model answer:** Because it is a dated legal act with a named content and a traceable consequence, whereas an era is a context. The act of November 1999 was designed to promote economic growth through competition and freedom, and what it actually did was cancel the regulation dating from the Depression of the 1930s which prevented investment banks from handling the savings and deposits of the general public, so that they now had access to far larger funds; it also allowed banks to act as insurance companies. A year later futures and all other derivatives were exempted from being classified as gambling and attempts to regulate the derivatives market were declared illegal. Each of those has a consequence the sources then record: by 2007 the total value of the world's stock market companies was $63 trillion while the total value of derivative investments stood at $596 trillion, eight times the size of the real economy. The historical point is sharper still, because the regulation cancelled was built in response to the very failure of 1929 in which banks speculated with the cash deposited with them and ordinary depositors who had had nothing to do with the speculation were ruined. By contrast, the claim that a quarter-century of neo-liberalism was mainly responsible is Paul Mason's judgement, which should be attributed to him; it identifies a climate rather than a mechanism. An examiner can verify the first kind of claim and can only agree or disagree with the second.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the broader judgement should therefore be omitted. It should be included and attributed, because the question of whether a policy climate produces particular legislation is a real historical question and the sources record a serious author making the argument, along with his assignment of responsibility to bankers, regulators, politicians and the media. The error is not citing the judgement; it is presenting it in the candidate's own voice as though it were a finding.

## Lesson 15 - Globalisation as uneven integration

Progress: 15/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: what deepened after 1990 - labour, capital, credit and production; the complaint that globalisation meant Americanization; the named instances of leverage through the Fund, the trade rules and communications; the patent settlement of 2003 and its ulterior motive; the BRIC expectation; and the one dated current linkage carried by this session

⚠️ The right-hand list is deliberately mixed. It contains coercion, hypocrisy and a genuine concession with a self-interested motive attached, which is exactly the texture the uneven-integration discipline requires.

### 1. The learner's problem: what is actually new?

⚠️ Lesson 1 established that a single world economy existed in 1900. So the question is not whether the world became connected but **what changed in the connection**, and the account answers with four specific deepenings rather than with a general claim.

### 2. The four deepenings

✅ **Fact.** Beginning in the last decade of the twentieth century, globalization played an important role, as national economies became interlinked as never before.

✅ **Labour.** In the twenty years after **1990** the world's labour force **doubled** and, with the increase in migration, became global. ✅ **China and the former Soviet bloc joined the world economy.**

⚠️ Pause there, because this is the single most important sentence for understanding the period and it is frequently missed. The doubling was not demographic; it was **political**. Populations that had always existed entered the same labour market as a result of decisions about economic systems. ⚠️ Note also that this is where the Cold War's end enters this topic - not as a political settlement, which belongs to its own owner, but as the largest single change in the supply of labour to the world economy in the century.

✅ **The wage consequence.** The greater availability of labour brought a fall in real wages in the leading western economies, including the USA, Japan and Germany.

⚠️ This answers the distributional question Lesson 13 left open. When the account asked whether the golden days of western prosperity could survive unless workers accepted lower wages or companies accepted lower profits, the recorded outcome was that real wages fell. ⚠️ That is why globalisation became politically contested inside rich countries and not only between rich and poor ones - a point that any answer on the politics of globalisation needs.

✅ **Capital.** At the same time capital flowed around as western financiers began to invest abroad more than ever before.

⚠️ Connect this to Lesson 7. Free capital movement was not a natural condition; it became the norm only after the fixed-rate element of the designed order broke down in the early 1970s. ⚠️ A candidate who makes that connection has explained why financial globalisation arrived two decades after trade globalisation.

✅ **Credit.** Yet consumption grew, made possible by a massive increase in credit and the heyday of the credit-card era. ✅ The credit boom seemed sustainable at first, but after **2000** the debts began to run out of control.

⚠️ And there is the mechanism of Lesson 3 again, at world scale: purchasing power below the level needed to absorb output, the gap bridged by borrowing, and borrowing concealing the limit rather than removing it. ⚠️ Saying that in an answer - that the credit boom of the 2000s performed the same function as the instalment credit of the 1920s - demonstrates exactly the long-range comparison this topic rewards.

### 3. "Globalization meant Americanization": the recorded instances

✅ **Fact.** Even during the Clinton administration the USA extended its economic principles into its global dealings; ✅ American interests usually came first, so much so that many people complained that globalization meant Americanization.

✅ During the **1990s** the USA gained control of the **International Monetary Fund**, which meant that the Americans could decide which countries should receive aid and could insist that governments adopted policies of which the USA approved. ✅ This happened to many Latin American countries as well as **Korea, Indonesia and Thailand**. ✅ Often the conditions imposed made recovery harder instead of easier. ✅ In **1995**, when the **World Bank** suggested that debt relief was vital for some poor countries, it met stiff opposition from the USA, and its chief economist felt compelled to resign. ✅ Basically these developments meant that the USA could control the world's financial system.

✅ In **1994** the USA used the **General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade** to force the EU to open all its voice communications - post, telephone and telegraphs - to international competition. ✅ In **1997** the **World Trade Organization**, which succeeded the General Agreement in **1995**, agreed that **70** countries should be opened up to US telecoms companies on American terms. ✅ By **2002** there were **180** commercial satellites orbiting in space, and **174** of them were American; ✅ the USA all but controlled the world's communications systems, and it was to counter this that the EU insisted on launching its own **Galileo** satellite system.

✅ In **March 2002** the Bush administration imposed import duties on foreign steel in order to protect the American steel industry. ✅ This brought bitter protests from the EU, since the function of the WTO was to encourage free trade. ✅ The USA resisted the pressure until **December 2003**; then, faced with threats of retaliatory duties on a wide range of American goods, President Bush cancelled the steel tariffs. ✅ In the same month, however, the US announced new tariffs on imports of textiles and television sets from China.

⚠️ That last pair of sentences is the sharpest single illustration in the lesson and it should be used in full. The tariffs were withdrawn under threat of retaliation rather than under argument, and replaced in the same month with tariffs against a partner less able to retaliate. ⚠️ The mechanism being demonstrated is not hypocrisy in the moral sense but **asymmetric enforceability**: rules bind those whose counterparties can impose costs.

### 4. The counter-instance, with its motive attached

✅ **Fact.** In **2003** there was one positive step which benefited poorer countries: responding to worldwide protests from states suffering the worst ravages of HIV/AIDS, President Bush agreed that the patents controlling the necessary drugs should be overridden, allowing far cheaper versions to be produced for sale in the worst-affected states. ✅ There was an ulterior motive, however: in return, the Americans were hoping to gain access to African oil and to set up military bases in strategic parts of the continent.

⚠️ Keep both halves. The concession was real and its consequences for the epidemic of Lesson 21 were real - ✅ antiretroviral drugs are separately recorded as much cheaper after pharmaceutical companies gave way to political pressure. ⚠️ And the motive is recorded alongside it. ⚠️ The analytical register to adopt is the one used for Marshall Aid in Lesson 2: state the action, state the interest it served, and decline to reduce it to either.

### 5. The assessment the account itself offers

✅ **Fact.** At the turn of the millennium "globalization" had been the buzzword. ✅ It seemed to promise huge benefits for the world - increased connectivity between countries, faster growth, greater transfer of knowledge and wealth, and perhaps even a fairer distribution of wealth. ✅ Economists talked about the **BRIC** countries, meaning **Brazil, Russia, India and China**, the world's fastest-growing and largest emerging market economies, containing between them almost half the world's population. ✅ Many economists predicted it was only a matter of time before China became the largest economy in the world, probably some time between **2030 and 2050**; ✅ **Goldman Sachs** believed that by **2043** all the BRIC countries would be in the world's top ten economies and that by **2050** they would be the top four, with China first and the USA relegated to fifth.

✅ But after the meltdown of 2008, many economists had believed it would be possible for the BRICs to "decouple" themselves from the West and continue growing; ✅ this turned out not to be the case, and many commentators began to doubt whether globalization had been a good thing after all. ✅ It seemed as if it had made the world economy less stable, more volatile, and more vulnerable to the danger of a crisis in one country infecting the rest of the world.

✅ There was also a long-standing verdict recorded at the beginning of the century: there was a long way to go before globalization produced a fair and just world in which wealth was more evenly distributed.

⚠️ Note that these predictions are recorded as predictions with their authors and their dates. ❌ This session does not report whether any of them came true, and an answer should not either; the forecast is evidence of what was expected at the time, which is a legitimate historical fact, and nothing more.

### 6. The single current linkage carried by this session

📰 **Current linkage, used once and limited here.** The International Monetary Fund's **World Economic Outlook Update of July 2026** projected global growth at **3.0 per cent for 2026** and **3.4 per cent for 2027**, and described an outcome that remains uneven across groups of economies.

⚠️ Three strict limits apply to this item and they are stated in the text rather than hidden in a ledger.

1. ⚠️ It is evidence of what the Fund projected on that date and of nothing else. A projection is not an outcome.
2. ⚠️ The vintage travels with the figure. A later update is a different document and the two are not interchangeable.
3. ⚠️ Its only legitimate use in a history answer is a single clause showing that the *pattern* this lesson describes - integration producing uneven results across economies - remained the organising feature of the world economy at the time of writing. ❌ It is not evidence about any past decade, and no other current economic or demographic claim appears anywhere in this session.

### Criticism and reply: is "uneven integration" a real verdict or a refusal to give one?

**The objection.** ⚠️ Saying that globalisation produced both gains and losses is safe to the point of emptiness. Every large process does. A verdict should tell us which predominated.

**The reply.** ⚠️ The formulation is not a refusal if it specifies **who** gained, **who** lost and **by what mechanism**, and this lesson does all three. ✅ Those with existing industrial capability gained, which is why the tiger economies converged; ✅ workers in the leading western economies lost real wages as the global labour force doubled; ✅ commodity exporters continued to face the terms of trade of Lesson 11; ✅ consumers everywhere gained cheaper manufactures; ✅ and those inside the credit boom gained consumption that later proved to be borrowed. ⚠️ A verdict that distributes gains and losses across named groups by a named mechanism is more informative than one that assigns a single sign to the whole process. ⚠️ The formulation to carry: **globalisation is not a policy to be approved or rejected but a distributional process to be described, and the description is the answer.**

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ World history from the eighteenth century onward, and the effects of economic systems on society. ⚠️ The theme is also owned in its contemporary form by the international-relations and Indian-society topics, which hold the present-day social and diplomatic questions; this session owns the historical formation of the process.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ "Assess globalisation", "why did globalisation not remove the North-South divide?", or as the penultimate section of an answer on the world economy since 1900. ⚠️ The structure that scores names the four deepenings, gives two named instances of asymmetry, concedes one genuine gain, and closes with a distributional verdict.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Dating globalisation's origin to 1990. ✅ A single world economy existed in 1900; what deepened after 1990 is specified.
2. ⚠️ Explaining the doubling of the labour force demographically. ✅ It followed China and the former Soviet bloc joining the world economy.
3. ⚠️ Omitting the wage consequence in rich countries. ✅ Real wages fell in the USA, Japan and Germany.
4. ⚠️ Treating the steel episode as one event. ✅ Tariffs imposed March 2002, cancelled December 2003 under threat of retaliation, and new tariffs on Chinese textiles and televisions announced in the same month.
5. ⚠️ Reporting the BRIC forecasts as outcomes. ✅ They are dated predictions by named forecasters and are recorded as such.
6. ⚠️ Importing current growth or population data. ❌ This session carries exactly one dated current item, limited in the text.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ From the last decade of the twentieth century national economies became interlinked as never before.
2. ✅ In the twenty years after 1990 the world's labour force doubled and, with increased migration, became global, as China and the former Soviet bloc joined the world economy.
3. ✅ The greater availability of labour brought a fall in real wages in the leading western economies, including the USA, Japan and Germany.
4. ✅ Capital flowed around as western financiers invested abroad more than ever before.
5. ✅ Consumption nevertheless grew, made possible by a massive increase in credit and the heyday of the credit-card era; after 2000 the debts began to run out of control.
6. ✅ Many complained that globalization meant Americanization; in the 1990s the USA gained control of the IMF and could decide who received aid and insist on approved policies, as happened to many Latin American countries and to Korea, Indonesia and Thailand, with conditions that often made recovery harder.
7. ✅ In 1995 the World Bank's suggestion that debt relief was vital met stiff opposition from the USA and its chief economist felt compelled to resign.
8. ✅ In 1994 the USA used GATT to force the EU to open its voice communications to competition; in 1997 the WTO agreed that 70 countries should be opened to US telecoms on American terms.
9. ✅ By 2002, 180 commercial satellites were in orbit and 174 were American; the EU launched Galileo to counter this.
10. ✅ Steel: duties imposed in March 2002, protested by the EU because the WTO's function was to encourage free trade, cancelled in December 2003 under threat of retaliation, and followed in the same month by new tariffs on Chinese textiles and television sets.
11. ✅ In 2003 drug patents were overridden to allow far cheaper HIV/AIDS medicines in the worst-affected states, with the ulterior motive of hoped-for access to African oil and military bases.
12. ✅ The BRIC expectation: Brazil, Russia, India and China, almost half the world's population; China predicted to become the largest economy between 2030 and 2050; Goldman Sachs expecting all four in the top ten by 2043 and the top four by 2050.
13. ✅ Decoupling failed after 2008, and commentators began to doubt whether globalization had been a good thing, since it seemed to have made the world economy less stable, more volatile and more vulnerable to contagion.
14. 📰 One dated current item is carried by this session: the IMF's World Economic Outlook Update of July 2026 projected global growth of 3.0 per cent for 2026 and 3.4 per cent for 2027, with the vintage attached.
15. ⚠️ Master formulation: globalisation is a distributional process to be described, not a policy to be approved or rejected.

### Concept check

**Question:** The world's labour force doubled in the twenty years after 1990. Explain why that fact is political rather than demographic, and trace two of its consequences.

**Model answer:** It is political because no population appeared; populations that already existed entered the same labour market. The account states the cause directly: in the twenty years after 1990 the world's labour force doubled and, with the increase in migration, became global, because China and the former Soviet bloc joined the world economy. Those societies had always contained workers; what changed was the decision to integrate their economies with the rest of the world, which is a political and systemic change with a demographic appearance. Two consequences follow in the record. The first is distributional inside rich countries: the greater availability of labour brought a fall in real wages in the leading western economies, including the USA, Japan and Germany - which answers the question the account had left open in the 1990s, when it asked whether western prosperity could survive unless workers accepted lower wages or companies accepted lower profits. The second is the mechanism that concealed the first: consumption nevertheless grew, made possible by a massive increase in credit and the heyday of the credit-card era, until after 2000 the debts began to run out of control. That pairing is the same structure as the 1920s, where wages rose about 8 per cent while profits rose 72 per cent and credit postponed the limit, and it is why the crisis of Lesson 16 has a recognisable shape.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the entry of these workers caused the fall in western real wages in a simple way, and therefore that the losers should blame the newcomers. What the record supports is that greater availability of labour brought falling real wages; what it also records is that the alternative adjustment - companies accepting lower profits - was available and was not taken. The distribution of the adjustment was a choice made within each economy, not a physical necessity imposed from outside, and an answer that notices this is making a far more defensible claim than one that treats the outcome as automatic.
