⚠️ The diagram's top line is there for a reason. A crisis that the chairman of the central bank declared impossible fourteen months before it happened is the strongest available evidence about what the system's own participants could see.

### 1. The learner's problem: why this belongs in a history paper

⚠️ Because it is the test case of the century's central proposition. Lessons 1 and 15 established an integrated world economy; the claim under examination is that integration distributes shocks as efficiently as it distributes growth. ⚠️ 2008 is where that claim was tested, and the answer is recorded in the account's own framing: globalisation seemed to have made the world economy less stable, more volatile, and more vulnerable to the danger of a crisis in one country infecting the rest of the world.

### 2. The confidence before the fall

✅ **Fact.** On **15 June 2007** **Ben S. Bernanke**, chairman of the American Federal Reserve Bank, made a long speech in which he extolled the virtues of the American financial system: in the United States, he said, a deep and liquid financial system had promoted growth by effectively allocating capital, and had increased economic resilience by increasing the ability to share and diversify risks both domestically and globally. ✅ There was, he said, no possibility of a financial crisis in America. ✅ Yet little over a year later the American system and the whole global economy seemed to be on the verge of total collapse. ✅ Some experts had been predicting collapse for some years, but had been proved wrong.

⚠️ Notice the second clause of that last sentence, because it is the honest one. Warnings existed and had repeatedly been wrong, which is exactly why they were discounted. ⚠️ An answer that presents the crisis as obviously foreseeable is writing with hindsight; an answer that notices that prediction had a poor record is describing the situation as its participants experienced it.

### 3. Cause one: deregulation and the derivatives market

✅ **Fact.** The November 1999 act and the derivatives exemption of the following year are the legal foundation, taught in Lesson 14. ✅ The deregulation, together with the spread of the latest computer technology, was certainly a bold incentive and encouragement to the bankers, who now had a free hand to indulge in all these types of speculation; ✅ it enabled the derivatives market to become global, and foreign-exchange dealing increased rapidly.

⚠️ Define the instruments, since the account does. ✅ A **future** is a contract in which you agree to buy something at a future date but at a price decided now, and the contract itself can be sold and resold several times before the agreed date; the risk is that the price falls but you still have to pay the agreed price. ✅ Another type of derivative develops when observers start betting among themselves on whether the original contract will be fulfilled. ✅ An **option** is similar to a future except that you agree the option to buy rather than actually buying the commodity.

✅ The scale: in the two years leading up to the crash there was a massive rush of money into derivatives and currency trading. ✅ In **2007** the total value of the world's stock market companies was **$63 trillion**, but the total value of derivative investments stood at **$596 trillion** - **eight times the size of the real economy**. ✅ It was as though there were two parallel economies: the real economy and a kind of phantom or fantasy economy which only existed on paper.

✅ Not all derivative dealings were speculative, but enough were risky to cause concern among perceptive financiers. ✅ As early as **2002** **Warren Buffett**, probably the world's most successful investor, warned that derivatives were a time bomb, financial weapons of mass destruction, because in the last resort neither banks nor governments knew how to control them. ✅ By **October 2008** even **Alan Greenspan**, a former chairman of the Federal Reserve who had always claimed that banks could be trusted to regulate themselves, was forced to admit that he had been wrong. ✅ By the time the crisis peaked, some **360** banks had received capital from the US government.

⚠️ The $63 trillion against $596 trillion comparison is the single most quotable figure in the lesson, and it should always be given as a **ratio with its year**: in 2007, derivative investments were eight times the value of the world's stock market companies.

### 4. Cause two: sub-prime lending and collateralised debt

✅ **Fact.** The long-running housing boom in the USA reached a peak towards the end of **2005**. ✅ House prices had reached levels that could not be sustained; too many houses had been built, demand gradually fell and so did prices. ✅ Many houses, especially during the latter stages of the boom, had been bought using **sub-prime mortgages** - mortgages lent to borrowers who have a high risk of being unable to keep up the payments, and who are for that reason charged a higher interest rate. ✅ When house prices began to fall, many lenders foolishly continued to push sub-prime mortgages and suffered heavy losses when the buyers defaulted. ✅ The more careful mortgage providers took out insurance to underwrite their loans, so insurance companies like **AIG, Freddie Mac and Fannie Mae** faced huge payouts.

✅ **Niall Ferguson**, in one of his 2012 Reith Lectures, suggested that Freddie and Fannie should take a large slice of the blame, because they encouraged people who really could not afford to do so to take out mortgages.

✅ Another practice was **collateral debt obligation** - the packaging together of different debts and bonds for sale as assets. ✅ A package might include sub-prime mortgages, credit-card debts and any kind of debt, and anybody buying the package would hope to receive reasonable interest payments. ✅ Since the year **2000**, buyers - including investment banks, pension funds and building societies - had been receiving interest payments on average between **2 and 3 per cent** higher than if the debts had not been bundled up. ✅ Then several things went wrong: house prices fell by around **25 per cent**, more people defaulted than had been expected, unemployment rose, and many people were unable to pay off their credit-card debts. ✅ One estimate put the likely losses to buyers at **$3.1 trillion**.

⚠️ Two points convert this from narrative into analysis.

First, ⚠️ **bundling converts visible risk into invisible risk**. A single sub-prime mortgage is transparently risky, and its lender prices that risk. A package of thousands of mixed debts is opaque, and the extra 2 to 3 per cent of interest was the payment for accepting an opacity the buyer could not evaluate. ⚠️ Pension funds and building societies - institutions whose entire purpose is caution - were among the buyers.

Second, ⚠️ **the mechanism is the mechanism of Lesson 3 in a new instrument**. Prices rose because people expected them to rise; lending expanded to meet the expectation; the expectation reversed; and the reversal produced the fall it had anticipated. ⚠️ Self-fulfilling expectation, named for 1929, describes 2005-8 exactly.

### 5. Cause three: leverage, short selling, short-termism and shadow banking

✅ **Leverage** is using borrowed money to increase your assets, which can then be sold at a profit when the value increases. ✅ Lehman was guilty of this, having a very high leverage level of **44** - meaning that every **$1 million** owned by the bank had been stretched by borrowing so that it could buy assets valued at **$44 million**. ✅ In a time of inflation like the period **2003-6** these assets could be sold at a comfortable profit, but it was a gamble, because only a small downward movement in the value of the assets would be enough to break the bank. ✅ As house prices collapsed and mortgage defaults soared, Lehman was left with debts of **$613 billion**. ✅ In Warren Buffett's words, "when the tide goes out it reveals those who are swimming naked".

⚠️ The arithmetic of leverage deserves one sentence of its own because it is exactly examinable. At a leverage of 44, a fall of a little over 2 per cent in asset values wipes out the entire capital. That is not a risky strategy; it is a strategy that requires asset prices never to fall.

✅ **Short selling** is a process in which the investor first borrows, for a fee, shares from a bank or other institution which is not planning to sell them, then sells them in the hope that the price will fall; if it does, he buys them back, returns them and keeps the difference. ✅ It is the company whose shares are being sold and bought that suffers, as illustrated by **Morgan Stanley**: as the crisis deepened investors began to move their money out, **10 per cent** of the cash on its books was withdrawn in three days, the share price began to fall, and this was the signal for short sellers to unload, sending the price plunging further.

✅ **Short-termism** is the common banking practice of lending money for long terms and borrowing it for short terms. ✅ When lending between banks dried up in **September 2008** following the rush of depositors to withdraw cash, many banks were unable to pay out, because they had lent too much on long-term loans which they could not get back immediately, and had failed to keep a large enough cushion to fall back on.

✅ **Shadow banking** was the response to that rule. ✅ In Paul Mason's account, the essence of the shadow banking system is that it is designed to get round the need for any capital cushion at all: almost everybody in it was borrowing short by buying a piece of paper on the vast international money market and lending long by selling a different piece of paper into the same market - traditional banking with no depositors, no shareholders and no capital cushion, pure intermediaries. ✅ It worked by exploiting a loophole to create two kinds of off-balance-sheet company known as **conduits** and **structured investment vehicles**, the conduits set up in offshore tax havens, with the bank theoretically liable for losses but not obliged to show them on its annual accounts. ✅ All this was kept secret from investors, which did not matter while all was running smoothly - ✅ but there was one huge flaw: it could only work as long as bankers continued to buy and sell everything on offer, and as soon as short-term credit was no longer available, bankers could not fund their long-term loans and some pieces of paper became unsaleable.

⚠️ The general principle to extract: a capital cushion is a rule about what a bank must hold in reserve; a system that exists to avoid holding reserves has, by construction, no capacity to absorb a shock. ⚠️ And note the phrase "kept secret from investors" - opacity is not an accidental feature of this episode but a designed one, here as in the collateralised debt packages.

### 6. Cause four: the watchmen

✅ **Fact.** Since **2000**, thanks to the actions of both the US and UK governments, regulation of the banking system had been exercised with what can only be described as a light touch. ✅ The politicians were apparently happy to continue this non-interventionist attitude since bankers had played an important part in achieving the consumer boom and full employment; ✅ they mistakenly believed that bankers could therefore be trusted not to do anything too risky.

✅ The credit-rating agencies were the second line of defence. ✅ The three main agencies are **Standard and Poor's, Moody's and Fitch**; ✅ their job is to carry out a risk-assessment process on banks, companies and assets and award grades showing investors whether it would be safe to do business with them, with **AAA** the safest and **BB or less** indicating high risk. ✅ Between **2001 and 2007** the amount of money paid to the three main agencies doubled, reaching a total of **$6 billion**. ✅ Yet an official report published in **July 2007** was highly critical of their work: they were accused of being unable to show convincing evidence that their methods of assessment were reliable, especially in the case of collateralised debt obligations, and of being unable to cope with the vast increase in new business since 2000.

✅ Many critics saw the whole system as suspect: the fact that the institutions issuing bonds actually paid for their own ratings invited collusion, since correct ratings risked upsetting the banking business and losing market share. ✅ As a result, no decisive action was taken until it was too late. ✅ It was only a matter of hours before the British **HBOS** collapsed in September 2008 that **Standard and Poor's** downgraded it - ✅ and even then the comforting phrase "but the outlook is stable" was added.

⚠️ Name the structural fault: **the issuer pays**. When the party being assessed selects and pays the assessor, and can take its business elsewhere, the incentive runs against severity. ⚠️ That single sentence explains the agencies' behaviour better than any accusation of incompetence, and it is a transferable analytical point about any system of paid certification.

### 7. The collapse and the rescues

✅ In **March 2008** it was revealed that **Bear Stearns**, one of the oldest and most respected Wall Street investment banks, was in serious trouble: it had lost **$1.6 billion** when some affiliated hedge funds collapsed, and had a problem with bad debts estimated at **$220 billion**. ✅ The treasury secretary **Henry Paulson** decided it could not be allowed to collapse, and since there was a rule that the US government should never bail out an investment bank, it was arranged that **J.P. Morgan** be provided with Federal Reserve funds to take it over. ✅ This indirect bailout saved the system from collapsing, but it also left the impression that any other bank in difficulty could rely on a government rescue - ✅ described in financial circles as **moral hazard**, the idea that some investors believe they are "too big to fail" and therefore take reckless risks.

✅ **Lehman Brothers**, the fourth largest bank on Wall Street, had been struggling for over a year with bad debts and a shortage of capital. ✅ In September its European branch was put into administration, and there was wide expectation of a Bear Stearns-type deal, but **Tim Geithner** of the Federal Reserve of New York state announced that there was "no political will" for a Federal rescue. ✅ Lehman was allowed to go bankrupt; it was the largest US company until then ever to go bust, and the collapse sent shock waves around the world as share prices plummeted. ✅ The refusal was deliberate: state takeovers should not become a habit, because that was seen as state capitalism, and in a country that almost worshipped free-market capitalism the idea that private companies and banks should be subsidised or taken over by the government was sacrilege. ✅ One leading financier remarked: "I just think it is disgusting; this is not American."

✅ The crisis then worsened rapidly and the government found it impossible to maintain its free-market stance. ✅ **Merrill Lynch** was taken over by the **Bank of America**. ✅ Then the giant insurance company **AIG** asked the government for a loan of **$40 billion** to stave off bankruptcy; because it was so big and had done so much business with most major financial institutions worldwide, it was decided that it should be bailed out with a government loan of **$85 billion**, although the state took an **80 per cent** stake - ✅ in effect the US government had nationalised AIG, though the word itself was never used.

✅ In the United Kingdom, the mortgage bank **Northern Rock** collapsed in **September 2007** and was eventually nationalised at a cost of some **£100 billion**. ✅ In **September 2008** **Halifax Bank of Scotland** was saved from collapse when taken over by **Lloyds TSB** for **£12 billion**, but its share price fell so rapidly that a few weeks later its value had slumped to **£4 billion**, bringing Lloyds to its knees so that it too had to be rescued. ✅ The **Royal Bank of Scotland** was partly nationalised, becoming **83 per cent** taxpayer-owned. ✅ **Fortis**, the huge Dutch-Belgian bank, lost almost half its value in just a few days and was taken into joint ownership by the two governments; ✅ in Germany, France, the Irish Republic and Iceland similar bailouts were taking place - ✅ and most of this happened in just a few days in September 2008.

✅ The US Treasury eventually acquired stakes in **Goldman Sachs, Morgan Stanley, J.P. Morgan Chase** and the two mortgage underwriters **Freddie Mac and Fannie Mae**, whose function was not to provide mortgages directly but to underwrite mortgages given by other banks. ✅ Much of the help was provided under the Bush administration's **Troubled Asset Relief Program** and later by the Obama administration's **Public-Private Investment Program**. ✅ The bailout was extremely controversial: President Bush was accused of being un-American and of introducing socialism, and to get the programme approved by Congress it was necessary to attach conditions - limits on executive pay, a cap on dividends, and the right of the government to take stakes in the ailing banks.

> ⚠️ **Magnitude not asserted.** The account states a figure for the total taxpayer debt attributed to the rescue programme in an official report of July 2009 which cannot be reconciled with its own adjacent magnitudes, including the $787 billion stimulus and the $63 trillion valuation of world stock markets. ❌ That figure is not reproduced anywhere in this session, and the discrepancy is recorded in the final ledger. ⚠️ Teach the event - stakes acquired, conditions attached, controversy over socialism - not the disputed total.

⚠️ And note the accusation itself, because it is the exact echo of Lesson 6. A Republican administration rescuing banks was accused of introducing socialism, just as the New Deal had been attacked as socialism for saving capitalism. ⚠️ Seventy-five years apart, the same charge attends the same act.

### 8. The aftermath, and the two instruments

✅ Although the capitalist financial system had been saved from total collapse, the consequences were going to be felt for a long time. ✅ As the money supply dried up, demand for goods fell, manufacturing slumped across the world, many of the weakest companies went to the wall and unemployment rocketed. ✅ In the USA, in the first few months of **2009**, it was calculated that around half a million jobs a month were being lost. ✅ The great exporting nations - **China, Japan, South Korea and Germany** - suffered huge falls in exports. ✅ Although central bank interest rates were almost zero in the USA and Britain, nobody was investing to try to stimulate the still-declining economy.

✅ **Fiscal stimulus.** ✅ The Chinese government decided to supply cash worth **$580 billion** over two years to fund various environmental projects, with banks encouraged to lend vast state-guaranteed sums; millions of new jobs were created and within a few months China's growth rate had recovered and surpassed its previous high point, the main problem being uncertainty about how risky those massive bank loans were. ✅ In the USA, President **Obama**'s fiscal stimulus of **$787 billion** went into operation in **February 2009**; it was controversial because the Republican party was totally against it, believing even in a crisis this serious that the state should not provide help, and a right-wing Republican group calling themselves the **Tea Party Movement** launched an anti-stimulus campaign encouraging Republican state governors not to accept stimulus money. ✅ The US economy began to grow again towards the end of 2009 and continued slowly through 2010.

✅ In the EU the effects varied among its **27** member states; ✅ the average growth reduction at the end of **2009** was **4.7 per cent**; ✅ the three Baltic states fared worst, with Estonia's GDP falling **14 per cent**, Lithuania's **15 per cent** and Latvia's **18 per cent**, while **France** did best, losing only **3 per cent**. ✅ Most states borrowed heavily to launch stimulus packages: in 2009 France's borrowing was equivalent to **8 per cent** of GDP and Britain's **11 per cent**, and although these were small compared with America's and China's, they left massive national debts and broke the **Maastricht** rules that borrowing must not exceed **3 per cent** of GDP and total debt be limited to **60 per cent**.

✅ **Quantitative easing.** ✅ This was the practice, first thought of by **John Maynard Keynes** back in the 1930s, of increasing the amounts of cash in circulation by "printing money"; ✅ nowadays central banks simply create more money which is added to their reserves and used to buy up government debts. ✅ The **UK** was the first to use it in **March 2009**, when a modest **£150 billion** was created, which to some extent helped to put demand back into the system; ✅ the **USA** adopted it soon after. ✅ The **European Central Bank** rejected it on the grounds that it would threaten the stability of the euro, arguing that making more of the existing money available to eurozone banks and buying AAA-rated bonds would be enough - ✅ but demand was not sufficiently stimulated and consequently the value of the euro was weakened.

⚠️ Note the attribution in the quantitative-easing sentence and note how narrow it is. ✅ The account attributes the *idea* to Keynes in the 1930s. ❌ It does not thereby attribute the New Deal's fiscal policy to him, and neither does this session; Lesson 5's control stands.

### 9. The eurozone, and the institutional diagnosis

✅ The financial crisis in **Greece** sparked things off. ✅ In **October 2009** the newly elected government discovered that the country's budget deficit - which stood at **6 per cent** according to the previous government - was in reality **12.7 per cent**. ✅ Over half its actual debt, with a little assistance from **Goldman Sachs**, had been moved into the shadow banking system, off balance sheet. ✅ It later emerged that there were serious flaws in the Greek system that had allowed massive tax evasion and other corrupt practices, such as pensions still being paid to families of the deceased. ✅ The immediate problem was that Greece had financed its national debt with short-term loans, a quarter of which were due for repayment in **2010**.

✅ The first step was strict austerity - cuts in pensions, wages and social services and a campaign to eliminate tax evasion. ✅ Eventually in **May 2010** the eurozone banks and the IMF agreed a loan of **110 billion** to Greece, provided the austerity programme was fulfilled; ✅ this was extremely unpopular and resulted in strikes and two general elections over the next two years. ✅ By the autumn of **2011** there seemed a real danger of default, and leaders agreed to write off half of Greece's debts to private creditors. ✅ In **November 2010** the Republic of **Ireland** had to be helped with a bailout of **85 billion**. ✅ The account prints this as November 2011, a year late, and the corrected date is verified and registered in the final ledger; ⚠️ the slip is of a familiar kind, since the account groups the Irish programme with the autumn-2011 Greek write-off that followed it. ✅ **Portugal**, suffering crippling competition from Germany and China, was on the verge of bankruptcy, was downgraded by **Moody's** to junk status in **July 2011**, received an IMF bailout in October, had the lowest GDP per capita in western Europe and unemployment of around **15 per cent** in March 2012. ✅ By August 2011 **Spain and Italy** had drifted into the danger zone and the European Central Bank was forced to break its own rules and start buying their debt.

✅ The institutional diagnosis is stated in five words: **"Monetary union without fiscal union had failed."**

⚠️ That sentence is the most transferable in the lesson and it generalises the whole session's theme. A shared currency removes the exchange-rate adjustment of Lesson 4 while providing no common fiscal capacity to replace it - so the adjustment falls entirely on wages, pensions and public services, which is internal devaluation under another name. ⚠️ Mussolini's lira decision of the 1930s and Greek austerity after 2010 are the same mechanism in different institutional clothing.

### 10. Transmission to the Global South: the Indian case

✅ **Fact.** India's economy had been expanding rapidly, and words like "dynamic" and "rampaging Asian tiger" had been used to describe it. ✅ However, as the financial crisis hit the USA and Europe, demand for Indian goods plummeted and was still falling in 2012; ✅ Indian exports fell by a further **3 per cent** in the year from **May 2011 to May 2012**. ✅ As the economy slowed, investors began to desert India, preferring something safer like the US dollar, which sent the value of the **rupee** plunging until in **June 2012** it reached a record low against the dollar. ✅ In theory this should help Indian exports, which would be cheaper, but it made India's imports more expensive, pushing up the cost of living and making even essentials difficult to afford. ✅ In addition, much of India's infrastructure was in a dilapidated state and businesses complained of being hampered by corruption, bribery and unnecessary bureaucracy. ✅ The current account deficit stood at **$49 billion** in **June 2011** and was estimated at **$72 billion** at the end of **2012**, which would be over **4 per cent** of GDP, against **Morgan Stanley**'s view that a sustainable deficit ought to be no more than **2 per cent**. ✅ **Standard and Poor's** and **Fitch** both reduced their ratings of the Indian economy to "negative", though **Moody's** continued to rate it as "stable". ✅ Clearly India had failed to decouple itself from the problems of the eurozone; ✅ and in **June 2012**, desperate for the crisis to be resolved, India joined **China** in making a substantial contribution to the IMF's emergency fund.

⚠️ This is the session's most directly usable Indian illustration and it should be deployed carefully. Three things make it analytically valuable.

First, ⚠️ it shows transmission working through **three separate channels at once** - export demand, portfolio capital flight and the currency - none of which required any Indian institution to have made a mistake.

Second, ⚠️ the currency movement demonstrates the two-sidedness of devaluation exactly as Lesson 4 described it: cheaper exports and dearer imports, with the second effect landing on households through the cost of essentials.

Third, ⚠️ the final fact reverses the century's usual direction of assistance. ✅ In June 2012 India and China contributed to a fund intended to address a European crisis. ⚠️ That single sentence is the best available evidence in this session that the North-South relationship of Lesson 10 had become something more complicated than the Brandt line described - and it should be used with the restraint the evidence permits, as one dated episode rather than as a claim about a reversal of roles.

✅ The account records the same failure of decoupling elsewhere: ✅ the financial crisis of 2008 caused an immediate drop in China's exports; ✅ Brazil initially responded well with a massive property-building project that created thousands of jobs and cut unemployment to its lowest level for many years, becoming the sixth largest economy in the world by 2012 with the incomes of the poorest 50 per cent of the population rising by almost 70 per cent over the preceding few years, but faced a doubling of Rio house prices since 2008 and a **30 per cent** fall in oil prices; ✅ and Russia, whose GDP had increased tenfold in ten years of growth to 2008 thanks mainly to high oil prices, with oil and gas worth around **$200 billion** by 2008 or about one-third of total revenue, saw the price per barrel plunge from **$140 to $40** in 2008, forcing the government to provide **$200 billion** of liquidity while the central bank spent a third of its **$600 billion** reserve fund slowing the rouble's devaluation.

⚠️ The Russian case is Lesson 12's symmetry lesson proved once more: the fact that the economy was dependent on the price of oil meant that there could be no decoupling from the rest of the world's economic problems.

### Criticism and reply: did 2008 disprove globalisation?

**The objection.** ⚠️ If integration transmitted the crisis to India, China, Brazil, Russia and every European economy, and if commentators began to doubt whether globalisation had been a good thing, the process stands condemned.

**The reply.** ⚠️ The record supports a narrower and more accurate claim. ✅ What 2008 exposed was vulnerability, not the end of integration: the system was saved, trade and capital flows continued, and the same integrated channels that carried the losses had carried the previous decade's growth. ✅ The account's own formulation is that globalisation seemed to have made the world economy less stable, more volatile and more vulnerable to contagion - a statement about the distribution of risk, not a verdict that integration had ceased. ⚠️ Note also the counter-evidence inside the crisis itself: the Chinese stimulus restored growth within months, and by June 2012 India and China were contributing to a rescue fund for a European problem. ⚠️ The formulation to carry: **2008 demonstrated that integration transmits losses as efficiently as gains; it did not demonstrate that the world could or would disconnect.**

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ World history from the eighteenth century onward. ⚠️ The recorded chapter demand is to explain why there was a crisis of capitalism in the decade leading up to 2012, and this lesson answers it directly.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ "Explain the causes of the global financial crisis", or as the closing section of an answer on the world economy since 1900, or as evidence in an answer on globalisation's costs. ⚠️ The structure that scores is the four causes with one named figure each, the collapse in three named episodes, the responses in two instruments, and the transmission through one named economy - India being the most useful for an Indian examination.

> **Boundary.** The contemporary politics, diplomacy and institutional reform of the post-crisis world belong to the topics that own the new world order and international relations. ❌ This session carries no claim about any economy after the dated material above, except the single current linkage in Lesson 15.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating Lehman's collapse as the cause. ⚠️ It is the symptom-not-cause discipline again; the causes are the four recorded families.
2. ⚠️ Quoting the derivatives figure without its year or its comparator. ✅ In 2007, $596 trillion against $63 trillion, eight times the real economy.
3. ⚠️ Describing the rating agencies as merely incompetent. ✅ The structural fault is that issuers paid for their own ratings.
4. ⚠️ Saying globalisation ended in 2008. ✅ The record says vulnerability was exposed, not that integration ceased.
5. ⚠️ Reproducing the disputed rescue-programme total. ⚠️ It cannot be reconciled with the account's own adjacent magnitudes and is not asserted here.
6. ⚠️ Omitting the Indian channels. ✅ Exports, investor flight and the rupee all moved, and India and China contributed to the IMF's emergency fund in June 2012.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ On 15 June 2007 the chairman of the American Federal Reserve extolled a deep and liquid financial system and said there was no possibility of a financial crisis in America; little over a year later the global economy seemed on the verge of total collapse.
2. ✅ Deregulation: the November 1999 act and the following year's derivatives exemption gave bankers a free hand; the derivatives market became global.
3. ✅ In 2007 the world's stock market companies were worth $63 trillion and derivative investments $596 trillion - eight times the real economy; a parallel phantom economy existing only on paper.
4. ✅ Warren Buffett warned in 2002 that derivatives were a time bomb and financial weapons of mass destruction; in October 2008 Alan Greenspan admitted he had been wrong that banks could regulate themselves; some 360 banks received US government capital.
5. ✅ The housing boom peaked towards the end of 2005; sub-prime mortgages were lent to high-risk borrowers at higher interest; lenders continued pushing them as prices fell; AIG, Freddie Mac and Fannie Mae faced huge payouts.
6. ✅ Collateral debt obligations packaged mixed debts for sale; since 2000 buyers received 2-3 per cent more interest; house prices then fell around 25 per cent and one estimate put likely losses at $3.1 trillion.
7. ✅ Lehman's leverage was 44 - every $1 million owned bought $44 million of assets - and it was left with debts of $613 billion.
8. ✅ Short selling hurt the company whose shares were traded: Morgan Stanley lost 10 per cent of its cash in three days and short sellers drove the price down further.
9. ✅ Short-termism meant lending long and borrowing short; interbank lending dried up in September 2008 and banks lacked a large enough cushion.
10. ✅ Shadow banking used conduits and structured investment vehicles in offshore tax havens to avoid any capital cushion, kept secret from investors, and failed as soon as short-term credit disappeared.
11. ✅ Regulation was light-touch from 2000; the three rating agencies were paid by the issuers they rated, payments doubling to $6 billion between 2001 and 2007; a July 2007 official report found no convincing evidence their methods were reliable; S&P downgraded HBOS hours before collapse while adding that the outlook was stable.
12. ✅ Bear Stearns was rescued indirectly through J.P. Morgan in March 2008, creating moral hazard; Lehman was allowed to fail for want of political will; AIG asked $40 billion and received $85 billion with the state taking an 80 per cent stake, effectively nationalised without the word being used.
13. ✅ UK and European rescues: Northern Rock nationalised at some £100 billion; HBOS taken by Lloyds for £12 billion and worth £4 billion weeks later; RBS 83 per cent taxpayer-owned; Fortis into joint Dutch-Belgian ownership; bailouts in Germany, France, Ireland and Iceland, mostly in a few days of September 2008.
14. ✅ Responses: China's $580 billion over two years; Obama's $787 billion stimulus of February 2009 opposed by Republicans and the Tea Party Movement; EU average growth reduction 4.7 per cent at end-2009 with Estonia -14, Lithuania -15, Latvia -18 and France -3 per cent; quantitative easing first in the UK in March 2009 at £150 billion, then the USA, rejected by the ECB.
15. ✅ Eurozone: Greek deficit revealed in October 2009 as 12.7 per cent not 6 per cent, with over half the debt moved off balance sheet with Goldman Sachs' help; May 2010 loan of 110 billion with austerity; half of Greek private debt written off in autumn 2011; Ireland 85 billion in November 2010 (the account prints November 2011; corrected against a verified source registered in the final ledger); Portugal junk-rated in July 2011. "Monetary union without fiscal union had failed."
16. ✅ India: exports fell a further 3 per cent from May 2011 to May 2012; investors deserted for the dollar and the rupee hit a record low in June 2012; the current account deficit was $49 billion in June 2011 and an estimated $72 billion at end-2012, over 4 per cent of GDP against a sustainable 2 per cent; S&P and Fitch cut to negative while Moody's held at stable; India joined China in contributing to the IMF's emergency fund in June 2012.
17. ⚠️ Master formulation: integration transmits losses as efficiently as gains, and 2008 exposed vulnerability rather than ending integration.

### Concept check

**Question:** Identify the common structural feature linking collateralised debt obligations, shadow banking and the credit-rating agencies, and explain why naming it is better than listing the three separately.

**Model answer:** The common feature is that each was designed or incentivised to make risk invisible to the party bearing it. Collateral debt obligations packaged sub-prime mortgages, credit-card debts and any kind of debt into a single asset, and the 2 to 3 per cent of extra interest paid since 2000 was in effect the price of an opacity the buyer - including pension funds and building societies - could not evaluate. Shadow banking went further: its essence, in Paul Mason's account, was to get round the need for any capital cushion at all, using conduits set up in offshore tax havens and structured investment vehicles whose losses the bank was theoretically liable for but did not have to show on its annual accounts, and all of it was kept secret from investors. The rating agencies were the safeguard against exactly this, but they were paid by the institutions whose bonds they rated, payments doubling to $6 billion between 2001 and 2007, so severity threatened their own business - which is why Standard and Poor's downgraded HBOS only hours before its collapse and still added that the outlook was stable. Naming the common feature is better than listing the three because it converts a catalogue into an explanation: the system did not simply take large risks, it systematically removed the information by which risk is priced, which is why the correction, when it came, arrived as a shock rather than as an adjustment.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating opacity as fraud and stopping there. Almost all of it was legal, and much of it was made legal deliberately: the act of November 1999 cancelled the Depression-era separation of investment banking from public deposits, and the following year's measure exempted derivatives from classification as gambling while declaring attempts at regulation illegal. An answer that reaches for individual wrongdoing misses the examinable point, which is that a legal and institutional framework had been constructed in which invisible risk was profitable and no one was required to look.

### Visual: the growth model, the bill and when it was first read

   ======================================

   increasingly aware that all was not well with their environment"
   -- the SAME DECADE as the oil shock (L12), the North-South debate
      (L10) and the end of the monetary order (L7). These belong in ONE
      paragraph, not four.
