⚠️ The diagram's second box is the one that matters for a history answer. Dating environmental awareness to the 1970s places it in the same decade as the oil decision, the North-South debate and the collapse of the monetary order - and that coincidence is not accidental, because all four are consequences of the same growth model reaching different limits at the same time.

### 1. The learner's problem: why is this in a history paper at all?

⚠️ Because the account treats it as economic history and places it in the same chapter as the world economy, and because the historical claim it makes is precise: environmental damage was not an accident of industrial growth but a **structural feature of the growth model**, since the motive was the creation of wealth and profit and very little attention was paid to side effects.

⚠️ That is a claim about how production was organised, which is a historian's question, not a scientist's. ⚠️ It also converts the world-economy topic from "growth and inequality" into "growth, inequality **and** the physical limits of the growth model" - which is exactly the difference between a competent fifteen-mark answer and a twenty-mark one.

### 2. The model, and the honesty of its scope

✅ **Fact.** As the twentieth century wore on, and the **North** became more and more obsessed with industrialisation, new methods and techniques were invented to help increase production and efficiency. ✅ The main motive was the creation of wealth and profit, and very little attention was paid to the side effects all this was having.

⚠️ Two boundaries on this claim must be respected. First, it is a claim about **the industrialised North's model**, and the account says so; ❌ it is not a blanket verdict on all industrialisation everywhere. Second, ✅ the account itself records that the USSR and the communist states of eastern Europe were guilty of carrying out "the dirtiest industrialization", the whole region being badly polluted by years of poisonous emissions - ⚠️ which establishes that the problem followed from industrial growth as such rather than from private ownership, and is therefore a point about the growth model rather than about capitalism specifically.

⚠️ That second observation is worth a sentence in any answer, because it prevents the lazy conclusion that environmental damage is a capitalist phenomenon, and it does so using the account's own evidence.

### 3. When awareness arrived, and why the date is the point

✅ **Fact.** During the **1970s** people became increasingly aware that all was not well with their environment, and that industrialisation was causing several major problems: ✅ exhaustion of the world's resources of raw materials and fuel; ✅ massive pollution of the environment, which scientists realised was likely to severely damage the ecosystem; and ✅ global warming.

⚠️ Note precisely what is being dated: **awareness**, not damage and not policy. ⚠️ The damage was accumulating throughout the century; what changed in the 1970s is that it became a political subject. ❌ Do not claim policy outcomes from this dating; awareness and action are different things, and the interval between them is itself a historical finding.

### 4. Resource exhaustion, and its connection to Lesson 12

✅ **Fossil fuels** - coal, oil and natural gas - are the remains of plants and living creatures which died hundreds of millions of years ago; ✅ they cannot be replaced and are rapidly being used up. ✅ There is probably plenty of coal left, but nobody is quite sure how much remains of the natural gas and oil. ✅ Oil production increased enormously during the twentieth century, and some experts believed all the oil reserves would be used up early in the twenty-first century. ✅ **This was one of the reasons why OPEC tried to conserve oil during the 1970s.** ✅ The British responded by successfully drilling for oil in the **North Sea**, which made them less dependent on oil imports, and another response was to develop alternative sources of power, especially **nuclear power**.

⚠️ That link between scarcity and the 1973 decision is the most valuable single connection in this lesson, and it should be made explicitly in any answer on the oil shocks. ⚠️ It means the producers' action was not only a revenue or a political decision but also a response to a physical fact about a finite resource - which is precisely what Lesson 12 recorded from the other side.

✅ **Minerals.** Tin, lead, copper, zinc and mercury were being seriously depleted; experts suggested these might all be used up early in the twenty-first century, ✅ and again it was the Third World which was being stripped of the resources it needed to help it escape from poverty.

⚠️ Stop on that clause, because it joins this lesson to Lesson 11 in one sentence. The resources being exhausted were, disproportionately, the export base of the poorest states - so resource depletion is simultaneously an environmental fact and a development fact.

✅ **Timber.** About half of the world's tropical rainforests had been lost by **1987**, and it was calculated that about **80,000 square kilometres** - an area roughly the size of Austria - was being lost every year, with the loss of many animal and insect species that had lived in the forests.

✅ **Fish and whales.** Too many fish were being caught and too many whales killed.

✅ **Phosphates**, used for fertilizers, were being rapidly used up: the more fertilizers farmers used to increase yields in an attempt to keep pace with the rising population, the more phosphate rock was quarried - an increase of **4 per cent a year since 1950** - and supplies were expected to be exhausted by the middle of the twenty-first century.

✅ **Fresh water.** Most of the planet's fresh water is tied up in the polar ice caps and glaciers or deep in the ground, and all living organisms rely on rain. ✅ With the world's population growing by **90 million a year**, scientists at **Stanford University** found that in **1995** humans and their farm animals, crops and forestry plantations were already using up **a quarter** of all the water taken up by plants, leaving less moisture to evaporate and therefore a likelihood of less rainfall.

✅ **Land and soil.** The amount of land available for agriculture was dwindling, partly because of spreading industrialisation and the growth of cities but also because of wasteful use of farmland: ✅ badly designed irrigation schemes increased salt levels in the soil, and irrigation sometimes took so much water from lakes and rivers that whole areas were turned into deserts. ✅ Soil erosion was another problem: scientists calculated that every year about **75 billion tons** of soil were washed away by rain and floods or blown away by winds, ✅ with losses depending on farming practice - on average **17 tons** of topsoil per hectare per year in western Europe and the USA where methods were good, **40 tons** in Africa, Asia and South America, **220 tons** on steep slopes in one recorded case and **400 tons** in parts of Jamaica.

✅ An encouraging sign was the setting-up of the **World Conservation Strategy** in **1980**, which aimed to alert the world to all these problems.

⚠️ Note the soil figures, because they carry an argument most scripts miss: the difference between 17 tons and 40 tons per hectare is a difference in **practice and capability**, not in geography alone. ⚠️ Environmental degradation, like everything else in this topic, is distributed according to what a society can afford to do differently.

### 5. Pollution, and the vocabulary the account supplies

✅ Discharges from heavy industry polluted the atmosphere, rivers, lakes and the sea. ✅ In **1975** all five **Great Lakes** of North America were described as "dead", meaning they were so heavily polluted that no fish could live in them, ✅ and about **10 per cent** of the lakes in Sweden were in the same condition. ✅ **Acid rain** - rain polluted with sulphuric acid - caused extensive damage to trees in central Europe, especially in **Germany and Czechoslovakia**. ✅ The USSR and the communist states of eastern Europe were guilty of carrying out the dirtiest industrialisation, the whole region badly polluted by years of poisonous emissions. ✅ Sewage from the world's great cities was a problem, some countries dumping it untreated or only partially treated into the sea, with the sea around New York badly polluted and the Mediterranean heavily polluted mainly by human sewage. ✅ Farmers in richer countries contributed through artificial fertilizers and pesticides draining into streams and rivers.

✅ **Chlorofluorocarbons**, used in aerosol sprays, refrigerators and fire extinguishers, were found to be harmful to the ozone layer which protects the Earth from the sun's harmful ultraviolet radiation. ✅ In **1979** scientists discovered a large hole in the ozone layer over the **Antarctic**; ✅ by **1989** the hole was much larger and another had been discovered over the **Arctic**, meaning people were more likely to develop skin cancers. ✅ Some progress was made and many countries banned the use of CFCs; ✅ in **2001** the **World Meteorological Organization** reported that the ozone layer seemed to be mending.

✅ **Nuclear power** causes pollution when radioactivity leaks into the environment, and it is now known that this can cause cancer, particularly leukaemia. ✅ Of all the people who worked at the **Sellafield** nuclear plant in Cumbria between **1947 and 1975**, a quarter of those who have since died, died of cancer. ✅ There was a constant risk of major accidents like the explosion at **Three Mile Island** in the USA in **1979**, which contaminated a vast area. ✅ The worst ever nuclear accident happened in **1986** at **Chernobyl** in Ukraine, then part of the USSR, when a reactor exploded, ⚠️ which the account records as killing **35** people, and releasing a huge radioactive cloud which drifted across most of Europe; ⚠️ that figure is the account's own and is best understood as the immediate recorded death toll rather than a universal or final count, since the same passage goes on to record continuing health effects, and it should be attributed when used rather than asserted flatly; ✅ ten years later hundreds of cases of thyroid cancer were appearing in areas near Chernobyl, and even in Britain hundreds of square miles of sheep pasture in Wales, Cumbria and Scotland were still contaminated, affecting **300,000** sheep which had to be checked before they could be eaten. ✅ In **2011** a huge tsunami hit the north-east coast of Japan, flooding a nuclear power station at **Fukushima**: the six reactors were battered by high waves and the basement housing the emergency generators was submerged, disabling the entire plant, with widespread radioactive contamination and a great outburst of anti-government feeling when it emerged that the authorities had ignored and then lied about reports of design weaknesses in the reactors.

✅ The account also records why remedy was slow: ✅ it would cost vast sums to put these problems right; ✅ industrialists argued that cleaning up their factories would make their products more expensive; ✅ governments and local authorities would have to spend extra cash on sewage works and on cleaning rivers and beaches; ✅ and in **1996** there were still **27** power-station reactors operating in eastern Europe of similar elderly design to the one which exploded at Chernobyl, threatening further disasters, while governments claimed they could afford neither safety improvements nor closure.

⚠️ The vocabulary is worth taking exactly as given, because it lets a candidate discuss the subject precisely without importing an environment syllabus: ✅ the **ecosystem** is the system by which living creatures, trees and plants function within the environment, all interconnected, and ✅ **ecology** is the study of the ecosystem.

⚠️ And note the final passage's logic, because it is the most examinable idea in this section: the obstacle to remedy was **cost incidence**. Cleaning up raises the producer's costs and the government's expenditure, while the benefit is diffuse and shared. ⚠️ That is the same collective-action structure as the tariff spiral of Lesson 4 - individually rational decisions producing a collectively irrational outcome.

### 6. Global warming: the definition, the cause, and the boundary

✅ **Fact.** In the early **1970s** scientists became concerned about what they called the **greenhouse effect** - the apparently uncontrollable warming of the earth's atmosphere, or **global warming** as it became known. ✅ It was caused by large amounts of **carbon dioxide, methane and nitrous oxide**, three gases produced during various industrial processes and by the burning of fossil fuels, being released into the atmosphere, where they acted like the glass roof of a greenhouse, trapping and magnifying the sun's heat. ✅ The account defines it as the uncontrollable warming of the Earth's atmosphere caused by the large quantities of gases emitted from industry.

> ⚠️ **Boundary, stated openly and held deliberately.** The account continues into the international politics of climate change - conferences, protocols, targets, ratification and their outcomes. ❌ None of that appears in this session. Climate policy, treaties, conference outcomes, emission targets and current climate status are owned by the environment-and-ecology topic, dated and sourced separately there, and this session hands them across. ⚠️ What a world-history answer owns is the historical claim: that the warming is attributed to gases emitted by industrial processes and fossil-fuel burning, that it is therefore a consequence of the growth model described at the start of this lesson, and that awareness of it dates from the same decade as the other limits.

⚠️ This boundary is not a limitation on the answer; it is what makes the answer a history answer. ⚠️ A candidate who writes two sentences of properly dated economic history about industrial emissions will outscore one who writes six sentences of climate policy in the wrong paper.

### 7. Technology as a trade question: the genetically modified crop dispute

✅ **Fact.** One of the economic issues that came to the forefront during the **1990s**, and which developed into a political confrontation between the **USA and the EU**, was the growing of **genetically modified crops** - plants injected with genes from other plants to give them extra characteristics such as herbicide tolerance, pest resistance or salt tolerance. ✅ The main GM crops grown are wheat, barley, maize, oilseed rape, soya beans and cotton. ✅ Advocates claimed they provide healthier food produced more efficiently, and, given the growing world population and the difficulty of feeding everybody, saw them as perhaps a vital breakthrough in solving the world food problem. ✅ By **2004** they were being grown by at least **6 million** farmers in **16** countries, including the USA, Canada, **India**, Argentina, Mexico, China, Colombia and South Africa, with the Americans the main supporters and the world's largest exporter.

✅ Objections were that the technology creates unnatural organisms; that genes might escape into wild plants and create "superweeds"; that GM crops might harm other species and in the long term the humans who eat them; and that escaping genes might pollinate organic crops, ruining organic farmers who could then find themselves being sued for having GM genes in their crops even though they had not knowingly planted such seeds. ✅ The main objections came from Europe; ✅ scientists on the whole reserved judgement, claiming there should be long field trials. ✅ Another European objection was that the industry was controlled by a few giant agriculture businesses, most of them American - ✅ by 2004 the American company **Monsanto** was producing more than **90 per cent** of GM crops worldwide - ✅ and that such companies had too much control over world food production, enabling them to pressure countries into buying their products and force traditional farmers out of the market.

✅ The controversy came to a head in **April 2004** when the USA called on the **World Trade Organization** to take action, accusing the EU of breaking free-trade rules by banning GM imports without scientific evidence.

⚠️ Include this in an answer for one purpose and keep it to two sentences. ⚠️ It shows that by the 1990s **technology itself had become a trade dispute**, adjudicated in the institution created in 1995, with a developing-country dimension attached - India being among the sixteen adopting countries - and with market concentration at more than 90 per cent in a single firm as part of the complaint. ⚠️ That is the same asymmetry pattern as the steel and telecoms cases of Lesson 15, transferred to agriculture.

### Criticism and reply: is the environment a digression in an economy topic?

**The objection.** ⚠️ A question on the world economy since 1900 asks about production, trade, finance and distribution. Soil erosion and ozone holes belong to a different paper.

**The reply.** ⚠️ They would, if they were introduced as scientific facts. They are introduced here as facts about the **organisation of production**, and that is why they belong. ✅ The account places the environment in the same chapter as the world economy and states the connection as a claim about motive: the main motive was the creation of wealth and profit, and very little attention was paid to the side effects. ⚠️ Three of this session's own threads then run straight through the section - the OPEC conservation motive of Lesson 12, the stripping of Third World resources from Lesson 11, and the cost-incidence obstacle that repeats the collective-action failure of Lesson 4. ⚠️ The formulation to carry: **the environmental record is the growth model's balance sheet, and a world-economy answer that omits it has reported the revenue and not the costs.**

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ World history from the eighteenth century onward, and the effects of economic systems on society. ⚠️ The contemporary environmental agenda is owned elsewhere and is handed across in the boundary above.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Rarely as a standalone world-history question; usually as the closing movement of a twenty-mark answer on the world economy since 1900, or as one dimension in an answer on the costs of industrial growth. ⚠️ Three sentences are enough: the motive claim, one exhaustion mechanism with its figure, and the definition of global warming with its industrial cause.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Importing climate policy. ❌ Treaties, targets, conferences and current status belong to the environment owner.
2. ⚠️ Treating environmental damage as a capitalist phenomenon. ✅ The USSR and eastern Europe are recorded as carrying out the dirtiest industrialisation.
3. ⚠️ Dating damage to the 1970s. ✅ What is dated is awareness; the damage accumulated through the century.
4. ⚠️ Separating the oil shock from resource exhaustion. ✅ Scarcity is recorded as one of the reasons OPEC tried to conserve oil in the 1970s.
5. ⚠️ Giving soil or forest figures without their units and years. ✅ Half the tropical rainforests by 1987, about 80,000 square kilometres a year; about 75 billion tons of soil a year; 17, 40, 220 and 400 tons per hectare in the named cases.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ As the century wore on and the North became more obsessed with industrialisation, new methods were invented to increase production and efficiency; the main motive was the creation of wealth and profit and very little attention was paid to side effects.
2. ✅ During the 1970s people became increasingly aware that all was not well with their environment; the three named problems were resource exhaustion, massive pollution and global warming.
3. ✅ Fossil fuels cannot be replaced and are rapidly being used up; oil production increased enormously; this was one of the reasons why OPEC tried to conserve oil during the 1970s; Britain drilled the North Sea and nuclear power was developed as an alternative.
4. ✅ Tin, lead, copper, zinc and mercury were seriously depleted, and it was the Third World which was being stripped of the resources it needed to escape poverty.
5. ✅ About half the world's tropical rainforests had been lost by 1987, with about 80,000 square kilometres - roughly the size of Austria - lost every year.
6. ✅ Phosphate quarrying rose 4 per cent a year from 1950; supplies were expected to be exhausted by the middle of the twenty-first century.
7. ✅ With world population growing by 90 million a year, Stanford scientists found in 1995 that humans, farm animals, crops and plantations were using a quarter of all the water taken up by plants.
8. ✅ About 75 billion tons of soil were lost each year; 17 tons per hectare in western Europe and the USA, 40 in Africa, Asia and South America, 220 on steep slopes in one case and 400 in parts of Jamaica.
9. ✅ The World Conservation Strategy was set up in 1980 to alert the world to these problems.
10. ✅ In 1975 all five Great Lakes were described as dead and about 10 per cent of Swedish lakes were the same; acid rain damaged trees in Germany and Czechoslovakia; the USSR and eastern Europe carried out the dirtiest industrialisation.
11. ✅ The ecosystem is the system by which living creatures, trees and plants function within the environment, all interconnected; ecology is the study of the ecosystem.
12. ✅ CFCs harmed the ozone layer; a hole was found over the Antarctic in 1979 and was much larger by 1989 with another over the Arctic; many countries banned CFCs and in 2001 the World Meteorological Organization reported the layer seemed to be mending.
13. ✅ Nuclear pollution: a quarter of those who worked at Sellafield between 1947 and 1975 and have since died, died of cancer; Three Mile Island in 1979; Chernobyl in 1986, which the account records as killing 35 and spreading a cloud across Europe (⚠️ attribute that figure to the account as an immediate recorded toll, never as a universal final count), with 300,000 British sheep still affected; Fukushima flooded by a tsunami in 2011 after the authorities had ignored and then lied about design weaknesses.
14. ✅ Remedy was slow because of cost incidence: cleaning up made products more expensive and required public spending; in 1996 there were still 27 elderly Chernobyl-type reactors in eastern Europe that governments could afford neither to improve nor to close.
15. ✅ Global warming is defined as the uncontrollable warming of the Earth's atmosphere caused by large quantities of gases emitted from industry - carbon dioxide, methane and nitrous oxide from industrial processes and fossil-fuel burning.
16. ✅ Genetically modified crops became a USA-EU confrontation; by 2004 at least 6 million farmers in 16 countries including India were growing them and Monsanto produced more than 90 per cent worldwide; in April 2004 the USA called on the WTO to act against the EU ban.
17. ⚠️ Master formulation: the environmental record is the growth model's balance sheet, and a world-economy answer that omits it has reported the revenue and not the costs.
18. ❌ Boundary: climate treaties, targets, conference outcomes and current climate status belong to the environment-and-ecology owner.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how the environmental evidence in this lesson strengthens rather than interrupts an answer on the world economy since 1900, using two specific connections.

**Model answer:** It strengthens the answer because the account frames environmental damage as a property of how production was organised, not as a separate scientific subject: as the century wore on and the North became more obsessed with industrialisation, new methods were invented to increase production and efficiency, the main motive was the creation of wealth and profit, and very little attention was paid to the side effects. Two connections make the integration concrete. The first runs to the oil shock. Fossil fuels cannot be replaced and were being rapidly used up, oil production had increased enormously, and this is recorded as one of the reasons why OPEC tried to conserve oil during the 1970s - so the price decision of 1973 was partly a response to a physical limit, which is exactly what its stated motive of conserving supplies means. The second runs to the North-South divide. Tin, lead, copper, zinc and mercury were being seriously depleted, and it was the Third World which was being stripped of the resources it needed to help it escape from poverty, so exhaustion fell hardest on the economies whose export base it destroyed. A third link is available if space allows: the obstacle to remedy was cost incidence, since cleaning up made products more expensive and required public spending while the benefit was diffuse - the same collective-action failure as the tariff spiral of the 1930s. Used this way, the environment is not an appendix but the balance sheet of the growth model the rest of the answer has described.

**Misconception to avoid:** Converting this material into a climate-policy answer. The account continues into conferences, protocols and targets, but those are owned by the environment-and-ecology syllabus, dated and sourced separately, and importing them signals that the candidate cannot tell which paper is being written. What world history owns is the definition, the industrial cause, the dating of awareness to the 1970s and the economic mechanisms of exhaustion, pollution and cost incidence. Two well-dated sentences of economic history score better here than six of policy narrative.

---
## Lesson 18 - Population I: why the twentieth-century explosion was a mortality event

Progress: 18/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the long series from AD 1 to 2011; the accelerating growth rates and the annual increments; regional variation in timing; the European and North American causes including public health, infant mortality and immigration; the three causes in the poorer regions; the demographic transition as a framework; and Malthus and Boserup as the debate's two poles

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's chapter on the world's population, comprising the long series to 7 billion at the end of 2011, the growth-rate series and annual increments, the regional variation, the reasons for increase in Europe and North America with the infant-mortality table and the immigration figures, and the three main causes in the poorer regions. Malthus and Boserup are established by the single declared verification pass recorded in the final ledger; the demographic-transition model itself is owned by the geography owner and is used here only as a framework
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 15 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the curve, its acceleration and the two lines that make the gap

```text
   THE LONG SERIES, AS RECORDED
   ============================
   AD 1 .......... doubling took until 1650
   1650 .......... about 500 MILLION
   1850 .......... more than doubled to 1200 MILLION (1.2 billion)
   1927 .......... 2 BILLION
   2000 .......... passed 6 BILLION
   end of 2011 ... 7 BILLION

   THE ACCELERATION, IN GROWTH RATES
   =================================
   1850-1900 ..... averaging 0.6% every year
   1900-1950 ..... averaging 0.9% a year
   after 1960 .... 1.9% a year  <-- "the full force of the 'explosion'"

   THE ANNUAL INCREMENTS
   =====================
   1990 .......... roughly A MILLION EVERY WEEK; total 5300 million
   1994 .......... +95 million, "the biggest ever increase in a single
                   year so far"
   1995 .......... +100 million, to 5750 million (record broken again)
   1996 .......... +90 million further
   -- the Population Institute in Washington: 90% of the growth was in
      poor countries "torn by civil strife and social unrest"

   WHY THE CURVE BENT: TWO LINES, ONE GAP
   ======================================
        DEATH RATE          ------\
                                   \
                                    \_____________     <- falls FAST:
                                                          medicine, hygiene,
                                                          clean water,
                                                          child survival
        BIRTH RATE          ------------------\
                                               \____   <- falls LATE and
                                                          SLOWLY
                            |<--- THE GAP --->|
                            = THE "POPULATION EXPLOSION"

   THE FRAMEWORK (model ownership handed to the geography owner)
   =============================================================
   Stage 1  high births, high deaths            -> slow growth
   Stage 2  DEATHS FALL, births stay high       -> RAPID GROWTH  <-- here
   Stage 3  births begin to fall                -> growth slows
   Stage 4  low births, low deaths              -> stabilisation, ageing

   THE TIMING DIFFERED BY REGION - which is the whole point
   =======================================================
   Europe and North America: "their most rapid increase BEFORE the First
     World War; after that their rate of increase slowed considerably"
   Africa, Asia, Latin America: "the rate of population increase
     ACCELERATED AFTER THE SECOND WORLD WAR"
   -> Latin America begins to slow after 1950; in Asia and Africa the
      rate continued to increase
```

⚠️ The two-line diagram is the single most important image in the population half of this topic. Everything else in Lessons 18 to 20 is a consequence of the gap between those two curves and of the fact that different regions opened and closed the gap decades apart.

### 1. The learner's problem: what exactly exploded?

⚠️ Not births. That is the misconception this lesson exists to destroy, and it is the most commonly tested discrimination in the demographic half of the syllabus.

⚠️ Population growth is the difference between births and deaths. It can rise because births rise, because deaths fall, or both. ⚠️ In the twentieth century the dominant movement was the second: death rates fell rapidly because of medicine, hygiene, clean water and child survival, while birth rates continued at their existing levels for decades before adjusting. ⚠️ The "explosion" is the widening gap between the two curves, and it is therefore, in the first instance, **a success of public health rather than a failure of restraint**.

### 2. The series, and how to use it

✅ **Fact.** Before the seventeenth century the world's population increased very slowly; it has been estimated that by **1650** the population had doubled since the year AD 1, to about **500 million**. ✅ Over the next 200 years the rate of increase was much faster, so that by **1850** the population had more than doubled to **1,200 million**. ✅ After that, growth accelerated so rapidly that people talked about a population "explosion": ✅ in **1927** it reached the **2 billion** mark; ✅ by the year **2000** it had passed **6 billion**; ✅ and at the end of **2011** it reached **7 billion**.

✅ **The growth rates.** Between **1850 and 1900** the world's population was increasing on average by **0.6 per cent** every year. ✅ During the next fifty years the rate averaged **0.9 per cent** a year. ✅ It was after **1960** that the full force of the explosion was felt, with the total world population increasing at the rate of **1.9 per cent** a year on average.

✅ **The increments.** In **1990** the population was increasing by roughly **a million every week**, and the total had reached **5,300 million**. ✅ In **1994** there was an increase of **95 million**, the biggest ever increase in a single year so far; ✅ in **1995** the record was broken again as the total grew by **100 million** to **5,750 million**; ✅ during **1996** a further **90 million** were added. ✅ According to the **Population Institute in Washington**, **90 per cent** of the growth was in poor countries "torn by civil strife and social unrest".

⚠️ Use the three series for different purposes. The long series shows the shape of the curve. The growth rates show that the acceleration was in the **rate** and not merely in the total - which is the difference between a large population and an explosion. The increments show what a 1.9 per cent rate means in human beings: a million a week.

⚠️ And note the methodological warning built into the last figure. A percentage rate applied to a growing base produces larger and larger absolute increments even when the rate itself is falling; a candidate who cites the record annual increases of 1994 and 1995 without knowing that the rate had already begun to fall in parts of Asia has misread the data. ⚠️ Always state whether you are discussing the rate or the increment.

### 3. Why the rate rose in Europe and North America

✅ **Fact.** The population increase in Europe and North America in the later part of the nineteenth and the early twentieth centuries had several causes.

✅ **Prosperity.** Increasing industrialisation, economic growth and prosperity meant that the necessary resources were available to sustain a larger population, and the two seemed to go hand in hand.

⚠️ Notice what that clause does to the usual argument. Here population growth and economic growth are recorded as moving *together* and reinforcing each other, which is the opposite of the relationship assumed when population growth in poorer regions is discussed. ⚠️ Hold that asymmetry; Lesson 19 will explain it.

✅ **Public health.** There was a great improvement thanks to advances in medical science and sanitation: ✅ the work of **Louis Pasteur** and **Joseph Lister** in the **1860s** on germs and antiseptic techniques helped to reduce the death rate, ✅ while the big industrial cities introduced piped water supplies and drainage schemes which all helped to reduce disease.

✅ **Infant mortality.** There was a decline in the number of babies who died before the age of one, mainly thanks to medical improvements which reduced deaths from diseases such as **scarlet fever, diphtheria and whooping cough**. ✅ The recorded series of deaths within one year of birth, per thousand births, is:

| ✅ Country | ✅ 1880-90 | ✅ 1931-38 |
|---|---|---|
| England | 142 | 52 |
| Switzerland | 165 | 43 |
| France | 166 | 65 |
| Italy | 195 | 104 |
| Austria | 256 | 80 |

⚠️ That table is the best evidence in the whole topic for the mortality-before-fertility argument, and it should be used rather than described. Infant mortality in Austria fell from 256 to 80 per thousand in roughly fifty years. ⚠️ A fall of that magnitude changes a society's arithmetic completely: a family that previously needed many births to see a few children survive no longer does - but the *behaviour* takes a generation or more to catch up with the *fact*, and that lag is the gap.

✅ **Immigration** helped to swell the population of the USA and, to a lesser extent, Canada, Argentina and Brazil: ✅ in the hundred years after **1820** some **35 million** people entered the USA, ✅ and in the last few years before **1914** they were arriving at a rate of **a million a year**.

⚠️ Migration belongs in this topic for two reasons. It redistributes population between regions without changing the world total, so it affects national demography and world demography differently. And it is itself an economic process: the flow followed employment and land availability, and it was interrupted by war and restriction. ⚠️ Note also that the same mechanism reappears in Lesson 15, where the world's labour force doubled after 1990 "with the increase in migration" - the migration of the 2000s being to labour markets as the migration of the 1900s was to settlement frontiers.

✅ **Then the slowdown.** After **1900** the growth rate in Europe began to slow down, mainly because more people were using modern contraceptive techniques; ✅ later, the economic depression of the **1930s** discouraged people from having as many children.

⚠️ That second clause deserves attention because it is a clean case of economics acting on fertility within a single decade. ⚠️ It also connects the two halves of this topic directly: the Depression of Lessons 3 to 6 is recorded here as a demographic event.

### 4. Why the rate rose in the poorer regions, and why later

✅ **Fact.** The rapid population growth in the less developed regions had **three main causes**.

✅ **First, medicine and hygiene.** Modern medical and hygiene techniques began to make an impact for the first time; the child mortality rate fell and people lived longer, as killer diseases like **smallpox, malaria and typhoid** were gradually brought under control.

✅ **Second, the absence of family limitation.** At the same time the vast majority of the population made no attempt to limit their families by using contraceptives; ✅ this was partly through ignorance and partly because contraceptives were too expensive for ordinary people to buy.

✅ **Third, religious teaching.** The **Roman Catholic Church** said that contraception was forbidden for its members, on the grounds that it prevented the natural creation of new lives and was therefore sinful; ✅ since the Church was strong in Central and South America, its teaching had important effects. ✅ The population growth rate for many countries in those areas was over **3 per cent** per annum; ✅ the average for the whole of **Latin America** was **2.4 per cent** in **1960**, whereas the average for **Europe** was only **0.75 per cent**. ✅ An increase of 2 per cent per annum means that the population of that country doubles in about **30 years**, ✅ which happened in **Brazil and Mexico** in the thirty years up to 1960.

✅ **And a fourth pressure, stated separately.** Many countries had a long tradition of people having as many children as possible to combat high infant mortality, in order to make sure their family continued; ✅ some cultures, Muslims for example, attach great value to having many sons; ✅ and the same attitudes persisted in spite of the reduction in infant mortality.

⚠️ That last clause is the mechanism of the lag, stated by the account itself, and it is the most important sentence in this section. ⚠️ High-fertility behaviour was a **rational response to high child mortality**: if half your children will not reach adulthood, having many is how a family survives. ⚠️ When mortality falls, the rationale disappears but the norm persists, because norms are transmitted between generations rather than recalculated each year. ⚠️ The gap between the two curves is therefore not ignorance and not irresponsibility; it is a **lag between a change in facts and a change in expectations**.

⚠️ Note also the doubling arithmetic and use it: at 2 per cent a year a population doubles in about thirty years, a rule the account applies to Brazil and Mexico. ⚠️ It converts an abstract rate into something a reader can feel.

### 5. The framework, and the ownership boundary

⚠️ The standard framework for describing these movements is the **demographic transition**: high births and high deaths giving slow growth; then falling deaths with high births giving rapid growth; then falling births giving slower growth; then low births and low deaths giving stabilisation and, eventually, ageing.

> ⚠️ **Model ownership, stated openly.** The demographic transition model itself - its stages, its assumptions, its criticisms and the population-resource debates built on it - is owned and taught by the geography owner, along with population composition, pyramids and structure. ⚠️ **World history's role is different and is retained here:** this session owns the *historical* population question - why the mortality decline of the twentieth century happened when it did, what its social and economic effects were, how states responded, and how an epidemic shock interacted with development. ⚠️ Use the geography owner for the model and this session for the history; ❌ do not duplicate either, and ❌ do not attribute the model to a named demographer here.

⚠️ Used as a framework rather than as a doctrine, the transition does one indispensable job in this topic: it converts description into analysis by identifying **which stage** a region is in and therefore **which problem** it faces. ⚠️ That is the whole content of Lesson 19.

### 6. The debate's two poles: Malthus and Boserup

⚠️ **Facts established by the declared verification pass.** **Thomas Robert Malthus**, in *An Essay on the Principle of Population* (**1798**), argued that population growth would outrun the growth of food production unless checked. ⚠️ **Ester Boserup**, in *The Conditions of Agricultural Growth* (**1965**), argued the contrary case: that population pressure is itself a stimulus to agricultural innovation and to increased food production.

⚠️ These two names are the poles of every population-policy argument in this topic and they should be used as an axis rather than as a pair of authorities. ⚠️ State the disagreement precisely: they differ about whether agricultural technique is **fixed or responsive**. If technique is fixed, more people means less food per person, and the conclusion is restraint. If technique responds to need, more people means more pressure to innovate, and the conclusion is investment.

⚠️ The evidence in this session speaks to both poles, and an answer that says so is doing genuine analysis.

- ⚠️ **For the responsive view.** ✅ All areas of the world succeeded in increasing food production during the late 1960s and 1970s thanks to the **green revolution**, with new strains of heavy-cropping rice and wheat on short, fast-growing stems, helped by fertilizers and irrigation; ✅ for a time food supplies seemed well ahead of population growth, even a densely populated country like **India** was able to export food, and **China** became self-sufficient.
- ⚠️ **For the limits view.** ✅ In the mid-1980s, with the world's population growing faster than ever, the green revolution was running into problems: ✅ a point had been reached beyond which crop yields could not be increased any further, and there was a limit to the water supply, topsoil and phosphates for fertilizers.

⚠️ The disciplined verdict, and it is genuinely the account's own position rather than a compromise: **innovation responded, and then met physical limits.** ⚠️ Neither pole survives the twentieth century intact, which is exactly why an answer should present them as an axis and place the evidence along it.

> **Boundary.** The population-resource debate in its theoretical form belongs to the geography owner. This session uses Malthus and Boserup only to name the axis on which its own historical evidence falls.

### Criticism and reply: is "a success of public health" too benign a description?

**The objection.** ⚠️ Calling the explosion a success of public health sounds like a defence of it, and it sits awkwardly beside the overcrowding, unemployment and shanty towns of Lesson 19.

**The reply.** ⚠️ The description is about causation, not about consequences, and keeping the two apart is the point. ✅ What caused the rise was that killer diseases such as smallpox, malaria and typhoid were gradually brought under control, that child mortality fell and that people lived longer - and no one proposes that those developments should not have happened. ⚠️ What produced the difficulties was the **lag** before fertility adjusted and the **absence of resources** to absorb the additional people, both of which are separate problems with separate remedies. ⚠️ The formulation to carry: **the explosion was caused by something desirable and produced consequences that required policy; confusing the two produces either complacency or alarmism, and the syllabus rewards neither.**

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ World history from the eighteenth century onward. ⚠️ The chapter's own recorded demands are to explain the causes and consequences of the rapid growth in the world's population during the twentieth century, and to explain why the rate slowed in Europe while accelerating in Africa and other poorer regions.

**Verified question linkage.** ⚠️ The demographic-transition and population questions in the verified ledgers held in this repository are routed to the geography and Indian-society owners, not to this topic; the complete audit is recorded in the final verified-question section. ⚠️ What this session supplies to those answers is the historical causation - why mortality fell when it did and why fertility followed later.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Explaining the explosion as "more births". ✅ It is the gap between a fast-falling death rate and a slowly-adjusting birth rate.
2. ⚠️ Confusing rates with increments. ✅ 1.9 per cent a year after 1960 is a rate; 95 million in 1994 and 100 million in 1995 are increments on a growing base.
3. ⚠️ Treating high fertility as ignorance. ✅ The account records the tradition of having as many children as possible to combat high infant mortality and ensure family continuation; the behaviour was rational under the old mortality regime.
4. ⚠️ Attributing the demographic transition model to a named demographer here. ⚠️ The model's owner is the geography topic.
5. ⚠️ Using Malthus or Boserup as a conclusion. ⚠️ Use them as an axis: technique responded and then met limits.
6. ⚠️ Forgetting migration. ✅ Some 35 million entered the USA in the hundred years after 1820, a million a year just before 1914.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ By 1650 the world's population had doubled since AD 1 to about 500 million; by 1850 it had more than doubled to 1,200 million; it reached 2 billion in 1927, passed 6 billion by 2000 and reached 7 billion at the end of 2011.
2. ✅ Growth rates averaged 0.6 per cent a year between 1850 and 1900, 0.9 per cent over the next fifty years, and 1.9 per cent a year after 1960, when the full force of the explosion was felt.
3. ✅ In 1990 the population was growing by roughly a million a week, with a total of 5,300 million; 1994 added 95 million, 1995 added 100 million to reach 5,750 million and 1996 a further 90 million.
4. ✅ The Population Institute in Washington recorded that 90 per cent of the growth was in poor countries torn by civil strife and social unrest.
5. ✅ Europe and North America had their most rapid increase before the First World War and slowed considerably afterwards; in Africa, Asia and Latin America the rate accelerated after the Second World War, slowing in some Latin American countries after 1950 while continuing to rise in Asia and Africa.
6. ✅ European and North American causes: industrialisation and prosperity providing resources; public health advances, with Pasteur and Lister in the 1860s on germs and antiseptics and piped water and drainage in the industrial cities; falling infant mortality from scarlet fever, diphtheria and whooping cough; and immigration.
7. ✅ Infant deaths per thousand births fell between 1880-90 and 1931-38 from 142 to 52 in England, 165 to 43 in Switzerland, 166 to 65 in France, 195 to 104 in Italy and 256 to 80 in Austria.
8. ✅ Some 35 million people entered the USA in the hundred years after 1820, arriving at a million a year in the last few years before 1914, with smaller flows to Canada, Argentina and Brazil.
9. ✅ After 1900 European growth slowed, mainly because more people used modern contraceptive techniques; the depression of the 1930s further discouraged large families.
10. ✅ Three main causes in the poorer regions: modern medicine and hygiene bringing smallpox, malaria and typhoid under control so child mortality fell and people lived longer; no attempt to limit families, through ignorance and the cost of contraceptives; and the Roman Catholic prohibition on contraception, strong in Central and South America.
11. ✅ Many Latin American countries had growth rates over 3 per cent; the Latin American average was 2.4 per cent in 1960 against 0.75 per cent for Europe; at 2 per cent a population doubles in about thirty years, as happened in Brazil and Mexico in the thirty years to 1960.
12. ✅ A long tradition of having as many children as possible to combat high infant mortality and ensure family continuity persisted in spite of falling infant mortality; some cultures, Muslims for example, attach great value to having many sons.
13. ⚠️ The lag between falling mortality and adjusting fertility is the explosion; high fertility had been rational under the old mortality regime.
14. ⚠️ The demographic transition is used here as a framework only; the model itself belongs to the geography owner.
15. ⚠️ Malthus (1798) held that population would outrun food; Boserup (1965) held that population pressure stimulates agricultural innovation. The green revolution supports Boserup until the mid-1980s, when yields, water, topsoil and phosphates set limits.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using the infant-mortality table and the account's statement about family traditions, explain in causal terms why fertility did not fall as soon as mortality did.

**Model answer:** Because the two are connected by expectations rather than by arithmetic, and expectations change more slowly than facts. The table shows how fast the facts changed: deaths within one year of birth per thousand births fell between 1880-90 and 1931-38 from 142 to 52 in England, 165 to 43 in Switzerland, 166 to 65 in France, 195 to 104 in Italy and 256 to 80 in Austria, and a comparable decline followed later in the poorer regions as modern medical and hygiene techniques brought smallpox, malaria and typhoid under control, so child mortality fell and people lived longer. The behaviour that had been built on the old figures did not change with them. The account records the mechanism directly: many countries had a long tradition of people having as many children as possible to combat high infant mortality, in order to make sure their family continued, and the same attitudes persisted in spite of the reduction in infant mortality; in some cultures, Muslims for example, great value was attached to having many sons. High fertility had therefore been a rational family strategy under a regime in which many children would not survive, and it remained the norm for a generation or more after the regime had changed - reinforced where contraceptives were unknown or too expensive, and where religious teaching forbade them. The gap between a death rate that had already fallen and a birth rate still set for the previous mortality regime is precisely the population explosion.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the lag as a failure of intelligence or of responsibility on the part of the populations concerned. Nothing in the evidence supports that, and the account points the other way: it names ignorance of methods, the expense of contraceptives and religious prohibition as constraints, and it describes large families as a strategy for family continuation under high infant mortality. An answer that frames the gap as a transmission problem between generations, reinforced by cost and doctrine, is both more accurate and far better placed to explain why the policies of Lesson 20 worked where they addressed those constraints and failed where they did not.

---

## Lesson 19 - Population II: consequences - ageing, cities, food and the diversion of resources

Progress: 19/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the benign consequences in the industrialising nations and the ageing that followed; overcrowding, migration to cities and the shanty towns; the green revolution and its ceiling; the diversion of Third World resources from development to subsistence; the healthcare gap; megacities; and the correlation-and-causation discipline applied to population and poverty

### Visual: the same phenomenon, two opposite consequences

⚠️ The two columns are the lesson. The same demographic event - a falling death rate - produced a stimulus where resources existed to absorb it and a crisis where they did not. ⚠️ That is the strongest available refutation of any claim that population growth has a single economic meaning.

### 1. The benign case, and why it must be taught first

✅ **Fact.** The population growth of the nineteenth century helped to stimulate further economic development in Europe and North America. ✅ There was a plentiful workforce and more people to buy goods, and this encouraged more investment and enterprise. ✅ Nor were there any great problems about feeding and educating these growing numbers, because prosperity meant that the necessary resources were available.

⚠️ Teaching this first is a deliberate pedagogical choice. It establishes, before any discussion of overcrowding, that population growth is not inherently a problem - it was a growth input in one setting and a crisis in another. ⚠️ The variable is not the number of people but **whether the resources to absorb them exist**, and an answer built on that variable is analytically far stronger than one built on numbers alone.

### 2. Ageing: the unexpected second consequence

✅ **Fact.** Later on there were unexpected effects on the age structure of the population in the developed nations. ✅ This was especially true in Europe where, because of the very low birth rates and longer life expectancy, a growing proportion of the population was over **65**. ✅ By the **1970s**, in countries such as **Sweden, France and Britain**, about **15 per cent** of the population were over 65. ✅ In the early **1990s**, with this proportion still increasing, questions were being asked about whether state welfare systems would be able to afford to pay pensions to all old people if this trend continued into the twenty-first century.

⚠️ Three things follow and they are all examinable.

First, ⚠️ **ageing is the fourth stage of the same process**, not a separate subject. A society that completes the transition - low deaths, then low births - necessarily acquires an older age structure. ⚠️ The regions worrying about too many people and the regions worrying about too few were at different points on one curve.

Second, ⚠️ **the problem ageing creates is fiscal and it is specific**: state welfare systems financed by current contributions depend on the ratio between contributors and beneficiaries, and that ratio is a demographic fact. ⚠️ This is the exact point at which the population half of the topic meets the welfare settlement of Lesson 8: the European model's generous systems financed by taxation and social security contributions have a demographic precondition.

Third, ⚠️ **it destroys the framing of population as a poor-country problem.** ✅ The account's own evidence records rich countries anxious about too few working-age people in the same decades in which poor countries were anxious about too many. ❌ An answer that treats population as a Third World issue has ignored half the record.

### 3. Overcrowding, cities and the shanty town

✅ **Fact.** The rapid population growth caused serious problems: ✅ some countries, like **India, Pakistan and Bangladesh**, became overcrowded and there was insufficient land to go round. ✅ This forced people to move into towns and cities, but these were already overcrowded and there were not enough houses or jobs for all the new arrivals. ✅ Many people were forced to live on the streets; ✅ some cities, especially those in **Latin America**, were surrounded by **shanty towns** and slums which had no proper water supply, sanitation or lighting.

⚠️ State the urbanisation mechanism precisely, because "people moved to cities" is not an explanation. The sequence recorded is: population rises; land per household falls below viability; rural households are pushed towards towns; the towns have neither housing nor employment to absorb them; the surplus settles in unserviced peripheral housing. ⚠️ This is **push-driven urbanisation** - migration driven by rural non-viability rather than by urban opportunity - and it is a different phenomenon from the pull-driven urbanisation of an industrialising economy where towns are generating jobs.

⚠️ That distinction is the most transferable idea in the section. ⚠️ The nineteenth-century European city grew because industry was hiring; the mid-twentieth-century shanty town grew because the countryside could no longer support the people living in it. ⚠️ The same word, urbanisation, describes two economically opposite processes.

> **Boundary.** Contemporary Indian urbanisation, migration policy and slum questions are owned by the geography and Indian-society topics. This session supplies the historical mechanism and the world context.

### 4. Food: the green revolution and its ceiling

✅ **Fact.** All areas of the world succeeded in increasing food production during the late **1960s** and **1970s**, thanks to what became known as the **green revolution**. ✅ Scientists developed new strains of heavy-cropping rice and wheat on short, fast-growing stems, helped by fertilizers and irrigation schemes. ✅ For a time, food supplies seemed to be well ahead of population growth: ✅ even a densely populated country like **India** was able to export food, and **China** became self-sufficient. ✅ In the USA crop yields increased **threefold between 1945 and 1995**, and the Americans were able to export surplus crops to over a hundred countries.

✅ **Then the limits.** In the mid-**1980s**, with the world's population growing faster than ever, the green revolution was running into problems and scientists became concerned about the future. ✅ A point had been reached beyond which crop yields could not be increased any further, and there was a limit to the water supply, topsoil and phosphates for fertilizers. ✅ A survey carried out by scientists at **Stanford University** in **1996** found that the amount of farmland available was dwindling because of industrialisation, the spread of cities and soil erosion, and they calculated that the number of mouths to feed in the USA would double by **2050** while there seemed no way in which food production could be doubled from less land; ✅ in **1996** there were **1.8 acres** of cropland to each American and by 2050 there was likely to be only **0.6** of an acre per head, ✅ leading the Stanford scientists to conclude that the solution was for people to eat less meat, with the suggestion that by 2050 the US diet would probably be about **85 per cent** vegetarian.

✅ ⚠️ Matters were made worse in parts of Africa - **Ethiopia, Mozambique and Somalia** - during the 1980s and 1990s by drought and civil wars, which played a part in causing severe food shortages and tens of thousands of deaths from starvation.

⚠️ Two analytical points, and the second is the one that separates answers.

First, ⚠️ the green revolution is the Boserup case in its strongest form: population pressure met technological response, and for two decades the response won. ⚠️ India exporting food and China becoming self-sufficient are the two facts to cite, because they are counter-intuitive and therefore memorable.

Second, ⚠️ the ceiling is where Lesson 17 re-enters: the limits named are **water supply, topsoil and phosphates**, every one of which appeared in the resource-exhaustion section. ⚠️ The food problem and the environmental problem are therefore the same problem observed from two directions, and an answer that says so has connected two chapters that most candidates treat separately.

### 5. The diversion of resources, and the healthcare gap

✅ **Fact.** Third World governments were forced to spend their valuable cash to feed, house and educate their growing populations. ✅ But this used up resources which they would have preferred to spend on industrialising and modernising their countries, and so their economic development was delayed.

⚠️ This is the strongest causal link the account draws between population and development, and it should be stated precisely because it is narrower than it first appears. The claim is not that people consume too much; it is that **expenditure composition shifted from investment towards subsistence**, so the growth that would have relieved the pressure was itself postponed. ⚠️ That is a real mechanism and it should be written as one.

✅ **The healthcare consequence.** The general shortage of resources meant that the poorest countries also lacked sufficient cash to spend on healthcare. ✅ Following a meningitis epidemic in **Niger**, **Save the Children** reported in **April 1996** that one-sixth of the world's population - over **800 million** people - had no access to healthcare. ✅ Health systems in many poorer countries were collapsing, and the situation was becoming worse because richer countries were reducing aid. ✅ The report estimated that it cost at least **$12** a person a year to provide basic healthcare, ✅ but **16** African countries including **Niger, Uganda, Zaire, Tanzania, Mozambique and Liberia**, plus **Bangladesh, India, Pakistan, Nepal and Vietnam**, were spending much less than that. ✅ In comparison, **Britain** was spending the equivalent of **$1,039** - **£723**. ✅ **Zaire** was spending only **40 cents** per head a year, while **Tanzania** managed **70 cents**. ✅ This meant that simple immunisation against easily preventable diseases was not being carried out, ✅ widespread epidemics could be expected before the end of the century, and a rise in the child mortality rate. ✅ When the AIDS epidemic spread around the turn of the century, it was clear that Africa in particular would be in dire crisis. ✅ Another disturbing fact was that almost all these states were spending vastly more per head on defence than on healthcare.

⚠️ The comparison of $1,039 with 40 cents is the most arresting figure in the population chapter, and it should always be quoted as a **pair with its year**: in 1996, Britain spent the equivalent of $1,039 per head on health while Zaire spent 40 cents.

⚠️ And note the closing observation, because it introduces the agency that Lesson 11's structural account partly lacked: ✅ almost all these states were spending vastly more per head on defence than on healthcare. ⚠️ That is a statement about **domestic allocation**, not about external constraint, and an honest answer must carry it alongside the structural mechanisms.

⚠️ Note finally the forward reference that the account itself makes: this healthcare collapse is the condition into which the epidemic of Lesson 21 arrived.

### 6. Megacities, and the security argument

✅ **Fact.** **Paul Liotta and James Miskel** highlight another worrying aspect of the still-growing population: the growth of huge cities with populations of over **10 million**. ✅ They calculate that by **2025** there will be at least **27** of these megacities around the globe. ✅ In Africa, Asia, the Middle East and South America these massive concentrations of people inevitably include a large proportion of poverty-stricken have-nots; ✅ in the authors' words, "crowded masses within these unaccommodating spaces will have literally nowhere else to go; if left to their own devices by inept or uncaring governments, collective rage, despair and hunger will inevitably erupt". ✅ They argue that megacities are attracting terrorists and various types of criminal gangs, and that unless governments meet this challenge by taking effective counter-measures, some will present a serious security threat to the rest of the world.

⚠️ Attribute this to its authors, as the account does, and treat it as an argument rather than a finding. ⚠️ Note also its internal conditional - "if left to their own devices by inept or uncaring governments" - which makes the prediction contingent on governance rather than on numbers. ⚠️ That conditional is worth pointing out in an answer, because it concedes the very variable that Lesson 22 will make central.

### 7. Correlation and causation: does population growth cause poverty?

⚠️ This is the discipline's most important application in the entire session, and the evidence held here points in both directions.

- ⚠️ **Evidence that growth aggravated poverty.** ✅ Governments were forced to spend on feeding, housing and educating growing populations, using up resources they would have preferred to spend on industrialising and modernising, so economic development was delayed. ✅ Overcrowding produced insufficient land, insufficient jobs and unserviced shanty towns. ✅ The Population Institute recorded that 90 per cent of the growth was in poor countries torn by civil strife and social unrest.
- ⚠️ **Evidence that poverty sustained high fertility.** ✅ Contraceptives were too expensive for ordinary people to buy. ✅ Large families were a tradition adopted to combat high infant mortality and ensure family continuation. ✅ Falling infant mortality is what eventually changed the calculation, and it fell where health provision improved.
- ⚠️ **Evidence that growth accompanied prosperity elsewhere.** ✅ In nineteenth-century Europe and North America population growth helped to stimulate economic development, because prosperity meant the necessary resources were available.
- ⚠️ **Evidence that allocation mattered independently.** ✅ Almost all the states with the lowest health spending were spending vastly more per head on defence than on healthcare.

⚠️ The disciplined conclusion is a **two-way relationship with a mediating variable**: population growth and poverty reinforce each other, and the variable that determines which dominates is whether resources exist and are allocated to absorb the additional people. ❌ A one-directional claim in either form - "population growth causes poverty" or "poverty causes population growth" - is contradicted by evidence inside this very session.

### Criticism and reply: is the Stanford projection usable evidence?

**The objection.** ⚠️ A 1996 calculation that American mouths to feed would double by 2050 and that the diet would become 85 per cent vegetarian is a projection, and projections about 2050 made in 1996 are not historical evidence.

**The reply.** ⚠️ Correct, and the honest use is narrow. ⚠️ The projection is evidence of **what specialists in 1996 concluded from the data then available**, which is a legitimate historical fact about the state of the debate; it is not evidence about 2050. ⚠️ What carries independent weight in the same passage is the observed rather than projected material: that the green revolution was running into problems by the mid-1980s, that crop yields had reached a point beyond which they could not be increased, and that water, topsoil and phosphates were limits. ⚠️ Cite the projection with its date and its authors or not at all - and note that this is the same discipline applied in Lesson 15 to the BRIC forecasts.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ World history from the eighteenth century onward. ⚠️ The chapter's recorded demand is the causes *and consequences* of rapid population growth; Lesson 18 owns the causes and this lesson owns the consequences.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ "Explain the causes and consequences of the rapid growth of world population", or as a component in answers on development, urbanisation or food security. ⚠️ The structure that scores places the two-column contrast first, then urbanisation as push-driven, then the food ceiling with its named limits, then resource diversion with the healthcare figures, closing on the two-way relationship.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating population growth as intrinsically harmful. ✅ In nineteenth-century Europe and North America it helped to stimulate economic development.
2. ⚠️ Treating population as a poor-country problem. ✅ By the 1970s about 15 per cent of Swedes, French and British were over 65, and the affordability of pensions was already being questioned in the early 1990s.
3. ⚠️ Explaining urbanisation as opportunity-seeking. ✅ The recorded mechanism is insufficient land and forced movement into cities that had neither houses nor jobs.
4. ⚠️ Presenting the green revolution as an unqualified success. ✅ It ran into a yield ceiling and into water, topsoil and phosphate limits by the mid-1980s.
5. ⚠️ Citing the Stanford 2050 projection as fact. ⚠️ It is a dated calculation by named scientists.
6. ⚠️ Omitting domestic allocation. ✅ Almost all the lowest-spending states spent vastly more per head on defence than on healthcare.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Nineteenth-century population growth in Europe and North America helped to stimulate economic development: a plentiful workforce, more buyers, more investment and enterprise, with no great problem feeding or educating the growing numbers because prosperity made resources available.
2. ✅ The later, unexpected effect was on age structure: very low birth rates and longer life expectancy meant a growing proportion over 65; by the 1970s about 15 per cent of the population in Sweden, France and Britain, with questions in the early 1990s about whether welfare systems could afford pensions.
3. ✅ In the poorer regions, India, Pakistan and Bangladesh became overcrowded with insufficient land, forcing movement into towns that lacked houses and jobs; many lived on the streets and cities, especially in Latin America, were ringed by shanty towns without proper water supply, sanitation or lighting.
4. ⚠️ Push-driven urbanisation - movement caused by rural non-viability - is economically the opposite of pull-driven urbanisation caused by industrial hiring, though both are called urbanisation.
5. ✅ The green revolution of the late 1960s and 1970s produced new strains of heavy-cropping rice and wheat on short, fast-growing stems, helped by fertilizers and irrigation; India was able to export food and China became self-sufficient; US crop yields increased threefold between 1945 and 1995 with exports to over a hundred countries.
6. ✅ By the mid-1980s a point had been reached beyond which yields could not be increased, with limits to water supply, topsoil and phosphates; the Stanford survey of 1996 found farmland dwindling from industrialisation, the spread of cities and soil erosion, with 1.8 acres of cropland per American in 1996 and a projected 0.6 by 2050.
7. ✅ Drought and civil wars in Ethiopia, Mozambique and Somalia in the 1980s and 1990s caused severe food shortages and tens of thousands of deaths from starvation.
8. ✅ Third World governments were forced to spend on feeding, housing and educating growing populations, using up resources they would have preferred to spend on industrialising and modernising, so development was delayed.
9. ✅ Save the Children, April 1996: over 800 million people - one-sixth of the world's population - had no access to healthcare; basic healthcare cost at least $12 a person a year; 16 African countries plus Bangladesh, India, Pakistan, Nepal and Vietnam spent much less; Britain spent the equivalent of $1,039, Zaire 40 cents and Tanzania 70 cents.
10. ✅ Simple immunisation was not being carried out, widespread epidemics were expected and child mortality was set to rise; almost all these states spent vastly more per head on defence than on healthcare.
11. ✅ Liotta and Miskel calculate at least 27 megacities of over 10 million by 2025, with large proportions of have-nots, and argue that without effective counter-measures some will present a serious security threat.
12. ⚠️ The relationship between population and poverty is two-way, mediated by whether resources exist and are allocated to absorb the additional people.
13. ⚠️ The food ceiling and the environmental limits of Lesson 17 are the same constraint seen from two directions: water, topsoil and phosphates appear in both.

### Concept check

**Question:** The same demographic change produced a stimulus in one setting and a crisis in another. Identify the mediating variable and demonstrate it with evidence from both settings.

**Model answer:** The mediating variable is whether the resources to absorb the additional people existed and were allocated to do so. In nineteenth-century Europe and North America the account records that population growth helped to stimulate further economic development, because there was a plentiful workforce and more people to buy goods, which encouraged more investment and enterprise, and because there were no great problems about feeding and educating the growing numbers since prosperity meant the necessary resources were available. The final clause is the variable stated explicitly. In the poorer regions after 1945 the same falling death rate produced the opposite result: India, Pakistan and Bangladesh became overcrowded with insufficient land to go round, people were forced into towns and cities that were already overcrowded and had neither houses nor jobs, many lived on the streets, and cities especially in Latin America were surrounded by shanty towns without water supply, sanitation or lighting. The fiscal expression of the same absence is that governments were forced to spend their valuable cash to feed, house and educate their growing populations, using up resources they would have preferred to spend on industrialising and modernising, so their economic development was delayed. Allocation matters as well as availability, which is why the account's closing observation belongs in the answer: almost all the states spending least on health were spending vastly more per head on defence.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that population growth is therefore harmless and that only resources matter. The evidence does not support that either. Growth imposed real costs even where it was absorbed, and in the poorer regions it converted a shortage of resources into a postponement of the development that would have relieved the shortage - a genuinely self-reinforcing trap. The accurate statement is conditional rather than absolute: population growth amplifies whatever a society's resource position already is, which is why the same event stimulated one set of economies and overwhelmed another.

---

## Lesson 20 - Population III: control, coercion and the ethics of population policy

Progress: 20/22 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the early scientific interest and the first congresses; the United Nations Population Commission and the national campaigns; what the campaigns achieved; the contraceptive and birth-rate evidence; the regional growth and density table; the Chinese one-child policy and the coercion recorded by its own historians; the criticism inside and outside China; and the consumption argument that answers the numbers argument

The Cairo conference of 1994 and the formal end of the one-child policy are established by the single declared verification pass recorded in the final ledger

### Visual: two routes to lower fertility, and the evidence on each
