⚠️ The table at the bottom left is the lesson's evidential centre. It is also the lesson's central trap, because a correlation between contraceptive use and falling birth rates can be read in two directions, and the reading matters for policy.

### 1. The learner's problem: an ethical question with an evidential core

⚠️ The account poses the question itself, and it is the right one: **how far should a government go in its efforts to control population?** ⚠️ It is genuinely an ethical question, and the temptation is to answer it with sentiment. ⚠️ The disciplined approach answers it with the record of what each route actually achieved and at what cost, and only then states a verdict.

### 2. The early history, which most candidates do not know

✅ **Fact.** For many years people had been giving serious thought to controlling the population before the world became too overcrowded. ✅ Soon after the First World War, scientists in a number of countries first began to be concerned at the population growth and felt that it was a problem that should be studied at international level. ✅ The first **World Population Congress** was held in **Geneva in 1925**, ✅ and the following year an **International Union for the Scientific Study of Population** was set up in **Paris**. ✅ As well as scientists, the organisation included statisticians and social scientists concerned about the probable economic and social effects if the world's population continued to grow; ✅ they did valuable work collecting statistics and encouraging governments to improve their data systems so that accurate information about population trends could be obtained.

⚠️ Two points worth carrying. First, the dating: international concern about population predates the post-1945 explosion by a generation, which is a useful corrective to the assumption that the subject began with decolonisation. Second, the first activity was **measurement**, not intervention - and that sequence is itself a finding, because policy on a population cannot precede knowledge of it.

### 3. The United Nations and the national campaigns

✅ **Fact.** When the **United Nations Organization** was set up in **1945**, a **Population Commission** was included among its many agencies. ✅ When the Third World population began to explode during the 1950s, it was the UN which took the lead in encouraging governments to introduce birth-control programmes.

✅ **India and Pakistan** set up **family-planning clinics** to advise people about the various methods of birth control available, and to provide them with cheap contraceptives. ✅ Huge publicity campaigns were launched with government posters recommending a maximum of **three children per family**. ✅ Many **African** governments also recommended a maximum of three children, ✅ while the **Chinese** government went further and fixed the legal maximum at **two children** per family.

✅ But progress was very slow: ✅ ancient practices and attitudes were difficult to change, especially in countries like India and Pakistan, ✅ and in the Roman Catholic countries of South America the Church continued to forbid artificial birth control.

⚠️ Note the design of the Indian and Pakistani campaigns, because it maps exactly onto the constraints identified in Lesson 18. ⚠️ The account had named ignorance of methods and the expense of contraceptives as two of the three reasons families were not limited; ✅ the clinics addressed the first by advising on methods and the second by providing cheap contraceptives. ⚠️ The campaigns were, in other words, correctly targeted on the stated causes - and they still moved slowly, because the third constraint, the norm itself, was not something a clinic could supply.

### 4. What the campaigns achieved

✅ **Fact.** The best that can be said is that in parts of **Asia** the population growth rate was beginning to fall slightly during the **1980s**, but in many **African and Latin American** countries it was still rising.

✅ The evidence on contraceptive use and the fall in the birth rate between **1978 and 1986**, with the percentage of married women using contraceptives in **1986**, is:

| ✅ Country | ✅ Married women using contraceptives, 1986 | ✅ Fall in the birth rate, 1978-86 |
|---|---|---|
| India | 35 per cent | 4.5 to 3.2 |
| China | 74 per cent | 3.2 to 2.1 |
| Colombia | 65 per cent | 4.3 to 2.6 |
| South Korea | 70 per cent | 3.5 to 1.6 |
| Kenya | under 20 per cent | 4.6, constant |
| Pakistan | under 20 per cent | 4.6, constant |

✅ The regional picture recorded for **1986**, against the growth rates of **1950-9**, is:

| ✅ Region | ✅ 1986 population, millions | ✅ Annual growth 1950-9 | ✅ Annual growth 1980-5 | ✅ Density per sq km, 1986 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| North America | 266 | 1.75 | 0.9 | 12 |
| Europe | 493 | 0.75 | 0.3 | 100 |
| USSR | 281 | 1.4 | 1.0 | 13 |
| Oceania | 25 | 2.4 | 1.5 | 3 |
| Africa | 572 | 1.9 | 2.9 | 19 |
| Latin America | 414 | 2.4 | 2.3 | 20 |
| East Asia | 1,264 | 1.5 | 1.2 | 105 |
| South Asia | 1,601 | 2.2 | 2.2 | 101 |
| World total | 4,916 | 1.7 | 1.5 | 36 |

⚠️ Read the second table row by row and one fact leaps out: ✅ **Africa is the only region whose growth rate rose between the two periods**, from 1.9 to 2.9 per cent, while every other region fell or held steady. ✅ The account confirms it: the most rapid growth rate in 1986 was in Africa, where some countries had rates of over 3 per cent a year.

⚠️ And read the density column against the growth column, because they answer different questions. ✅ Europe had a density of 100 per square kilometre against Africa's 19, yet Europe's growth was 0.3 per cent and Africa's 2.9. ✅ The account draws the conclusion explicitly: overcrowding was not so serious in the developed nations of Europe, which had the prosperity and resources to support their populations, but in the poorer nations of Asia it meant grinding poverty. ✅ **Bangladesh** was probably the world's most crowded country with an average of **700** people to every square kilometre, ✅ and the comparison with Britain is stark - at the growth rates then prevailing, Bangladesh would double its population of **125 million** in less than **30 years** while Britain's **58.6 million** would take **385 years**.

⚠️ That pair of numbers - thirty years against three hundred and eighty-five - is the single most quotable comparison in the population chapter, and it makes the point that density and growth are independent variables far better than any explanation.

✅ ⚠️ The **Population Institute** predicted in **December 1995** that, with effective birth control, the global population could stabilise by **2015** at about **8 billion**, but that without effective promotion of family planning the total could well have reached **14 billion** by **2050**; ✅ and with Europe and North America growing so slowly, an ever-increasing proportion of the world's population would be poor.

⚠️ Cite that projection with its date and its author, as with every other forecast in this session. ❌ It is evidence of what was expected in 1995, not of what happened.

### 5. The dissent, and why it matters

✅ **Fact.** Some historians feel that the fears about the population explosion have been exaggerated. ✅ **Paul Johnson**, for example, believes that there is no need to panic: once Asia, Latin America and Africa become more successfully industrialised, living standards will rise, and this economic betterment, along with more effective use of contraception, will slow down the birth rate. ✅ According to Johnson, the example of China is most encouraging: "The most important news during the 1980s, perhaps, was that the population of China appeared virtually to have stabilised."

⚠️ Johnson's position is the welfare-route argument in its purest form, and it has a specific implication for policy: if development itself reduces fertility, then the most effective population policy is an economic policy. ⚠️ Note also the evidential problem it raises, which Lesson 18's discipline requires us to state. Johnson cites China's stabilisation as support for the development route - but China's stabilisation coincided with the most coercive policy in the record. ⚠️ The same outcome is therefore claimed by both routes, which is precisely why the correlation table above cannot settle the argument on its own.

### 6. China, and the coercion recorded by its own historians

✅ **Fact.** In **1978** a group of scientists calculated that unless Chinese women were limited to one child each, China would face disaster - the country's resources would simply not be sufficient to feed the population; ✅ conversely, if the one-woman-one-child limit could be achieved, then the Chinese would become prosperous and assume their rightful place among the world's leading nations. ✅ In **1980** the government duly announced the **one-child policy**.

✅ The historian **Matthew Connelly** describes what happened next: ✅ "This was the most coercive phase in the whole history of China's one-child policy... All women with one child were to be inserted with a stainless steel, tamper-resistant IUD, all parents with two or more children were to be sterilized, and all unauthorized pregnancies terminated. There was not even a pro forma injunction to avoid coercion... In **1983** more than **16 million** women and more than **4 million** men were sterilized in China, nearly **18 million** women were inserted with IUDs, and over **14 million** underwent abortions."

✅ There was widespread criticism of this policy **in China itself**: ✅ the **All-China Women's Federation** demanded an end to "infanticide and the abuse of women". ✅ There was outrage among Roman Catholics and pro-life supporters around the world, especially in the USA. ✅ Eventually the Chinese government softened the policy, but claimed that it had been successful and was therefore justified. ✅ Now that China's population has stabilised and the birth rate is even falling, this means that there are fewer people to share the available resources, so standards of living should rise and poverty should be reduced.

⚠️ **Fact established by the declared verification pass.** The policy was formally ended when a universal two-child policy was announced in **October 2015** and took effect from **2016**.

⚠️ Four points of handling, and every one of them is an examinable judgement.

First, ⚠️ **attribute the figures**. The 1983 numbers are Connelly's, quoted by the account, and they should travel with his name. ❌ They are not rounded, totalled or generalised to other years anywhere in this session.

Second, ⚠️ **note where the domestic criticism came from**. ✅ The All-China Women's Federation is a body within the system, and its demand for an end to infanticide and the abuse of women is therefore not external commentary but internal dissent - which is much stronger evidence.

Third, ⚠️ **state the justification as the government stated it**, because a fair answer must. ✅ The claim is that the policy was successful and was therefore justified, and that a stabilised population means fewer people sharing available resources, so living standards should rise and poverty fall. ⚠️ That is a consequentialist argument and it should be named as one before it is assessed.

Fourth, ⚠️ **assess it on its own terms**. ⚠️ Even if the consequences claimed are granted, two objections survive. The methods were applied to individuals who had not consented and could not refuse, which is a cost borne by identifiable people for a benefit accruing to an aggregate. And the counterfactual is unproven: the same period saw fertility fall substantially in South Korea, from 3.5 to 1.6 between 1978 and 1986 with 70 per cent contraceptive use, and in Colombia from 4.3 to 2.6, neither by coercion. ⚠️ The existence of those comparators is the strongest single argument against the necessity claim, and it comes from the account's own table.

> ⚠️ **Trap recorded by the advanced owner and worth repeating.** ❌ Do not discuss population policy only numerically. The syllabus expects the **human consequences** as well, and an answer that reports the 1983 figures without addressing consent has described a programme rather than assessed it.

⚠️ **Fact established by the declared verification pass.** The **International Conference on Population and Development**, held in **Cairo in 1994**, marked the shift in international population policy away from demographic targets towards reproductive health and rights, women's education and development. ⚠️ Use this in one sentence, as the institutional expression of the welfare route's victory in the international debate, and hand the contemporary policy detail to the owners that hold it.

### 7. The consumption argument, which answers the numbers argument

⚠️ This is the most sophisticated material in the population chapter, and an answer that reaches it stands out.

✅ **Connelly's objection.** Although stabilisation is a great achievement, it does not solve the problems facing the ecosystem: ✅ "If Asians have only 2.1 children, but also air conditioning and automobiles, they will have a greater impact on the global ecosystem than a billion more subsistence farmers... [because] they tend to consume more of everything per capita, whether fuel, or water, or wide open spaces."

✅ **The corroboration.** A joint report by a group of scientists from **105 institutions**, published shortly before the Earth Summit conference of **2012**, confirmed that one of the main causes of the rapid rise in consumption was "the growing middle class in developed countries and the very lavish lifestyles of the very rich across the planet".

✅ **Ehrlich.** The American biologist **Paul Ehrlich** put it this way: "The current redistribution of wealth from poor to rich must be halted, and overconsumption by the rich must be controlled with programs such as those that transformed consumption patterns in the United States when it entered World War II."

✅ **Birara.** The former World Bank economist **Aklog Birara** suggested that the world can no longer afford to follow the same economic and social model of insatiable demand and concentration of consumption and wealth in a few hands: ✅ "I cannot imagine that the rest of the world would tolerate continuation of 20 per cent of humanity consuming 80 per cent of the world's goods and services, while one-fifth of the poorest consume only 1.3 per cent."

⚠️ Set out what these four items establish, because it is precise. ⚠️ The variable that determines environmental impact is **population multiplied by consumption per head**, not population alone. ⚠️ It follows that a policy aimed only at the first term addresses only part of the quantity it claims to control, and that a fertility decline accompanied by rising consumption per head can leave total impact unchanged or higher.

⚠️ This also reframes the entire population debate in a way that connects it to the rest of this session. ⚠️ The food comparison of Lesson 10 - 70 per cent of the world's population consuming 30 per cent of the world's food while the USA, with perhaps 8 per cent of the population, ate 40 per cent - is the same argument made in 1980 with different figures. ⚠️ And Lesson 17's account of resource exhaustion attributed the depletion to the industrialised North's growth model, not to the numbers of people in poorer regions.

⚠️ The formulation to carry: **the population question and the consumption question are one question asked from opposite ends, and any answer that treats them separately has halved its own argument.**

✅ ⚠️ The account's own closing position is balanced and should be reproduced: as the world population reached 7 billion at the end of 2011, the majority view was still that efforts to reduce population growth in areas like Africa must not be relaxed, with greater efforts to provide contraceptives to everybody in the developing world who wants them and greater use of the internet to spread information about methods.

⚠️ Note the formulation of that last recommendation - contraceptives "to everybody in the developing world who **wants** them". ⚠️ The word carries the entire ethical distinction between the two routes, and a candidate who notices it has understood the lesson.

### Criticism and reply: does the contraceptive table prove that supply drives fertility?

**The objection.** ⚠️ The table appears decisive: 74 per cent contraceptive use in China with a birth rate falling from 3.2 to 2.1, 70 per cent in South Korea with 3.5 to 1.6, against under 20 per cent in Kenya and Pakistan with 4.6 unchanged. Surely the conclusion is that supplying contraceptives reduces fertility.

**The reply.** ⚠️ The correlation is real and the causal reading is plausible, but it cannot be read in one direction only, and this is the session's correlation-and-causation discipline in its sharpest form. ⚠️ Contraceptive use is both a cause of lower fertility and a **measure of the desire for it**: a married woman using contraception has already decided to limit her family, so the 74 and 70 per cent figures record demand as well as supply. ⚠️ Three further complications appear in the evidence. Paul Johnson argues that industrialisation and rising living standards will slow the birth rate independently. The Chinese case pairs the highest recorded use with the most coercive enforcement, so its 74 per cent is not a pure measure of voluntary demand. And the account itself names ignorance and expense as constraints, which implies that supply matters at the margin. ⚠️ The disciplined conclusion is that **supply is necessary and not sufficient**: where the desire to limit families exists, availability converts it into practice, and where it does not, availability alone does little - which is exactly why India's and Pakistan's clinics moved slowly while their publicity campaigns ran.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ World history from the eighteenth century onward. ⚠️ The chapter's own recorded demand is exactly this lesson: what methods were used to try to control population growth in the second half of the twentieth century, and why did some of them arouse criticism?

**Verified question linkage.** ⚠️ The population-policy and empowerment questions in the verified ledgers held in this repository are routed to the Indian-society and geography owners; the complete audit appears in the final verified-question section. ⚠️ What this session supplies to such answers is the international history: the routes, the record of each, and the ethical argument.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Always as a two-route comparison with an ethical verdict. ⚠️ The structure that scores names both routes, gives the evidence on each, states the Chinese case with its figures attributed, reports the domestic criticism, sets out the government's own justification, and then assesses it - closing with the consumption argument, which very few candidates reach.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Discussing population policy only numerically. ⚠️ The syllabus expects the human consequences.
2. ⚠️ Treating the 1983 figures as unowned facts. ✅ They are Matthew Connelly's, quoted by the account.
3. ⚠️ Presenting the Chinese policy as simply effective or simply wrong. ✅ Both its claimed results and its recorded coercion are in the evidence, along with internal Chinese criticism.
4. ⚠️ Reading the contraceptive table as one-directional proof. ⚠️ Use is both a cause of lower fertility and a measure of the desire for it.
5. ⚠️ Omitting the consumption argument. ✅ Connelly, the 105-institution report, Ehrlich and Birara all place consumption per head alongside numbers.
6. ⚠️ Quoting the 1995 Population Institute projection as an outcome. ⚠️ It is a dated forecast: stabilisation at about 8 billion by 2015 with effective birth control, or 14 billion by 2050 without.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Scientific concern began soon after the First World War; the first World Population Congress met in Geneva in 1925, and an International Union for the Scientific Study of Population was set up in Paris in 1926, doing valuable work collecting statistics and encouraging better data systems.
2. ✅ A Population Commission was included among the UN's agencies in 1945; when the explosion began in the 1950s the UN took the lead in encouraging birth-control programmes.
3. ✅ India and Pakistan set up family-planning clinics advising on methods and providing cheap contraceptives, with publicity campaigns recommending a maximum of three children; many African governments recommended the same maximum; China fixed a legal maximum of two.
4. ✅ Progress was very slow because ancient practices and attitudes were difficult to change, especially in India and Pakistan, and the Church continued to forbid artificial birth control in Roman Catholic South America.
5. ✅ By the 1980s growth rates were beginning to fall slightly in parts of Asia but were still rising in many African and Latin American countries.
6. ✅ Contraceptive use in 1986 and the birth-rate fall 1978-86: India 35 per cent, 4.5 to 3.2; China 74 per cent, 3.2 to 2.1; Colombia 65 per cent, 4.3 to 2.6; South Korea 70 per cent, 3.5 to 1.6; Kenya and Pakistan both under 20 per cent, 4.6 constant.
7. ✅ The 1986 regional table shows Africa as the only region whose growth rate rose between 1950-9 and 1980-5, from 1.9 to 2.9 per cent, with the world total falling from 1.7 to 1.5.
8. ✅ Density and growth are independent: Europe at 100 per square kilometre grew 0.3 per cent while Africa at 19 grew 2.9; Bangladesh, at about 700 per square kilometre, would double its 125 million in under 30 years while Britain's 58.6 million would take 385 years.
9. ✅ The Population Institute predicted in December 1995 that effective birth control could stabilise the world at about 8 billion by 2015, and that without it the total could reach 14 billion by 2050.
10. ✅ Paul Johnson argued the fears were exaggerated: industrialisation would raise living standards and, with more effective contraception, slow the birth rate; he cited China's apparent stabilisation in the 1980s.
11. ✅ In 1978 Chinese scientists calculated that without a one-child limit the country would face disaster; the one-child policy was announced in 1980.
12. ✅ Matthew Connelly records the most coercive phase: IUDs for all women with one child, sterilisation for all parents with two or more, termination of all unauthorised pregnancies, and no pro forma injunction against coercion; in 1983 more than 16 million women and more than 4 million men were sterilized, nearly 18 million women received IUDs and over 14 million underwent abortions.
13. ✅ The All-China Women's Federation demanded an end to infanticide and the abuse of women; there was outrage among Roman Catholics and pro-life supporters abroad; the government eventually softened the policy while claiming it had been successful and was therefore justified.
14. ⚠️ The policy was formally ended with a universal two-child policy announced in October 2015, effective from 2016; the Cairo conference of 1994 had already shifted international policy towards reproductive health and rights.
15. ✅ Connelly's consumption objection: Asians with 2.1 children plus air conditioning and automobiles would have a greater impact on the ecosystem than a billion more subsistence farmers, because they consume more of everything per capita.
16. ✅ A report by scientists from 105 institutions before the 2012 Earth Summit blamed the growing middle class in developed countries and the lavish lifestyles of the very rich; Ehrlich called for the redistribution from poor to rich to be halted and overconsumption controlled; Birara said the world could not tolerate 20 per cent of humanity consuming 80 per cent of goods and services while the poorest fifth consumed 1.3 per cent.
17. ⚠️ Master formulation: impact is population multiplied by consumption per head, so the population question and the consumption question are one question asked from opposite ends.
18. ✅ The majority view at 7 billion was that efforts in areas like Africa must not be relaxed, with contraceptives for everybody in the developing world who wants them - the word "wants" carrying the whole ethical distinction between the routes.

### Concept check

**Question:** Set out the strongest case for and the strongest case against the Chinese one-child policy using only the evidence in this session, and state a reasoned verdict.

**Model answer:** The case for it is consequentialist and is the government's own. In 1978 scientists calculated that unless Chinese women were limited to one child each the country's resources would simply not be sufficient to feed the population, whereas if the limit were achieved the Chinese would become prosperous; the policy was announced in 1980; the government, having eventually softened it, claimed it had been successful and was therefore justified, since a stabilised population means fewer people sharing available resources, so living standards should rise and poverty fall. Paul Johnson, writing from outside, treated China's apparent stabilisation in the 1980s as the most encouraging news of the decade. The case against has three independent parts. First, the methods: Matthew Connelly records that all women with one child were to receive an IUD, all parents with two or more to be sterilized and all unauthorized pregnancies terminated, with not even a pro forma injunction to avoid coercion, and that in 1983 more than 16 million women and over 4 million men were sterilized, nearly 18 million women received IUDs and over 14 million underwent abortions. Second, the criticism came from inside the system: the All-China Women's Federation demanded an end to infanticide and the abuse of women. Third, the necessity claim is unproven, because the account's own table shows South Korea's birth rate falling from 3.5 to 1.6 and Colombia's from 4.3 to 2.6 between 1978 and 1986 with contraceptive use of 70 and 65 per cent and no comparable coercion. The reasoned verdict follows from the third point: even granting the demographic result, the existence of voluntary routes achieving comparable falls in the same years removes the argument from necessity, and a benefit accruing to an aggregate cannot justify costs imposed on identifiable individuals who could not refuse.

**Misconception to avoid:** Settling the question by citing the demographic outcome alone. The policy's own defenders and its critics agree that births fell; they disagree about whether that result required these means and whether the result can justify them. An answer that reports the fall and concludes that the policy worked has answered a demographic question when an ethical one was asked - which is exactly the error the syllabus's emphasis on the effect of policies on society is designed to catch.

---
## Lesson 21 - HIV/AIDS as a development shock

Progress: 21/22 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the epidemic's early course and its arrival in the poorest countries; the different epidemics in China, Russia and Ukraine; southern Africa's scale, life-expectancy collapse and orphans; the economic effects on labour and households; why the epidemic was worse in poor conditions; the responses from Uganda's ABC campaign to international funds; and why health and development are one subject

The chapter's internal numbering of this section is inconsistent and the point is recorded in the final ledger

CA found: none carried for this lesson

### Visual: one virus, several epidemics, and the economic transmission
