⚠️ The last box is the entire reason this lesson sits inside an economics topic rather than beside it.

### 1. The learner's problem: why an epidemic belongs in economic history

⚠️ Because it is the cleanest natural experiment the century offers on the relationship between health and development. ⚠️ It removed a specific age group - working-age adults - from specific economies, and the consequences can be traced through labour supply, food production, household structure and public expenditure. ⚠️ It also arrived precisely where Lesson 19 left the healthcare systems: collapsing, underfunded and in some cases spending 40 cents a head a year.

⚠️ And it makes an argument that a purely economic history cannot: **development gains are reversible without war.** That is the sentence to carry.

### 2. The early course, and the reason for the delay

✅ **Fact.** In the early **1980s** AIDS - Acquired Immune Deficiency Syndrome - was thought to be a disease that mainly affected homosexual men, and some people called it the "gay plague". ✅ Another group which contracted the disease were people who used unsterilized syringes to inject themselves with drugs. ✅ At first it was in the wealthy countries of the West, particularly the USA, that most cases were reported, ✅ but after governments had launched campaigns about sexual health and the use of condoms to prevent the transmission of HIV - Human Immunodeficiency Virus - the outbreaks seemed to have been brought under control. ✅ The widespread use of **anti-retroviral (ARV)** drug therapy slowed down the development of the virus and enabled people to live much longer.

✅ It was something of a shock when, during the **1990s**, the world became aware that the disease had spread to the poorest countries in the world, and that in Africa it had reached epidemic proportions. ✅ Scientists now know that it takes an average of **eight to ten years** for HIV infection to develop into full-blown AIDS, which is why the virus was able to spread so widely before it was recognised.

⚠️ That incubation period is the epidemiological fact with the largest economic consequence, and it deserves a sentence of analysis. A disease whose symptoms appear a decade after infection is invisible to a public-health system for a decade. ⚠️ By the time the case numbers appear, the infections that will produce the next decade's cases have already occurred. ⚠️ No policy response, however fast, can act on what has already happened - which is why early warning, and the suppression of early warning, matter so much in the next section.

### 3. Several epidemics, not one

✅ **Fact.** The epidemic also spread to **India, China** and the countries of the former **USSR**. ✅ **Tony Barnett and Alan Whiteside**, in *AIDS in the 21st Century* (**2002**), showed how each epidemic was different.

✅ In **China** the main causes were contaminated needles and the practice of selling blood at state-run blood collection points in the early 1990s. ✅ The **World Health Organization** estimated that **two-thirds** of injections given in China were unsafe and that much of the collected blood plasma was infected. ✅ When the symptoms of AIDS began to appear, local officials tried to suppress the news. ✅ It was only in **2003** that the government admitted publicly that over **a million** of its citizens were HIV-positive; the infection was increasing by **30 per cent** a year and **10 million** could be affected by 2010.

✅ In **Russia and Ukraine** the highest rates were among injecting drug-users, especially those in prison. ✅ Experts calculate that once HIV enters the general population and infects around **5 per cent** of adults, a general epidemic is likely to follow, as it has in southern Africa.

⚠️ Three analytical points follow.

First, ⚠️ **"the AIDS epidemic" is a misleading singular**. The transmission route differed by country - blood products and injections in China, injecting drug use in Russia and Ukraine, unprotected heterosexual sex in southern Africa - and the policy response that works depends on which route dominates. ⚠️ An answer that names the different routes has demonstrated exactly the comparative precision this topic rewards.

Second, ⚠️ **the suppression of information is itself an economic decision with consequences**, and the Chinese case records it plainly: officials tried to suppress the news, and public admission came only in 2003. ⚠️ Combined with the eight-to-ten-year incubation, concealment converts a manageable problem into an unmanageable one.

Third, ⚠️ **the 5 per cent threshold is a genuine threshold effect**, and it is worth using because thresholds are rare in this topic. Below it, an epidemic is confined to identifiable groups and can be addressed with targeted measures; above it, transmission becomes general and the measures must be general too - which is far more expensive, and expense is precisely what the affected states lacked.

### 4. Southern Africa: scale, and the collapse of a development indicator

✅ **Fact.** The first cases to be reported in Africa were in a fishing village in south-west **Uganda** in the mid-**1980s**. ✅ The HIV virus spread rapidly, transmitted mainly by unprotected heterosexual sex. ✅ Governments were slow to realise the significance of what was happening and aid agencies made no provision for dealing with the disease in their assistance programmes.

✅ In **2001** a report by the **International Crisis Group** sounded alarm bells, saying that the impact of HIV on Africa was as though it was involved in a major war; the report concentrated on **Botswana**, but warned that the impact on Africa as a whole was likely to be devastating within a few years if nothing was done. ✅ The report was not exaggerating: in **2001**, **3 million** people died from the disease in Africa and **5 million** became infected. ✅ By **2003** it was estimated that **29.4 million** people were living with HIV or AIDS in Africa - about **70 per cent** of the global total - and a further **3 million** died from the virus in Africa during 2003.

✅ Prevalence levels had risen to horrifying proportions: ✅ in **Botswana and Swaziland**, almost **40 per cent** of adults were living with the virus or with full-blown AIDS, and the percentage was almost as high in **Zimbabwe**; ✅ in **South Africa** the prevalence level was **25 per cent**.

✅ **Life expectancy** in southern Africa, which had reached the **sixties** by **1990**, had fallen again to the **lower forties**; ✅ in **Zimbabwe** it was down to **33**.

✅ One of the tragic side effects was the huge number of children left without parents: ✅ in **Uganda** there were over **a million** orphans, and the **WHO** estimated that by **2010** there were likely to be **20 million** AIDS orphans in Africa.

⚠️ The life-expectancy series is the most important evidence in this lesson and it should be quoted in exactly that form: **from the sixties by 1990 to the lower forties, and to 33 in Zimbabwe**. ⚠️ Life expectancy is the headline indicator of human development, and this is a record of it being driven backwards by roughly two decades within a single generation, without war, famine or natural disaster. ⚠️ No other evidence in this session demonstrates as sharply that development gains are reversible.

### 5. The economic transmission, stated as a mechanism

✅ **Fact.** There were economic effects too: ✅ a substantial proportion of the labour force was being lost, with all its skills and experience. ✅ This was being felt especially in **farming and food production**, ✅ while the deaths of so many young women was an irreplaceable loss to the domestic economy and to child-rearing. ✅ At the same time there was an increased demand for people to nurse the sick and care for orphaned children.

⚠️ Set that out as a four-step mechanism, because the steps are separable and each is examinable.

1. ⚠️ **The cohort removed is the productive cohort.** Unlike most mortality shocks, which fall on the very young or the very old, this one concentrated on working-age adults - the group that produces output, pays taxes, raises children and holds accumulated skill.
2. ⚠️ **Skill is destroyed, not merely labour.** The account's phrase is "with all its skills and experience". A farmer's knowledge of a particular soil, a teacher's training, a nurse's experience - none of these transfers automatically to a replacement.
3. ⚠️ **The sectoral incidence falls on food.** Loss concentrated in farming and food production means the shock hits subsistence directly, in economies already facing the food constraints of Lesson 19.
4. ⚠️ **The survivors are diverted.** Increased demand for people to nurse the sick and care for orphans withdraws further labour from production into care - which is unpaid, largely female and invisible in output statistics.

⚠️ Add the demographic consequence and the picture completes itself: ✅ with up to 20 million orphans projected for Africa by 2010, a cohort was growing up without parental income, without inherited skill and often without schooling. ⚠️ That is a shock transmitted forward across generations, not a shock confined to one.

### 6. Why the epidemic was worse in poverty

✅ **Fact.** HIV was able to spread more quickly in conditions of poverty, where there was very little access to information and education about the virus and how to prevent it spreading. ✅ Widespread hunger reduced resistance to the disease and accelerated the progress from HIV to AIDS. ✅ Nor were any of the expensive anti-retroviral drugs available for Africans. ✅ The large number of civil wars in Africa produced thousands of refugees, who were often cut off from their normal healthcare services, and in such emergencies there was a greater danger of the virus being spread through contaminated blood. ✅ Most African governments took a long time to acknowledge what was happening, partly because of the stigma attached to the disease - the belief that it was caused by homosexual sex and the general reluctance to discuss sexual habits. ✅ **South Africa** itself was one of the slowest to take action, mainly because President **Mbeki** refused to accept the link between HIV and AIDS.

⚠️ Sort those six reasons, because they are of three different kinds and an answer that distinguishes them demonstrates analytical control.

- ⚠️ **Material constraints:** no access to information or education; no ARV drugs.
- ⚠️ **Compounding conditions:** hunger reducing resistance and accelerating progression; civil war producing refugees cut off from healthcare and exposed to contaminated blood.
- ⚠️ **Political and cultural constraints:** stigma, reluctance to discuss sexual habits, delayed governmental acknowledgement, and one head of state's refusal to accept the causal link.

⚠️ The third category is the one that matters for the argument of Lesson 22, because it is not a resource constraint at all. ⚠️ Uganda, a poor country, responded early and successfully; South Africa, a far richer one, responded late. ⚠️ The difference is not money.

### 7. What was done, and what it achieved

✅ **Fact.** The experts know what needs to be done: people must be persuaded to have safe sex and use condoms, and governments must be able to provide cheap ARV treatment. ✅ **Brazil** is one country where the campaign has slowed down the spread of the disease.

✅ In Africa, governments concentrated on the so-called **ABC** message: "Abstain from sex, Be faithful to one partner, and if you cannot, use a Condom."

✅ **Uganda provides the great African success story.** ✅ The government admitted to the WHO in **1986** that they had some AIDS cases, and President **Museveni** personally took charge of the campaign, travelling from village to village to talk about the problem and what should be done. ✅ Uganda was the first country in Africa to launch the ABC campaign and provide cheap condoms for its people; ✅ people were encouraged to come forward voluntarily for testing; ✅ the programme was financed jointly by the government, by aid agencies and by religious organisations and churches. ✅ Uganda's meagre resources were strained to the limits, but the campaign worked, even though very few people had access to ARV drugs: ✅ Uganda's HIV prevalence rate had peaked at **20 per cent** in **1991**, but by the end of **2003** it had fallen to about **5 per cent**. ✅ The epidemic had passed its acute stage, but the problem of orphaned children was just reaching its height.

✅ Elsewhere in Africa and in China, governments were slow off the mark and the epidemic took a firmer hold, reaching crisis proportions in 2003. ✅ Some African countries began to follow Uganda's example: in **Malawi**, President **Muluzi** set up an AIDS commission and appointed a special minister.

✅ **The three-pronged attack** that huge sums were needed to finance across southern Africa was: ✅ ABC campaigns or some equivalent; ✅ anti-retroviral drugs, much cheaper since pharmaceutical companies gave way to political pressure and allowed drugs to be supplied more cheaply to poorer countries; ✅ and healthcare systems and infrastructures, which in most poor states needed modernising to cope with the magnitude of the problem, with more doctors and nurses required.

✅ **The international agencies** are the UN's **Global Fund to Fight AIDS, TB and Malaria**, the **World Health Organization** and **UNAIDS**. ✅ In **December 2003**, UN Secretary-General **Kofi Annan** complained that he was "angry, distressed and helpless"; ✅ 1 December was World AIDS Day, but the outlook was bleak - reports from all over the Third World showed that the war against the disease was being lost, the virus was still spreading and **40 million** people were living with HIV. ✅ The UN Fund said it would need **£7 billion** by 2005 and the WHO wanted **£4 billion**. ✅ Many wealthy countries gave generously - the USA, for example, promised **$15 billion** over five years, but insisted that the money be spent in the way it specified, the Bush administration favouring programmes which promoted abstinence over those advocating the use of condoms. ✅ The Roman Catholic Church also continued to oppose the use of condoms, even though scientists had shown it to be the best means of prevention available. ✅ Annan's judgement was that the war was not being won "because I don't think the leaders of the world are engaged enough".

✅ **The position recorded for 2012.** ✅ By 2012 well over **30 million** people had died from AIDS since the first cases were identified in **1981**. ✅ An estimated **1.8 million** of them died in **2010** alone, two-thirds of them in southern Africa, where nearly **15 million** children were left orphaned; ✅ in the same year around **2.7 million** people became infected with HIV. ✅ According to the WHO, attempts to control the epidemic have been intensified: from **2002 to 2008** spending on the campaign in low- and middle-income countries increased **sixfold**, and since 2008 the level has at least been maintained. ✅ In **May 2012** the WHO published a plan of priority action for the next two years, focusing on prevention, encouraging regular testing for those at risk, providing wider and cheaper access to ARV drugs and modernising healthcare systems, especially in southern Africa. ✅ There were some encouraging signs: more people than ever before were receiving ARV treatment, the annual number of AIDS deaths had declined and the global percentage of people infected seemed to have stabilised. ✅ However, the UN agencies warned that recent achievements should not lead to complacency: in eastern Europe infection rates were still rising, and in the USA in **June 2012** more than a million people were living with HIV, but probably **20 per cent** of them did not know they were infected.

⚠️ Two observations about the Ugandan case, because it is the most instructive in the lesson.

First, ⚠️ it succeeded **without** the most expensive instrument. ✅ Prevalence fell from 20 per cent in 1991 to about 5 per cent by the end of 2003 even though very few people had access to ARV drugs. ⚠️ What it used instead were early acknowledgement, political leadership at the highest level, voluntary testing, cheap condoms and a financing coalition that included religious organisations - which is to say, a set of instruments a poor state could actually deploy.

Second, ⚠️ the sequencing of its success and its costs is important and is recorded: ✅ the epidemic had passed its acute stage while the problem of orphaned children was just reaching its height. ⚠️ A demographic shock's costs arrive years after its epidemiological peak, which is a general finding about this kind of event.

⚠️ And note the conditionality attached to international money. ✅ The USA promised $15 billion over five years but insisted the money be spent in the way it specified, favouring abstinence over condoms. ⚠️ That is the same conditionality structure as the tied aid of Lesson 11 and the Fund conditions of Lesson 15, appearing here in a public-health form - and its content contradicted what ✅ scientists had shown to be the best means of prevention available.

### Criticism and reply: is this section a health topic in a history session?

**The objection.** ⚠️ Prevalence rates, incubation periods and drug therapies are epidemiology. A world-history answer that recites them has changed subject.

**The reply.** ⚠️ It would have, if the material stopped at the epidemiology. What makes it history is the economic and social transmission, which the account states directly: a substantial proportion of the labour force lost with all its skills and experience, the loss concentrated in farming and food production, the deaths of young women an irreplaceable loss to the domestic economy and to child-rearing, and an increased demand for carers withdrawing further labour. ⚠️ Add the reversal of life expectancy from the sixties in 1990 to the lower forties, and to 33 in Zimbabwe, and the subject is unmistakably development. ⚠️ The formulation to carry: **this is the clearest single case in the century that health and economic development are the same subject, because a working-age mortality shock removes exactly the cohort that produces output and raises the next generation.**

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ World history from the eighteenth century onward; the account places the epidemic inside its population chapter, which is where its ownership lies for this topic.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ As a dimension of a population answer, as evidence in an answer on development, or as the closing paragraph of an answer on the consequences of population change. ⚠️ Two or three sentences are usually enough, and they should be the life-expectancy reversal, the labour-force mechanism and the Ugandan counter-case.

> **Boundary.** The contemporary epidemiology, current treatment regimes and present-day global health governance belong to other subjects and are not carried here. ❌ No figure in this lesson is updated beyond the dated material recorded above.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating HIV/AIDS as a purely medical subject. ✅ The account places it in the population chapter and states its labour, household and life-expectancy effects.
2. ⚠️ Writing about "the AIDS epidemic" as one phenomenon. ✅ Barnett and Whiteside record different transmission routes in China, in Russia and Ukraine, and in southern Africa.
3. ⚠️ Giving prevalence figures without dates. ✅ Almost 40 per cent of adults in Botswana and Swaziland and 25 per cent in South Africa are 2003 figures.
4. ⚠️ Concluding that only money determined the response. ✅ Uganda succeeded with meagre resources and almost no ARV access; South Africa was among the slowest because its president refused to accept the link.
5. ⚠️ Omitting the conditionality of aid. ✅ The USA promised $15 billion but insisted on its own specification, favouring abstinence over condoms.
6. ⚠️ Treating the orphan problem as a side effect. ✅ It is a forward-transmitted shock: over a million orphans in Uganda, up to 20 million projected for Africa by 2010 and nearly 15 million in southern Africa recorded for 2010.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ In the early 1980s AIDS was thought mainly to affect homosexual men, with a second group among users of unsterilized syringes; most early cases were in wealthy western countries, particularly the USA, and campaigns on sexual health and condoms appeared to bring outbreaks under control.
2. ✅ ARV therapy slowed the virus and enabled people to live much longer; it takes an average of eight to ten years for HIV infection to develop into full-blown AIDS, which is why it spread so widely before recognition.
3. ✅ During the 1990s the world became aware that the disease had spread to the poorest countries and had reached epidemic proportions in Africa; it also spread to India, China and the former USSR.
4. ✅ Barnett and Whiteside (2002) showed each epidemic was different: contaminated needles and the sale of blood at state-run collection points in China in the early 1990s, with the WHO estimating two-thirds of injections unsafe and much plasma infected; injecting drug users, especially in prison, in Russia and Ukraine.
5. ✅ Chinese officials tried to suppress the news; only in 2003 did the government admit over a million citizens were HIV-positive, with infection rising 30 per cent a year and 10 million possibly affected by 2010.
6. ✅ Once HIV enters the general population and infects around 5 per cent of adults, a general epidemic is likely to follow.
7. ✅ The first African cases were in a fishing village in south-west Uganda in the mid-1980s, spread mainly by unprotected heterosexual sex; governments were slow to realise and aid agencies made no provision.
8. ✅ An International Crisis Group report in 2001 said the impact on Africa was as though it was involved in a major war; in 2001, 3 million died in Africa and 5 million became infected.
9. ✅ By 2003 an estimated 29.4 million people were living with HIV or AIDS in Africa, about 70 per cent of the global total, with a further 3 million African deaths that year.
10. ✅ Prevalence in 2003: almost 40 per cent of adults in Botswana and Swaziland, almost as high in Zimbabwe, and 25 per cent in South Africa.
11. ✅ Life expectancy in southern Africa had reached the sixties by 1990 and fell to the lower forties, and in Zimbabwe to 33.
12. ✅ Orphans: over a million in Uganda; the WHO estimated 20 million AIDS orphans in Africa by 2010; nearly 15 million children orphaned in southern Africa recorded for 2010.
13. ✅ Economic effects: a substantial proportion of the labour force lost with all its skills and experience, especially in farming and food production; the deaths of so many young women an irreplaceable loss to the domestic economy and child-rearing; increased demand for people to nurse the sick and care for orphans.
14. ✅ Why it was worse in poverty: little access to information and education; hunger reducing resistance and accelerating progression; no access to expensive ARV drugs; civil wars producing refugees cut off from healthcare and exposed to contaminated blood; stigma and reluctance to discuss sexual habits delaying acknowledgement; and President Mbeki's refusal to accept the link between HIV and AIDS in South Africa.
15. ✅ Responses: the ABC message; Brazil's campaign slowing the spread; Uganda's admission to the WHO in 1986, President Museveni's personal village-to-village campaign, the first African ABC campaign with cheap condoms, voluntary testing and joint financing by government, aid agencies and religious organisations, cutting prevalence from a 20 per cent peak in 1991 to about 5 per cent by end-2003 with very few ARVs; Malawi's AIDS commission and special minister under President Muluzi.
16. ✅ The three-pronged attack required: ABC campaigns, cheaper ARV drugs after pharmaceutical companies gave way to political pressure, and modernised healthcare systems with more doctors and nurses.
17. ✅ International agencies: the UN's Global Fund to Fight AIDS, TB and Malaria; the WHO; UNAIDS. In December 2003 Kofi Annan was "angry, distressed and helpless" with 40 million living with HIV, the UN Fund needing £7 billion by 2005 and the WHO £4 billion; the USA promised $15 billion over five years but specified how it was to be spent, favouring abstinence over condoms.
18. ✅ By 2012 well over 30 million had died since 1981; an estimated 1.8 million died in 2010, two-thirds in southern Africa, with around 2.7 million newly infected that year; spending in low- and middle-income countries rose sixfold from 2002 to 2008; the WHO's May 2012 plan focused on prevention, testing, wider cheap ARV access and healthcare modernisation; deaths had declined and the global infected percentage seemed to have stabilised, but rates were still rising in eastern Europe and about 20 per cent of the more than a million Americans living with HIV in June 2012 did not know it.
19. ⚠️ Master formulation: a working-age mortality shock removes exactly the cohort that produces output and raises the next generation, which is why health and development are one subject.

### Concept check

**Question:** Uganda was poorer than South Africa and had almost no access to antiretroviral drugs, yet its epidemic was brought under control far sooner. Explain what that comparison establishes.

**Model answer:** It establishes that the decisive variable in the early response was political rather than financial. Uganda admitted to the WHO in 1986 that it had AIDS cases, President Museveni personally took charge and travelled from village to village to discuss the problem, the country became the first in Africa to launch the ABC campaign and to provide cheap condoms, people were encouraged to come forward voluntarily for testing, and the programme was financed jointly by the government, aid agencies and religious organisations. Its resources were strained to the limits and very few people had access to ARV drugs, yet prevalence fell from a peak of 20 per cent in 1991 to about 5 per cent by the end of 2003. South Africa, a far richer country, was among the slowest to act, mainly because President Mbeki refused to accept the link between HIV and AIDS, and its prevalence stood at 25 per cent in 2003. The account records the general form of the same variable elsewhere: most African governments took a long time to acknowledge what was happening, partly because of the stigma attached to the disease and the general reluctance to discuss sexual habits. The comparison therefore separates three things usually run together - resources, information and political acknowledgement - and shows that acknowledgement had to come first, because the incubation of eight to ten years means every year of delay commits the following decade's caseload.

**Misconception to avoid:** Generalising from Uganda that money did not matter. It mattered greatly for everything except the initial response. The three-pronged attack the account sets out - prevention campaigns, ARV drugs and modernised healthcare systems with more doctors and nurses - required huge sums; the UN Fund said it needed £7 billion by 2005 and the WHO £4 billion; and the states worst affected were the same ones recorded in the previous lesson as spending 40 and 70 cents a head a year on health. The accurate conclusion is sequential: acknowledgement and prevention could begin without money, and containment, treatment and care could not.

---

## Lesson 22 - Explaining the century: dependency, institutions, inequality and the answer architecture

Progress: 22/22 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the four available explanations and what each can and cannot account for; the correlation-and-causation discipline restated; inequality between and within countries; India and the Global South as evidence; the demand map; mark-scaled structures; qualified theses; verdict scaffolds; and the factual-risk control list

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the canonical owners' directive and demand maps, qualified thesis templates, mark-scaled structures and factual-risk controls, together with the core account's own end-of-chapter questions for the world-economy and population chapters
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 15 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four explanations, and what each can and cannot carry

| Explanation | ✅ The evidence in this session that supports it | ⚠️ What it cannot explain | ⚠️ Best use in an answer |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Structural dependency** - the world economy assigns roles and rewards existing capability | The 1900 division into manufacturers and primary producers; colonial price-fixing at both ends; the one-product economies left from colonial days; the terms-of-trade collapse of 1975-80; UNCTAD's failure; tied and interest-bearing aid; the debt of $500 billion by 1980 | Why four Pacific tigers converged with European Community incomes under the same conditions; why Libya built a welfare state from oil revenue while other producers did not; why Uganda contained an epidemic that richer South Africa did not | As the account of the *terms* every developing economy faced - never as a prediction of any single outcome |
| **Domestic capability and institutions** - what a state did with the terms it faced | Some states industrialised rapidly and with great success; Libya's use of oil profits against the record that governments did not necessarily spend wisely; Uganda's early acknowledgement against South Africa's delay; states spending vastly more per head on defence than on healthcare | Why states with strong domestic effort still faced collapsing commodity prices they did not set; why Indian exports, the rupee and the current account all moved in 2011-12 without any Indian policy failure | As the variable that explains *divergence* within the same external environment |
| **Policy regimes and design** - what governments and international institutions chose | The tariff spiral of the 1930s against Bretton Woods in 1944; the New Deal instruments; the 1971 devaluation and duty; monetarism from 1981; the deregulation of 1999-2000 and the derivatives exemption; Brandt's unimplemented recommendations | Why the same design produced different results in different economies; why crises recurred under opposite regimes | As the explanation of *turning points* - regimes changed at identifiable moments and the moments are datable |
| **Physical and demographic limits** - what the growth model ran into | Resource exhaustion, soil loss, water and phosphate limits; the green revolution's ceiling in the mid-1980s; global warming attributed to industrial emissions; the mortality-driven population rise and the resource diversion it forced | Why limits bound some societies and not others at the same moment; why the same constraint produced famine in one place and a policy debate in another | As the *closing* movement of a 20-mark answer, converting growth and inequality into growth, inequality and limits |

⚠️ Read the second column of every row before writing. Each explanation is defeated by evidence in this session, and the ability to say *which* evidence defeats *which* explanation is the difference between a script that argues and a script that asserts.

### 1. The learner's problem: how to use a theory without being captured by it

⚠️ A theory in an answer is a tool for organising evidence, not a conclusion to be defended. ⚠️ The failure mode is easy to describe: a candidate adopts dependency or institutions as a position, then selects the evidence that fits and omits the rest, producing a script that a well-read examiner can dismantle in one sentence.

⚠️ The method that works is the reverse. Take the strongest single claim of each explanation; find the evidence in this session that defeats it; and then state the synthesis that survives. ⚠️ That produces an answer which has already anticipated its own counter-argument, which is what "critically examine" means.

### 2. What dependency actually claims, and where it holds

⚠️ The claim, stated at its strongest, is that the structure of the world economy assigns roles that reproduce themselves: those who supply manufactures accumulate capability, and those who supply primary products do not, so integration widens rather than narrows the gap.

⚠️ The evidence in this session for that claim is substantial and it should be marshalled in order. ✅ In 1900 a few industrialised countries provided manufactures while the rest supplied primary products, and European nations decided what their colonies produced and fixed the prices at both ends. ✅ Many states could not break away from the one-product economies left from colonial days because their governments lacked the cash to diversify. ✅ The North expected the South to keep providing food and raw materials and did not encourage it to develop its own industries. ✅ A tonne of copper bought 115 barrels of oil in 1975 and 58 in 1980, with cocoa, coffee and cotton falling in the same proportion, while the prices of manufactures continued to rise. ✅ UNCTAD tried to negotiate fairer prices and achieved no real improvement. ✅ Aid carried interest and was sometimes tied to the lender's goods. ✅ By 1980 the Third World owed $500 billion with $50 billion of annual interest, some of it borrowed to pay the interest on earlier loans. ✅ In 1989-90 the GNP of the North averaged over 24 times that of the South.

⚠️ That is a strong case, and an answer should make it before qualifying it.

### 3. The evidence that constrains it

⚠️ Three findings in this session are fatal to dependency in its strong form.

✅ **Convergence happened.** Some Third World states industrialised rapidly and with great success - Singapore, Taiwan, South Korea and Hong Kong, with Thailand, Malaysia, Brazil and Mexico among others - and the GNPs of the four tigers compared favourably with those of many European Community countries. ⚠️ A structure that made development impossible would not have produced them.

✅ **The same windfall produced different results.** The Middle Eastern producers, Nigeria and Libya all made huge profits from oil, and the account states that this did not necessarily mean their governments spent the money wisely or for the benefit of their populations - while Libya used much of the profits on agricultural and industrial development and to set up a welfare state. ⚠️ External revenue was identical in kind; the outcomes were not.

✅ **Domestic allocation mattered independently.** Almost all the states spending least per head on healthcare were spending vastly more per head on defence. ⚠️ No external structure compelled that ratio.

⚠️ And a fourth, from the population half of the session: ✅ Uganda, poorer than South Africa and with almost no ARV access, cut prevalence from 20 per cent in 1991 to about 5 per cent by the end of 2003, while South Africa was among the slowest to act because its president refused to accept the link between HIV and AIDS.

### 4. The domestic-capability explanation, and its own limits

⚠️ The mirror claim is that outcomes are determined by what states do - their industrial policy, their infrastructure, their administrative competence, their willingness to acknowledge a problem.

⚠️ Its limits are equally clear in the evidence. ✅ India's exports fell a further 3 per cent between May 2011 and May 2012, investors deserted for the dollar, the rupee reached a record low in June 2012 and the current account deficit rose from $49 billion in June 2011 to an estimated $72 billion at the end of 2012 - and none of that was caused by an Indian decision; it was transmitted from a crisis that began in American housing finance. ✅ Russia's GDP had grown tenfold on high oil prices and the price per barrel plunged from $140 to $40 in 2008, forcing a $200 billion liquidity operation - and no Russian policy set the world oil price. ✅ Ghana's cocoa and Zambia's copper faced a dramatic fall in world prices in the 1970s that no domestic measure could have prevented.

⚠️ The formulation that follows is the session's central analytical claim and it should be memorised: **the external structure set the terms on which every economy had to operate; domestic capability determined what each made of those terms; and neither alone predicts an outcome.**

### 5. Correlation and causation: the four standing cautions

⚠️ Four pairs recur throughout this topic and each is a trap.

1. ⚠️ **Population growth and poverty.** ✅ Growth diverted resources from industrialising to subsistence and delayed development; ✅ poverty and high infant mortality sustained large families, and contraceptives were too expensive; ✅ and in nineteenth-century Europe growth accompanied prosperity because resources were available. ⚠️ The relationship is two-way with a mediating variable, and ❌ a one-directional claim is contradicted by evidence in this session.
2. ⚠️ **Globalisation and inequality.** ✅ Integration deepened after 1990 and real wages fell in the leading western economies; ✅ it also coincided with the tiger economies' convergence. ⚠️ The same process moved different groups in different directions, which is why the verdict must be distributional.
3. ⚠️ **Deregulation and the crisis of 2008.** ⚠️ The traceable claim is the specific one: the November 1999 act and the derivatives exemption, followed by a derivatives market of $596 trillion against a $63 trillion stock market in 2007. ⚠️ The broader claim that a quarter-century of neo-liberalism was mainly responsible is Paul Mason's judgement and should be attributed.
4. ⚠️ **Economic inequality and political violence.** ✅ The account records a widespread contemporary belief that the growth of terrorism was a desperate response to the failure of peaceful attempts at a fairer world economic system, and ✅ Liotta and Miskel argue that megacities will present a security threat if left to inept or uncaring governments. ⚠️ Both are recorded as beliefs and arguments, with the second explicitly conditional on governance. ❌ Neither is converted into a causal finding here, and the substantive politics belong to the topic that owns the new world order.

### 6. Inequality between and within countries

⚠️ Most scripts discuss only the first. The evidence in this session requires both, and saying so is itself a mark-earning move.

✅ **Between countries.** The GNP of the North averaged over 24 times that of the South in 1989-90; Japan was over $28,000 per head in 1992 and Ethiopia $110; Norway's life expectancy was 78.7 with virtually 100 per cent literacy and income just under $30,000, against Sierra Leone's life expectancy of about 35, literacy of 35 per cent and income averaging $470; and at the end of 2003 the richest 1 per cent of the world's population, around 60 million people, received as much income as the poorest 57 per cent.

✅ **Within countries.** Real wages fell in the leading western economies, including the USA, Japan and Germany, as the global labour force doubled; the OECD reported in June 2012 that two of the main American problems were record long-term unemployment and the widening gap between the poor and the wealthy, with about 5.3 million Americans, 40 per cent of the unemployed, out of work for six months or more, and only Chile, Mexico and Turkey ranking higher among the 34 member states in income inequality. ✅ In the other direction, Brazil's poorest 50 per cent saw their incomes rise by almost 70 per cent over the few years before 2012.

⚠️ The analytical payoff: **integration raised aggregate output and did not by itself determine distribution**, which is why the same decades produced convergence between some countries and divergence inside many. ⚠️ That sentence, deployed early in an answer on globalisation, sets up everything that follows.

### 7. India and the Global South, gathered

⚠️ For an Indian examination it is worth collecting the session's Indian and Global South material in one place, because it is scattered by design across the chronology.

- ✅ **Non-alignment and leadership.** Nehru of India (1947-64) saw himself as a sort of unofficial leader of the Third World, which he thought could be a powerful force for world peace; by the Havana conference of 1979, 92 nations were represented, roughly 70 per cent of the world's population.
- ✅ **Food.** Even a densely populated country like India was able to export food during the green revolution, and China became self-sufficient.
- ✅ **Population policy.** India and Pakistan set up family-planning clinics advising on methods and providing cheap contraceptives, with publicity campaigns recommending a maximum of three children; progress was slow because attitudes were difficult to change. Indian contraceptive use among married women stood at 35 per cent in 1986 with the birth rate falling from 4.5 to 3.2 between 1978 and 1986.
- ✅ **Healthcare.** India was among the countries spending much less than the $12 a head a year that Save the Children estimated basic healthcare required in April 1996.
- ✅ **Technology and trade.** India was among the 16 countries where at least 6 million farmers were growing genetically modified crops by 2004.
- ✅ **Industrial relocation.** India is named among the countries into which industrial production and some service industries moved from the western nations, where labour was much cheaper.
- ✅ **The crisis of 2008 and after.** Indian exports fell a further 3 per cent from May 2011 to May 2012; investors deserted for the dollar and the rupee hit a record low in June 2012; the current account deficit rose from $49 billion in June 2011 to an estimated $72 billion at end-2012, over 4 per cent of GDP against a sustainable 2 per cent; S&P and Fitch cut their ratings to negative while Moody's held at stable; India had failed to decouple; and in June 2012 India joined China in making a substantial contribution to the IMF's emergency fund.

> **Boundary.** India's own 1991 liberalisation, its industrial policy and its domestic economic reform belong to the economy owner and are not retold here. ⚠️ In world-history terms India can be cited as an example of how post-Cold-War globalisation pulled states more deeply into world markets, with the cross-reference stated rather than the content reproduced.

⚠️ The single most useful Indian formulation available from this session: **India's experience between 2011 and 2012 demonstrates that integration transmits through three channels at once - export demand, portfolio capital and the exchange rate - and that a state can be hit by all three without having made a mistake.**

### 8. The demand map: what each question is really testing

| ⚠️ If the question says | ⚠️ It is testing | ❌ Do not write |
|---|---|---|
| *Trace the major changes in the world economy since 1900* | Phase logic and what ended each phase | A century-long chronicle |
| *How did a national slump become a world crisis?* | The transmission channels, ranked | "Economies are connected" |
| *What policy instruments were deployed to contain the Great Depression?* | Instruments grouped by function, with purpose and result | A New Deal narrative or a list of Acts |
| *Did the New Deal save capitalism?* | Legitimacy and output as two separate tests | Yes or no without separating them |
| *What is meant by the North-South divide?* | The category's origin, its evidence and its later breakdown | A list of poor countries |
| *Why did globalisation not remove the divide?* | The mechanisms of uneven gain | "Globalisation was unfair" |
| *Effects of the oil shocks* | Redistribution of purchasing power, importer strain, terms of trade, debt sequence | "Oil got expensive" |
| *Explain rapid population growth* | The gap between falling mortality and lagging fertility | "More births" |
| *Consequences of population growth* | The two-column contrast: stimulus where resources existed, crisis where they did not | Malthusian alarm |
| *Methods of population control and why they were criticised* | Two routes, the record of each, and the ethics of consent | A numerical account |
| *Capitalism versus socialism in practice* | Variation within each philosophy and social outcome | Ideological assertion |
| *HIV/AIDS as a development issue* | Labour, skill, food production, households, life expectancy and unequal burden | A medical account |
| *Economic growth and the environment* | The growth model, exhaustion, pollution and the industrial cause of warming | An environment-paper answer with no history in it |
| *Why was there a crisis of capitalism before 2012?* | Deregulation, sub-prime and collateralised debt, leverage and shadow banking, failed supervision | A narrative of bank failures |

### 9. Mark-scaled structures

| Marks | ⚠️ Structure |
|---|---|
| **10** | Thesis, then **two** phases or **two** mechanisms with named evidence, then one qualification, then a verdict |
| **15** | Thesis, then the phase sequence or the full mechanism, then the divergence mechanism, then one social consequence, then a verdict |
| **20** | All of the above, **plus** the institutional architecture, **plus** the population or environmental dimension, **plus** genuine regional variation, then a graded verdict |

### 10. Qualified theses, ready to deploy

- ⚠️ **World economy.** "The twentieth-century world economy moved from European-centred liberal integration, through war-and-depression fragmentation, to a designed and United States-led rules-based order, and finally to a globalised system that spread growth and contagion through the same channels."
- ⚠️ **Divergence.** "Globalisation did not fail to reduce the North-South gap; it reduced it for some countries and widened it for others, which is why 'the South' ceased to be an analytically useful single category."
- ⚠️ **Depression.** "The Crash of October 1929 was the moment the American economy's structural imbalance became visible, not the cause of it; overproduction, an income distribution too narrow to absorb that output, a tariff regime that strangled the export escape and a speculative boom had already exhausted the conditions for growth."
- ⚠️ **Instruments.** "The instruments deployed against the Depression were of four kinds - stabilising the financial system, supporting incomes, coordinating production and building institutional protection against recurrence - and it is their combination, not any single measure, that arrested the collapse without ending it."
- ⚠️ **Population.** "The population explosion was a mortality event before it was a fertility event: death rates fell faster than birth rates adjusted, and the gap between the two curves is the explosion."
- ⚠️ **Population policy.** "The policy answer that worked was education, child survival, availability and women's employment rather than coercion, and the states that achieved comparable fertility declines without compulsion are the strongest argument against the claim that compulsion was necessary."
- ⚠️ **Inequality.** "Integration raised aggregate output and did not by itself determine distribution, which is why the same decades produced both convergence between some countries and widening inequality inside many."
- ⚠️ **Limits.** "The growth model that produced the century's wealth also produced its resource exhaustion, its pollution and its warming, which is why any full account of the world economy since 1900 must report the costs as well as the revenue."

### 11. Verdict scaffolds

- ⚠️ **"Changes since 1900?"** "The century moved the world economy's centre out of Europe, replaced an emergent liberal order with a designed one after 1944, and ended by integrating production and finance so completely that the same channels carried growth and crisis."
- ⚠️ **"Why did the divide persist?"** "Because integration rewards existing capability: countries able to supply manufactures converged, and countries able to supply only commodities and debt service did not."
- ⚠️ **"Population growth?"** "Not a failure of restraint but a success of public health arriving decades before the social changes that reduce fertility."
- ⚠️ **"Capitalism versus socialism?"** "The comparison cannot be settled on output alone, and this session's evidence settles a narrower and more useful point: a single economic philosophy produced several institutional forms with different social outcomes, which is what the syllabus clause actually asks about."
- ⚠️ **"Did globalisation fail?"** "It succeeded and failed for different participants by the same mechanism, and the answer is therefore a distribution rather than a verdict."

### 12. Factual-risk controls: the list to check before submitting

1. ❌ Do not write that the Wall Street Crash caused the Depression.
2. ❌ Do not construct a gold-standard argument; the evidence held here does not support one.
3. ❌ Do not attribute the New Deal's fiscal expansion to Keynes; only the Brain Trust is named.
4. ❌ Do not adopt the First, Second and Third New Deal periodisation; Michael Heale is recorded as regarding it as an oversimplification.
5. ❌ Do not present Nazi rearmament recovery as a policy success without the trade-union abolition and the racial and political purges.
6. ❌ Do not date Bretton Woods other than 1944, the General Agreement other than 1947, or the World Trade Organization other than 1995; and do not call the WTO a UN specialized agency.
7. ❌ Do not assert that oil surpluses financed developing-world debt.
8. ❌ Do not use "stagflation" with figures attached.
9. ❌ Do not treat the Third World as a single economic actor after the divergence of the 1970s.
10. ❌ Do not quote the 2007 derivatives figure without its comparator and year.
11. ❌ Do not reproduce the disputed rescue-programme total recorded in the source for July 2009.
12. ❌ Do not explain the population explosion as "more births".
13. ❌ Do not attribute the demographic transition model to a named demographer here.
14. ❌ Do not present the Chinese figures of 1983 as unowned facts; they are Matthew Connelly's.
15. ❌ Do not import climate treaties, targets or conference outcomes; they belong to the environment owner.
16. ❌ Do not import India's 1991 reform content; cross-refer to the economy owner.
17. ❌ Do not report any forecast - Brandt targets, the Population Institute's 1995 projection, the Stanford 2050 calculation, the Goldman Sachs BRIC ranking or the IMF projection of July 2026 - as an outcome.
18. ❌ Do not give any figure without the year it describes.

### Criticism and reply: is a synthesis just fence-sitting?

**The objection.** ⚠️ An answer that says the structure set the terms and domestic capability determined the outcome has agreed with everybody. Examiners reward argument, not balance.

**The reply.** ⚠️ A synthesis is fence-sitting only when it fails to specify. This one specifies three things and can be tested on each. ⚠️ It predicts that outcomes will vary **within** a given external environment, and they did - Singapore against Sierra Leone, Libya against other oil producers, Uganda against South Africa. ⚠️ It predicts that outcomes will vary **with** the external environment for a given level of domestic capability, and they did - Indian exports, currency and current account all moved in 2011-12 without any Indian policy change. ⚠️ And it identifies the interaction as the object of study rather than either term. ⚠️ A single-cause answer, by contrast, must either deny the tiger economies or deny the terms of trade, and both denials are refutable from one page of evidence. ⚠️ The formulation to carry: **a synthesis that names what each explanation cannot account for is an argument; one that merely says both matter is not.**

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The whole of the world-economy and population material, and the political-philosophies clause. ⚠️ This lesson is the architecture, not new content.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Any evaluative directive - assess, critically examine, discuss, comment - on globalisation, on the North-South divide, on population, or on the century's economic change. ⚠️ Use the demand map to identify the family, the mark-scaled structure to allocate the space, a qualified thesis to open, and a verdict scaffold to close.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Adopting a theory as a position. ⚠️ Use it as an organising tool and state what it cannot explain.
2. ⚠️ Discussing inequality only between countries. ✅ Real wages fell in the leading western economies and the OECD recorded record long-term American unemployment and a widening gap in June 2012.
3. ⚠️ Writing an Indian economic-reform answer. ⚠️ Cross-refer to the economy owner and keep the world-history frame.
4. ⚠️ Reporting forecasts as outcomes. ⚠️ Every projection in this session is dated and attributed.
5. ⚠️ Closing without a graded verdict. ⚠️ Every thesis above has a qualification built into it; use it.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ Four explanations are available: structural dependency, domestic capability and institutions, policy regimes and design, and physical and demographic limits. Each is defeated by specific evidence in this session, and naming that evidence is the analytical move.
2. ⚠️ Dependency's strongest evidence: the 1900 role division; colonial price-fixing at both ends; one-product economies; the 1975-80 terms-of-trade collapse; UNCTAD's failure; tied and interest-bearing aid; $500 billion of debt by 1980; a North-South GNP ratio over 24 to 1 in 1989-90.
3. ⚠️ Dependency's limits: the four tigers converging with European Community incomes; Libya's welfare state against other producers' unwise spending; states spending more per head on defence than on healthcare; Uganda containing an epidemic that richer South Africa did not.
4. ⚠️ Domestic capability's limits: Indian exports, the rupee and the current account in 2011-12; Russian dependence on an oil price that fell from $140 to $40 in 2008; the 1970s collapse of cocoa, copper, coffee and cotton prices.
5. ⚠️ Master synthesis: the external structure set the terms; domestic capability determined what each state made of them; neither alone predicts an outcome.
6. ⚠️ Four standing correlation cautions: population and poverty; globalisation and inequality; deregulation and 2008; inequality and political violence.
7. ✅ Inequality between countries: 24 times in 1989-90; Japan $28,000 and Ethiopia $110 in 1992; Norway 78.7 years against Sierra Leone about 35; the richest 1 per cent equalling the poorest 57 per cent at the end of 2003.
8. ✅ Inequality within countries: falling real wages in the USA, Japan and Germany; the OECD's June 2012 finding of record American long-term unemployment, 5.3 million out of work six months or more, and only Chile, Mexico and Turkey more unequal among 34 states; against Brazil's poorest 50 per cent gaining almost 70 per cent.
9. ⚠️ Integration raised aggregate output and did not by itself determine distribution.
10. ✅ India's thread: Nehru's unofficial leadership of the Third World and the 92 nations at Havana in 1979; food exports during the green revolution; family-planning clinics and 35 per cent contraceptive use in 1986 with the birth rate falling from 4.5 to 3.2; healthcare spending below $12 a head in 1996; GM crops in 2004; industrial relocation; and the triple transmission of 2011-12 ending with the IMF contribution of June 2012.
11. ⚠️ Eight qualified theses and five verdict scaffolds are listed above; each carries its own qualification.
12. ⚠️ The eighteen factual-risk controls are the final check before submitting any answer on this topic.

### Concept check

**Question:** Construct the strongest single paragraph you could write in answer to "why did globalisation not remove the North-South divide?", and identify what makes it strong.

**Model answer:** "Globalisation did not fail to reduce the North-South gap; it reduced it for some countries and widened it for others, which is why 'the South' ceased to be an analytically useful category. Integration rewards existing capability. States able to supply manufactures converged: Singapore, Taiwan, South Korea and Hong Kong reached GNPs comparing favourably with many European Community countries, having attracted northern firms seeking much cheaper labour. States able to supply only primary products did not, because the terms on which they traded moved against them - a tonne of copper bought 115 barrels of oil in 1975 and 58 in 1980, with cocoa, coffee and cotton falling similarly, while manufactured prices rose and UNCTAD's attempts to negotiate fairer prices achieved no real improvement. Their debt then compounded: $500 billion owed by 1980, with $50 billion of annual interest, some of it met by further borrowing. Meanwhile the gains inside rich countries were also uneven, since the doubling of the world's labour force after 1990 brought falling real wages in the USA, Japan and Germany. The conclusion is therefore distributional rather than evaluative: integration raised aggregate output and did not by itself determine distribution." What makes it strong is that it opens with a qualified thesis rather than a description, supports each half with dated evidence, names the mechanism - integration rewards existing capability - rather than asserting unfairness, includes inequality within countries as well as between them, and closes on a verdict that the evidence actually supports.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a stronger answer would have taken a firmer side. The evidence in this session makes both one-sided verdicts refutable in a sentence: a candidate claiming globalisation impoverished the South must explain the tiger economies, and one claiming it enriched the world must explain the terms of trade, the compounding debt and the falling western real wages. The firmest defensible position is the distributional one, and stating it with the mechanism attached is what converts balance into argument.
