## The legacy Great Depression demand, rendered neutrally

⚠️ A General Studies Paper I demand on the **policy instruments deployed to contain the Great Economic Depression** is a recorded recurring demand from the pre-2018 period.

⚠️ The routing ledgers held in this repository begin at 2018, so **no verbatim text, question number or mark allocation is held locally**. ❌ None is invented here. ⚠️ The demand is therefore rendered neutrally and answered in full by Lesson 5, whose functional grouping - financial stabilisation, income and agricultural support, coordinated production and public works, and institutional capacity - is precisely the structure such a demand requires, with Lesson 6 supplying the achievements, limits and graded verdict.

⚠️ **Answer approach for that demand family.** Open with the thesis that the instruments were of four kinds and that it is their combination rather than any single measure that arrested the collapse without ending it. Give two or three named measures per family with their purpose. Concede the recovery failure explicitly - unemployment under 8 million by 1937, 10.5 million after the 1938 cut, and below a million only in 1943 through the war effort. Close on the graded verdict: decisive for the survival of democratic capitalism, insufficient for recovery.

## Probable framings recorded by the owners and by the core account

⚠️ Since no question is owned, the best available guide to how this topic would be examined is the set of framings recorded by the two canonical owners and by the account itself. ⚠️ All of them are covered by the original Mains practice below.

✅ **From the canonical owner:** trace the major changes in the world economy since 1900; explain the causes and consequences of rapid population growth in the twentieth century; discuss why globalisation did not remove the North-South divide. ✅ Its directive and demand map adds the framings on capitalism versus socialism in practice, on HIV/AIDS as a development issue, on the causes and containment of the Great Depression, on the effects of the oil shocks, and on economic growth and the environment.

✅ **From the advanced owner:** "Globalization widened both opportunity and vulnerability." Discuss; why did the North-South divide persist despite the growth of the world economy; and examine the causes and implications of the population explosion in the twentieth century. ✅ Its Mains framework table adds three flows - change in the world economy, the population explosion, and the assessment of globalisation.

✅ **From the core account's own end-of-chapter questions:** what is meant by the term "North-South divide", what attempts have been made since 1980 to close the gap between North and South, and how successful have they been; assess the reasons why global warming is seen as such a serious problem for the world's future and to what extent it is the twenty-first century's major problem; explain why there was a "crisis of capitalism" in the decade leading up to 2012; explain the causes and consequences of the rapid growth in the world's population during the twentieth century; what methods were used to try to control population growth in the second half of the twentieth century, and why did some of them arouse criticism; and why was it that in the second half of the twentieth century the rate of population growth in Europe slowed down while in Africa and other Third World areas it accelerated.

## What a topic with no owned question requires

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a nil return across nine years should change preparation rather than reduce it, and four things follow.

1. ⚠️ **The topic is examined through other papers and other owners, constantly.** ✅ The audit above records at least fourteen questions in the span whose subject matter this session teaches, every one routed elsewhere. ⚠️ The material taught here is the historical foundation of answers written in Geography, Indian Society, Economy and International Relations, and a candidate who treats a world-history nil return as permission to skip the topic will meet globalisation, demographic transition, urbanisation and the world financial institutions in four other papers without the depth that makes an answer good.
2. ⚠️ **The Paper I syllabus clause covers it directly.** ✅ The syllabus names world history from the eighteenth century onward, including the industrial revolution, colonisation and decolonisation, and political philosophies such as communism, capitalism and socialism, their forms and their effect on society. ⚠️ This topic is where the last clause is answered with evidence rather than with definitions.
3. ⚠️ **The Depression is the one guaranteed point of contact.** ⚠️ It is a causal variable in at least two other World History topics and a recorded recurring demand in its own right, which is why four of this session's twenty-two lessons are devoted to it.
4. ⚠️ **Rehearse architectures, not answers.** ⚠️ Lesson 22 lists eight qualified theses and five verdict scaffolds. ⚠️ The two that transfer fastest to an unanticipated stem are the uneven-integration thesis - that integration rewards existing capability, so the same process converged some economies and not others - and the mortality-before-fertility thesis, that the population explosion was a mortality event before it was a fertility event.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks rather than within a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - the inherited structure and the wars, Lessons 1 to 2

### Cumulative check A1 - what changed and what did not, 1900 to 1945

**Question:** Identify one thing about the world economy that was already true in 1900 and did not change by 1945, and one thing that changed completely, supporting each with dated evidence.

**Model answer:** What did not change is the division of roles. In 1900 a few highly industrialised countries - mainly the USA, Britain and Germany - provided the world's manufactured goods while the rest of the world provided raw materials and food, and European nations usually decided what their colonies produced, making Uganda and the Sudan grow cotton for British textiles and doing the same in Mozambique, while fixing colonial export prices as low as possible and manufactured import prices as high as possible. That structure survived both wars intact, which is why the same relationship reappears after 1945 as the North-South divide, with the North expecting the South to continue providing food and raw materials and not encouraging it to develop its own industries. What changed completely is who occupied the top of the structure. In 1880 Britain produced roughly twice as much coal and pig iron as the USA; by 1900 the USA produced more coal and about twice as much pig iron and steel; by 1914 it was the world's leading industrial nation; and at the end of the Second World War it was producing 43 per cent of the world's iron ore, 45 per cent of its crude steel, 60 per cent of its railway locomotives and 74 per cent of its motor vehicles, with incomes twice those of Britain and seven times those of the USSR in 1945. The structure persisted; the occupant of its apex changed.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the two world wars as the cause of American dominance. The coal and pig-iron crossing was complete by 1900 and the account states plainly that the USA was the world's leading industrial nation by 1914. What the wars supplied was acceleration and consolidation - the permanent trade switch of Chinese and Latin American buyers, the creditor position from war-loan interest, and a near-monopoly of complex assembly by 1945 - not the original transfer.

### Cumulative check A2 - the two postwar moments compared

**Question:** Contrast the American position after 1918 with the American position after 1945, and explain why the difference matters for what followed.

**Model answer:** After 1918 the United States became a creditor without becoming a manager. It was the economic winner of the war and became richer still through interest on the war loans made to Britain and her allies; it was the only state rich enough to provide the loans that encouraged German recovery in the 1920s; and yet the Fordney-McCumber tariff of 1922 kept foreign goods out, denying Europeans the profits needed to buy American goods or service their war debts, and many states retaliated. No institution existed to manage the resulting strain, so when the USA suffered its great slump, dated 1929-35, the loans were called in and Europe and the rest of the world were thrown into depression, with about 14 million unemployed in the USA by 1933, roughly a quarter of its labour force. After 1945 the United States became a creditor and a designer. It supplied Marshall Aid, with the two recorded ulterior motives of keeping the American boom going through European purchases and making western Europe less likely to go communist; it had already helped construct, at Bretton Woods in 1944, a Fund to manage balance-of-payments adjustment and a Bank to finance reconstruction; and trade proceeded through negotiated reciprocal reduction from 1947. The difference matters because the second arrangement was designed against the failures of the first, which is why 1945 did not repeat 1919 - and why the strains that eventually broke it in 1971 arose from a different source altogether.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the postwar order as generosity replacing selfishness. The account records ulterior motives in both periods and does not moralise about either. The analytically useful distinction is institutional rather than ethical: money without rules in the first case, money with rules in the second.

## Block B - the Depression, Lessons 3 to 6

### Cumulative check B1 - ranking the causes with the conditional

**Question:** Rank the four causes of the Depression using the account's own conditional, and explain what the ranking changes in an answer.

**Model answer:** The conditional is the ranking device: even with overproduction and an income distribution too narrow to absorb it, a slump was still not inevitable provided the Americans could export their surplus products. That sentence promotes the export cause from third in the list to first in importance. The four causes are then ordered as follows. Falling export demand is load-bearing, because the Fordney-McCumber tariff of 1922 kept foreign goods out, prevented Europeans from earning the profits needed to buy American goods or service war debts, and provoked retaliation, so the escape route was closed by policy. Domestic overproduction created the surplus that needed the escape route: high profits and mechanisation produced more than the home market could absorb, unsold stocks built up, output fell, men were laid off, and with no unemployment benefit their families bought less. Unequal income explains why the home market could not absorb it: wages rose about 8 per cent between 1923 and 1929, only 1.4 per cent in real terms, while profits rose 72 per cent, and credit postponed the limit without removing it. Speculation, with momentum from about 1926 and the average share rising from $9 in 1924 to $26 in 1929, determined the form of the reckoning rather than its necessity. The ranking changes the answer because it converts a list into an argument with a counterfactual attached, and because it explains why an international policy - a tariff - stands at the centre of what is usually written as a domestic story.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the ranking as a claim that the other causes were minor. The conditional says a slump was not inevitable if exports had held; it does not say the imbalance was harmless. The strongest formulation keeps the structure: overproduction and unequal income created the gap, the tariff closed the escape, and speculation set the manner of collapse.

### Cumulative check B2 - the two tests of the New Deal

**Question:** Why must "did the New Deal succeed?" be answered with two separate verdicts, and what are they?

**Model answer:** Because the programme had three declared aims and they are tested differently. Relief meant direct help to the destitute millions without food or homes; recovery meant reducing unemployment, stimulating demand and getting the economy moving; reform meant preventing a repeat. On recovery the verdict is failure on its own terms: unemployment fell to under 8 million by 1937 but remained serious, the 1938 spending cut caused another recession and 10.5 million unemployed, and the account states that the New Deal did not rescue the USA from the depression - only the war effort brought unemployment below a million in 1943, with GNP almost doubling between 1939 and 1945. On relief and reform the verdict is success: relief for the destitute and the creation of millions of extra jobs was the chief early success; confidence was restored in the financial system and in government, and some historians think it may even have prevented a violent revolution; the public works schemes and the Tennessee Valley Authority provided services of lasting value; the 1935 Social Security Act was an important step towards a welfare state; and national direction of resources, accepted collective bargaining and increased federal authority became permanent, so that Washington had the structures to manage the economy and social policy. The combined verdict is therefore that it saved the political legitimacy of capitalism without restoring its output - decisive for the survival of democratic capitalism, insufficient for recovery.

**Misconception to avoid:** Judging the reform instruments by the unemployment series. The Securities Exchange Commission of 1934 and the Social Security Act of 1935 were designed against the next crisis, not this one, and measuring them against 1937 employment produces the false conclusion that the whole programme failed.

### Cumulative check B3 - writing about the authoritarian comparison

**Question:** State the rules this topic requires for comparing the American and German responses to the Depression, and apply them.

**Model answer:** Three rules. First, state the recorded outcome exactly: Nazi Germany combined vast public works - slum clearance, land drainage and autobahn building - with rearmament from 1934 and conscription from 1935, and unemployment fell from over six million at Hitler's accession to just over two million by the end of 1935 and to negligible levels by 1939, with the military budget reaching 52 per cent of government spending in 1938-9. Second, attach the qualification in the same paragraph, never later: that programme rested on the abolition of trade unions and on purges of Jews and anti-Nazis from the civil service, law, education, journalism, broadcasting, theatre and music, which themselves created vacancies. Third, identify what the comparison actually proves, which is a point about scale and constraint rather than about regime quality: mass unemployment was solvable by public spending at sufficient scale, and that scale was politically available only to a government that did not have to satisfy a supreme court which struck down the NRA, state governments that resented federal interference, or an electorate. The American evidence confirms the same fiscal finding from the other direction, since the 1938 cut produced another recession and only war production ended the problem.

**Misconception to avoid:** Allowing the speed of the German fall in unemployment to function as praise. Speed measured the absence of constraint, and the output was armaments, which need no civilian market. An answer that reports full employment without naming the exclusions has adopted the regime's own accounting.

## Block C - the designed order and the divide, Lessons 7 to 11

### Cumulative check C1 - the order and its critics, in one chain

**Question:** Trace the chain from the failures of 1929-33 to the Brandt Report's second recommendation of 1980.

**Model answer:** The failures came first. Credit was withdrawn without warning, producing banking collapse abroad; tariffs were raised in self-defence, contracting world trade; currencies were devalued or wages cut, deepening the demand collapse; and no institution existed that could lend, arbitrate or coordinate. The design answered each: the Bretton Woods conference of July 1944, with delegates from 44 Allied nations, created the International Monetary Fund to manage balance-of-payments adjustment under fixed exchange rates and the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development to finance reconstruction and development, both United Nations specialized agencies, while trade liberalisation proceeded through the General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade of 1947 and, from 1995, the World Trade Organization, which is not a specialized agency. The criticism followed from the design's authorship. Most of Africa and much of Asia were still colonies in 1944 and were not represented as states; by 1970 the Third World consisted of Africa, Asia except the USSR and China, India, Pakistan, Bangladesh, Latin America and the Middle East, almost all former colonies left undeveloped at independence; and by 1979, 92 nations representing roughly 70 per cent of the world's population were meeting at Havana. The Brandt Report of 1980 then asked not only for aid at 0.7 per cent of national income by 1985 and 1.0 per cent by 2000, but for a new World Development Fund in which decision-making would be more evenly shared between lenders and borrowers - explicitly unlike the Fund and the Bank, which it described as dominated by the USA. That is the chain: a designed order can be criticised for its authorship, and the demand of 1980 was representational before it was financial.

**Misconception to avoid:** Presenting the Brandt demand as simply a request for more money. Its aid target failed - by 1985 only Norway, Sweden, Denmark, the Netherlands and France had reached 0.7 per cent, the USA giving 0.24 and Britain 0.11 - but the recommendation that proved hardest to grant was the one about shared decision-making, and noticing that is what distinguishes an analytical answer.

### Cumulative check C2 - six mechanisms, one budget

**Question:** Show how three of the six mechanisms of Third World poverty converge on a single national budget, using named cases.

**Model answer:** Take a one-product economy such as Ghana with cocoa or Zambia with copper. The first mechanism is structural: states could not break away from the one-product economies left behind from colonial days because governments lacked the cash needed to diversify, so export earnings are the budget. The second is the terms of trade: in the 1970s there was a dramatic fall in the world price of cocoa, copper, coffee and cotton, and the recorded measure is what a tonne could buy - copper fell from 115 barrels of oil in 1975 to 58 in 1980, cocoa from 148 to 63, with the dollar equivalents falling proportionately - while the prices of manufactured imports continued to rise and UNCTAD's efforts to negotiate fairer prices achieved no real improvement. The third is food displacement: in states like Ghana, which depended on exporting crops, too little food was left for the population, so scarce money had to be spent importing expensive food. The three converge on the same line of the budget. Earnings fall because the terms of trade move; the import bill rises because manufactures cost more and food must be bought; and the revenue that would have financed diversification is consumed by subsistence. The fourth mechanism then completes the trap: aid carried interest and was sometimes tied to the lender's goods, some states borrowed directly from western banks, and by 1980 the Third World owed the equivalent of $500 billion with about $50 billion of annual interest, some states borrowing again merely to pay it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Listing the mechanisms as separate problems. They are one problem observed at four points, and the marks are in showing the convergence - which is why the terms-of-trade table, measuring purchasing power rather than price, is the evidential centre of any such answer.

## Block D - oil, divergence, doctrine and integration, Lessons 12 to 17

### Cumulative check D1 - what turned one South into several

**Question:** Explain, with evidence, why the single category "Third World" had lost its analytical value by the 1990s, and name the two routes that broke it.

**Model answer:** Because the differences inside it had become larger than the difference across its boundary. Two routes produced the split. The first was resource wealth: in 1973 OPEC members, partly in an attempt to conserve oil supplies, began to charge more for their oil, and the Middle East producers, Nigeria and Libya made huge profits - though the account adds that this did not necessarily mean governments spent the money wisely, with Libya the recorded exception, using much of the profits on agricultural and industrial development and a welfare state, and reaching a GNP of £5,460 in 1989, almost as successful as Greece and Portugal. The second was industrialisation: some states industrialised rapidly and with great success - Singapore, Taiwan, South Korea and Hong Kong, the four Pacific tigers, with Thailand, Malaysia, Brazil and Mexico among others - and the GNPs of the four tigers compared favourably with those of many European Community countries. The engine was the relocation of production, since these states attracted northern firms keen to take advantage of much cheaper labour, some shifting all production. Meanwhile Ethiopia was recorded at $110 per head in 1992, the second lowest GNP in the world. A category containing both Singapore and Ethiopia no longer carries information, which is why any answer that treats the developing world as one economic actor after the 1970s has already lost marks.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the tiger record disproves the structural argument. It disproves its strong form, that integration makes development impossible, and confirms its weak form, that integration rewards existing capability - which is exactly why the states that converted relocation into lasting industry were those already able to supply skilled labour, infrastructure and policy direction, while the frontier of cheap labour moved on to Malaysia, the Philippines, Indonesia and China.

### Cumulative check D2 - one decade, four crises

**Question:** The 1970s produced a monetary break, an energy shock, a development confrontation and an environmental awakening. Show that these are connected rather than coincidental.

**Model answer:** They are four expressions of the same growth model reaching different limits. The monetary break came first: defence costs and the Vietnam war were a constant drain, there was a budget deficit every year in the late 1960s covered by selling gold reserves, and by 1971 the dollar, once considered as good as gold, was weakening, so Nixon devalued by about 12 per cent and imposed a 10 per cent duty on most imports - ending the fixed-rate element of the designed order and making floating rates and freer capital movement the norm. The energy shock followed in 1973, when OPEC members, partly in an attempt to conserve oil supplies, began to charge more, redistributing purchasing power towards producers and straining importers, with America's balance-of-payments deficit worsening. The environmental awakening is dated to the same decade - during the 1970s people became increasingly aware that all was not well with their environment - and it shares a cause with the energy shock, since fossil fuels cannot be replaced and were being rapidly used up, which the account records as one of the reasons why OPEC tried to conserve oil during the 1970s. The development confrontation completes the set: commodity prices fell dramatically in the 1970s, a tonne of copper buying 115 barrels of oil in 1975 and 58 in 1980, and the Brandt Report of 1980 arrived at the end of the decade demanding aid targets and a fund with shared decision-making. Scarcity, price, payments and distribution are four faces of one structure.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the environment as a separate subject that happens to share a decade. The account places it in the same chapter as the world economy and states the connection as a claim about motive - the main motive was the creation of wealth and profit, and very little attention was paid to the side effects - which makes it a fact about the organisation of production rather than a scientific aside.

### Cumulative check D3 - the same mechanism in 1929 and 2008

**Question:** Identify three mechanisms that appear in both the Depression and the crisis of 2008, and state what the repetition demonstrates.

**Model answer:** First, credit substituting for purchasing power. In the 1920s wages rose about 8 per cent between 1923 and 1929 while profits rose 72 per cent, and the public could absorb the output for a limited time with the help of credit until by 1929 they were fast approaching the limit. After 1990 the world's labour force doubled and real wages fell in the leading western economies including the USA, Japan and Germany, yet consumption grew, made possible by a massive increase in credit and the heyday of the credit-card era, until after 2000 the debts began to run out of control. Second, self-fulfilling expectation. In 1929 investors' own selling produced the collapse of share prices they feared; after 2005 house prices had reached levels that could not be sustained, and the reversal produced the defaults and losses it anticipated. Third, banks using depositors' money for speculative purposes: in 1929 banks speculated in shares with the cash deposited with them, and ordinary depositors who had had nothing to do with the speculation were ruined - which is exactly the regulation that the act of November 1999 cancelled, allowing investment banks to handle the savings and deposits of the general public. The repetition demonstrates that the 1930s settlement had identified the mechanisms correctly and legislated against them, and that the crisis of 2008 followed the removal of those specific protections rather than the discovery of a new kind of failure.

**Misconception to avoid:** Pushing the parallel into a claim that 2008 was a repeat of 1929. The instruments, the scale and above all the policy response differed: in 2008 governments and central banks bailed out the banking system, launched fiscal stimulus of $580 billion in China and $787 billion in the USA and used quantitative easing, and the system was saved from total collapse. The parallel is in the mechanisms, not in the outcomes.

## Block E - population, health and the argument, Lessons 18 to 22

### Cumulative check E1 - the gap, and why timing is everything

**Question:** Explain why regional differences in the timing of the demographic transition matter more than the global totals, using two regions.

**Model answer:** Because the timing determines which problem a society faces, and the two problems are opposite. Europe and North America had their most rapid increase before the First World War and slowed considerably afterwards, mainly because more people were using modern contraceptive techniques and later because the depression of the 1930s discouraged large families. The consequence arrived decades later and in a form nobody had planned for: very low birth rates and longer life expectancy produced a growing proportion over 65, about 15 per cent in Sweden, France and Britain by the 1970s, and by the early 1990s questions were being asked about whether state welfare systems could afford to pay pensions to all old people. In Africa, Asia and Latin America the rate accelerated after the Second World War as modern medical and hygiene techniques brought smallpox, malaria and typhoid under control while the vast majority made no attempt to limit their families, so those regions faced the opposite problem - overcrowding in India, Pakistan and Bangladesh, insufficient land, forced movement into cities with neither houses nor jobs, and shanty towns without water supply, sanitation or lighting. The global totals conceal this entirely: the world rate fell from 1.7 per cent in 1950-9 to 1.5 in 1980-5, while Africa's rose from 1.9 to 2.9. Two societies at different points on one curve were facing youth pressure and ageing pressure in the same years.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating ageing and population growth as separate subjects. They are stages two and four of a single transition, and an answer that grasps this can explain why "too many people" and "too few people" were simultaneous world problems - which is precisely the sophistication a demographic-transition question rewards.

### Cumulative check E2 - what the evidence says about population policy

**Question:** Using the evidence across Lessons 18 to 21, state what actually reduced fertility and what did not.

**Model answer:** Four things are supported. First, falling child mortality, because the tradition of having as many children as possible existed to combat high infant mortality and ensure family continuation, and the European infant-mortality series - from 142 to 52 per thousand in England and 256 to 80 in Austria between 1880-90 and 1931-38 - marks the change in the underlying calculation. Second, availability and information, since the account names ignorance of methods and the expense of contraceptives as two of the three reasons families were not limited, and India and Pakistan's clinics addressed exactly those by advising on methods and supplying cheap contraceptives. Third, contraceptive use itself, where the correlation is strong: India at 35 per cent in 1986 with the birth rate falling from 4.5 to 3.2 between 1978 and 1986, China 74 per cent with 3.2 to 2.1, Colombia 65 per cent with 4.3 to 2.6 and South Korea 70 per cent with 3.5 to 1.6, against Kenya and Pakistan both under 20 per cent with 4.6 unchanged. Fourth, rising living standards, which Paul Johnson argued would slow the birth rate as Asia, Latin America and Africa industrialised. What is not supported is the necessity of coercion: China's fall accompanied the most coercive phase in the history of the one-child policy, with more than 16 million women and over 4 million men sterilized in 1983 alone according to Matthew Connelly, yet South Korea and Colombia achieved comparable falls in the same years without it. Nor is publicity alone sufficient - the three-child campaigns moved slowly because ancient practices and attitudes were difficult to change.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the contraceptive table as one-directional proof that supply drives fertility. Use is both a cause of lower fertility and a measure of the desire for it, and China's 74 per cent was recorded under compulsion. The defensible claim is that supply is necessary but not sufficient: it converts an existing desire to limit families into practice, and does little where the desire has not formed.

### Cumulative check E3 - the century in one causal statement

**Question:** Write a single paragraph that states the relationship between the economic and the demographic halves of this topic, with evidence from each.

**Model answer:** They are the same history observed through two variables, and the causal traffic runs both ways. Economic change produced the demographic change: increasing industrialisation, economic growth and prosperity meant the resources were available to sustain a larger population in nineteenth-century Europe and North America; advances in medical science and sanitation, with Pasteur and Lister in the 1860s and piped water and drainage in the industrial cities, reduced the death rate; and in the poorer regions modern medical and hygiene techniques brought smallpox, malaria and typhoid under control so that child mortality fell and people lived longer. The demographic change then acted on the economy: in Europe it stimulated development, providing a plentiful workforce and more buyers, while in the poorer regions governments were forced to spend their valuable cash feeding, housing and educating growing populations, using up resources they would have preferred to spend on industrialising and modernising, so development was delayed. Health and development proved to be one subject in the sharpest possible way when life expectancy in southern Africa, which had reached the sixties by 1990, fell back to the lower forties and to 33 in Zimbabwe, as a working-age mortality shock removed the cohort that produced output and raised children. And both halves met the same limit, since the green revolution's ceiling in the mid-1980s was set by water supply, topsoil and phosphates - the identical constraints named in the account of resource exhaustion.

**Misconception to avoid:** Writing the two halves as separate essays joined by a transition sentence. The examinable insight is the interaction: public health was an output of economic development and an input into demographic change; population growth was an outcome of falling mortality and a constraint on investment; and the physical limits bound the food supply and the growth model simultaneously.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ All six questions below are **original practice**, because this topic owns no verified question in the 2018-2026 span. ⚠️ Each model answer is written to the stated word ceiling and is not to be exceeded; each carries a distinct scoring note.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

**Question:** Examine why the Wall Street Crash of October 1929 is treated as a symptom rather than as the cause of the Great Depression. *(Answer in 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** Because the conditions were in place before the market fell. Industrialists, helped by mechanisation, were already producing more than the home market could absorb; stocks built up, output was cut, men were laid off and, with no unemployment benefit, their families bought less. Purchasing power could not close the gap: wages rose about 8 per cent between 1923 and 1929, only 1.4 per cent in real terms, against a 72 per cent rise in profits, and credit had postponed the limit rather than removed it. The escape route was closed by policy: the Fordney-McCumber tariff of 1922 denied Europeans the earnings to buy American goods and provoked retaliation. The account's own conditional is decisive: a slump was still not inevitable provided exports held. The Crash of 24 October 1929 destroyed confidence, triggered bank runs and converted a developing contraction into collapse - a transmission event, not a cause.

> **Scoring note.** The discriminator is the conditional. A script that lists four causes and then says the Crash was a symptom has asserted the examiner's own point; a script that quotes the conditionality about exports has shown *why* the tariff, not the stock market, carries the causal weight. Two figures - 8 against 72 per cent, and 1922 - are enough evidence at this length.

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

**Question:** Discuss why the twentieth-century population explosion is better described as a mortality event than as a fertility event. *(Answer in 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** Because the curve that moved first was the death rate. In Europe and North America, medical science and sanitation reduced mortality - Pasteur and Lister on germs and antiseptics in the 1860s, piped water and drainage in the industrial cities - and infant deaths per thousand births fell between 1880-90 and 1931-38 from 142 to 52 in England and 256 to 80 in Austria. In poorer regions the same movement came later, as hygiene and medicine controlled smallpox, malaria and typhoid, so child mortality fell and lives lengthened. Fertility did not follow, because large families had been a rational strategy against high infant mortality and the attitude persisted, reinforced where contraceptives were unknown or too expensive and where the Church forbade them. The explosion is the gap between a death rate that fell quickly and a birth rate still set for the previous mortality regime.

> **Scoring note.** The top band requires the *reason* for the lag, not merely its existence. Naming the old high-mortality rationale for large families and observing that norms are transmitted rather than recalculated converts a demographic observation into a social explanation - and it is also what makes the policy conclusion of the welfare route intelligible.

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

**Question:** "Globalisation reduced the gap between nations for some and widened it for others." Critically examine. *(Answer in 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** The statement is correct, and its value lies in identifying the mechanism rather than the verdict: integration rewards existing capability.

The reduction is documented. Some states industrialised rapidly and with great success - Singapore, Taiwan, South Korea and Hong Kong, with Thailand, Malaysia, Brazil and Mexico among others - and the GNPs of the four Pacific tigers compared favourably with many European Community countries. The engine was relocation: these states attracted northern firms seeking much cheaper labour, and some shifted production entirely.

The widening is equally documented. States left with the one-product economies of colonial days lacked the cash to diversify, and their terms of trade collapsed: a tonne of copper bought 115 barrels of oil in 1975 and 58 in 1980, with cocoa, coffee and cotton falling similarly, while manufactured prices rose and UNCTAD achieved no real improvement. Debt then compounded - $500 billion owed by 1980, with $50 billion of annual interest, some met by further borrowing - and in 1989-90 the GNP of the North averaged over 24 times that of the South.

Two qualifications prevent a one-sided verdict. Gains and losses ran inside countries too: after 1990 the world's labour force doubled and real wages fell in the USA, Japan and Germany, which is why globalisation became contested in rich states. And external structure did not determine outcomes, since Libya built a welfare state from oil revenue that other producers did not spend wisely.

Integration therefore raised aggregate output and did not by itself determine distribution.

> **Scoring note.** The mark ceiling is reached by the mechanism sentence, not by the two lists. A script that alternates success stories and failures without stating that integration rewards existing capability has illustrated the quotation rather than examined it. The internal-inequality qualification is the second discriminator, because most scripts discuss only inequality between nations.

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

**Question:** Compare the welfare and coercive routes to fertility reduction, and account for the criticism that the coercive route attracted. *(Answer in 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** The routes differ in what they act upon, and evidence separates them.

The welfare route acts on the conditions that make large families rational. Falling child mortality removed the purpose of high fertility, a tradition combating infant mortality and ensuring family continuation. Availability converted intention into practice: India, Pakistan and many African governments established family-planning clinics advising on methods, supplied cheap contraceptives and campaigned for a maximum of three children. Where use was high, fertility fell - India 35 per cent of married women in 1986, birth rate 4.5 to 3.2 between 1978 and 1986; Colombia 65 per cent, 4.3 to 2.6; South Korea 70 per cent, 3.5 to 1.6 - while Kenya and Pakistan, both under 20 per cent, stayed at 4.6. Paul Johnson argued industrialisation and rising living standards would do the same work.

The coercive route acts on the birth directly. In 1978 Chinese scientists calculated that without a one-child limit the country faced disaster; the policy came in 1980. Matthew Connelly records the most coercive phase: intrauterine devices for all women with one child, sterilisation for parents with two or more, termination of unauthorised pregnancies, and in 1983 over 16 million women and 4 million men sterilized.

Criticism followed from three facts. The methods were applied without consent; the All-China Women's Federation, a body inside the system, demanded an end to infanticide and abuse of women; and the necessity claim fails: South Korea and Colombia achieved comparable falls voluntarily in those years.

> **Scoring note.** Credit the necessity argument above all. Most scripts either condemn the policy morally or accept the government's consequentialist defence that it succeeded and was therefore justified. The answer that scores highest is the one that uses the account's own comparative table to show that equivalent declines occurred elsewhere without compulsion, because that defeats the defence on its own terms.

## Question 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

**Question:** Assess the proposition that the twentieth-century world economy was designed after 1944 and undesigned after 1971. *(Answer in 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** The proposition is sound and requires two qualifications.

The design is real. The failures of 1929-33 were specific - credit withdrawn without warning, defensive tariffs contracting trade, unilateral currency choices deepening demand collapse, no institution able to lend or coordinate - and the answer matched them. Bretton Woods in July 1944 created the International Monetary Fund for balance-of-payments adjustment and the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development for long-term finance, both United Nations specialized agencies, while trade proceeded by negotiated reciprocal reduction through the General Agreement of 1947 and the World Trade Organization from 1995.

The undesigning is equally real, and began with the designer. Defence costs and the Vietnam war drained the treasury, deficits ran every year in the late 1960s covered by selling gold reserves, and by 1971 the dollar was weakening, so Nixon devalued by about 12 per cent and imposed a 10 per cent duty on most imports - abandoning fixed rates and open trade at once. Floating rates and freer capital movement followed, the precondition for financial globalisation.

The first qualification: design did not cease; it changed direction. The act of November 1999 cancelled the Depression-era separation of investment banking from public deposits, and derivatives were exempted from regulation - decisions, not accidents.

The second: rules bound asymmetrically throughout. Steel duties of March 2002 were cancelled in December 2003 under threat of retaliation, with new tariffs on Chinese textiles in the same month.

The order was therefore redesigned, not abandoned.

> **Scoring note.** Two moves lift this to the top band: recognising that deregulation is itself a design decision rather than an absence of one, and using the steel episode to show that enforcement depended on the counterparty's capacity to retaliate. A script that treats 1971 as the end of rules has missed that the rules after 1971 were different rules, and that someone chose them.

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

**Question:** "The economic and demographic histories of the twentieth century cannot be written separately." Evaluate with reference to the period since 1900. *(Answer in 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** The claim holds, and traffic runs both ways.

Economic change produced demographic change. Industrialisation and prosperity made resources available for larger populations in Europe and North America, while medical science and sanitation reduced mortality - Pasteur and Lister in the 1860s, piped water and drainage in industrial cities - and infant deaths per thousand births fell between 1880-90 and 1931-38 from 142 to 52 in England and 256 to 80 in Austria. In poorer regions the same techniques controlled smallpox, malaria and typhoid decades later, so the explosion is a mortality event before a fertility one.

Demographic change then acted on economies, in opposite directions. In nineteenth-century Europe growth stimulated development through a plentiful workforce and more buyers. In poorer regions governments spent on feeding, housing and educating growing populations with resources they would have preferred to spend on industry, so development was delayed - and people forced off insufficient land reached cities with neither houses nor jobs.

Clearest proof: a health shock. Life expectancy in southern Africa, in the sixties by 1990, fell to the lower forties and to 33 in Zimbabwe, as a substantial part of the labour force was lost with its skills, especially in farming and food production.

Both histories met one limit: the green revolution's ceiling of the mid-1980s, set by water supply, topsoil and phosphates - the constraints named in the account of resource exhaustion.

A separate demographic history would have no causes; a separate economic history would have no consequences.

> **Scoring note.** The discriminator is the shared constraint at the end. Many scripts can connect public health to population; very few notice that the food ceiling and the resource-exhaustion limits are the same three items - water, topsoil and phosphates - which converts the two-variable argument into a single physical account of the growth model. The life-expectancy reversal is the second discriminator, because it proves development gains are reversible without war.

# REMEDIATION

⚠️ Six error families, each with the correction and the evidence that settles it. ⚠️ These are the mistakes that most often cost marks on this topic, drawn from the traps recorded lesson by lesson.

## Error family A - dates and what they date

| ❌ The error | ✅ The correction |
|---|---|
| Dating American industrial leadership to 1945 | Britain produced roughly twice as much coal and pig iron as the USA in 1880; by 1900 the USA produced more coal and about twice as much pig iron and steel; by 1914 it was the world's leading industrial nation |
| Treating the Crash as the start of the Depression's causes | The tariff dates from 1922, the wage-profit divergence from 1923-29, speculative momentum from about 1926; the Crash is 24 October 1929 and rock bottom is 1932 |
| Running the New Deal dates together | Measures ran 1933-1940; Farmers' Relief Act and Tennessee Valley Authority 1933; Securities Exchange Commission 1934; Social Security Act 1935; Fair Labour Standards Act 1938; the spending cut and second recession 1938; unemployment below a million in 1943 |
| Confusing the three institutional dates | Bretton Woods July 1944; General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade signed 1947; World Trade Organization from 1995 |
| Dating the end of the monetary order to 1973 | The devaluation of about 12 per cent and the 10 per cent import duty were in 1971; the OPEC price rise was 1973 and the second shock 1979 |
| Collapsing the population series | About 500 million by 1650; 1,200 million by 1850; 2 billion in 1927; past 6 billion by 2000; 7 billion at the end of 2011 |
| Collapsing the population-policy chronology | Geneva congress 1925; the union in Paris 1926; the UN Population Commission from 1945; China's one-child policy announced 1980 after the 1978 calculation; Cairo 1994; the universal two-child policy announced October 2015 |
| Running the 2008 sequence together | Bernanke's speech 15 June 2007; Northern Rock September 2007; Bear Stearns March 2008; Lehman, AIG, HBOS and Fortis in September 2008; Obama's stimulus February 2009; UK quantitative easing March 2009; the Greek revision October 2009 |

## Error family B - figures and their attribution

| ❌ The error | ✅ The correction |
|---|---|
| Quoting figures without their year | Every figure in this topic is dated: 43, 45, 60 and 74 per cent are 1945 production shares; the 24-fold GNP ratio is 1989-90; the Japan, Norway and Ethiopia figures are 1992 |
| Reproducing the disputed rescue-programme total | The account's figure for the total taxpayer debt attributed to the American rescue programme in an official report of July 2009 cannot be reconciled with its own adjacent magnitudes; teach the event, not the total |
| Reproducing the internally impossible population quotation | A quoted comparison of world population twenty years apart in the population chapter is arithmetically impossible against the chapter's own series of 5,300 million in 1990 and 7 billion in 2011; it is not reproduced |
| Using the American unemployment rate given for the end of 2010 | The account gives 15 million unemployed at the end of 2010 in one passage and a 15 per cent rate in another; only the first is used here |
| Treating Connelly's Chinese figures as unowned facts | More than 16 million women and over 4 million men sterilized, nearly 18 million IUD insertions and over 14 million abortions are Matthew Connelly's figures for 1983, quoted by the account |
| Merging the derivatives figure with a market total | In 2007 the world's stock market companies were worth $63 trillion and derivative investments $596 trillion - the comparison is the point and neither figure stands alone |
| Generalising the Japanese silk ratio | Raw silk fell to less than one-fifth of its 1923 price by 1932; the ratio belongs to that commodity and no other |
| Averaging or updating any series | No figure anywhere in this session is rounded, totalled, averaged, converted between currencies or carried forward to a later year |

## Error family C - mechanisms and causation

| ❌ The error | ✅ The correction |
|---|---|
| "The Crash caused the Depression" | It was a symptom of a problem whose real causes lay much deeper; it destroyed confidence and triggered bank runs, converting a contraction into a collapse |
| "The Depression spread because economies are connected" | Name the channels in order of speed: credit withdrawal, banking failure, trade contraction, tariff retaliation, commodity collapse, currency policy |
| "The population explosion was caused by more births" | Death rates fell rapidly through medicine, hygiene and clean water while birth rates adjusted slowly; the explosion is the gap |
| "Population growth caused poverty" | The relationship is two-way with a mediating variable: growth diverted resources from investment to subsistence, while poverty, high infant mortality and the cost of contraceptives sustained high fertility |
| "Oil surpluses financed the debt crisis" | Not established by the evidence held; write the sequence - heavy borrowing preceded the crisis and the debt became self-compounding |
| "Neoliberalism caused the 2008 crisis" | Name the November 1999 act and the derivatives exemption; the broader judgement is Paul Mason's and should be attributed |
| "Globalisation caused inequality" | It deepened integration, which redistributed gains by capability; real wages fell in the leading western economies while tiger economies converged |
| "The Depression caused Nazi rule" | A necessary condition, not a sufficient one; the same slump struck the USA, Britain and France without the same constitutional outcome |

## Error family D - categories and comparisons

| ❌ The error | ✅ The correction |
|---|---|
| Treating "capitalism" as one system | Two models coexisted inside the capitalist bloc, differing on welfare, regulation, union strength and whether shareholders or the whole business come first |
| Treating "the Third World" as one economic actor | Some states industrialised rapidly and with great success while others remained at $110 per head; the category lost its analytical value |
| Calling the WTO a UN specialized agency | The Fund and the Bank are; the WTO belongs to the wider multilateral trade system and is not |
| Confusing external and internal devaluation | Mussolini refused to devalue the lira until 1936 and cut wages and salaries instead; refusing the first forces the second |
| Confusing push-driven and pull-driven urbanisation | Nineteenth-century European cities grew because industry was hiring; post-1945 shanty towns grew because the countryside could no longer support its people |
| Treating "the AIDS epidemic" as one phenomenon | Blood products and injections in China, injecting drug use in Russia and Ukraine, unprotected heterosexual sex in southern Africa |
| Treating rates and increments as the same | 1.9 per cent a year after 1960 is a rate; 95 million in 1994 and 100 million in 1995 are increments on a growing base |
| Treating ageing as a separate subject from growth | They are stages four and two of one transition, which is why rich and poor societies faced opposite anxieties in the same decades |

## Error family E - evidence boundaries and what must not be asserted

| ❌ The error | ✅ The correction |
|---|---|
| Constructing a gold-standard argument | The sources held here do not discuss the gold standard, convertibility or departures from it; their only gold reference is the sale of American reserves in the late 1960s, used in Lesson 7 as recorded strain. Use the lira case and say the wider regime is outside the evidence |
| Attributing New Deal fiscal policy to Keynes | Only the Brain Trust of economists and university academics is named; the account attributes quantitative easing to Keynes in the 1930s, which is a separate claim |
| Adopting the First, Second and Third New Deal periodisation | Michael Heale is recorded as regarding it as an oversimplification and the administration as never governed by a single political ideology |
| Using "stagflation" with figures | The sources do not use or define the term and supply no inflation or unemployment series |
| Asserting communist-bloc economic performance from this session | The passage describing the bloc is degraded in the evidence held; the records belong to the topics that own them |
| Attributing the demographic transition model to a named demographer | The model and its criticisms belong to the geography owner |
| Reporting any forecast as an outcome | The Brandt targets, the Population Institute's projection of December 1995, the Stanford calculation of 1996, the Goldman Sachs BRIC ranking and the IMF projection of July 2026 are all dated expectations |
| Importing climate treaties, targets or conference outcomes | Owned by the environment-and-ecology topic, dated and sourced separately |

## Error family F - scope, voice and the wrong paper

| ❌ The error | ✅ The correction |
|---|---|
| Narrating the Cold War | This topic owns its economic consequence - armament and space expenditure and the contemporary argument about its opportunity cost - not its diplomacy |
| Narrating the first Industrial Revolution | Owned by its own topic; this session begins from the world that process had already produced |
| Narrating the fascist regimes | Owned by their topic; German, Italian and Japanese evidence is used here only to show what the Depression did |
| Retelling Latin America's import substitution and debt crisis | Owned by the Latin American topic; this session teaches the general mechanism and hands the regional case across |
| Writing India's 1991 reform content | Owned by the economy topic; cross-refer and keep any comparison to a signposted clause |
| Writing a climate-policy answer | The world-history claim is the growth model, the industrial cause of warming and the dating of awareness to the 1970s |
| Opening with a current growth forecast | A history answer that begins with this year's projection has announced that it does not know which century it is writing about |
| Moralising instead of naming a mechanism | "Exploitation" is a verdict; administered prices at both ends, value added elsewhere and a self-reinforcing revenue trap are checkable claims |
