# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## 1. The world economy of 1900

In one sense a single world economy already existed: a few highly industrialised countries - mainly the USA, Britain and Germany - provided the world's manufactured goods while the rest supplied raw materials and food, known as primary products. European nations usually decided what their colonies produced, Britain making Uganda and the Sudan grow cotton for its textile industry and Portugal doing the same in Mozambique, and prices were fixed as low as possible for colonial products and as high as possible for manufactured exports; Basil Davidson's formulation, quoted by the account, is that "the Africans had to sell cheap and buy dear". The USA treated Latin America, especially Mexico, as an area of "influence" in the same way. Industrial leadership was already moving: Britain produced roughly twice as much coal and pig iron as the USA in 1880, the position was reversed by 1900, and by 1914 the USA was the world's leading industrial nation. Master formulation: integration without convergence - gains are distributed by what each participant can supply, and what most of the world could supply had been decided for it. Boundary: the first Industrial Revolution and Latin America's regional history belong to their own owners.

## 2. The two wars as economic events

The First World War disrupted trade so that China and the Latin American states bought from the USA and Japan instead, and after the war they continued to do so; the USA was the economic winner and grew richer on interest from war loans to Britain and her allies; only the USA was rich enough to lend for German recovery in the 1920s, which linked Europe too closely with the USA financially and economically, so that when the USA suffered its slump of 1929-35 the rest of the world was thrown into depression, with American unemployment at around 14 million in 1933, about a quarter of the total labour force (⚠️ the account's separate 25 million American and 50 million world pair diverges from this measure and must be attributed if used). The Second World War left the USA the greatest industrial and military power: at its end the USA produced 43 per cent of the world's iron ore, 45 per cent of its crude steel, 60 per cent of its railway locomotives and 74 per cent of its motor vehicles, and in 1945 American incomes were twice Britain's and seven times the USSR's, with production almost doubling again over the next thirty years. The boom continued as industry switched to consumer goods that had been in short supply; Marshall Aid followed, with the two recorded ulterior motives of keeping the American boom going through European purchases and making western Europe less likely to go communist. Master formulation: 1918 produced American money without American rules; 1945 produced American money with American rules.

## 3. The Depression: causes

Domestic overproduction, as industrialists helped by mechanisation produced more than the home market could absorb, with unsold stocks, reduced output, lay-offs and, with no unemployment benefit, falling family purchasing power - the same logic applying to farmers. Unequal income: wages rose about 8 per cent between 1923 and 1929, only 1.4 per cent in real terms, while profits rose 72 per cent, and credit postponed the limit until by 1929 the public was fast approaching it; at the beginning of 1929 millions still had no radio, washing machine or car. The decisive conditional: a slump was still not inevitable provided the Americans could export their surplus. Falling exports: the Fordney-McCumber tariff of 1922 kept foreign goods out, denied Europeans the profits needed to buy American goods or service war debts, and provoked retaliation - "a slump of some sort was clearly on the way". Speculation from about 1926: the average share rose from $9 in 1924 to $26 in 1929, Radio Corporation from $85 early in 1928 to $505 in September 1929 while paying no dividends, with savings spent, money borrowed, brokers selling on credit and banks speculating with deposits; the reversal mechanism was self-fulfilling expectation. The Crash: 24 October 1929, nearly 13 million shares dumped; about 25 per cent of the previous peak by mid-1930; rock bottom in 1932. The decisive sentence: the Crash did not cause the depression, it was just a symptom of a problem whose real causes lay much deeper.

## 4. The Depression: transmission

Six channels, ranked by speed. Credit withdrawal - the USA stopped further loans and called in short-term loans to Germany, requiring no fall in trade at all. Banking failure - over 25,000 US banks in 1929 and fewer than 15,000 by 1933, with ordinary depositors ruined, and in Germany a crisis of confidence in the currency and a run on the banks. Trade contraction - worldwide over-production, German exports severely reduced, Japanese exports shrinking disastrously, Italian exports falling further. Tariff retaliation - other countries raising tariffs against Japanese goods to safeguard their own industries, a collective-action failure. Commodity collapse - Japanese raw silk, sold mostly to the USA, below one-fifth of its 1923 price by 1932, with about half of Japan's farmers dependent on silk as well as rice. Currency policy - Mussolini refusing to devalue the lira until 1936 and cutting wages and salaries instead, with wages falling faster than prices and workers losing over 10 per cent in real terms. Then mass unemployment - over 6 million in Germany by 1932, 1.1 million in Italy, around 14 million in the USA or about a quarter of the labour force - and political transmission: extreme right-wing governments in Japan and Germany, and the judgement that without the crisis Hitler would probably never have come to power, which is a necessary and not a sufficient condition. No gold-standard mechanism is asserted anywhere.

## 5. The Depression: the instruments

Three declared aims - relief, recovery and reform - with measures running 1933-1940, advice from the Brain Trust of economists and university academics, and a complete change from the laissez-faire Republicans. Four functional families. Financial stabilisation: temporary government control of the banks and a guarantee to depositors against loss in a future crisis, which restored confidence so that money flowed back in, and the Securities Exchange Commission of 1934 requiring a down payment of at least 50 per cent instead of 10 per cent on shares bought on credit. Income and agricultural support: the Farmers' Relief Act of 1933 and the Agricultural Adjustment Administration paying compensation for reduced output to raise prices, which helped farmers but threw many farm labourers out of work and did little for the Kansas, Oklahoma and Texas dustbowl; the Fair Labour Standards Act of 1938 with a maximum working week, a minimum wage in certain low-paid trades and most child labour made illegal; accepted collective bargaining, with General Motors and US Steel forced to recognise unions; the Social Security Act of 1935 as an important step towards a welfare state; mortgage loans for householders; higher taxes on the wealthy; and trade agreements reducing American tariffs reciprocally. Coordinated production and public works: public works schemes; the Tennessee Valley Authority of 1933 building dams for cheap electricity and organising conservation, irrigation and afforestation; slum clearance; and the first-phase codes and agencies including the NRA, which the Supreme Court ruled unconstitutional. Institutional capacity: willingness to intervene and to spend, national direction of resources and increased federal authority over the states. No attribution of the fiscal policy to Keynes; no adoption of the First/Second/Third New Deal periodisation, which Michael Heale is recorded as regarding as an oversimplification.

## 6. The Depression: verdict and counter-case

Social effects: investors and their creditors ruined; bank collapse destroying the savings of millions who had had nothing to do with the speculation; industrial production in 1933 only half the 1929 total; around 14 million unemployed, about a quarter of the labour force; one in eight farmers losing all their property; bread queues, soup kitchens and evictions. Achievements: relief and millions of extra jobs; confidence restored in the financial system and in government, possibly preventing a violent revolution; public works and the TVA of lasting value; the 1935 Social Security Act; and permanent national direction of resources, accepted collective bargaining and increased federal authority. Opposition from four directions: business objecting to unions, wage and hour regulation and higher taxes as stifling private enterprise; state governments resenting federal interference; the Supreme Court ruling several measures including the NRA unconstitutional before becoming more amenable after five further appointments; and socialists who thought it insufficient, with Huey Long's Share Our Wealth of 1934 financed by taxing the rich, and his assassination in September 1935. Limits: unemployment under 8 million by 1937 but still serious, 10.5 million after the 1938 cut, and below a million only in 1943 through the war effort, with GNP almost doubling 1939-45 and about 7 million extra jobs by 1945. Counter-case: Nazi public works with rearmament from 1934 and conscription from 1935 cut unemployment from over 6 million to just over 2 million by end-1935, with a military budget at 52 per cent of state spending in 1938-9 - achieved through the abolition of trade unions and purges of Jews and anti-Nazis from professional employment. Graded verdict: decisive for the survival of democratic capitalism, insufficient for recovery.

## 7. The designed order, 1944-1971

The Bretton Woods conference of July 1944, with 44 Allied nations, created the International Monetary Fund to manage balance-of-payments adjustment under fixed exchange rates and the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development to finance reconstruction and development; both are United Nations specialized agencies. Trade liberalisation proceeded through the General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade signed in 1947, after the planned International Trade Organization was never established, and the World Trade Organization succeeded it in 1995 - part of the wider multilateral trade system but not a UN specialized agency. The strain: defence costs and the Vietnam war a constant drain; a budget deficit every year in the late 1960s covered by selling gold reserves; by 1971 a dollar once considered as good as gold weakening in value; Nixon forced to devalue by about 12 per cent and to place a 10 per cent duty on most imports; rising oil prices worsening the payments deficit and leading to more nuclear power. After the break, floating rates and freer capital movement became the norm - the precondition of 1990s financial globalisation. Master formulation: 1944 was the first attempt to design a world economy rather than let one emerge, which is why its authorship could be contested. No quota, voting weight, lending figure or programme name appears anywhere in this session.

## 8. The postwar settlement and two models of capitalism

The capitalist bloc - the USA, Canada, western Europe, Japan, Australia and New Zealand - believed in private enterprise, private ownership of wealth, profit as the great motivating influence and, ideally, minimum state interference. The American model held the law of private property and freedom from government interference supreme, with the federal government interfering as little as possible and its main function national security; attitudes to welfare were divided between rugged individualism and self-help on one side and a social contract under which the state provides basic welfare in return for respect and obedience on the other, the latter producing the New Deal and the Great Society; for 16 of the 24 years preceding 2005 the USA had Republican governments favouring the right-wing approach. Rawls's *A Theory of Justice* (first published 1971; the account cites the 1973 edition) argued justice as fairness, equality and a governmental duty to provide welfare and redistribute; Nozick's *Anarchy, State and Utopia* (1974) argued strict property rights and minimal government, taxation, welfare and redistribution, greatly influencing the New Right and the neo-conservative Republicans under Reagan (1981-9) and George W. Bush (2001-9) - and, extended internationally, producing American reluctance on debt relief, trade and global warming. The European model provided social welfare and public services, particularly education and health, and reduced inequality, with an active regulating and redistributing state and business responsibilities to employees, wages and the environment; shareholders were paramount in the USA while in most of Europe the interests of the entire business came first, with low dividends protecting investment and stronger but responsible unions. Volkswagen is the structural case: about 20 per cent of shares held by the state government of Lower Saxony, voting rights capped at 20 per cent and only 16 per cent of profits paid as dividends, none of which would be allowed in the USA; Michelin, Nokia and Airbus are the other named successes; even in Italy, Spain, Greece and Portugal the social contract exists and unemployment insurance is the highest in Europe. The 1990s dispute over European unemployment - American blame on taxation, welfare, unions and regulation, European blame on the disinflation needed for the single currency launched in 1999 - is not settled by the evidence held.

## 9. Two blocs, two systems

After 1945 the world split into capitalist and communist blocs, and the following forty or so years seemed like a contest. The passage describing the communist bloc's economic principles is degraded in the evidence held here, and its record belongs to the topics that own the USSR and China; this session does not reconstruct it. The recorded economic consequence is that both blocs spent enormous amounts of cash on building nuclear weapons and other armaments and on even more expensive space programmes, and that many people argued that much of this money could have been better spent helping to solve the problems of the world's poorer nations - an opportunity-cost claim about displacement, attributed rather than asserted, and best paired with the Brandt arithmetic that for the price of one jet fighter, about $20 million, 40,000 village pharmacies could be set up. The syllabus clause on political philosophies and their effect on society is best answered by showing variation within each philosophy, using the New Deal as the internal-alteration case and the shared armament criticism as the comparative point that requires no verdict.

## 10. The Third World and the North-South divide

The term Third World was coined in the 1950s for states outside the industrialised capitalist First World and the industrialised communist Second World, and their number grew rapidly as the European empires broke up; by 1970 the group comprised Africa, Asia except the USSR and China, India, Pakistan, Bangladesh, Latin America and the Middle East, almost all former colonies or mandates left undeveloped at independence. Non-alignment meant avoiding close association with either bloc and deep suspicion of the motives of both; Nehru of India (1947-64) saw himself as a sort of unofficial leader of a Third World he thought could be a powerful force for world peace; neo-colonialism named the continued interference of both blocs, with Guatemala 1954, the Dominican Republic 1965 and Chile 1973 as the American cases and British, French and Soviet interference in the Middle East; at Havana in 1979, 92 nations were represented, by then roughly 70 per cent of the world's population. The poverty evidence: 70 per cent of the world's population consumed 30 per cent of the world's food while the USA, with perhaps 8 per cent, ate 40 per cent, causing shortages of proteins and vitamins, poor health and a high death rate. The Brandt Report of 1980, chaired by Willy Brandt and including Edward Heath, divided the world into a North of North America, Europe, the USSR and Japan plus Australia and New Zealand and a South of most Third World countries, concluding that the North was getting richer and the South poorer; in 1989-90 the North's GNP averaged over 24 times the South's, and in 1992 Japan was over $28,000 per head and Norway $25,800 against Ethiopia's $110, the second lowest in the world. Its recommendations were 0.7 per cent of national income in aid by 1985 and 1.0 per cent by 2000; a new World Development Fund with decision-making shared more evenly between lenders and borrowers, unlike the IMF and World Bank which it described as dominated by the USA; an international energy plan; and an agricultural campaign with an international food programme. By 1985 only Norway, Sweden, Denmark, the Netherlands and France had reached 0.7 per cent, the USA giving 0.24 and Britain 0.11; famine struck Ethiopia and the Sudan; at the end of 2003 the UN reported 21 Third World states, 17 in Africa, in crisis from natural disasters, AIDS, global warming and civil wars, while the richest 1 per cent, around 60 million people, received as much income as the poorest 57 per cent - Norway's life expectancy 78.7 with virtually 100 per cent literacy and income just under $30,000 against Sierra Leone's 35, 35 per cent and $470. UN economic advisers called on the West to remove trade barriers, dismantle over-generous subsidies, provide greater debt relief and double aid from $50 billion to $100 billion a year.

## 11. Why the South stayed poor

Six mechanisms. Dependence through neo-colonialism, with the North expecting the South to keep providing food and raw materials and not encouraging its industries. One-product economies left from colonial days that governments lacked the cash to diversify - Ghana with cocoa, Zambia with copper. Export crops displacing food, so scarce money went on expensive food imports. Commodity price collapse in the 1970s in cocoa, copper, coffee and cotton, hitting Ghana and Cameroon, Zambia, Chile and Peru, Mozambique, Egypt and the Sudan, and Ivory Coast, Zaire and Ethiopia. Rising manufactured prices with no real improvement from UNCTAD's attempts to negotiate fairer terms. And aid on a business basis carrying interest and sometimes tied to the lender's goods, with direct bank borrowing producing a debt of about $500 billion by 1980 on which the annual interest was about $50 billion and some states borrowed again merely to pay it - the compounding trap. The terms-of-trade table is the evidential centre: a tonne of copper bought 115 barrels of oil in 1975 and 58 in 1980, cocoa 148 and 63, coffee 148 and 82, cotton 119 and 60, with dollar equivalents falling proportionately. Two further pressures: populations growing much faster than in the North, with world population about 4,000 million in 1975 and expected to reach 6,000 million by 1997, so a larger share of humanity than ever would be poor; and long crippling wars in Ethiopia, Nicaragua, Guatemala, Lebanon, the Congo/Zaire, Sudan, Somalia, Liberia, Sierra Leone, Mozambique and Angola, with drought in Niger, which in 1974 produced only half the food crops of 1970 and lost about 40 per cent of its cattle. Sort the list as five structural mechanisms, one financial mechanism, one distributional fact and two shocks.

## 12. Oil, 1973

In 1973 OPEC members, partly in an attempt to conserve oil supplies, began to charge more for their oil; the organisation had been founded at Baghdad in September 1960 by Iran, Iraq, Kuwait, Saudi Arabia and Venezuela, and resource exhaustion is separately recorded as one of the reasons it tried to conserve oil during the 1970s. The Middle East producers, Nigeria and Libya made huge profits, though this did not necessarily mean governments spent the money wisely; Libya is the recorded exception, Gaddafi using much of the profits on agricultural and industrial development and a welfare state and reaching a GNP of £5,460 in 1989. For importers, rising oil prices worsened America's balance-of-payments deficit and led to more nuclear power; for single-commodity exporters the same shock arrived twice over, since the terms of trade collapsed simultaneously. The recycling link - that producer surpluses financed developing-world borrowing - is not established by the evidence held, and the sequence is written instead: heavy borrowing preceded the crisis and the debt became self-compounding. A second shock followed in 1979 with the Iranian revolution. "Stagflation" is not used or defined in these sources and carries no figures. Master formulation: 1971 ended the monetary order; 1973 redistributed the real economy inside the disorder that followed. Boom and bust are the same structural vulnerability.

## 13. The split in the South

Two routes produced divergence. Resource wealth, with the qualification above. And industrialisation: Singapore, Taiwan, South Korea and Hong Kong, the four Pacific tigers, with Thailand, Malaysia, Brazil and Mexico among others, achieving GNPs comparing favourably with many European Community countries. The engine was stated plainly: these states attracted firms from the North keen to take advantage of much cheaper labour, and some firms shifted all production, undercutting northern goods. The consequence in the North was serious problems and high unemployment during the 1990s, with the recorded observation that the golden days of western prosperity might have gone unless workers accepted lower wages or companies accepted lower profits - a distributional choice. The frontier then moved: by the mid-1990s the tigers were losing jobs to Malaysia and the Philippines, with Indonesia and China industrialising on even lower wages and longer hours. Chirac's intervention of April 1996 asked that developing countries not compete by allowing miserable wages and working conditions, calling for union freedom and collective bargaining and the abolition of forced and child labour - conditions most developing countries had accepted on joining the International Labour Organization, though accepting conditions and keeping to them were two different things. The demand is simultaneously principled and self-interested. Master formulation: the same world economy that produced Singapore produced Sierra Leone, and any explanation that cannot accommodate both is not an explanation.

## 14. The neoliberal turn

Strain first: the Vietnam drain, annual deficits in the late 1960s covered by selling gold reserves, a weakening dollar, the devaluation and import duty of 1971 and an oil-driven payments problem. Doctrine second: Reagan (1981-9) refused to cut defence spending and tried policies recommended by Milton Friedman, who argued that governments should abandon all attempts to plan their economies and concentrate on monetarism - tight control of the money supply by keeping interest rates high, so as to force businesses to be more efficient - policies Margaret Thatcher was already trying in Britain, and which at first seemed to be working. Sir Keith Joseph, a supporter, defined neo-liberalism as the strict and unflinching control of money supply, substantial cuts in tax and public spending and bold incentives and encouragements to the wealth creators. Instrument third: in November 1999 an act designed to promote growth through competition and freedom cancelled the Depression-era regulation preventing investment banks from handling the savings and deposits of the general public, giving them access to far larger funds and allowing banks to act as insurance companies; a year later futures and all other derivatives were exempted from classification as gambling and attempts to regulate the market were declared illegal. High interest rates are the channel that connects the doctrine to sovereign borrowers. Paul Mason's judgement that neo-liberalism was mainly responsible for the 2008 catastrophe, with responsibility shared by bankers, regulators, politicians and the media, is attributed. Master formulation: the turn began as an answer to a real breakdown and ended by dismantling the protections built after the previous one.

## 15. Globalisation as uneven integration

Four deepenings after 1990. Labour: the world's labour force doubled in the twenty years after 1990 and, with increased migration, became global, as China and the former Soviet bloc joined the world economy. Wages: the greater availability of labour brought a fall in real wages in the leading western economies, including the USA, Japan and Germany. Capital: western financiers invested abroad more than ever before. Credit: consumption nevertheless grew, made possible by a massive increase in credit and the heyday of the credit-card era, until after 2000 the debts began to run out of control. The Americanization complaint is documented: in the 1990s the USA gained control of the IMF and could decide who received aid and insist on approved policies, as happened to many Latin American countries and to Korea, Indonesia and Thailand, with conditions that often made recovery harder; in 1995 the World Bank's debt-relief proposal met stiff opposition from the USA and its chief economist felt compelled to resign; in 1994 the USA used GATT to force the EU to open voice communications to competition and in 1997 the WTO agreed that 70 countries should be opened to US telecoms on American terms; by 2002, 174 of 180 commercial satellites were American and the EU launched Galileo to counter this; steel duties were imposed in March 2002, cancelled in December 2003 under threat of retaliation and followed in the same month by new tariffs on Chinese textiles and televisions. The counter-instance is the overriding of HIV/AIDS drug patents in 2003, with the recorded ulterior motive of hoped-for access to African oil and military bases. The BRIC expectation - Brazil, Russia, India and China, almost half the world's population, with China predicted to lead between 2030 and 2050 and Goldman Sachs expecting all four in the top ten by 2043 - is a dated forecast, and decoupling failed after 2008. One current linkage is carried by this session: the IMF's World Economic Outlook Update of July 2026 projected global growth of 3.0 per cent for 2026 and 3.4 per cent for 2027, with the vintage attached and no other current claim made. Master formulation: globalisation is a distributional process to be described, not a policy to be approved or rejected.

## 16. The crisis of 2008

On 15 June 2007 the chairman of the American Federal Reserve extolled a deep and liquid financial system and said there was no possibility of a financial crisis in America. Four causes. Deregulation and derivatives: the 1999-2000 measures let the derivatives market become global, and by 2007 the world's stock market companies were worth $63 trillion against $596 trillion of derivative investments, eight times the real economy; Warren Buffett had warned in 2002 that derivatives were a time bomb and financial weapons of mass destruction, and Alan Greenspan admitted in October 2008 that he had been wrong; some 360 banks received US government capital. Sub-prime lending and collateralised debt: the housing boom peaked towards the end of 2005, lenders continued pushing sub-prime mortgages as prices fell, AIG, Freddie Mac and Fannie Mae faced huge payouts, collateral debt obligations paid buyers 2-3 per cent more interest from 2000, house prices then fell around 25 per cent and one estimate put likely losses at $3.1 trillion. Leverage, short selling, short-termism and shadow banking: Lehman's leverage of 44 and debts of $613 billion; Morgan Stanley losing 10 per cent of its cash in three days before short sellers drove the price down; lending long while borrowing short, with interbank lending drying up in September 2008; and conduits and structured investment vehicles in offshore tax havens designed to get round the need for any capital cushion at all, kept secret from investors. Regulators and rating agencies: light-touch regulation from 2000; Standard and Poor's, Moody's and Fitch paid by the issuers they rated, with payments doubling to $6 billion between 2001 and 2007, an official report of July 2007 finding no convincing evidence their methods were reliable, and HBOS downgraded only hours before collapse with the outlook described as stable. The collapse and rescues: Bear Stearns rescued indirectly in March 2008, creating moral hazard; Lehman allowed to fail for want of political will; AIG bailed out with $85 billion and an 80 per cent state stake, nationalised without the word being used; Northern Rock nationalised at some £100 billion; HBOS taken by Lloyds for £12 billion and worth £4 billion weeks later; RBS 83 per cent taxpayer-owned; Fortis into joint Dutch-Belgian ownership; bailouts in Germany, France, Ireland and Iceland. Responses: China's $580 billion, Obama's $787 billion of February 2009 opposed by Republicans and the Tea Party Movement, an EU average growth reduction of 4.7 per cent at end-2009 with Estonia -14, Lithuania -15, Latvia -18 and France -3 per cent, and quantitative easing first in the UK in March 2009 at £150 billion, rejected by the ECB. The eurozone: the Greek deficit revealed in October 2009 as 12.7 per cent not 6 per cent, with over half the debt moved off balance sheet with Goldman Sachs' help; a loan of 110 billion in May 2010 with austerity; half the private debt written off in autumn 2011; Ireland at 85 billion in November 2010 (the account prints November 2011; corrected against a verified source registered in the final ledger); Portugal junk-rated in July 2011 - "Monetary union without fiscal union had failed." India: exports down a further 3 per cent from May 2011 to May 2012, investors deserting for the dollar, the rupee at a record low in June 2012, the current account deficit rising from $49 billion to an estimated $72 billion or over 4 per cent of GDP against a sustainable 2 per cent, ratings cut to negative by S&P and Fitch while Moody's held at stable, decoupling failed, and India joining China in contributing to the IMF's emergency fund in June 2012. Master formulation: integration transmits losses as efficiently as gains; 2008 exposed vulnerability rather than ending integration.

## 17. Growth and its physical limits

As the century wore on and the North became more obsessed with industrialisation, new methods were invented to increase production and efficiency, the main motive was the creation of wealth and profit and very little attention was paid to side effects. During the 1970s people became increasingly aware that all was not well, and three problems were named: exhaustion, pollution and global warming. Exhaustion: fossil fuels cannot be replaced and were being rapidly used up, oil production had increased enormously, and this was one of the reasons OPEC tried to conserve oil during the 1970s, with Britain drilling the North Sea and nuclear power developed as an alternative; tin, lead, copper, zinc and mercury were seriously depleted, and it was the Third World being stripped of the resources it needed to escape poverty; about half the tropical rainforests were lost by 1987 with about 80,000 square kilometres going each year; phosphate quarrying rose 4 per cent a year from 1950; with world population growing by 90 million a year, Stanford scientists found in 1995 that humans, farm animals, crops and plantations were using a quarter of all water taken up by plants; about 75 billion tons of soil were lost annually, at 17 tons per hectare in western Europe and the USA, 40 in Africa, Asia and South America, 220 on steep slopes in one case and 400 in parts of Jamaica; the World Conservation Strategy was set up in 1980. Pollution: all five Great Lakes described as dead in 1975 and about 10 per cent of Swedish lakes the same; acid rain damaging German and Czechoslovak trees; the USSR and eastern Europe carrying out the dirtiest industrialisation; the ozone hole found over the Antarctic in 1979, larger by 1989 with another over the Arctic, and reported by the World Meteorological Organization in 2001 as seeming to be mending; Sellafield, Three Mile Island in 1979, Chernobyl in 1986 killing 35 and contaminating British sheep pasture, and Fukushima flooded in 2011 after design weaknesses had been ignored and then denied; and remedy obstructed by cost incidence, with 27 elderly Chernobyl-type reactors still operating in eastern Europe in 1996. Terminology: the ecosystem is the interconnected system by which living creatures, trees and plants function within the environment, and ecology is its study; global warming is defined as the uncontrollable warming of the Earth's atmosphere caused by the large quantities of gases emitted from industry - carbon dioxide, methane and nitrous oxide from industrial processes and fossil-fuel burning. Genetically modified crops became a USA-EU confrontation, grown by at least 6 million farmers in 16 countries including India by 2004 with Monsanto producing more than 90 per cent worldwide, and the USA calling on the WTO to act in April 2004. Boundary: climate treaties, targets and conference outcomes belong to the environment owner. Master formulation: the environmental record is the growth model's balance sheet.

## 18. Population: the explosion as a mortality event

About 500 million by 1650; 1,200 million by 1850; 2 billion in 1927; past 6 billion by 2000; 7 billion at the end of 2011. Growth averaged 0.6 per cent a year in 1850-1900, 0.9 per cent over the next fifty years and 1.9 per cent after 1960; in 1990 the increase was roughly a million a week on a total of 5,300 million, with 95 million added in 1994, 100 million in 1995 to reach 5,750 million and 90 million in 1996, and the Population Institute recording that 90 per cent of the growth was in poor countries torn by civil strife and social unrest. Timing differed by region: Europe and North America peaked before the First World War and slowed afterwards, while Africa, Asia and Latin America accelerated after the Second World War, with Latin America slowing after 1950 and Asia and Africa continuing to rise. European and North American causes: industrialisation and prosperity making resources available; public health, with Pasteur and Lister in the 1860s and piped water and drainage in the industrial cities; falling infant mortality from scarlet fever, diphtheria and whooping cough, with deaths within one year of birth per thousand falling between 1880-90 and 1931-38 from 142 to 52 in England, 165 to 43 in Switzerland, 166 to 65 in France, 195 to 104 in Italy and 256 to 80 in Austria; and immigration, with some 35 million entering the USA in the hundred years after 1820 and a million a year just before 1914. After 1900 Europe slowed as modern contraceptive techniques spread, and the depression of the 1930s further discouraged large families. Three causes in the poorer regions: modern medicine and hygiene bringing smallpox, malaria and typhoid under control; no attempt to limit families, through ignorance and the cost of contraceptives; and the Roman Catholic prohibition, strong in Central and South America, where many countries exceeded 3 per cent growth and the Latin American average was 2.4 per cent in 1960 against Europe's 0.75, with 2 per cent doubling a population in about thirty years as happened in Brazil and Mexico to 1960. And the lag: a long tradition of having as many children as possible to combat high infant mortality and ensure family continuity persisted in spite of the reduction, with great value attached to many sons in some cultures. The demographic transition is used as a framework only; the model belongs to the geography owner. Malthus (1798) held that population would outrun food; Boserup (1965) held that population pressure stimulates agricultural innovation; the green revolution supports Boserup until the mid-1980s, when yields, water, topsoil and phosphates set limits.

## 19. Population: consequences

In nineteenth-century Europe and North America growth helped to stimulate economic development, with a plentiful workforce, more buyers, more investment and enterprise, and no great problem feeding or educating the growing numbers because prosperity made the resources available. The later, unexpected effect was on age structure: very low birth rates and longer life expectancy produced a growing proportion over 65, about 15 per cent in Sweden, France and Britain by the 1970s, and by the early 1990s questions about whether welfare systems could afford pensions. In the poorer regions, India, Pakistan and Bangladesh became overcrowded with insufficient land, forcing movement into towns that lacked houses and jobs, with many living on the streets and cities, especially in Latin America, ringed by shanty towns without water supply, sanitation or lighting - push-driven urbanisation, the economic opposite of pull-driven urbanisation. Food: the green revolution of the late 1960s and 1970s produced new strains of heavy-cropping rice and wheat on short, fast-growing stems helped by fertilizers and irrigation, so that India exported food and China became self-sufficient, while US yields tripled between 1945 and 1995 with exports to over a hundred countries - until the mid-1980s ceiling set by yields, water supply, topsoil and phosphates, with the Stanford survey of 1996 finding farmland dwindling and projecting 0.6 of an acre per American by 2050 against 1.8 in 1996. Drought and civil war in Ethiopia, Mozambique and Somalia caused severe shortages and tens of thousands of deaths from starvation. Resource diversion: governments were forced to spend on feeding, housing and educating growing populations, using resources they would have preferred to spend on industrialising and modernising, so development was delayed. Healthcare: Save the Children reported in April 1996 that over 800 million people, one-sixth of the world's population, had no access to healthcare; basic provision cost at least $12 a head a year; 16 African countries plus Bangladesh, India, Pakistan, Nepal and Vietnam spent much less, Britain spending the equivalent of $1,039, Zaire 40 cents and Tanzania 70 cents; immunisation was not being carried out, epidemics were expected and child mortality was set to rise - and almost all these states spent vastly more per head on defence than on healthcare. Liotta and Miskel calculate at least 27 megacities of over 10 million by 2025, arguing that without effective counter-measures some will present a serious security threat, a prediction explicitly conditional on governance. The relationship between population and poverty is two-way, mediated by whether resources exist and are allocated to absorb the additional people.

## 20. Population: control and its ethics

Scientific concern began soon after the First World War, with the first World Population Congress at Geneva in 1925 and an International Union for the Scientific Study of Population at Paris in 1926, whose first activity was measurement. A Population Commission was included among the UN's agencies in 1945, and from the 1950s the UN led in encouraging birth-control programmes: India and Pakistan established family-planning clinics advising on methods and providing cheap contraceptives, with campaigns recommending a maximum of three children, as in many African states, while China fixed a legal maximum of two. Progress was slow because attitudes were difficult to change and the Church continued to forbid artificial birth control in Roman Catholic South America. The evidence for 1978-86, with contraceptive use among married women in 1986: India 35 per cent, 4.5 to 3.2; China 74 per cent, 3.2 to 2.1; Colombia 65 per cent, 4.3 to 2.6; South Korea 70 per cent, 3.5 to 1.6; Kenya and Pakistan both under 20 per cent, 4.6 unchanged. The 1986 regional table shows Africa as the only region whose growth rate rose between 1950-9 and 1980-5, from 1.9 to 2.9 per cent, with the world total falling from 1.7 to 1.5; density and growth are independent, Europe at 100 per square kilometre growing 0.3 per cent against Africa at 19 growing 2.9, and Bangladesh at about 700 per square kilometre doubling its 125 million in under thirty years while Britain's 58.6 million would take 385 years. The Population Institute projected in December 1995 stabilisation at about 8 billion by 2015 with effective birth control, or 14 billion by 2050 without. Paul Johnson argued the fears were exaggerated and that industrialisation would slow the birth rate. China: scientists calculated in 1978 that without a one-child limit the country would face disaster, the policy was announced in 1980, and Matthew Connelly records the most coercive phase - IUDs for all women with one child, sterilisation for parents with two or more, termination of all unauthorised pregnancies, no pro forma injunction against coercion, and in 1983 more than 16 million women and over 4 million men sterilized, nearly 18 million IUD insertions and over 14 million abortions. The All-China Women's Federation demanded an end to infanticide and the abuse of women; there was outrage among Roman Catholics and pro-life supporters abroad; the government softened the policy while claiming it had succeeded and was therefore justified. The policy was formally ended with a universal two-child policy announced in October 2015, and the Cairo conference of 1994 had already shifted international policy towards reproductive health and rights. The consumption argument answers the numbers argument: Connelly's observation that Asians with 2.1 children plus air conditioning and automobiles would affect the ecosystem more than a billion more subsistence farmers; a report by scientists from 105 institutions blaming the growing middle class in developed countries and the lavish lifestyles of the very rich; Ehrlich's call to halt the redistribution from poor to rich and control overconsumption; and Birara's observation that 20 per cent of humanity consumes 80 per cent of the world's goods and services while the poorest fifth consumes 1.3 per cent. Master formulation: impact is population multiplied by consumption per head.

## 21. HIV/AIDS as a development shock

In the early 1980s AIDS was thought mainly to affect homosexual men and users of unsterilized syringes, with most cases in wealthy western countries; campaigns on sexual health and condoms appeared to bring outbreaks under control, and ARV therapy slowed the virus. Because HIV takes an average of eight to ten years to develop into full-blown AIDS, it spread widely before recognition, and during the 1990s the world became aware that it had reached epidemic proportions in Africa and had spread to India, China and the former USSR. Barnett and Whiteside (2002) showed the epidemics differed: contaminated needles and the sale of blood at state-run collection points in China in the early 1990s, with the WHO estimating two-thirds of injections unsafe, officials suppressing the news and public admission coming only in 2003 of over a million HIV-positive citizens with infection rising 30 per cent a year; injecting drug users, especially in prison, in Russia and Ukraine; and unprotected heterosexual sex in southern Africa, where the first African cases were reported in a fishing village in south-west Uganda in the mid-1980s. Once around 5 per cent of adults are infected a general epidemic is likely. The scale: an International Crisis Group report of 2001 said the impact on Africa was as though it was involved in a major war; 3 million African deaths and 5 million infections in 2001; 29.4 million living with HIV or AIDS in Africa by 2003, about 70 per cent of the global total, with a further 3 million deaths that year; prevalence of almost 40 per cent of adults in Botswana and Swaziland, almost as high in Zimbabwe and 25 per cent in South Africa; life expectancy in southern Africa falling from the sixties in 1990 to the lower forties and to 33 in Zimbabwe; over a million orphans in Uganda and an estimated 20 million AIDS orphans in Africa by 2010. The economic transmission: a substantial proportion of the labour force lost with all its skills and experience, especially in farming and food production; the deaths of so many young women an irreplaceable loss to the domestic economy and child-rearing; and increased demand for carers withdrawing further labour. Why it was worse in poverty: little access to information and education; hunger reducing resistance; no ARV access; civil war producing refugees cut off from healthcare; stigma and reluctance to discuss sexual habits; and President Mbeki's refusal to accept the link between HIV and AIDS in South Africa. Responses: the ABC message; Brazil's campaign; Uganda's admission to the WHO in 1986, President Museveni's personal campaign, cheap condoms, voluntary testing and joint financing, cutting prevalence from a 20 per cent peak in 1991 to about 5 per cent by end-2003 with very few ARVs; Malawi's AIDS commission; the three-pronged requirement of campaigns, cheaper ARVs and modernised healthcare; and the Global Fund, the WHO and UNAIDS, with Kofi Annan "angry, distressed and helpless" in December 2003 at 40 million living with HIV, and the USA promising $15 billion over five years but specifying abstinence over condoms. By 2012 well over 30 million had died since 1981, an estimated 1.8 million in 2010 alone with nearly 15 million southern African children orphaned and around 2.7 million newly infected, spending having risen sixfold from 2002 to 2008, deaths declining and the global infected percentage apparently stabilising, though rates were still rising in eastern Europe. Master formulation: a working-age mortality shock removes exactly the cohort that produces output and raises the next generation, which is why development gains are reversible without war.

## 22. Explaining the century

Four explanations, each defeated by specific evidence. Structural dependency is supported by the 1900 role division, colonial price-fixing, one-product economies, the 1975-80 terms of trade, UNCTAD's failure, tied and interest-bearing aid, $500 billion of debt by 1980 and a North-South GNP ratio over 24 to 1 - and is defeated by the four tigers, by Libya against other producers, by defence spending above health spending and by Uganda against South Africa. Domestic capability is supported by those same divergences - and is defeated by India in 2011-12, by Russia's oil price falling from $140 to $40 in 2008 and by the 1970s commodity collapse. Policy regimes explain turning points: 1922, 1944, 1947, 1971, 1981, 1995 and 1999-2000. Physical limits explain the closing movement: exhaustion, soil, water, phosphates, the green revolution's ceiling and warming. The synthesis that survives: the external structure set the terms on which every economy had to operate, domestic capability determined what each made of them, and neither alone predicts an outcome. Four correlation cautions: population and poverty; globalisation and inequality; deregulation and 2008; inequality and political violence. Inequality must be discussed between countries - 24 times in 1989-90, $28,000 against $110 in 1992, Norway's 78.7 years against Sierra Leone's 35, the richest 1 per cent equalling the poorest 57 per cent in 2003 - and within them, with falling real wages in the USA, Japan and Germany and the OECD's June 2012 finding of record American long-term unemployment and only Chile, Mexico and Turkey more unequal among 34 states, against Brazil's poorest 50 per cent gaining almost 70 per cent. India's thread runs from Nehru's leadership of the non-aligned and the 92 nations at Havana in 1979, through green-revolution food exports, family-planning clinics and 35 per cent contraceptive use in 1986, healthcare below $12 a head in 1996, GM crops in 2004 and industrial relocation, to the triple transmission of 2011-12 and the IMF contribution of June 2012. Eight qualified theses, five verdict scaffolds and eighteen factual-risk controls close the session.

## Degraded and blank pages in the extraction

1. ⚠️ The pages carrying the opening of the Great Depression section and the transition from Hoover's measures to the start of the New Deal are blank or heavily degraded in the extraction. The material actually used from that region - the Reconstruction Finance Corporation, Hoover's individualism and the inauguration - is therefore either taken from the canonical owner's sourced summary or omitted. ❌ No Hoover-era measure is credited with recovery and no figure from those pages is reproduced.
2. ⚠️ The page carrying the first-phase codes and agencies of the New Deal is blank in the extraction. The NRA is attested by the opposition passage, which records that the Supreme Court ruled several measures including the NRA unconstitutional, and the public works and Social Security material is attested by the achievements passage. ⚠️ Lesson 5 therefore teaches industrial coordination from the acronym, its purpose and its fate, and states that limit rather than supplying an unsourced account of the agency's operation.
3. ⚠️ The passage describing the communist bloc's economic principles in section 27.1(b) is materially degraded, preserving only that the bloc consisted of the USSR and the states aligned with it and that the following forty or so years seemed like a contest. ❌ Lesson 9 accordingly asserts nothing about the bloc's economic record and states the limitation openly.
4. ⚠️ The passage recording Willy Brandt's years in office is degraded and renders them in a form that cannot be relied on. ⚠️ Lesson 10 describes him as a former West German chancellor, which the passage supports, and does not reproduce the years.
5. ⚠️ Parts of the sub-prime and capital-flow passages in section 27.7(b) are degraded. ⚠️ Only phrases legible with confidence are used, and the foreign-currency-reserve series that begins in that passage is not reproduced.
6. ⚠️ The passage giving the American diet's animal-product share in section 28.2(c) is degraded. ⚠️ Lesson 19 uses the cropland acreage figures and the vegetarian-diet projection, which are legible, and omits the degraded share.

## Discrepancies found in the evidence base and how they were handled

| Discrepancy | ⚠️ Handling |
|---|---|
| **The rescue-programme total.** Section 27.7(a) states a figure for the total taxpayer debt attributed to the American rescue programme in an official report of July 2009 whose order of magnitude cannot be reconciled with the chapter's own adjacent figures, including the $787 billion stimulus and the $63 trillion valuation of world stock markets | ⚠️ Lesson 16 teaches the **events** - the stakes acquired in named institutions, the conditions attached by Congress and the accusation of introducing socialism - and expressly does not assert the total. ❌ The figure appears nowhere in this session and a remediation entry warns against reproducing it |
| **The American unemployment measure for 1933.** The world-economy chapter states that about 25 million were out of work in the USA and as many as 50 million in the world as a whole, while the Depression chapter gives American unemployment as around 14 million, about a quarter of the total labour force | ⚠️ The two American figures cannot be measuring the same quantity and nothing held here establishes which definition each uses. ✅ Lessons 2, 3 and 5 and register notes 2, 3 and 6 teach the canonical measure - around 14 million, about a quarter of the labour force - which is also the only American unemployment figure permitted by the canonical owner's factual-risk control. ⚠️ The larger pair is retained only where Lesson 2 handles the divergence - its source-divergence note, its trap and its revision note - and once more in register note 2, and it is explicitly attributed to the account and marked as a divergent measure in every one of those places. ❌ No ratio between the American and world totals is derived anywhere, and the earlier inference that roughly half the world's recorded unemployment was American has been removed as unsupported |
| **The date of the Irish bailout.** The eurozone passage of section 27.7 places the Irish programme of 85 billion in November 2011, grouping it with the autumn-2011 Greek write-off | ⚠️ The programme was agreed in **November 2010**, a year before the date printed. ✅ Lesson 16, its revision notes and register note 16 carry the corrected month and year with the account's printed date shown alongside as a divergence, so that a learner who returns to the book is not confused. ❌ No other element of the eurozone sequence is altered |
| **The American unemployment measure for the end of 2010.** Section 27.7(c) records 15 million unemployed at the end of 2010 while section 27.8(e) gives 15 per cent at the same date | ⚠️ Lesson 16 uses the absolute figure in its own passage and does not assert the rate; the divergence is recorded rather than harmonised, and ❌ no third figure is inferred |
| **The quoted world-population comparison in section 28.3.** A quotation compares world population twenty years apart in figures that are arithmetically impossible against the chapter's own series of 5,300 million in 1990 and 7 billion at the end of 2011 | ⚠️ Lesson 20 teaches the substantive point - rising demand for food and energy against a growing population - without the quoted magnitudes, and the discrepancy is recorded here. ❌ No corrected figure is substituted |
| **The internal numbering of the HIV/AIDS section.** The section appears within Chapter 28 but carries a section number belonging to Chapter 27 | ⚠️ A printing or extraction slip. ⚠️ Lesson 21 treats the material as belonging to the population chapter, which its placement and subject matter establish, and does not cite a section number |
| **The date of the Rio conference of 2012.** Section 27.5 places it in June and section 28.3 places the associated report shortly before a July conference | ⚠️ Lesson 20 uses the year only and does not assert a month; the divergence is recorded rather than resolved |
| **The month of the Japanese tsunami of 2011.** Section 27.4(b) gives a month for the tsunami that flooded the Fukushima plant | ⚠️ Lesson 17 uses the year only and does not reproduce the month, since the bounded base offers no corroboration within itself |
| **Table 27.1.** The table is headed as gross national product per head in a single year but lists a series of years | ⚠️ The table is internally inconsistent and is **not used** anywhere in this session. ⚠️ The Japanese expansion it illustrates is not asserted |
| **The Chinese stimulus date.** Section 27.7(c) dates the Chinese fiscal decision in a way that sits awkwardly with the sequence of the surrounding narrative | ⚠️ Lesson 16 states the amount and the two-year horizon as recorded and does not build any argument on the month |
| **The account's own framing of terrorism's causes.** Sections 27.2(d) and 28.4 record a widespread belief, and separately a stated root cause, connecting terrorism to world economic inequality | ⚠️ Lessons 10 and 22 reproduce these as recorded beliefs and arguments with their attributions intact, and ❌ neither is converted into a causal finding. ⚠️ The substantive politics are owned by the topic on the new world order |

## The single bounded material-fact pass

⚠️ One verification pass was conducted for this session on 29 September 2026, limited to a finite list of standard connecting names and dates fixed before searching. ⚠️ The list was declared as: the Bretton Woods conference with its date, place, membership and two institutions, together with the founding year of the General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade and the commencement of the World Trade Organization; the end of dollar-gold convertibility and the subsequent currency realignment of 1971; the founding of the Organization of Petroleum Exporting Countries and the second oil shock of 1979; Malthus and Boserup with their works and dates; the International Conference on Population and Development of 1994; and the formal end of China's one-child policy. ⚠️ Each was needed because the bounded base either omits the item entirely or is damaged at exactly the point where it would appear, and its omission would leave a real syllabus gap. ⚠️ The minimum reliable source set was used, the pass stopped when the declared list was resolved, and no other item was pursued **within that pass**. ⚠️ One further and separately declared bounded extension was opened later, during the verification and repair of this artifact, solely to settle two items the artifact itself had got wrong; it is declared, listed and closed immediately below, and nothing beyond its two items was pursued.

| Item verified | Where used | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The Bretton Woods conference of July 1944 at Bretton Woods, New Hampshire, with delegates from 44 Allied nations, creating the International Monetary Fund and the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development | Lesson 7 sections 2-3; Map 1; register note 7 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the core account does not narrate the conference, while the canonical owner marks the institutional chronology as standard and cross-verifiable and the required scope names the Bretton Woods order expressly |
| The General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade signed in 1947, after the separately planned International Trade Organization was never established; the World Trade Organization commencing 1 January 1995 | Lesson 7 section 4; Map 1; register note 7 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the core account supplies the 1995 succession but not the Agreement's founding year, and the distinction between an agreement and an organization with a standing dispute mechanism is examinable |
| The end of dollar-gold convertibility announced on 15 August 1971 and the currency realignment of December 1971 | Consulted; ❌ **not asserted in the teaching text** | ✅ Verified but deliberately unused. ⚠️ The canonical owner forbids constructing a gold-standard or monetary-regime mechanism from this folder's evidence, so Lesson 7 teaches only the account's own record - annual deficits covered by selling gold reserves, a weakening dollar, and the devaluation of about 12 per cent with a 10 per cent import duty in 1971 - and states the evidence boundary openly |
| The Organization of Petroleum Exporting Countries founded at a conference in Baghdad in September 1960 by Iran, Iraq, Kuwait, Saudi Arabia and Venezuela | Lesson 12 section 2; Map 1; register note 12 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the core account introduces the organisation only at the moment of the 1973 price rise, leaving a body whose founding a close-option question would test unsourced |
| The second oil shock of 1979, associated with the Iranian revolution and the sharp reduction in Iranian output | Lesson 12 section 6; Map 1; register note 12 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the core account records the 1973 decision but not the second shock, and the required scope names the oil shocks in the plural |
| Thomas Robert Malthus, *An Essay on the Principle of Population* (1798); Ester Boserup, *The Conditions of Agricultural Growth* (1965) | Lesson 18 section 6; register note 18 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the required scope names Malthus and Boserup where owned, while the core account supplies the substantive evidence on both sides - the green revolution and its ceiling - without naming either pole of the debate |
| The International Conference on Population and Development, Cairo, 1994, and its shift from demographic targets towards reproductive health and rights | Lesson 20 section 6; register note 20 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the core account's population-policy narrative runs from 1925 to the one-child policy and the consumption debate without recording the international turning point that the welfare route's victory produced |
| The formal end of China's one-child policy: a universal two-child policy announced in October 2015 and effective from 2016 | Lesson 20 section 6; register note 20 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the core account records only that the government eventually softened the policy while claiming it had been justified, leaving a reader unable to date the change |

⚠️ **Items identified but deliberately not pursued:** any quota, voting weight, lending figure or programme name for the Fund or the Bank; any inflation, unemployment or output series for the 1970s that would support a stagflation mechanism; any source establishing that producer surpluses financed developing-world borrowing; any substitute for the irreconcilable rescue-programme total; any corrected figure for the impossible population quotation; any named attribution for the demographic transition model; and any post-2012 economic or demographic datum other than the single declared current linkage. ⚠️ Each is either recorded below as an open gap, forbidden by a canonical control, or a magnitude the bounded base cannot settle, and none is filled by inference or memory. ⚠️ That list governs the original pass; the only additions to this session's external verification since then are the two items declared in the correction extension immediately below.

## The declared bounded correction extension

⚠️ A second and strictly bounded verification was opened on 29 September 2026, during the validation and repair of this artifact, and only because two dates already written into the session were wrong. ⚠️ The extension was declared before searching and limited to exactly two items: the date of the Irish assistance programme of 85 billion, and the first publication date of Rawls's *A Theory of Justice*. ⚠️ It was opened for correction, not for enrichment: nothing new is taught as a result, no new figure, name, institution or argument enters the session, and the extension closed when both items were settled. ⚠️ No third item was checked, and the not-pursued list above continues to hold unchanged.

| Item | Result | Verifying source | Effect in this session |
|---|---|---|---|
| The date of the Irish assistance programme of 85 billion | ✅ Ireland requested assistance on **21 November 2010** and the 85 billion programme was agreed in **November 2010**, with the agreements finalised in December 2010; the external component of 67.5 billion comprised 22.5 billion each from the European Financial Stability Facility, the European Financial Stabilisation Mechanism and the International Monetary Fund, with bilateral loans from the United Kingdom, Sweden and Denmark, and Ireland itself contributed 17.5 billion | European Commission summary of financial assistance to Ireland, published on EUR-Lex; International Monetary Fund, *IMF Survey*, 16 December 2010, recording approval of the Fund's 22.5 billion loan | ✅ The corrected month and year are taught in Lesson 16, its revision notes and register note 16, each showing the account's printed November 2011 alongside. ❌ The composition of the package and the December 2010 signature are **not** taught anywhere; they are recorded here only as the evidence on which the correction rests |
| The first publication date of Rawls, *A Theory of Justice* | ✅ First published by **Harvard University Press in 1971**; a revised edition followed in 1999 | Harvard University Press, publisher's record for *A Theory of Justice*, revised edition | ✅ Lesson 8, its revision notes and register note 8 now carry 1971 as the date of the work and 1973 as the date of the edition the account cites. ⚠️ The publisher and series of that 1973 edition were **not** verified and are therefore not named anywhere; the session says only that the account cites a 1973 edition |

## Open gaps recorded rather than filled

1. ⚠️ **The communist bloc's economic record.** The passage describing its principles is materially degraded in the extraction, and the Soviet and Chinese economic records are owned by other topics. Lesson 9 states the limitation openly, teaches what the evidence does support - the shared armament and space expenditure and the contemporary opportunity-cost argument - and hands the comparison across. ❌ No claim about planned-economy performance appears anywhere in this session.
2. ⚠️ **The first-phase New Deal agencies.** The page carrying the codes and agencies is blank in the extraction. Lesson 5 teaches industrial coordination from the acronym, its purpose and its constitutional fate, all of which are attested elsewhere in the chapter, and records the gap rather than narrating the agency's operation.
3. ⚠️ **The Hoover-era measures.** The pages covering them are blank or heavily degraded. The canonical owner's sourced judgement that Hoover's efforts failed to make a significant impression is used to date the change of doctrine, and ❌ no Hoover measure is credited with recovery and no figure from those pages is reproduced.
4. ⚠️ **The monetary regime of the interwar and postwar periods.** The sources held do not discuss the gold standard, convertibility or the sequence of departures from it, and their only gold reference is the sale of American reserves in the late 1960s recorded in the postwar chapter. ❌ No gold-standard mechanism is constructed anywhere, the Italian currency case is used as evidence of the choice between external and internal devaluation, and the boundary is stated in the teaching text of Lessons 4 and 7.
5. ⚠️ **Climate policy after the definition.** The core account narrates conferences, protocols, targets and ratification decisions. ❌ None appears here, under the canonical owner's explicit control that climate figures, targets, treaties and conference outcomes belong to the environment-and-ecology owner, dated and sourced separately. ⚠️ This is a deliberate bounded omission, not an oversight, and it is stated in Lesson 17.
6. ⚠️ **The second dated population anchor.** The advanced owner records a United Nations population estimate and projection alongside the IMF item as the topic's current anchors. ⚠️ Only one current linkage is carried by this session, and the economic item was selected because the canonical basic owner separately forbids supplying population figures. ❌ The second anchor is therefore not reproduced, and its omission is recorded here rather than concealed.
7. ⚠️ **India's 1991 liberalisation.** Owned by the economy topic. ⚠️ Lesson 22 states the cross-reference and the world-history use - that post-Cold-War globalisation pulled states more deeply into world markets - and ❌ reproduces no reform content.
8. ⚠️ **Quantitative series beyond the dated figures held.** ❌ No growth, output, trade, population, fertility, life-expectancy, debt or prevalence series is asserted beyond the specific dated figures the sources state, each carried with its year and, where applicable, its reporting body. ❌ No regional or world aggregate is constructed, averaged or updated anywhere.
9. ⚠️ **The Islamism section of the population chapter.** The core account devotes a section of Chapter 28 to population growth and Islamism, including the clash-of-civilizations debate and its critics. ❌ That material is not used here, because its substance is owned by the topic on the new world order; only its economic sentence on the root causes of terrorism is referred to, and it is attributed rather than asserted.
10. ⚠️ **Post-2012 developments.** The core account closes at 2012 and the canonical owners carry only the dated current items recorded above. ❌ No claim about any economy, population or policy after 2012 is asserted anywhere in this session, except the single dated current linkage in Lesson 15 and the two verified population-policy dates of 1994 and 2015 recorded in the material-fact pass.
